# Extracted Student Misconceptions

**Model:** gpt-5.4-mini
**Topic group:** mds_IMFs
**Total misconceptions:** 636
**Papers processed:** 62

## Misconception 1: Students treat properties such as pressure, temperature, boiling point, structure of an atom, and electron configuration as if they are not consistently categorized; the paper notes opposite answers for similar items, indicating confusion about which are macroscopic versus sub-microscopic.

**Topic:** Properties of matter / Macroscopic vs sub-microscopic properties
**Correct concept:** Macroscopic properties are bulk, observable properties; sub-microscopic properties concern particles and their structure/configuration.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should ask whether the property describes the bulk sample that can be directly observed/measured (macroscopic) or the particles/particles' arrangement and electron structure (sub-microscopic).
**Concept category:** surface features
**Prevalence:** About half of students (average performance in Question 1: 58% correct; 40–64% correct by item)
**Population:** BSc first-, second-, and third-year chemistry students (n=148 total; 46 first year, 55 second year, 47 third year)
**Source:** Students' Conceptions about the Sub-Microscopic Approach to Explanations in Chemistry Throughout their BSc Degree Course (08/08.md)
**Evidence:** "About a half of the students tested had difficulty in answering Question 1 correctly"

## Misconception 2: Students think that a statement like 'copper metal conducts electricity because all metals conduct electricity' is an explanation rather than just a description or classification.

**Topic:** Explanation vs description / Chemical explanations
**Correct concept:** An explanation must give the mechanism at the particle level, not merely cite a general rule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish a general descriptive statement from a mechanism-based explanation by asking whether the statement says why at the particle level; for metal conduction, the explanation should refer to electron flow in metals.
**Concept category:** explanation vs description
**Prevalence:** Two-thirds of students (Question 2(d))
**Population:** BSc first-, second-, and third-year chemistry students
**Source:** Students' Conceptions about the Sub-Microscopic Approach to Explanations in Chemistry Throughout their BSc Degree Course (08/08.md)
**Evidence:** "two-thirds of the students thought erroneously that 'copper metal conducts electricity because all metals conduct electricity' was an explanatory statement."

## Misconception 3: Students do not know that liquid water consists of H2O molecules; some may not represent liquid water at the molecular level correctly.

**Topic:** Particle nature of substances / Liquid water
**Correct concept:** Liquid water is made of H2O molecules as the stable particles present.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the substance and recall its stable molecular particles; for liquid water, the particles are intact H2O molecules, not separate atoms or ions.
**Concept category:** particle identity
**Prevalence:** More than 60% incorrect
**Population:** BSc first-, second-, and third-year chemistry students
**Source:** Students' Conceptions about the Sub-Microscopic Approach to Explanations in Chemistry Throughout their BSc Degree Course (08/08.md)
**Evidence:** "more than 60 % of the students tested did not know that the particles that exist in liquid water (Question 3(c)) ... are H2O molecules."

## Misconception 4: Students believe that water vapour contains particles other than intact H2O molecules, including H2 and O2 molecules or H atoms and O atoms, or that H2O decomposes on boiling.

**Topic:** Particle nature of substances / Water vapour
**Correct concept:** Water vapour consists of H2O molecules; boiling is a phase change, not decomposition.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that a phase change changes only particle spacing/arrangement, not chemical identity; therefore molecules remain H2O in the vapour.
**Concept category:** phase change / particle identity
**Prevalence:** More than 60% incorrect on related item(s); Question 4 average 24% correct
**Population:** BSc first-, second-, and third-year chemistry students
**Source:** Students' Conceptions about the Sub-Microscopic Approach to Explanations in Chemistry Throughout their BSc Degree Course (08/08.md)
**Evidence:** "The main error in Question 4 was due to students thinking that H2O molecules in liquid water decompose into H2 and H2 0 molecules when it is boiled."

## Misconception 5: Students consider only the solute particles in aqueous solutions and do not recognize that water molecules are also present in aqueous sodium chloride and glucose solutions.

**Topic:** Aqueous solutions / Solute vs solvent particles
**Correct concept:** Aqueous solutions contain H2O molecules along with the dissolved solute particles.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that 'aqueous' means the solvent is water, so water molecules remain in the solution as part of the particle mixture.
**Concept category:** solution particles
**Prevalence:** Described as the main source of error; Question 3(f) and 3(g) were very poorly answered (overall average for Question 3: 19% correct)
**Population:** BSc first-, second-, and third-year chemistry students
**Source:** Students' Conceptions about the Sub-Microscopic Approach to Explanations in Chemistry Throughout their BSc Degree Course (08/08.md)
**Evidence:** "the main source of error was due to students not recognizing that aqueous solutions always contain H2O molecules: they considered only the solute particles."

## Misconception 6: Students think molecules are distributed uniformly between the liquid and vapour phases in a closed vessel at equilibrium.

**Topic:** Liquid-vapour equilibrium / Particle distribution in coexisting phases
**Correct concept:** At liquid-vapour equilibrium, the same substance is present in both phases, but particle density is much higher in the liquid than in the vapour; the distribution is not uniform across phases.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason from phase density: particles are closely packed in the liquid and much more dispersed in the vapour, even though exchange balances at equilibrium.
**Concept category:** particle arrangement
**Prevalence:** Majority of students chose the incorrect uniform-distribution option; only about a quarter correct on Question 5
**Population:** BSc first-, second-, and third-year chemistry students
**Source:** Students' Conceptions about the Sub-Microscopic Approach to Explanations in Chemistry Throughout their BSc Degree Course (08/08.md)
**Evidence:** "The majority thought, surprisingly, that the molecules are distributed uniformly in the liquid and vapour phases (response (e))."

## Misconception 7: Students think the main reason a gas expands on heating at constant pressure is simply the increase in temperature or the increase in molecular speed.

**Topic:** Heating gases / Reason for gas expansion
**Correct concept:** Heating increases molecular speed, which leads to greater spacing between molecules; the increase in volume is due to increased space between molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect higher temperature to faster molecular motion, then infer that faster motion at constant pressure requires a larger average separation, increasing gas volume.
**Concept category:** gas behavior
**Prevalence:** Only about 15% correct on Question 10
**Population:** BSc first-, second-, and third-year chemistry students
**Source:** Students' Conceptions about the Sub-Microscopic Approach to Explanations in Chemistry Throughout their BSc Degree Course (08/08.md)
**Evidence:** "The majority of students thought erroneously that response (e) was correct. Increase in temperature cannot, however, be considered to be the reason for the increase in gas volume when it is heated: it is an experimental fact. Similarly, increase in the speed of the molecules (response (c)) is not the main reason for the increase in volume."

## Misconception 8: Students think the bonds that break when ice melts are covalent O-H bonds within H2O molecules.

**Topic:** Phase changes / Melting ice
**Correct concept:** Melting breaks intermolecular forces between water molecules, not the covalent bonds inside each molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish intramolecular bonds, which hold atoms together within a molecule, from intermolecular bonds, which hold molecules together; melting disrupts the latter only.
**Concept category:** intermolecular vs intramolecular bonds
**Prevalence:** More than half incorrect; Question 11 average 42% correct
**Population:** BSc first-, second-, and third-year chemistry students
**Source:** Students' Conceptions about the Sub-Microscopic Approach to Explanations in Chemistry Throughout their BSc Degree Course (08/08.md)
**Evidence:** "More than half of the students tested had difficulty and they thought that the bonds that break when ice melts are covalent O-H bonds within the molecules."

## Misconception 9: Students write reaction equations in molecular form (e.g. HCl + NaOH -> NaCl + H2O) when the question asks for the particles actually involved, i.e. ions/electrons.

**Topic:** Chemical equations / Ionic reactions vs molecular equations
**Correct concept:** For ionic reactions, equations should be written using the reacting ions and relevant particles only, such as H+ and OH- for acid-base neutralization.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the species actually present and changing in solution, omit spectator ions, and write the balanced ionic equation based on particles involved.
**Concept category:** particle-level equations
**Prevalence:** About 70% gave the molecular equation instead of the requested particle-level equation for Question 12(d)
**Population:** BSc first-, second-, and third-year chemistry students
**Source:** Students' Conceptions about the Sub-Microscopic Approach to Explanations in Chemistry Throughout their BSc Degree Course (08/08.md)
**Evidence:** "Most students wrote the equations correctly in terms of molecules. For example, about 70 % of them wrote correctly the equation for the reaction between hydrochloric acid and sodium hydroxide using molecules (HCl + NaOH → NaCl + H2O)."

## Misconception 10: Students do not write reactions in terms of bonds broken and formed, missing that chemical change involves breaking and making specific covalent bonds.

**Topic:** Chemical equations / Bond changes in reactions
**Correct concept:** Chemical reactions can be represented by counting bonds broken in reactants and bonds formed in products.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should translate each molecule into its bond structure, then count bonds broken and formed while conserving atoms.
**Concept category:** bond changes
**Prevalence:** Less than 10% correct on Question 13
**Population:** BSc first-, second-, and third-year chemistry students
**Source:** Students' Conceptions about the Sub-Microscopic Approach to Explanations in Chemistry Throughout their BSc Degree Course (08/08.md)
**Evidence:** "The very poor performance was due to students not writing the given balanced equations in terms of the bonds in the reactant and product molecules."

## Misconception 11: Students cannot write the half-equation for removal of two electrons from a copper atom.

**Topic:** Chemical reactions / Copper oxidation
**Correct concept:** Removing two electrons from copper is Cu -> Cu2+ + 2e-.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that electron loss produces a positively charged ion and balance charge and electrons on both sides.
**Concept category:** electron transfer
**Prevalence:** About 30% could not answer part 12(a)
**Population:** BSc first-, second-, and third-year chemistry students
**Source:** Students' Conceptions about the Sub-Microscopic Approach to Explanations in Chemistry Throughout their BSc Degree Course (08/08.md)
**Evidence:** "it was surprising that they could not write the simple equation for the removal of two electrons from a copper atom (Cu → Cu2+ + 2e-)."

## Misconception 12: Students believe solid NaCl conducts electricity simply because it contains Na+ and Cl- ions.

**Topic:** Electrical conductivity of ionic compounds / Solid sodium chloride
**Correct concept:** Solid NaCl does not conduct electricity because its ions are not mobile; conductivity requires mobile ions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish the presence of ions from their mobility: in a solid lattice the ions are fixed, so current cannot flow.
**Concept category:** ionic conduction
**Prevalence:** About 54% correct, so about 46% held the misconception
**Population:** BSc first-, second-, and third-year chemistry students
**Source:** Students' Conceptions about the Sub-Microscopic Approach to Explanations in Chemistry Throughout their BSc Degree Course (08/08.md)
**Evidence:** "(b) Solid NaCl consists of Na+ ions and Cl– ions and hence will conduct electricity"

## Misconception 13: Students do not correctly relate molecular mass to molecular speed, or cannot order gases by speed and density using mass reasoning.

**Topic:** Molecular properties and gas laws / Molecular speed vs mass
**Correct concept:** At the same temperature, lighter gas molecules move faster than heavier ones; gas density at fixed temperature and pressure increases with molecular mass.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare relative molecular masses: lower mass means higher average speed and lower density under the same T and P.
**Concept category:** kinetic molecular theory
**Prevalence:** Only about 20% correct on Question 15
**Population:** BSc first-, second-, and third-year chemistry students
**Source:** Students' Conceptions about the Sub-Microscopic Approach to Explanations in Chemistry Throughout their BSc Degree Course (08/08.md)
**Evidence:** "Student performance in the next question, which tests explanation of molecular speed and gas density in terms of the mass of the molecules, was very poor."

## Misconception 14: Students struggle to connect molecular shape and intermolecular forces to density and melting point in packed hydrocarbon solids.

**Topic:** Molecular structure and physical properties / Packing and intermolecular forces
**Correct concept:** Molecular shape affects packing efficiency and intermolecular interactions, which in turn affect density and melting point.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare linear vs branched molecules: better packing usually gives higher density and, depending on packing/intermolecular contacts, affects melting point.
**Concept category:** structure-property relationships
**Prevalence:** About half correct; about half incorrect on Question 16
**Population:** BSc first-, second-, and third-year chemistry students
**Source:** Students' Conceptions about the Sub-Microscopic Approach to Explanations in Chemistry Throughout their BSc Degree Course (08/08.md)
**Evidence:** "Though Question 16 appears to be more difficult than Question 15, because its solution needs the correlation of density/melting point with some properties of molecules and intermolecular forces, student performance was much better."

## Misconception 15: Some first-year students do not recognize that a functional group can affect a molecule's boiling point, implying they think compounds with the same carbon atoms should have the same boiling point or that functional group differences are not important.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and boiling point / Functional groups and hydrogen bonding
**Correct concept:** Functional groups can change the type and strength of intermolecular forces, which changes boiling point; alcohols boil higher than ethers because alcohols can form hydrogen bonds while ethers cannot.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the functional groups, identify whether hydrogen bonding is possible, and then infer that stronger intermolecular forces lead to a higher boiling point.
**Concept category:** boiling point factors / functional group effects
**Prevalence:** 50% of first-year students answered correctly; 100% of third-year students answered correctly
**Population:** First-year (2023) and third-year (2021) chemistry education students; 10 students in each cohort
**Source:** University Students' Understanding of Intermolecular Forces: A Comparison of First & Third-Year Students (1.+Vol+1+Issue+1/1.+Vol+1+Issue+1.md)
**Evidence:** "Some first-year students seem unaware that a functional group can affect a molecule's boiling point."

## Misconception 16: Several first-year students believe that the most volatile compounds are the ones with the highest boiling points and molar masses, and that higher molar mass means lower boiling point because the intermolecular bonds are weaker in larger nonpolar hydrocarbon molecules.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and boiling point / Molar mass / volatility / nonpolar molecules
**Correct concept:** Higher molar mass and greater polarizability generally strengthen London dispersion forces, which can raise boiling point; nonpolar molecules still experience van der Waals (dispersion) forces.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should relate molecular size and electron cloud polarizability to dispersion forces, then predict that stronger dispersion forces usually increase boiling point rather than decrease it.
**Concept category:** boiling point factors / molar mass and dispersion
**Prevalence:** 50% of first-year students answered correctly; 100% of third-year students answered correctly
**Population:** First-year (2023) and third-year (2021) chemistry education students; 10 students in each cohort
**Source:** University Students' Understanding of Intermolecular Forces: A Comparison of First & Third-Year Students (1.+Vol+1+Issue+1/1.+Vol+1+Issue+1.md)
**Evidence:** "Several first-year students assume that the most volatile compounds are the compounds with the highest boiling points and molar masses."

## Misconception 17: Some students failed to connect larger atomic size with easier polarization, suggesting they do not reason that a larger iodine atom is more polarizable than bromine because its valence electrons are less tightly held by the nucleus.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and boiling point / Polarizability, atomic size, and steric effects
**Correct concept:** A larger atom with more weakly held valence electrons is more easily polarized, which strengthens London dispersion forces and can affect boiling point.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare atomic size, infer electron-cloud polarizability, and then connect greater polarizability to stronger dispersion forces and higher boiling point.
**Concept category:** dispersion forces / polarizability
**Prevalence:** 70% of third-year students answered correctly; 20% of first-year students answered correctly
**Population:** First-year (2023) and third-year (2021) chemistry education students; 10 students in each cohort
**Source:** University Students' Understanding of Intermolecular Forces: A Comparison of First & Third-Year Students (1.+Vol+1+Issue+1/1.+Vol+1+Issue+1.md)
**Evidence:** "They failed to realize that the size of the iodine atom is greater than the bromine atom, so the valence electron in the iodine atom is weakly bound by the nucleus more than the bromine atom."

## Misconception 18: Some first-year students appear to think that the higher the hydrocarbon compound's molar mass, the lower its boiling point because of weaker bonds between hydrocarbon molecules and their nonpolar character, rather than recognizing that nonpolar molecules still interact through van der Waals forces.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Molecular polarity and intermolecular interactions
**Correct concept:** Nonpolar molecules do experience London dispersion forces; as molar mass and polarizability increase, these forces generally become stronger and can increase boiling point.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the molecule as nonpolar, then apply London dispersion forces rather than dismissing intermolecular interactions altogether.
**Concept category:** nonpolar molecules / van der Waals forces
**Prevalence:** 50% of first-year students answered correctly
**Population:** First-year (2023) chemistry education students; 10 students in the cohort
**Source:** University Students' Understanding of Intermolecular Forces: A Comparison of First & Third-Year Students (1.+Vol+1+Issue+1/1.+Vol+1+Issue+1.md)
**Evidence:** "They failed to recognize that the van der Waals forces exist in non-polar molecules."

## Misconception 19: Some students did not identify London dispersion forces as the interaction between methane molecules, implying they may think nonpolar molecules have no intermolecular forces or choose stronger polar interactions instead.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Methane and nonpolar molecules
**Correct concept:** Methane is nonpolar, so the dominant intermolecular force between methane molecules is London dispersion force.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should determine that methane is nonpolar, then select London dispersion forces as the appropriate intermolecular interaction.
**Concept category:** London dispersion force / nonpolar molecules
**Prevalence:** 20% of first-year students answered correctly; 50% of third-year students answered correctly
**Population:** First-year (2023) and third-year (2021) chemistry education students; 10 students in each cohort
**Source:** University Students' Understanding of Intermolecular Forces: A Comparison of First & Third-Year Students (1.+Vol+1+Issue+1/1.+Vol+1+Issue+1.md)
**Evidence:** "the interaction between the methane molecules is London dispersion forces because methane is a nonpolar molecule."

## Misconception 20: Students may incorrectly think hydrogen bonding occurs whenever a molecule contains hydrogen atoms, rather than only when hydrogen is covalently bonded to a highly electronegative atom and interacts with a lone pair on another electronegative atom.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Requirements for hydrogen bonding
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding requires H covalently bonded to N, O, or F and attraction to a lone pair on another electronegative atom.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should inspect the bonding environment of hydrogen and verify whether the donor atom is sufficiently electronegative before concluding that hydrogen bonding is possible.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding / hydrogen-bond donor requirement
**Population:** Students discussed in cited prior research (Cooper et al., 2015; related studies mentioned in the paper)
**Source:** University Students' Understanding of Intermolecular Forces: A Comparison of First & Third-Year Students (1.+Vol+1+Issue+1/1.+Vol+1+Issue+1.md)
**Evidence:** "Students assume hydrogen bonds involve hydrogen atoms, occurring when a C atom binds to an H atom in the molecule."

## Misconception 21: Heating water breaks the covalent bond between the O and H atoms in the water molecule.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Phase change and bond breaking in water
**Correct concept:** Phase changes such as heating, melting, or evaporation do not break intramolecular covalent O–H bonds in water; they overcome intermolecular attractions between molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish intramolecular bonds from intermolecular forces: heating supplies energy to separate water molecules from each other during a phase change, but the O–H covalent bonds within each molecule remain intact unless a chemical reaction occurs.
**Concept category:** phase change / intramolecular vs intermolecular
**Prevalence:** 24.6% of 69 students
**Population:** Grade XI high school students (n=69 for the IFDI misconception table; overall class XI sample n=101)
**Source:** 2.2 Research instruments (10.1201_9781003261346-19_chapterpdf/10.1201_9781003261346-19_chapterpdf.md)
**Evidence:** "there was a covalent bond break between the O and H atoms in the water molecule due to heating"

## Misconception 22: Intermolecular forces are stronger than intramolecular forces.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Comparison of intermolecular and intramolecular forces
**Correct concept:** Intramolecular bonds are stronger than intermolecular forces; intermolecular forces are weaker attractions between molecules compared with covalent/ionic bonds within molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the kinds of interactions: bonds holding atoms together inside a molecule require more energy to break than the attractions between separate molecules, so intermolecular forces cannot be stronger than intramolecular bonds.
**Concept category:** intermolecular vs intramolecular
**Prevalence:** 23.1% of 69 students
**Population:** Grade XI high school students (n=69 for the IFDI misconception table; overall class XI sample n=101)
**Source:** 2.2 Research instruments (10.1201_9781003261346-19_chapterpdf/10.1201_9781003261346-19_chapterpdf.md)
**Evidence:** "Intermolecular force is stronger than intramolecular force"

## Misconception 23: The concave shape of a liquid surface is caused by dipole–dipole intermolecular force.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Liquid surface shape
**Correct concept:** The shape of a liquid surface (e.g., meniscus) is determined by the balance of adhesive and cohesive forces with the container surface, not simply by dipole–dipole attraction alone.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason from the interactions between the liquid and the container: if adhesion and cohesion differ, the liquid surface curves accordingly; the shape is not directly explained by naming one type of intermolecular force without considering the solid–liquid interactions and relative strengths.
**Concept category:** surface phenomena / intermolecular forces
**Prevalence:** 29.2% of 69 students
**Population:** Grade XI high school students (n=69 for the IFDI misconception table; overall class XI sample n=101)
**Source:** 2.2 Research instruments (10.1201_9781003261346-19_chapterpdf/10.1201_9781003261346-19_chapterpdf.md)
**Evidence:** "the surface shape of concave fluids is influenced by dipole–dipole intermolecular force"

## Misconception 24: During melting and freezing, the volumes of ice and water are the same.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Melting and freezing of water
**Correct concept:** Ice and liquid water have different volumes for the same mass because water expands on freezing and contracts on melting; density changes accompany the phase change.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare densities of the two phases: because the arrangement of water molecules in ice is more open than in liquid water, a given mass occupies a larger volume as ice than as liquid water.
**Concept category:** phase change / density and volume
**Prevalence:** 27.7% of 69 students
**Population:** Grade XI high school students (n=69 for the IFDI misconception table; overall class XI sample n=101)
**Source:** 2.2 Research instruments (10.1201_9781003261346-19_chapterpdf/10.1201_9781003261346-19_chapterpdf.md)
**Evidence:** "in the smelting and freezing process, the volume of ice and water is the same"

## Misconception 25: Substances with strong intermolecular forces have large vapor pressures.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Vapor pressure and intermolecular force strength
**Correct concept:** Stronger intermolecular forces lead to lower vapor pressure because molecules are less able to escape from the liquid into the gas phase.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect vapor pressure to molecular escape from the liquid surface: if attractions between molecules are strong, fewer molecules leave the liquid, so vapor pressure is lower, not higher.
**Concept category:** vapor pressure / intermolecular forces
**Prevalence:** 26.2% of 69 students
**Population:** Grade XI high school students (n=69 for the IFDI misconception table; overall class XI sample n=101)
**Source:** 2.2 Research instruments (10.1201_9781003261346-19_chapterpdf/10.1201_9781003261346-19_chapterpdf.md)
**Evidence:** "substances that have strong intermolecular forces have large vapor pressures"

## Misconception 26: Students think that a substance's higher boiling point can be explained without linking it to stronger intermolecular forces and the extra energy required to overcome them, or they give only a surface-level mention of hydrogen bonding with no causal mechanism.

**Topic:** Boiling point trends and intermolecular forces / Hydrogen bonding as evidence for higher boiling point
**Correct concept:** A higher boiling point is caused by stronger intermolecular forces; molecules with stronger attractions require more energy to separate before boiling occurs.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the compound with stronger intermolecular forces, explain that these stronger attractions make molecules harder to separate, and state that more energy is needed to overcome those interactions, leading to a higher boiling point.
**Concept category:** reasoning about intermolecular forces
**Prevalence:** 40% of Cohort 1 provided no explanation or non-normative responses; 50% of Cohort 4 gave the most sophisticated reasoning including energy
**Population:** General chemistry students across four cohorts (Cohort 1 N=119; Cohort 2 N=106; Cohort 3 N=86; Cohort 4 N=70)
**Source:** Investigating the impact of three-dimensional learning interventions on student understanding of structure-property relationships (10290156/10290156.md)
**Evidence:** "students may be able to easily make a claim, they can struggle with identifying appropriate evidence or being able to clearly link evidence to the claim through reasoning"

## Misconception 27: Students incorrectly choose dimethyl ether or cannot predict the higher-boiling compound when comparing ethanol and dimethyl ether.

**Topic:** Boiling point trends and intermolecular forces / Correct boiling point ranking for ethanol vs dimethyl ether
**Correct concept:** Ethanol has the higher boiling point than dimethyl ether because ethanol can form hydrogen bonds in addition to dipole-dipole interactions and London dispersion forces.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the intermolecular forces present in each compound, recognize that ethanol has hydrogen bonding while dimethyl ether does not, and conclude that ethanol’s stronger overall intermolecular attraction gives it the higher boiling point.
**Concept category:** claim selection / boiling point ranking
**Prevalence:** In Cohort 1, 35% selected ethanol as the correct claim before intervention; this increased to 52% in Cohort 2 and 80% in Cohort 3
**Population:** General chemistry students in the structure-property assessment at the end of GC2
**Source:** Investigating the impact of three-dimensional learning interventions on student understanding of structure-property relationships (10290156/10290156.md)
**Evidence:** "The majority of students in the control group incorrectly identified the claim"

## Misconception 28: Students draw hydrogen bonding within a molecule as if it were a covalent bond between H and O rather than as an intermolecular, non-covalent attraction between separate molecules.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces representations / Hydrogen bonding between ethanol molecules
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is a non-covalent interaction between different molecules, not a bond within one molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify donor and acceptor sites, then draw the hydrogen bond between molecules with a dashed or dotted line to show an intermolecular attraction rather than a covalent bond inside the ethanol molecule.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces vs bonding
**Prevalence:** Less than 20% of Cohort 1 correctly represented hydrogen bonding as between molecules; 52% of Cohort 2; 57% of Cohort 3; 79% of Cohort 4
**Population:** General chemistry students drawing hydrogen bonding for three ethanol molecules
**Source:** Investigating the impact of three-dimensional learning interventions on student understanding of structure-property relationships (10290156/10290156.md)
**Evidence:** "students have difficulty differentiating between bonding and intermolecular forces"

## Misconception 29: Students think covalent bonds are broken during the boiling process.

**Topic:** Boiling point and phase changes / What is broken during boiling
**Correct concept:** Boiling separates molecules by overcoming intermolecular forces; covalent bonds within molecules are not broken when a liquid boils.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish intramolecular covalent bonds from intermolecular attractions and reason that heat supplied during boiling is used to overcome intermolecular forces between molecules, not to break covalent bonds inside molecules.
**Concept category:** phase change / bonding vs intermolecular forces
**Population:** Students answering questions on hydrogen bonding and boiling point explanations
**Source:** Investigating the impact of three-dimensional learning interventions on student understanding of structure-property relationships (10290156/10290156.md)
**Evidence:** "along with the misunderstanding that covalent bonds are broken during the boiling process"

## Misconception 30: Students omit or fail to use the idea that stronger intermolecular forces require more energy to overcome, or they do not connect energy to the boiling point difference.

**Topic:** Boiling point explanations / Energy requirement for stronger interactions
**Correct concept:** Stronger intermolecular forces require more energy to overcome, which leads to a higher boiling point.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare interaction strengths, state that stronger attractions are harder to overcome, and infer that more energy is needed to boil the substance with stronger intermolecular forces.
**Concept category:** energy and intermolecular forces
**Prevalence:** 17-33% for Cohorts 1-3 versus 50% for Cohort 4
**Population:** Students responding to Question 6 on the structure-property assessment
**Source:** Investigating the impact of three-dimensional learning interventions on student understanding of structure-property relationships (10290156/10290156.md)
**Evidence:** "students were more likely than Cohorts 1-3 to also include the ideas of energy needed to overcome stronger forces for a more sophisticated explanation"

## Misconception 31: Students explain boiling point by saying one substance has 'more bonds' that must be broken to boil.

**Topic:** Boiling point explanations / Non-normative bond-counting explanation
**Correct concept:** Boiling point depends on intermolecular force strength, not on having more covalent bonds to break.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should not count bonds in the molecule; instead, they should focus on the strength and type of intermolecular forces between molecules and the energy required to overcome them.
**Concept category:** non-normative reasoning
**Population:** Students coded as giving non-normative reasoning in Question 6
**Source:** Investigating the impact of three-dimensional learning interventions on student understanding of structure-property relationships (10290156/10290156.md)
**Evidence:** "Because ethanol will have more bonds than dimethyl ether, therefore have more bonds to break to reach a boiling point"

## Misconception 32: Students think the relative boiling point can always be predicted only by the simple rule that a molecule with hydrogen bonding must boil higher than one with only London dispersion forces, and they do not apply this to exceptions where a larger molecule with stronger LDFs can boil higher than a smaller molecule with weak H-bonding.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / London dispersion forces and molecular size
**Correct concept:** Boiling point depends on the overall balance of intermolecular forces; a larger, more polarizable molecule can have stronger London dispersion forces and a higher boiling point than a smaller molecule even if the smaller one can hydrogen bond weakly.
**Expected reasoning:** Compare the size/polarizability and the types of intermolecular forces in both molecules, then weigh which set of attractions is stronger overall rather than relying on a single surface rule.
**Concept category:** boiling point trends / LDF vs hydrogen bonding
**Population:** college freshman-level chemistry students
**Source:** Gamifying intermolecular forces: Improving student performance outcomes for college freshman-level intermolecular forces (10293669/10293669.md)
**Evidence:** “they are often unable to reason that a large molecule with high LDFs may have a higher boiling point than a small molecule with weak H-bonding.”

## Misconception 33: Students may fail to recognize that a molecule can be capable of hydrogen bonding even when it is not a typical alcohol or common textbook example.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Hydrogen bonding capability
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding occurs when a molecule has H bonded to N, O, or F and a suitable lone-pair acceptor; uncommon or unstable molecules can still be hydrogen-bond donors/acceptors if they meet the structural criteria.
**Expected reasoning:** Inspect the functional groups and Lewis structure for N–H, O–H, or F–H bonds and lone pairs on N/O/F, then decide whether hydrogen bonding is possible regardless of whether the molecule is a familiar example.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding / functional group recognition
**Prevalence:** Pre-activity correct responses: 60.6%; post-activity correct responses: 61.9%
**Population:** students in the freshman-level IMF activity (matched n = 236 responses)
**Source:** Gamifying intermolecular forces: Improving student performance outcomes for college freshman-level intermolecular forces (10293669/10293669.md)
**Evidence:** “students were not significantly better or worse at recognizing that methylimine is ‘capable’ of hydrogen bonding as a cause for its higher boiling point compared to ethene.”

## Misconception 34: Students may not correctly determine how molecular geometry affects polarity, leading them to miss that a cis isomer can have stronger dipole-dipole interactions than the trans isomer.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Molecular geometry and polarity
**Correct concept:** Molecular geometry determines whether bond dipoles cancel; a cis isomer may be polar while the trans isomer is nonpolar or less polar, so the cis isomer can have stronger dipole-dipole interactions.
**Expected reasoning:** Draw or interpret the molecular geometry, add bond dipoles as vectors, and decide whether a net dipole remains; if the cis isomer has a net dipole and the trans does not, the cis isomer has stronger dipole-dipole forces.
**Concept category:** polarity / dipole-dipole interactions / isomer geometry
**Prevalence:** Pre-activity correct responses: 63.1%; post-activity correct responses: 67.4%
**Population:** students in the freshman-level IMF activity (matched n = 236 responses)
**Source:** Gamifying intermolecular forces: Improving student performance outcomes for college freshman-level intermolecular forces (10293669/10293669.md)
**Evidence:** “students were not significantly better or worse at recognizing that the cis- isomer of dichloro-difluroxenon has stronger dipole-dipole interactions than the trans- isomer.”

## Misconception 35: Students may fail to recognize that cis and trans alkene isomers can differ in polarity and dipole-dipole forces.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Molecular geometry and polarity
**Correct concept:** The cis isomer of dichloroethene has a net dipole and therefore stronger dipole-dipole interactions than the trans isomer, whose dipoles cancel more effectively.
**Expected reasoning:** Use the molecular geometry to assess whether the bond dipoles cancel; then compare the strength of dipole-dipole interactions based on the net molecular dipole.
**Concept category:** polarity / dipole-dipole interactions / isomer geometry
**Prevalence:** Pre-activity correct responses: 55.1%; post-activity correct responses: 66.1%
**Population:** students in the freshman-level IMF activity (matched n = 236 responses)
**Source:** Gamifying intermolecular forces: Improving student performance outcomes for college freshman-level intermolecular forces (10293669/10293669.md)
**Evidence:** “students were significantly better at recognizing that the cis- isomer of dichloro-ethene has stronger dipole-dipole interactions than the trans- isomer”

## Misconception 36: Students may incorrectly think that a substance with fewer hydrogen-bonding opportunities can still have the higher boiling point, rather than connecting more hydrogen bonding to stronger intermolecular attraction and higher boiling point.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Hydrogen bonding and boiling point comparison
**Correct concept:** More hydrogen bonding generally increases intermolecular attraction and raises boiling point; glycerol has more hydrogen bonding than pentanol, so glycerol boils higher.
**Expected reasoning:** Count the hydrogen-bond donors and acceptors available, compare the extent of hydrogen bonding in each molecule, and infer that more extensive hydrogen bonding usually raises boiling point.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding / boiling point trends
**Prevalence:** Pre-activity correct responses: 69.1%; post-activity correct responses: 76.3%
**Population:** students in the freshman-level IMF activity (matched n = 236 responses)
**Source:** Gamifying intermolecular forces: Improving student performance outcomes for college freshman-level intermolecular forces (10293669/10293669.md)
**Evidence:** “students are significantly better at recognising that glycerol has more hydrogen bonding than pentanol as a cause for glycerol having the higher boiling point.”

## Misconception 37: Students may think sulfur trioxide should have dipole-dipole interactions like sulfur dioxide, or may not understand that the stronger LDFs of the larger molecule can outweigh sulfur dioxide's dipole-dipole interactions in boiling point comparison.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Comparing LDFs and dipole-dipole interactions in sulfur oxides
**Correct concept:** Sulfur trioxide is nonpolar and has no dipole-dipole interactions; its larger electron cloud gives stronger London dispersion forces than sulfur dioxide, contributing to the higher boiling point.
**Expected reasoning:** Determine whether the molecule has a net dipole. If not, rule out dipole-dipole forces, then compare size/polarizability to judge London dispersion strength and its effect on boiling point.
**Concept category:** polarity / LDF vs dipole-dipole interactions / boiling point trends
**Prevalence:** Pre-activity correct responses: 30.9%; post-activity correct responses: 41.5%
**Population:** students in the freshman-level IMF activity (matched n = 236 responses)
**Source:** Gamifying intermolecular forces: Improving student performance outcomes for college freshman-level intermolecular forces (10293669/10293669.md)
**Evidence:** “students are significantly better at recognising that sulfur trioxide does not have dipole-dipole interactions, but has significantly stronger LDFs when compared to sulfur dioxide's weaker LDFs and dipole-dipole interaction, as a cause for sulfur trioxide having the higher boiling point.”

## Misconception 38: Dipole-dipole interactions occur within a single molecule, not between two molecules.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Location of dipole-dipole interactions
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole interactions are intermolecular forces that occur between separate molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify that the interaction is between the partial positive end of one polar molecule and the partial negative end of another polar molecule, not between atoms within the same molecule.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces
**Population:** students in general chemistry; discussion references prior work
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole—dipole interactions (10505036/10505036.md)
**Evidence:** “a large portion of the students believed that intermolecular forces were within a single molecule rather than between”

## Misconception 39: Any molecule containing nitrogen, oxygen, or fluorine can hydrogen bond.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / intermolecular forces / Functional group generalization
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding requires a hydrogen atom bonded to N, O, or F and interaction with a lone pair on N, O, or F; the presence of N, O, or F alone is not sufficient.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should check both the donor and acceptor conditions for hydrogen bonding, rather than assuming that the presence of N, O, or F automatically makes a molecule hydrogen-bonding capable.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces
**Population:** students in general chemistry; prior literature summarized in the paper
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole—dipole interactions (10505036/10505036.md)
**Evidence:** “students often generalize principles associated with intermolecular forces such that any molecule with either nitrogen, oxygen, or fluorine carries the ability to hydrogen bond”

## Misconception 40: Lone pairs or open electron sites are places that should bond with another atom, so the dipole-dipole interaction is treated like a covalent bond formation site.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole interactions / Bonding vs intermolecular attraction
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole interactions are attractions between partial charges on different molecules; lone pairs may influence polarity and molecular shape but are not themselves bonding sites for intermolecular force formation.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use molecular polarity and partial charges to locate the attraction between molecules, not infer that a lone pair is an available bonding site that must be filled.
**Concept category:** bonding availability / intermolecular vs intramolecular forces
**Prevalence:** Observed in 5 students in Version 1 (Sawyer, Sasha, Sadie, Sabrina, Sebastian) when discussing Lewis dot structures; also discussed as a broader pattern in the Discussion
**Population:** Version 1 students in second semester general chemistry (n=9 in Version 1; 18 total interviewed)
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole—dipole interactions (10505036/10505036.md)
**Evidence:** “the lone pairs represent sites that are free to bond with other atoms.”

## Misconception 41: If a representation shows no explicit lone pairs or bonds, the atom has an 'open spot' that needs to be filled by an intermolecular interaction to satisfy the octet.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole interactions / Open spots / octet completion
**Correct concept:** Missing dots or bonds in a representation do not mean an atom needs an additional bond; dipole-dipole interactions depend on partial charges between molecules, not octet completion in the displayed model.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should interpret the representation in terms of electron distribution and polarity, and recognize that intermolecular attractions do not add bonds to complete octets.
**Concept category:** octet / bonding availability
**Population:** students in both protocol versions; especially Version 1 space-filling and chemical formula tasks
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole—dipole interactions (10505036/10505036.md)
**Evidence:** “students considered the space around each atom as the location where dipole-dipole interactions could occur”

## Misconception 42: Molecular geometry is explicitly shown in Lewis dot structures, so a Lewis structure can be read as linear unless lone pairs are added mentally afterward.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole interactions / Geometry in Lewis structures
**Correct concept:** Lewis structures encode connectivity and lone pairs, and molecular geometry must be inferred using electron-domain considerations; lone pairs change shape and therefore polarity.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should examine the presence and arrangement of lone pairs to infer the true molecular geometry, then determine whether the molecule is polar and where dipole-dipole attraction can occur.
**Concept category:** geometry / representational interpretation
**Prevalence:** Observed in 3 students in Version 2 (Regan, Rachel, Rebecca) before correction
**Population:** Version 2 second semester general chemistry students
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole—dipole interactions (10505036/10505036.md)
**Evidence:** “students carried the assumption that molecular geometry was explicit in Lewis dot structures”

## Misconception 43: Selenium dioxide is linear and nonpolar when first viewed in Lewis dot form because lone pairs are not initially considered.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole interactions / Linear and nonpolar assumption for SeO2
**Correct concept:** Selenium dioxide is bent because lone pairs on selenium affect bond angle, making the molecule polar.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should count electron domains and include lone pairs in VSEPR reasoning; the bent geometry creates a net dipole that allows dipole-dipole interactions.
**Concept category:** geometry / polarity
**Prevalence:** Observed in 3 students in Version 2 before they corrected themselves
**Population:** Version 2 students working on Lewis dot structures
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole—dipole interactions (10505036/10505036.md)
**Evidence:** “designated the molecule as linear and non-polar, without taking into account the influence of lone pairs”

## Misconception 44: The larger atom in a ball-and-stick or space-filling model is the preferred location for dipole-dipole interaction because larger size means stronger attraction.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole interactions / Atom size as interaction site
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole interaction location is determined by molecular polarity and partial charges, not by atomic size; size in these models is not itself a proxy for dipole-dipole strength.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use electronegativity and molecular geometry to identify partial positive and negative regions, then place the interaction between opposite partial charges on different molecules.
**Concept category:** surface features / size
**Prevalence:** Observed in multiple Version 1 students; 3 students (Sandeep, Spencer, Sebastian) explicitly began to use size in ball-and-stick tasks, and 1 student (Sandeep) stayed with size in space-filling
**Population:** Version 1 second semester general chemistry students
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole—dipole interactions (10505036/10505036.md)
**Evidence:** “the bigger the two atoms that are bonding are, the weaker the forces are.”

## Misconception 45: In space-filling models, larger atom size directly corresponds to higher electronegativity and therefore identifies the interaction site.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole interactions / Size correlates with electronegativity
**Correct concept:** Atom size in a space-filling model is a graphical convention and is not equivalent to electronegativity; the interaction site should be inferred from polarity and partial charge, not from visual size alone.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish the representational cue of atomic size from chemical properties such as electronegativity, and use electron distribution to determine the dipole-dipole interaction.
**Concept category:** surface features / size
**Population:** Version 1 students; discussed in relation to space-filling representations
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole—dipole interactions (10505036/10505036.md)
**Evidence:** “I think it's just because seeing how one ball can be bigger than the other throws you off easily.”

## Misconception 46: Atoms in the molecules carry fixed valence charges or ionic charges that can be used to predict dipole-dipole attraction by pairing opposite charges.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole interactions / Valence charge as interaction criterion
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole interactions arise from partial charges in polar covalent molecules; atoms do not need to be treated as ionic species with fixed charges from the periodic table.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use electronegativity differences and molecular geometry to identify partial positive and partial negative ends, then connect those ends between molecules.
**Concept category:** charges / electrostatic reasoning
**Prevalence:** Observed in 4 students in Version 2 (Ria, Rachel, Rebecca, Richard) in the space-filling task
**Population:** Version 2 second semester general chemistry students
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole—dipole interactions (10505036/10505036.md)
**Evidence:** “began assigning charges to each of the atoms in the molecules”

## Misconception 47: A central atom cannot participate in or be used to locate dipole-dipole interactions because it is already in the middle of the molecule and is not available to connect.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole interactions / Central atom cannot interact
**Correct concept:** The relevant interaction site is the region of partial charge in the molecule, which may involve a central atom’s bonded environment; being central does not by itself prevent intermolecular attraction.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate the molecule’s polarity from electronegativity and geometry, rather than excluding atoms solely because of their position in the structure.
**Concept category:** structure-position reasoning
**Population:** Version 2 chemical formula task; reported for Rachel and also discussed for Ranger and Richard
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole—dipole interactions (10505036/10505036.md)
**Evidence:** “I don't know about this one because chlorine is the central... and I don't really know how it's going to connect to the hydrogen.”

## Misconception 48: A triple bond or greater number of bonds directly means a stronger dipole-dipole interaction or stronger intermolecular attraction.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole interactions / Bond order as force strength
**Correct concept:** Bond order affects intramolecular bonding, but dipole-dipole interactions depend on molecular polarity and the magnitude/direction of partial charges between molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should not infer intermolecular force strength from the number of covalent bonds; instead they should analyze electronegativity differences and geometry to locate the partial charges that attract between molecules.
**Concept category:** bond order / intermolecular force reasoning
**Prevalence:** Observed in Version 2 ball-and-stick task; explicitly noted for Ria, and Rebecca linked affinity to number of bonds
**Population:** Version 2 second semester general chemistry students
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole—dipole interactions (10505036/10505036.md)
**Evidence:** “the number of bonds signifies the strength of dipole-dipole interactions in the molecule.”

## Misconception 49: Dipole-dipole forces occur within a single molecule at polar bonds rather than only between molecules.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Location of dipole-dipole forces
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole forces are intermolecular attractions between separate molecules, not bonds within one molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should first determine the molecular dipole for each molecule from bonded-atom electronegativity differences, then identify that the attractive interaction occurs between the positive end of one molecule and the negative end of another molecule.
**Concept category:** location of intermolecular forces
**Prevalence:** 11 of 18 participants consistently showed dipole-dipole as an intramolecular force; 61% in prior work drew dipole-dipole within a molecule.
**Population:** Second semester general chemistry students (18 interview participants); prior cited study of students drawing ethanol molecules.
**Source:** Students' Approaches to Determining the Location of Intermolecular Force between Two Distinct Molecules (10505038/10505038.md)
**Evidence:** "identify the pair(s) of atoms where the strongest intermolecular force would occur?"

## Misconception 50: Any positive and negative partial charges or dipoles anywhere in a molecule pair can be directly connected as the location of dipole-dipole interaction, including when the charges were inferred from unbonded atoms or different molecules without first establishing molecular dipoles correctly.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Opposite charge attraction
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole attraction should be based on the interaction between the positive end of one molecular dipole and the negative end of another molecular dipole, derived from correct bonded-atom polarity analysis.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare electronegativities only for bonded atoms to identify bond dipoles, combine bond dipoles to determine each molecule's polarity, and then use the positive and negative molecular ends to locate the intermolecular attraction.
**Concept category:** electron transfer / partial charges
**Prevalence:** 10 of 18 participants used opposite charges attract as a basis; some varied in how they determined molecular dipoles.
**Population:** Second semester general chemistry interview participants
**Source:** Students' Approaches to Determining the Location of Intermolecular Force between Two Distinct Molecules (10505038/10505038.md)
**Evidence:** "In this case, since F would have the more negative partial charge in the iodine monofluoride and then Se would have the positive dipole on the selenium dioxide. It makes sense that they're connected."

## Misconception 51: Electronegativity should be compared between atoms on different molecules to decide where dipole-dipole attraction occurs, rather than only between bonded atoms within a molecule.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Electronegativity comparisons
**Correct concept:** Electronegativity is used first between bonded atoms to determine bond polarity; intermolecular dipole-dipole location is then inferred from the resulting molecular dipoles.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify bonded atoms, subtract electronegativity values only for those bonded pairs, decide which bonds are polar, determine each molecule's dipole, and then place the intermolecular force between opposite molecular ends.
**Concept category:** electronegativity comparisons
**Prevalence:** Observed in approach 1B and 2A/2B; e.g., one participant in approach 1B and three participants in approach 2A compared atoms on different molecules.
**Population:** Second semester general chemistry interview participants
**Source:** Students' Approaches to Determining the Location of Intermolecular Force between Two Distinct Molecules (10505038/10505038.md)
**Evidence:** "I think the sulfur [in SCl2] and the nitrogen [in NH3] might be able to, just because the electronegativity difference is 0.5."

## Misconception 52: Electronegativity can be compared between unbonded atoms within the same molecule to decide where dipole-dipole attraction is located.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Electronegativity comparisons
**Correct concept:** Electronegativity comparisons for bond polarity must be made only between atoms that share a chemical bond.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should restrict electronegativity differences to bonded atoms, use those bond polarities to infer molecular polarity, and then locate dipole-dipole attraction between molecules.
**Concept category:** electronegativity comparisons
**Prevalence:** Documented for Lam in approach 1C; Lam compared unbonded atoms within one molecule in one prompt.
**Population:** One second semester general chemistry interview participant (Lam)
**Source:** Students' Approaches to Determining the Location of Intermolecular Force between Two Distinct Molecules (10505038/10505038.md)
**Evidence:** "the chlorine [in CH3Cl] does have a stronger electronegativity than the hydrogen atoms [in CH3Cl]. So that tells me that the chlorine is found to be partially negative."

## Misconception 53: Any atom pair whose electronegativity difference is greater than 0.5 is the site of dipole-dipole interaction, even if the pair is within a molecule and not a bond site for intermolecular attraction.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Electronegativity threshold
**Correct concept:** A difference greater than 0.5 is used to classify a bond as polar covalent, not to directly locate an intermolecular dipole-dipole force.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use the 0.5 threshold only to decide bond polarity for bonded atoms, then use the set of bond dipoles to determine molecular polarity and the intermolecular attraction between molecules.
**Concept category:** electronegativity thresholds / bond polarity
**Prevalence:** 4 participants consistently used bonded-atom EN differences above 0.5 to assign dipole-dipole location within a molecule; additional students used this idea inconsistently.
**Population:** Second semester general chemistry interview participants
**Source:** Students' Approaches to Determining the Location of Intermolecular Force between Two Distinct Molecules (10505038/10505038.md)
**Evidence:** "bonds between S and chloride [in SCl2], and N and hydrogen [in NH3] are all polar bonds because the difference between electronegativity of S and Cl is 0.5."

## Misconception 54: The atom pair with the largest electronegativity difference is automatically where dipole-dipole force occurs.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Electronegativity threshold
**Correct concept:** Largest electronegativity difference does not by itself determine the location of dipole-dipole force; bond polarity and molecular dipoles must be considered.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare bonded atoms only, determine which bonds are polar, build each molecular dipole, and then identify the positive-to-negative attraction between molecules rather than simply choosing the maximum EN difference.
**Concept category:** electronegativity thresholds / heuristic selection
**Prevalence:** 4 participants (Ethan, Feng, Kavita, Mason) used the largest EN difference approach.
**Population:** Second semester general chemistry interview participants
**Source:** Students' Approaches to Determining the Location of Intermolecular Force between Two Distinct Molecules (10505038/10505038.md)
**Evidence:** "I feel like the more [EN] difference there is between the two, the more that they're going to interact."

## Misconception 55: The atom with the biggest electronegativity value is the one that attracts the other molecule most strongly and therefore marks the dipole-dipole location.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Meaning of electronegativity
**Correct concept:** Electronegativity is used comparatively to create bond dipoles; the larger raw value alone is not the site of intermolecular attraction.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use electronegativity differences between bonded atoms to establish polarity, then use the resulting molecular dipole directions to locate the intermolecular force.
**Concept category:** electronegativity magnitude
**Prevalence:** 3 participants (Ethan, Lam, Quinn) focused on the biggest EN values; Quinn used it consistently across all four prompts.
**Population:** Second semester general chemistry interview participants
**Source:** Students' Approaches to Determining the Location of Intermolecular Force between Two Distinct Molecules (10505038/10505038.md)
**Evidence:** "it's [N in NH3] more electronegative, so connect that to chlorine because that's [Cl in SCl2] also the most electronegative."

## Misconception 56: The largest atoms in the two molecules are the ones that will experience dipole-dipole attraction.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Atomic size
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole attraction depends on molecular polarity arising from bond polarity and molecular shape, not on atomic size.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should ignore atom size as the basis of intermolecular attraction, determine bond polarities from electronegativity differences, identify molecular dipoles, and then place the dipole-dipole interaction between opposite molecular ends.
**Concept category:** surface features / atomic size
**Prevalence:** 1 participant (Ethan) used atomic size as the basis in the prompts where size was visually explicit.
**Population:** One second semester general chemistry interview participant (Ethan)
**Source:** Students' Approaches to Determining the Location of Intermolecular Force between Two Distinct Molecules (10505038/10505038.md)
**Evidence:** "the I [in IF] is significantly bigger than the F [in IF]...It makes me feel that the I should have more attraction to it"

## Misconception 57: Bent molecular shape itself means the bonds inside that molecule are the dipole-dipole force location.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Molecular shape
**Correct concept:** Molecular shape helps determine molecular polarity, but dipole-dipole forces occur between molecules, not inside the bent molecule's bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use shape together with bond polarity to infer whether a molecule has a net dipole, then use the positive and negative ends of two molecules to locate the intermolecular attraction.
**Concept category:** molecular shape
**Prevalence:** 1 participant (Danh) consistently used molecular shape in all four prompts.
**Population:** One second semester general chemistry interview participant (Danh)
**Source:** Students' Approaches to Determining the Location of Intermolecular Force between Two Distinct Molecules (10505038/10505038.md)
**Evidence:** "because dipole-dipole exists where...molecules are bent, and these [sulfur to chlorine bonds] are in a bent shape right here. So these two bonds would be dipole-dipole."

## Misconception 58: If a molecule is not bent, it has no dipole and therefore cannot participate in dipole-dipole interactions.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Molecular shape and polarity
**Correct concept:** Molecular polarity depends on the vector sum of bond dipoles and overall geometry; a molecule can have a dipole even if a student does not recognize a bent shape in the representation.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should determine the bond dipoles from electronegativity differences, consider the molecular geometry, and decide whether the bond dipoles cancel or produce a net molecular dipole rather than using bend/no-bend as the only criterion.
**Concept category:** molecular shape / molecular polarity
**Prevalence:** 1 participant (Danh) explicitly said IF had no dipole because it was not bent.
**Population:** One second semester general chemistry interview participant (Danh)
**Source:** Students' Approaches to Determining the Location of Intermolecular Force between Two Distinct Molecules (10505038/10505038.md)
**Evidence:** "when asked whether there was a dipole in IF, Danh replied there was not a dipole because it did not show a bent geometry."

## Misconception 59: Intermolecular forces occur within a single molecule rather than between molecules.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / location of intermolecular forces
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces are interactions between molecules, not intramolecular forces within one molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the two separate molecules in the task and determine the strongest attractive interaction between them based on their electrostatic properties, not on bonds within one molecule.
**Concept category:** intramolecular vs intermolecular
**Prevalence:** over half of participants (from Cooper et al., cited in paper)
**Population:** general chemistry students in prior study cited by the paper
**Source:** An Investigation into Dissonance-Induced Changes in Intermolecular Force Conceptions through Electrostatic Potential Maps (10665154/10665154.md)
**Evidence:** "over half of participants held the conception that intermolecular forces occur within a single molecule rather than between molecules."

## Misconception 60: A lone pair on an atom is a direct indicator that a hydrogen can bond there in intermolecular force tasks.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / available bonding sites
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding occurs between a hydrogen attached to N, O, or F and a lone pair on another electronegative atom; lone pairs alone are not simply 'bonding sites' by themselves.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use the intermolecular force definition and identify complementary partial positive and partial negative regions between different molecules rather than treating any lone pair as an available bond site.
**Concept category:** availability for bonding / lone pairs
**Prevalence:** 5 students
**Population:** general chemistry II interview participants
**Source:** An Investigation into Dissonance-Induced Changes in Intermolecular Force Conceptions through Electrostatic Potential Maps (10665154/10665154.md)
**Evidence:** "Because I have a lone pair ... the hydrogen could bond there."

## Misconception 61: An atom's octet state or how 'surrounded' it is determines whether it can participate in intermolecular interactions, and an incomplete octet means it is a bonding site.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / available bonding sites
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular interactions are determined by partial charges and electrostatic attraction between molecules, not by whether an atom has a full octet or appears 'surrounded'.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should look for electrostatic complementarity between molecules and connect partial positive regions to partial negative regions, rather than inferring intermolecular interaction sites from octet completeness.
**Concept category:** availability for bonding / octet rule
**Prevalence:** 5 students
**Population:** general chemistry II interview participants
**Source:** An Investigation into Dissonance-Induced Changes in Intermolecular Force Conceptions through Electrostatic Potential Maps (10665154/10665154.md)
**Evidence:** "it doesn't have its full like ... it's not like surrounded"

## Misconception 62: An uneven distribution of electrons or extra lone pairs in a Lewis structure directly means the atom has a negative charge and is attractive for intermolecular forces.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / charge assignment from Lewis structures
**Correct concept:** Partial charges arise from differences in electron density and electronegativity across the molecule, not simply from counting lone pairs or noticing an 'uneven' Lewis structure.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason from electronegativity and electron density to determine where partial positive and partial negative regions exist, then infer the likely intermolecular interaction site from those partial charges.
**Concept category:** electrostatic reasoning / lone pairs
**Prevalence:** part of the 5-student availability-for-bonding group
**Population:** general chemistry II interview participants
**Source:** An Investigation into Dissonance-Induced Changes in Intermolecular Force Conceptions through Electrostatic Potential Maps (10665154/10665154.md)
**Evidence:** "there's an extra lone pair on the side and that gives it a negative charge."

## Misconception 63: Electronegativity values can be subtracted within a single molecule to decide which molecule has the strongest intermolecular force.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / charge assignment from Lewis structures
**Correct concept:** Electronegativity differences within a bond help infer bond polarity and molecular polarity, but intermolecular force strength is determined by interactions between molecules, not by the molecule with the largest internal electronegativity difference.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should first use electronegativity differences to infer bond polarity and then use molecular polarity and complementary partial charges to determine the strongest force between two molecules.
**Concept category:** electronegativity values
**Prevalence:** 5 students
**Population:** general chemistry II interview participants
**Source:** An Investigation into Dissonance-Induced Changes in Intermolecular Force Conceptions through Electrostatic Potential Maps (10665154/10665154.md)
**Evidence:** "participants subtracted the provided elemental electronegativity values within the same molecule and identified the one with the largest difference as having the strongest intermolecular force."

## Misconception 64: A Lewis structure's drawn appearance can be taken as the actual molecular geometry; for example, a molecule that appears linear in the Lewis structure is treated as linear and nonpolar.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / molecular geometry
**Correct concept:** Actual molecular geometry must be inferred from structure and electron-domain arrangement, not from the flat Lewis drawing; molecular shape determines polarity and thus intermolecular forces.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use VSEPR or equivalent geometry reasoning to determine the true 3D shape, then decide whether the molecule is polar and what intermolecular force is strongest.
**Concept category:** geometry / polarity
**Prevalence:** mentioned for Ellie
**Population:** general chemistry II interview participants
**Source:** An Investigation into Dissonance-Induced Changes in Intermolecular Force Conceptions through Electrostatic Potential Maps (10665154/10665154.md)
**Evidence:** "if it was linear for example, then you couldn't because it pulls equally in both directions, so no dipole"

## Misconception 65: The explicit lone pairs shown in a Lewis structure are the key features for determining intermolecular force strength and location, so forces are treated as if they depend on structural placeholders rather than molecule-to-molecule electrostatics.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / representational features
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular force location should be based on electrostatic attraction between partial charges on different molecules; explicit lone pairs are not the force itself.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should translate the structural information into polarity and partial charges, then identify where opposite charges on separate molecules attract.
**Concept category:** surface features / representational cues
**Prevalence:** 5 students
**Population:** general chemistry II interview participants
**Source:** An Investigation into Dissonance-Induced Changes in Intermolecular Force Conceptions through Electrostatic Potential Maps (10665154/10665154.md)
**Evidence:** "the explicit lone pairs on the Lewis Dot structure as indicators of available interaction sites."

## Misconception 66: The colors on an electrostatic potential map indicate force strength or magnitude of intermolecular attraction.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / color interpretation in electrostatic potential maps
**Correct concept:** The colors on an electrostatic potential map represent relative electron density/partial charge, not intermolecular force strength itself.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should interpret the color key as electron-density information, identify partial positive and partial negative regions, and then reason about which molecules will attract each other.
**Concept category:** EPM color meaning
**Prevalence:** several participants
**Population:** general chemistry II interview participants
**Source:** An Investigation into Dissonance-Induced Changes in Intermolecular Force Conceptions through Electrostatic Potential Maps (10665154/10665154.md)
**Evidence:** "several participants carrying intramolecular force conceptions viewed the colors on the EPM as a measure of force strength/magnitude."

## Misconception 67: Symmetrical nonpolar molecules should lack areas of high and low electron density on an EPM because opposing dipoles cancel out.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / electron density in symmetric molecules
**Correct concept:** Even symmetric molecules can show regions of different electron density on an EPM because the map displays local electron distribution; cancellation of the net dipole does not mean the map is uniform.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish between overall molecular polarity and local electron-density variation, recognizing that a nonpolar molecule can still have electron-density gradients across bonds and atoms.
**Concept category:** EPM interpretation / symmetry
**Prevalence:** several participants
**Population:** general chemistry II interview participants
**Source:** An Investigation into Dissonance-Induced Changes in Intermolecular Force Conceptions through Electrostatic Potential Maps (10665154/10665154.md)
**Evidence:** "several participants seemed to hold the conception that the EPM of symmetrical molecules would be devoid of areas of high and low electron density because of the opposite but equal dipole moments."

## Misconception 68: London dispersion forces result from random electron movement being directly visible as changing electron density on the map, so a static EPM can misrepresent dispersion as non-random or non-physical.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / dispersion forces
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces arise from temporary fluctuations in electron distribution; an EPM is a static representation of electron density, not a movie of electron motion.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should understand that an EPM shows a snapshot of electron density and that dispersion forces are described by temporary induced dipoles and fluctuating electron motion, not fixed map colors alone.
**Concept category:** dispersion forces / electron density
**Prevalence:** several participants
**Population:** general chemistry II interview participants
**Source:** An Investigation into Dissonance-Induced Changes in Intermolecular Force Conceptions through Electrostatic Potential Maps (10665154/10665154.md)
