**Evidence:** "the EPM presentation of electron density as a static characteristic which dissuaded students from a conception of London dispersion forces that result from random electron movement."

## Misconception 69: More electron density, more bonds, or a more 'balanced' electron-density pattern means a molecule is stronger and therefore has stronger intermolecular forces.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / overall force comparisons
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular force type and strength depend on the nature of intermolecular electrostatic interactions between molecules, not on how hard it seems to pull a molecule apart or on the number of bonds within it.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the types of intermolecular attractions possible between two molecules and use polarity/partial charges, not internal bonding or perceived molecular stability, to judge the strongest intermolecular force.
**Concept category:** intramolecular vs intermolecular
**Prevalence:** Ivy; part of intramolecular-force-conception group of 5
**Population:** general chemistry II interview participants
**Source:** An Investigation into Dissonance-Induced Changes in Intermolecular Force Conceptions through Electrostatic Potential Maps (10665154/10665154.md)
**Evidence:** "CCl4 has overall higher electron density, which I'm pretty sure means that it's harder to pull the atoms apart from each other in the molecule, which would mean that it's stronger."

## Misconception 70: Students think the rubber stopper pops out because the total amount of matter/number of moles increases, the solution becomes more filled, or air is released/fills the tube, rather than because hydrogen gas pressure builds up.

**Topic:** Gases / Metal-acid reaction pressure
**Correct concept:** The stopper is ejected because the reaction produces H2 gas, increasing internal pressure above external pressure.
**Expected reasoning:** Identify that Mg reacts with HCl to form hydrogen gas; in a closed container the produced gas increases the internal pressure, and when this pressure exceeds the outside pressure the stopper is pushed out.
**Concept category:** gas pressure in reactions
**Prevalence:** Question 1: specific alternative conceptions 9.19% (8/87); partial understanding with specific alternative conception 12.64% (11/87)
**Population:** 87 11th grade science students from three public schools in Erzurum, Turkey
**Source:** 11th Grade Students' Understanding Level of Gases in terms of Some Chemical Variables and the Determination of Alternative Conceptions (228-Article Text-1846-1-10-20201206/228-Article Text-1846-1-10-20201206.md)
**Evidence:** “the rubber stopper to jump was due to increase the amount of matter”

## Misconception 71: Students think hydrogen in the acid is a hydrogen element/ion that flies away as a gas or decomposes, rather than recognizing molecular hydrogen gas produced by the reaction.

**Topic:** Gases / Metal-acid reaction particle identity
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen gas is H2 formed by chemical reaction; H+ ions are not the same as hydrogen gas particles.
**Expected reasoning:** Distinguish ions from molecules and use the reaction equation to see that H+ ions are reduced to form H2 gas, which accumulates and raises pressure.
**Concept category:** ion/molecule distinction
**Prevalence:** Reported in group discussions; no percentage given
**Population:** 9 students in group discussions (from the same 87-student sample)
**Source:** 11th Grade Students' Understanding Level of Gases in terms of Some Chemical Variables and the Determination of Alternative Conceptions (228-Article Text-1846-1-10-20201206/228-Article Text-1846-1-10-20201206.md)
**Evidence:** “the hydrogen element and Mg metal reacted, the pressure increased”

## Misconception 72: Students think tire pressure rises because of elevation changes, outdoor pressure changes, or because the accelerator pedal presses on the wheels, rather than because heating from friction increases molecular kinetic energy and pressure.

**Topic:** Gases / Tire pressure and temperature
**Correct concept:** Tire pressure increases when friction heats the tire, increasing the kinetic energy of gas molecules and their collisions with the tire walls.
**Expected reasoning:** Link friction to temperature rise; higher temperature means faster-moving gas molecules and more forceful/more frequent collisions with the tire wall, which raises pressure in a fixed volume.
**Concept category:** pressure-temperature relationship
**Prevalence:** Question 2: specific alternative conceptions 18.39% (16/87); partial understanding with specific alternative conception 26.43% (23/87)
**Population:** 87 11th grade science students from three public schools in Erzurum, Turkey
**Source:** 11th Grade Students' Understanding Level of Gases in terms of Some Chemical Variables and the Determination of Alternative Conceptions (228-Article Text-1846-1-10-20201206/228-Article Text-1846-1-10-20201206.md)
**Evidence:** “Pressure from the accelerator pedal increases the gas pressure on the wheels.”

## Misconception 73: Students treat the ideal gas law as meaning pressure and volume always change inversely in every context and apply it without considering the specific variables held constant or the actual cause of pressure change.

**Topic:** Gases / Ideal gas law interpretation
**Correct concept:** The relationship between pressure and volume depends on the conditions; in a tire, heating increases molecular kinetic energy and pressure, and volume is not simply inferred from pressure alone.
**Expected reasoning:** Use the full gas model: friction raises temperature, higher temperature raises molecular kinetic energy, and that increases pressure at approximately constant volume. Do not infer a universal inverse pressure-volume relation without identifying the fixed variables.
**Concept category:** gas laws / PV=nRT
**Prevalence:** Question 2: partial understanding with specific alternative conceptions 26.43% (23/87)
**Population:** 87 11th grade science students from three public schools in Erzurum, Turkey
**Source:** 11th Grade Students' Understanding Level of Gases in terms of Some Chemical Variables and the Determination of Alternative Conceptions (228-Article Text-1846-1-10-20201206/228-Article Text-1846-1-10-20201206.md)
**Evidence:** “As the pressure increases, the volume decreases”

## Misconception 74: Students think that as you go higher, pressure increases and therefore the balloon volume increases or stays fixed, rather than recognizing that external pressure decreases with altitude and the balloon expands.

**Topic:** Gases / Balloon volume and altitude
**Correct concept:** At higher altitude external pressure decreases, so the balloon volume increases.
**Expected reasoning:** Compare the balloon’s internal gas pressure with the outside pressure; when outside pressure drops, the balloon expands until a new equilibrium is reached.
**Concept category:** external pressure / altitude
**Prevalence:** Question 3: specific alternative conceptions 9.19% (8/87); partial understanding with specific alternative conception 14.94% (13/87)
**Population:** 87 11th grade science students from three public schools in Erzurum, Turkey
**Source:** 11th Grade Students' Understanding Level of Gases in terms of Some Chemical Variables and the Determination of Alternative Conceptions (228-Article Text-1846-1-10-20201206/228-Article Text-1846-1-10-20201206.md)
**Evidence:** “As you go higher, the pressure becomes higher. Pressure and volume are inversely proportional.”

## Misconception 75: Students think gases expand in the cold or that decreasing temperature makes volume increase, rather than contract.

**Topic:** Gases / Balloon volume in cold conditions
**Correct concept:** Lower temperature causes gas volume to decrease at constant pressure.
**Expected reasoning:** Use the particle model: cooling lowers molecular kinetic energy, particles move more slowly, and the gas occupies less volume.
**Concept category:** temperature-volume relationship
**Prevalence:** Question 3: specific alternative conceptions included this statement; no exact count given for this specific misconception
**Population:** 87 11th grade science students from three public schools in Erzurum, Turkey
**Source:** 11th Grade Students' Understanding Level of Gases in terms of Some Chemical Variables and the Determination of Alternative Conceptions (228-Article Text-1846-1-10-20201206/228-Article Text-1846-1-10-20201206.md)
**Evidence:** “Gases expand in the cold environment.”

## Misconception 76: Students think no expansion occurs in a deflated/air-drained environment, or that the balloon volume decreases because there is no external air pressure or no oxygen.

**Topic:** Gases / Balloon and deflated environment
**Correct concept:** With lower external pressure, the balloon expands; the absence of external air does not prevent expansion.
**Expected reasoning:** Reason from pressure balance: if outside pressure is removed or reduced, the gas inside the balloon expands until internal and external pressures balance.
**Concept category:** external pressure / container pressure
**Prevalence:** Question 3: specific alternative conceptions 9.19% (8/87) included no-understanding/incorrect claims around this idea; exact count for this exact statement not separated
**Population:** 87 11th grade science students from three public schools in Erzurum, Turkey
**Source:** 11th Grade Students' Understanding Level of Gases in terms of Some Chemical Variables and the Determination of Alternative Conceptions (228-Article Text-1846-1-10-20201206/228-Article Text-1846-1-10-20201206.md)
**Evidence:** “No expansion occurs when the air is deflated.”

## Misconception 77: Students think gases become bulkier or that intermolecular distance increases when gas liquefies or when gas changes to solid.

**Topic:** Gases / State change and particle spacing
**Correct concept:** When a gas liquefies, particles come closer together and intermolecular distance decreases; going from gas to liquid to solid generally decreases spacing.
**Expected reasoning:** Use the particle model of states of matter: gases have the largest spacing, so condensation or solidification reduces spacing between particles.
**Concept category:** state change / particle spacing
**Prevalence:** Question 4: specific alternative conceptions 8.04% (7/87)
**Population:** 87 11th grade science students from three public schools in Erzurum, Turkey
**Source:** 11th Grade Students' Understanding Level of Gases in terms of Some Chemical Variables and the Determination of Alternative Conceptions (228-Article Text-1846-1-10-20201206/228-Article Text-1846-1-10-20201206.md)
**Evidence:** “If the gaseous material liquefies, it becomes bulky.”

## Misconception 78: Students think increasing pressure creates more substance between molecules or increases intermolecular distance, instead of reducing the distance by compressing the gas.

**Topic:** Gases / Pressure and intermolecular distance
**Correct concept:** At constant temperature, higher pressure compresses gas particles, decreasing intermolecular distance.
**Expected reasoning:** If pressure increases while temperature is constant, the volume decreases; with the same amount of gas in less volume, particles are closer together.
**Concept category:** pressure / particle spacing
**Prevalence:** Question 4: specific alternative conceptions included this statement; group discussion examples provided, no exact count for this exact misconception
**Population:** 87 11th grade science students from three public schools in Erzurum, Turkey
**Source:** 11th Grade Students' Understanding Level of Gases in terms of Some Chemical Variables and the Determination of Alternative Conceptions (228-Article Text-1846-1-10-20201206/228-Article Text-1846-1-10-20201206.md)
**Evidence:** “Increased pressure results in more substances between molecules.”

## Misconception 79: Students think increasing temperature weakens intermolecular bonds/interaction in gases in a way that directly explains particle spacing, rather than using kinetic particle motion and volume changes appropriately.

**Topic:** Gases / Temperature and intermolecular interaction
**Correct concept:** Temperature changes particle kinetic energy; it does not mean bonds simply weaken in the way students described. Increased temperature at constant volume is not reasoned as bond weakening increasing spacing.
**Expected reasoning:** Relate temperature to kinetic energy and collisions, then use the correct state/pressure-volume relationship to infer particle spacing, not a vague bond-breaking explanation.
**Concept category:** temperature / intermolecular forces
**Prevalence:** Question 4: specific alternative conceptions included this statement; no exact count given
**Population:** 87 11th grade science students from three public schools in Erzurum, Turkey
**Source:** 11th Grade Students' Understanding Level of Gases in terms of Some Chemical Variables and the Determination of Alternative Conceptions (228-Article Text-1846-1-10-20201206/228-Article Text-1846-1-10-20201206.md)
**Evidence:** “If the temperature increases at a constant volume, the intermolecular bond weakens.”

## Misconception 80: Students believe intermolecular forces are stronger than intramolecular forces, for example that a hydrogen bond is stronger than a covalent bond.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Relative strength of forces
**Correct concept:** Intramolecular bonds are generally much stronger than intermolecular forces.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should compare the types of interactions involved and recognize that covalent bonds hold atoms together within a molecule, whereas intermolecular forces act between molecules and are weaker.
**Concept category:** force strength
**Source:** Video with multiple representations approach to promote students' conceptual understanding of intermolecular forces (25243-58801-2-PB/25243-58801-2-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "IMFs are stronger than intramolecular forces (for example hydrogen bond is stronger than covalent bond)"

## Misconception 81: Students think IMFs are present only in polar molecular substances such as water.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Where IMFs are present
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces occur between molecules and can occur in many substances, including nonpolar substances through dispersion forces.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should identify the type of particles present and then determine which intermolecular attractions can operate between them, rather than restricting IMFs to polar substances only.
**Concept category:** presence/location of IMFs
**Source:** Video with multiple representations approach to promote students' conceptual understanding of intermolecular forces (25243-58801-2-PB/25243-58801-2-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "IMFs are present in polar molecular substancessuch as water"

## Misconception 82: Students think IMFs occur within a covalent molecule.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Location of forces
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces act between molecules, not within a covalent bond framework of a single molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should distinguish between bonds within molecules and attractions between separate molecules; if the interaction is inside one molecule, it is intramolecular, not intermolecular.
**Concept category:** intermolecular vs intramolecular
**Source:** Video with multiple representations approach to promote students' conceptual understanding of intermolecular forces (25243-58801-2-PB/25243-58801-2-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "IMFs occur within a covalent molecule"

## Misconception 83: Students believe that intramolecular bonds break when a substance changes state, rather than the intermolecular forces between molecules.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Phase changes
**Correct concept:** During a change of state, intermolecular forces are overcome or rearranged; covalent bonds within molecules usually remain intact.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should reason that melting or boiling changes the arrangement and separation of molecules without changing the identity of the molecules themselves.
**Concept category:** phase change
**Source:** Video with multiple representations approach to promote students' conceptual understanding of intermolecular forces (25243-58801-2-PB/25243-58801-2-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "Intramolecular bonds break on change of state, rather than IMFs"

## Misconception 84: Students think IMFs are the forces within the molecule.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Location of forces
**Correct concept:** IMFs are forces between molecules, while forces within a molecule are intramolecular bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should identify whether the interaction is inside one molecular structure or between separate molecules, then classify it accordingly.
**Concept category:** intermolecular vs intramolecular
**Source:** Video with multiple representations approach to promote students' conceptual understanding of intermolecular forces (25243-58801-2-PB/25243-58801-2-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "IMFs are the forces within the molecule"

## Misconception 85: Students believe strong intermolecular forces exist in a continuous covalent network solid as if those solids were held together by IMFs.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Network solids
**Correct concept:** Continuous covalent network solids are held together by covalent bonds throughout the structure, not by intermolecular forces.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should distinguish a network solid from a molecular solid by checking whether the structure is a continuous covalent lattice or discrete molecules held together by attractions.
**Concept category:** solid structure
**Source:** Video with multiple representations approach to promote students' conceptual understanding of intermolecular forces (25243-58801-2-PB/25243-58801-2-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "Strong IMFs exist in a continuous covalent (network) solid"

## Misconception 86: Students think intermolecular forces are influenced by gravity.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Gravity
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces arise from electrostatic interactions between molecules, not gravity.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should explain molecular attractions in terms of charge distribution, polarity, and induced dipoles rather than macroscopic gravitational effects.
**Concept category:** force origin
**Source:** Video with multiple representations approach to promote students' conceptual understanding of intermolecular forces (25243-58801-2-PB/25243-58801-2-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "IMFs are influenced by gravity"

## Misconception 87: Students believe intermolecular forces lead to reactions.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Chemical reactions
**Correct concept:** Chemical reactions involve breaking and forming chemical bonds; intermolecular forces mainly affect physical properties and phase behavior.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should separate physical attractions between molecules from bond rearrangements that produce new substances in reactions.
**Concept category:** reaction vs physical interaction
**Source:** Video with multiple representations approach to promote students' conceptual understanding of intermolecular forces (25243-58801-2-PB/25243-58801-2-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "IMFs led to reactions"

## Misconception 88: Students believe unpaired electrons are necessary for hydrogen bonds to form.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Hydrogen bonding
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding requires a hydrogen atom covalently bonded to a highly electronegative atom and a lone pair on a neighboring electronegative atom; unpaired electrons are not the requirement.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should identify the donor hydrogen and acceptor lone pair involved in hydrogen bonding rather than looking for unpaired electrons.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Source:** Video with multiple representations approach to promote students' conceptual understanding of intermolecular forces (25243-58801-2-PB/25243-58801-2-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "Unpaired electrons are necessary for hydrogen bonds to form"

## Misconception 89: Intermolecular forces are forces that occur between atoms to form molecules or compounds.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces are attractive forces between molecules or particles of different molecules, not the forces that make atoms bond within a molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish between intramolecular bonds, which hold atoms together inside a molecule, and intermolecular forces, which act between separate molecules and influence physical properties such as boiling point.
**Concept category:** definition
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "Intermolecular forces are forces that occur between atoms to form molecules or compound"

## Misconception 90: It takes a high boiling point to break the bonds in a liquid phase.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Strength and boiling point
**Correct concept:** Boiling occurs when intermolecular attractions between molecules are overcome; boiling point is related to the strength of intermolecular forces, not to breaking covalent bonds inside molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that stronger intermolecular forces require more energy to separate molecules into the gas phase, so higher boiling point indicates stronger attractions between molecules.
**Concept category:** strength of IMFs and effect on boiling point
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "It takes a high boiling point to break the bonds in a liquid phase."

## Misconception 91: The greater the boiling point, the more difficult it is for the forces between the molecules of the compound to be broken so that the forces between the molecules are stronger.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Strength and boiling point
**Correct concept:** A higher boiling point indicates stronger intermolecular forces between molecules, because more energy is needed to overcome those attractions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect boiling point to the amount of energy needed to separate molecules, and infer that stronger intermolecular attractions cause a higher boiling point.
**Concept category:** strength of IMFs and effect on boiling point
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "The greater the boiling point, the more difficult it is for the forces between the molecules of the compound to be broken so that the forces between the molecules are stronger"

## Misconception 92: London forces are forces that occur between nonpolar molecules and permanent dipoles.

**Topic:** London forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces arise from instantaneous induced dipoles and occur in all atoms and molecules, though they are the only intermolecular force in nonpolar substances.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify London dispersion as temporary electron-density fluctuations that induce attractions, rather than as attraction between a nonpolar molecule and a permanent dipole.
**Concept category:** definition
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "London forces are forces that occur between nonpolar molecules and dipole permanent."

## Misconception 93: London forces occur in ethanol between the ethyl group and the OH group.

**Topic:** London forces / Examples
**Correct concept:** Ethanol molecules experience London dispersion forces as well as dipole-dipole interactions and hydrogen bonding between molecules; London forces are not a force between parts of one ethanol molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that intermolecular forces act between separate ethanol molecules, while the ethyl group and OH group are parts of the same molecule and are connected by covalent bonds.
**Concept category:** examples
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "London forces occur in ethanol i.e. between ethyl and OH"

## Misconception 94: London forces occur in CH4 due to the tetrahedral shape of the CH4 molecule.

**Topic:** London forces / Examples
**Correct concept:** Methane has London dispersion forces because it is nonpolar and all molecules have temporary induced dipoles; its tetrahedral shape alone is not the reason London forces exist.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason from electron distribution and molecular polarity, not from shape alone, and understand that dispersion forces arise from temporary fluctuations in electron density.
**Concept category:** examples
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "London forces occur in CH4 due to the tetrahedral shape of the CH4 molecule"

## Misconception 95: London force is a nonpolar force.

**Topic:** London forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces are intermolecular attractions caused by instantaneous induced dipoles; they are not defined simply as a 'nonpolar force'.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain dispersion forces in terms of temporary electron redistribution and induced attractions, rather than labeling them as nonpolar without mechanism.
**Concept category:** definition
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "London force is a nonpolar force"

## Misconception 96: London force occurs between positive and negative ions on a molecule.

**Topic:** London forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces are not ion-ion attractions; they are weak intermolecular attractions due to induced dipoles in electron clouds.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish dispersion forces from electrostatic attraction between ions and from other types of intermolecular forces based on charge, polarity, and electron fluctuation.
**Concept category:** definition
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "London force occurs between positive and negative ions on a molecule"

## Misconception 97: The London force is a force of attraction that occurs between atoms.

**Topic:** London forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces are attractions between atoms or molecules arising from temporary induced dipoles, but the concept should not be reduced to a generic atom-to-atom attraction; the mechanism matters.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect the force to induced dipoles and recognize where it operates, rather than simply saying it is any attraction between atoms.
**Concept category:** definition
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "The London force is a force of attraction that occurs between atoms"

## Misconception 98: Nonpolar molecules can undergo permanent dipoles.

**Topic:** London forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** Nonpolar molecules do not have permanent dipoles; they can only develop temporary induced dipoles that give rise to dispersion forces.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should determine polarity from molecular symmetry and bond dipoles and understand that London forces come from temporary, not permanent, dipoles in nonpolar molecules.
**Concept category:** definition
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "Nonpolar molecules can undergo permanent dipoles"

## Misconception 99: The bonds in CH4 and O2 are weak bonds and this explains their low boiling point.

**Topic:** London forces / Strength and boiling point
**Correct concept:** Low boiling points of CH4 and O2 are due to weak intermolecular dispersion forces between molecules, not because the covalent bonds inside the molecules are weak.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should separate intramolecular covalent bond strength from intermolecular force strength, and relate low boiling point to weak attractions between molecules.
**Concept category:** strength of IMFs and effect on boiling point
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "The bond in the compounds CH4 and O2 is a weak bond"

## Misconception 100: Low boiling point is due to London force being very weak and not related to the number of electrons.

**Topic:** London forces / Strength and boiling point
**Correct concept:** London dispersion force strength increases with greater polarizability, which is often related to more electrons and larger electron clouds; these stronger dispersion forces can raise boiling point.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should relate electron count and polarizability to the strength of London dispersion forces, then connect stronger dispersion forces to higher boiling point.
**Concept category:** strength of IMFs and effect on boiling point
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "Students think that the low boiling point is due to the London force which is indeed very weak and is not related to the number of electrons"

## Misconception 101: HCl molecules are polar, so the interaction between HCl molecules is simply a polar intermolecular force.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole forces are attractions between the opposite partial charges of neighboring polar molecules; naming a substance as polar is not yet an explanation of the specific intermolecular interaction.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify partial positive and partial negative ends on adjacent polar molecules and explain the attraction between them.
**Concept category:** definition
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "HCI molecules are polar molecules, so the interaction between HCI molecules is a polar intermolecular force"

## Misconception 102: H atoms are positive atoms and Cl atoms are negative atoms, producing attractive forces.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** Partial charges in HCl arise from unequal electron sharing, but atoms are not fully positive or fully negative; dipole-dipole attraction is between partial charges of separate polar molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use electronegativity to assign partial charges and then describe attraction between the positive end of one molecule and the negative end of another, not between fully charged atoms.
**Concept category:** definition
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "H atoms as positive atoms and Cl as negative atoms produce attractive forces"

## Misconception 103: A polar molecule has a positive dipole and negative dipole in the polar region.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** A polar molecule has regions of partial positive and partial negative charge, and dipole-dipole forces are attractions between these regions on neighboring molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should understand dipoles as separated centers of partial charge, not as two independent 'positive' and 'negative' dipoles within one region.
**Concept category:** definition
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "This polar molecule has a positive dipole and negative dipole in the polar region"

## Misconception 104: Polar molecules have permanent dipoles so that positive dipoles will bind to negative dipoles.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** Polar molecules have permanent molecular dipoles, and dipole-dipole attraction occurs between the partial positive end of one molecule and the partial negative end of another molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should describe the interaction as attraction between opposite partial charges on different molecules rather than using the phrase 'positive dipoles bind to negative dipoles'.
**Concept category:** definition
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "Polar molecules have permanent dipoles so that positive dipoles will bind to negative dipoles."

## Misconception 105: Hydrogen bonds are formed due to shared electron usage.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Definition
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is an intermolecular attraction involving H bonded to N, O, or F interacting with a lone pair on another electronegative atom; it is not the covalent sharing of electrons within a bond.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish covalent electron sharing inside a molecule from hydrogen bonding between molecules based on electronegativity and lone pairs.
**Concept category:** definition
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bonds are formed due to shared electron usage"

## Misconception 106: Hydrogen bonds occur between N and H atoms.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Definition
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding occurs when H bonded to N, O, or F in one molecule interacts with a lone pair on N, O, or F in another molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify both the donor atom bonded to H and the acceptor lone pair on a neighboring molecule, rather than describing it as simply between N and H atoms.
**Concept category:** definition
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bonds occur between N and H atoms"

## Misconception 107: Hydrogen bonds use PEB for bonding.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Definition
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is based on electrostatic attraction between a hydrogen attached to N, O, or F and a lone pair on another molecule, not a generic 'PEB' concept as a bonding type.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain the specific donor-acceptor interaction involved in hydrogen bonding rather than using an unsupported abbreviation or vague bonding label.
**Concept category:** definition
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bonds uses PEB fot bonding"

## Misconception 108: Hydrogen bonds occur between atoms in a molecule, not intermolecular.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Definition
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is an intermolecular force between molecules, not a bond within one molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should determine whether the interaction is within one molecule or between separate molecules; hydrogen bonding is between molecules.
**Concept category:** definition
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "Students define that hydrogen bonds occur between atoms in a molecule, not intermolecular"

## Misconception 109: The difference in electronegativity of covalent compounds affects the boiling point.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Strength
**Correct concept:** Boiling point depends on the strength of intermolecular forces present; electronegativity differences matter insofar as they influence molecular polarity and hydrogen bonding, but boiling point is not directly determined by covalent bond electronegativity differences alone.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should link molecular structure and polarity to intermolecular forces, then connect those forces to boiling point.
**Concept category:** strength
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "Students assume that the difference in electronegativity of covalent compounds affects the boiling point."

## Misconception 110: The strength of the hydrogen bond is affected by the difference in electronegativity between H and N, O, and F.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Strength
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bond strength depends on the polarity of the H-X bond and the availability of a lone pair on a neighboring electronegative atom, along with molecular environment; it is not simply a direct function of electronegativity difference alone.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should consider the donor-acceptor geometry and the intermolecular context, not only the electronegativity difference between atoms.
**Concept category:** strength
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "The strength of the hydrogen bond is affected by the difference in electronegativity between H and the elements N, O, and F."

## Misconception 111: Hydrogen bond strength is affected by PEB.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Strength
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bond strength depends on the intermolecular attraction between a hydrogen donor and an electronegative acceptor, not on a vague PEB factor.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should base the explanation on donor-acceptor interactions and molecular structure, rather than an unsupported abbreviation or unrelated factor.
**Concept category:** strength
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bond strength is affected by PEB"

## Misconception 112: The strength of hydrogen bonds is related to the acidity of the substance.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Strength
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bond strength is not the same as acidity; acidity concerns proton donation, whereas hydrogen bonding concerns intermolecular attraction involving H attached to N, O, or F and a lone pair on another molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should separate acid-base behavior from intermolecular attraction and not use acidity as the basis for hydrogen bond strength.
**Concept category:** strength
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "The strength of hydrogen bonds is related to the acidity of the substance"

## Misconception 113: Hydrogen bonding occurs at the H atom with one of the F, O, and N atoms.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Formation
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding occurs between H bonded to N, O, or F in one molecule and a lone pair on N, O, or F in another molecule, not simply at an H atom with one of those atoms in the same molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify hydrogen bonding as an intermolecular donor-acceptor interaction and specify the two molecules involved.
**Concept category:** formation
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "Students assume that hydrogen bonding occurs at the H atom with one of the F, O, and, N atoms."

## Misconception 114: Hydrogen bonding occurs between O and H atoms in ether molecules.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Formation
**Correct concept:** Ethers do not hydrogen bond to each other as donors because they lack an O-H bond; oxygen in ether can act as a hydrogen-bond acceptor but not a donor.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should check whether the molecule has an H attached to O, N, or F to donate a hydrogen bond and whether another molecule has a lone-pair acceptor.
**Concept category:** formation
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bonding occurs between O and H atoms in ether molecules"

## Misconception 115: The bond between H and O in an alcohol molecule is a hydrogen bond.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Formation
**Correct concept:** The H-O bond within an alcohol is a covalent intramolecular bond; hydrogen bonding is the intermolecular attraction between alcohol molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish the covalent O-H bond inside the alcohol from the hydrogen bond that forms between the hydrogen of one molecule and the oxygen lone pair of another.
**Concept category:** formation
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "The bond between the elements H and O in the alcohol molecule is a hydrogen bond"

## Misconception 116: Hydrogen bonding is present in the OH group of polar molecules.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Formation
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is not determined just by the presence of an OH group or polarity; it requires a hydrogen attached to N, O, or F and an acceptor lone pair on a neighboring molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate both donor and acceptor requirements and remember that not every polar molecule with an OH group necessarily participates in hydrogen bonding the same way.
**Concept category:** formation
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bonding is present in the OH group of polar molecules"

## Misconception 117: Hydrogen bonding occurs in methane (C and H atoms).

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Formation
**Correct concept:** Methane does not form hydrogen bonds because H is bonded to carbon, not to N, O, or F, and methane lacks a sufficiently electronegative donor/acceptor system for hydrogen bonding.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should check the identity of the atom bonded to hydrogen and apply the rule that hydrogen bonding requires H attached to N, O, or F.
**Concept category:** formation
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students in a general chemistry course
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (35641-130089-1-PB/35641-130089-1-PB.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bonding occurs in methane (C and H atoms)"

## Misconception 118: Students attribute the yellowish-green colour of chlorine to chlorine atoms or chlorine molecules rather than to the macroscopic substance.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding and substance properties / Colour of substances
**Correct concept:** The colour is a macroscopic property of chlorine gas, not of isolated chlorine atoms or molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should distinguish the substance level from the particle level and explain colour as a property of the bulk sample under the given conditions, not as a property of individual atoms or molecules.
**Concept category:** macroscopic property to particles
**Prevalence:** 8.5% secondary school; 15.2% university for chlorine molecules; 8.5% secondary school for chlorine atoms
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "the yellowish green colour of chlorine is due to chlorine atoms"

## Misconception 119: Students think the water molecule itself has a boiling point of 100 °C.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding and substance properties / Boiling points
**Correct concept:** Boiling point is a property of the substance water, not of a single molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should reason that phase change temperatures describe the bulk substance at a given pressure, so the correct statement refers to liquid water as a substance.
**Concept category:** macroscopic property to particles
**Prevalence:** 10.0% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "The water molecule has a boiling point of 100 °C at 1-atm pressure."

## Misconception 120: Students believe branched organic compounds have stronger Van der Waals forces than straight-chain isomers.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding and intermolecular forces / Boiling points of organic compounds
**Correct concept:** For isomeric hydrocarbons, a less branched structure usually has greater surface contact and stronger intermolecular dispersion forces, giving a higher boiling point.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should compare molecular shape and surface area: greater contact surface allows stronger dispersion interactions, so the straight-chain isomer boils higher than the branched one.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces
**Prevalence:** 34.0% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "Van der Waals forces are more intense in branching organic compounds."

## Misconception 121: Students think Van der Waals forces become more intense simply as molar mass increases.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding and intermolecular forces / Boiling points of organic compounds
**Correct concept:** Boiling point depends on several factors, including surface area and molecular shape; molar mass alone does not determine intermolecular-force strength in such comparisons.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should compare the specific structural features of the molecules rather than using only molar mass; for isomers with equal molar mass, shape and contact area affect dispersion forces.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces
**Prevalence:** 8.0% secondary school; 23.0% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "Van der Waals forces are more intense as molar mass increases."

## Misconception 122: Students believe sodium iodide can dissolve in nonpolar solvents or that NaI is a molecule that dissolves there.

**Topic:** Solubility and conductivity / Solubility of ionic compounds
**Correct concept:** Ionic sodium iodide dissolves in polar solvents such as water because ion-dipole interactions stabilize the ions in solution.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should identify NaI as an ionic compound, then match it with polar solvents that can solvate and stabilize separated ions.
**Concept category:** ionic compounds as molecules
**Prevalence:** 10.0% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "The NaI molecules can be dissolved in nonpolar solvents."

## Misconception 123: Students think solid or molten/dissolved NaCl conducts because it has free electrons like a metal.

**Topic:** Solubility and conductivity / Electrical conductivity of ionic compounds
**Correct concept:** NaCl conducts when molten or dissolved because ions are mobile charges; in the solid, ions are fixed in the lattice.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should distinguish metallic conduction from ionic conduction and reason that electrical current in ionic compounds is carried by moving ions, not electrons.
**Concept category:** electron transfer / conductivity
**Prevalence:** 22.0% secondary school; 13.0% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "NaCl solid, molten or in solution, has free electrons that conduct electricity."

## Misconception 124: Students think NaCl exists as a molecule that dissociates into ions which conduct electricity.

**Topic:** Solubility and conductivity / Electrical conductivity of ionic compounds
**Correct concept:** NaCl is an ionic compound made of ions in a lattice; when molten or dissolved, the ions become mobile and conduct electricity.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should identify the substance as an ionic lattice rather than a molecule, and explain conduction by the movement of ions in the molten or aqueous state.
**Concept category:** ionic compounds as molecules
**Prevalence:** 14.0% secondary school; 11.0% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "NaCl molecule, molten or in solution, dissociates into a sodium ion and a chloride ion which are the conductors of the electric current."

## Misconception 125: Students believe graphite conducts electricity because its layers can slide over each other.

**Topic:** Solubility and conductivity / Conductivity of covalent substances
**Correct concept:** Graphite conducts because of delocalized electrons within layers, not because layers slide.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should connect conductivity to mobile charged particles; in graphite, delocalized electrons are available for charge transport.
**Concept category:** electrical conductivity
**Prevalence:** 22.0% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "Graphite conducts electricity because the layers of hexagonal carbon rings can slide over each other."

## Misconception 126: Students believe graphite conducts because its layers are bonded by Van der Waals forces.

**Topic:** Solubility and conductivity / Conductivity of covalent substances
**Correct concept:** Graphite conducts due to delocalized electrons in its structure, while Van der Waals forces are not the reason for conductivity.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should identify the charge carriers in graphite as delocalized electrons and not confuse weak intermolecular forces with electrical conduction.
**Concept category:** electrical conductivity
**Prevalence:** 20.0% secondary school; 12.0% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "Graphite conducts electricity because the layers of hexagonal carbon rings are bonded by Van der Waals forces."

## Misconception 127: Students believe diamond conducts electricity because it is a covalent 3D network with free electrons.

**Topic:** Solubility and conductivity / Conductivity of covalent substances
**Correct concept:** Diamond does not conduct electricity because its electrons are localized in covalent bonds and there are no mobile charge carriers.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should distinguish diamond from graphite and reason that a covalent network does not automatically imply conductivity; mobile electrons or ions are needed.
**Concept category:** electrical conductivity
**Prevalence:** 13.0% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "Diamond conducts electricity because is a three-dimensional network with covalent bonds between carbon atoms. Electrons are free and can conduct electrical current."

## Misconception 128: Students think NaCl is a molecular substance.

**Topic:** Intra- and intermolecular forces / Ionic vs molecular substances
**Correct concept:** NaCl is an ionic compound composed of ions in a lattice, not molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should classify NaCl by the presence of ions and ionic lattice structure, not by imagining discrete molecules.
**Concept category:** ionic compounds as molecules
**Prevalence:** 16.0% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "NaCl is a molecular substance."

## Misconception 129: Students think hydrogen bonds form in any molecule that contains H together with F, N, or O, even if H is not directly bonded to those atoms.

**Topic:** Intra- and intermolecular forces / Hydrogen bonding
**Correct concept:** A hydrogen bond requires hydrogen directly bonded to N, O, or F and attraction to a lone pair on another electronegative atom.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should check the bonding environment: only H attached to N/O/F can act as a hydrogen-bond donor, and an acceptor atom with lone pairs is needed.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Prevalence:** 19.0% secondary school; 16.0% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "H atoms plus atoms of N, or O or F"

## Misconception 130: Students think hydrogen bonds are stronger in compounds with nitrogen because nitrogen is more electronegative than oxygen.

**Topic:** Intra- and intermolecular forces / Hydrogen bond strength
**Correct concept:** For the comparison in the paper, ethanol has stronger hydrogen bonding than ethylamine because oxygen is more electronegative than nitrogen.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should compare the electronegativity of the atom bonded to hydrogen and the resulting hydrogen-bond strength, using the given molecules and their donor atoms.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Prevalence:** 15.0% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "N atom is more electronegative than O"

## Misconception 131: Students think hydrogen bonds are stronger in compounds containing more hydrogen atoms.

**Topic:** Intra- and intermolecular forces / Hydrogen bond strength
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen-bond strength depends on electronegativity and the donor-acceptor interaction, not simply on the number of hydrogen atoms present.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should focus on the nature of the hydrogen bond-forming atoms and the interaction, rather than counting hydrogens in the formula.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Prevalence:** 13.0% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "containing more hydrogen atoms"

## Misconception 132: Students think iodine is a molecular substance whose covalent bonds between iodine atoms are broken at the boiling point.

**Topic:** Intra- and intermolecular forces / Boiling point and bonding type
**Correct concept:** At the boiling point, intermolecular forces are overcome; covalent bonds within molecules are not broken for molecular iodine.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should distinguish intramolecular covalent bonds from intermolecular forces and know that boiling separates molecules, not atoms within molecules.
**Concept category:** intra- vs intermolecular forces
**Prevalence:** 34.2% secondary school; 19.2% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "iodine is a molecular substance with covalent bonds between iodine atoms that are broken at the boiling point"

## Misconception 133: Students classify HF as covalent simply because it consists of two non-metals sharing electrons, ignoring intermolecular forces and the substance type.

**Topic:** Intra- and intermolecular forces / Classification of substances
**Correct concept:** HF is a molecular substance with covalent H–F bonds between atoms and intermolecular forces, including van der Waals forces and hydrogen bonding, between molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should classify the substance by both intra- and intermolecular forces and not stop at the fact that the atoms share electrons.
**Concept category:** molecular vs covalent substances
**Prevalence:** 27.8% secondary school; 8.0% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "HF is a covalent substance since consists of two non-metallic elements bonded by sharing electrons."

## Misconception 134: Students classify HF as ionic because of the electronegativity difference between H and F.

**Topic:** Intra- and intermolecular forces / Classification of substances
**Correct concept:** HF is not ionic; it is a polar covalent molecular substance with hydrogen bonding between molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should use electronegativity difference to judge bond polarity, but also recognize that H–F is still covalent rather than ionic.
**Concept category:** bond type / electronegativity
**Prevalence:** 15.2% secondary school; 9.0% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "HF is an ionic substance since the difference in electronegativity of H and F atoms allows establishing and ionic bond."

## Misconception 135: Students think SiO2 is a molecular substance because it is made of two non-metallic elements linked by covalent bonds.

**Topic:** Intra- and intermolecular forces / Covalent networks vs molecular substances
**Correct concept:** SiO2 is a covalent network solid, not a molecular substance.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should recognize that covalent bonding can extend in a network, so the presence of covalent bonds does not imply discrete molecules.
**Concept category:** molecular vs covalent networks
**Prevalence:** 29.0% secondary school; 17.2% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "SiO2 is a molecular substance since it consists of two non-metallic elements linked by covalent bonds."

## Misconception 136: Students identify the arrangement of electron pairs around the central atom with the geometry of the molecule.

**Topic:** Geometry and polarity of molecules / Molecular geometry
**Correct concept:** Molecular geometry is the spatial arrangement of the atoms (nuclei) around the central atom; electron-pair arrangement determines it but is not the same as the molecular shape.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should first determine Lewis structure and electron-pair arrangement, then derive the actual molecular shape from atomic positions and bond angles.
**Concept category:** molecular geometry
**Prevalence:** 19.0% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "Identify the arrangement of electron pairs around the central atom with the geometry of the molecule."

## Misconception 137: Students attribute the shape of a molecule to the electron pairs, both lone and bonded, rather than to the atom arrangement.

**Topic:** Geometry and polarity of molecules / Molecular geometry
**Correct concept:** The shape of a molecule is the spatial arrangement of atoms around the central atom, while lone and bonded electron pairs are used to predict it.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should distinguish electron-pair geometry from molecular geometry and use the latter to describe the positions of atoms.
**Concept category:** molecular geometry
**Prevalence:** 50.6% secondary school; 83.8% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "Attribute the shape of the molecule to the electron pairs, both lone and bonded."

## Misconception 138: Students think bond polarity determines the shape of a molecule.

**Topic:** Geometry and polarity of molecules / Molecular geometry
**Correct concept:** Bond polarity influences molecular polarity, but molecular shape is determined by the arrangement of electron pairs and the positions of atoms.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should separate the question of molecular geometry from the question of whether bonds are polar; geometry comes from electron-pair repulsions and atom positions.
**Concept category:** molecular geometry
**Prevalence:** 15.2% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "Bond polarity determines the shape of a molecule."

## Misconception 139: Students assume tetrahedral geometry is enough to cancel bond dipoles regardless of the atoms attached to the central atom.

**Topic:** Geometry and polarity of molecules / Molecular polarity
**Correct concept:** Polarity depends on both molecular geometry and the identity/polarity of the bonds; tetrahedral geometry alone does not guarantee nonpolarity.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should evaluate the symmetry of the whole molecule and the nature of the substituent atoms before concluding that dipoles cancel.
**Concept category:** molecular polarity
**Prevalence:** 21.5% secondary school; 15.2% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "tetrahedral geometry is a necessary and sufficient condition to cancel the bond dipole moments regardless of the atoms bonded to the central atom."

## Misconception 140: Students identify the Lewis structure with the molecular shape and make wrong predictions of geometry and polarity.

**Topic:** Geometry and polarity of molecules / Molecular geometry and polarity
**Correct concept:** Lewis structure is used to infer electron-pair arrangement and then molecular geometry and polarity, but it is not itself the molecular shape.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should derive the Lewis structure first, then use it to determine electron-pair geometry, molecular geometry, and polarity separately.
**Concept category:** molecular geometry / polarity
**Prevalence:** 15.0% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "Identify the Lewis structure with the shape of the molecule and make wrong predictions of the geometry and polarity of molecules."

## Misconception 141: Students think H2S is linear because the two lone pairs are balanced and the resultant dipole moment is zero.

**Topic:** Geometry and polarity of molecules / Molecular polarity
**Correct concept:** H2S is bent; lone pairs affect its geometry, but they do not make it linear or necessarily zero-dipole by simple balancing.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should use VSEPR to determine that electron pairs around sulfur produce a bent molecular geometry, then assess polarity from the actual geometry and bond dipoles.
**Concept category:** molecular polarity
**Prevalence:** 10.0% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "H2S molecule is linear because the two lone electron pairs are balanced and the resultant dipole moment is zero."

## Misconception 142: Students believe any molecule with lone electron pairs is polar.

**Topic:** Geometry and polarity of molecules / Molecular polarity
**Correct concept:** Molecular polarity depends on both bond polarity and molecular geometry; lone pairs do not automatically make a molecule polar.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should determine whether bond dipoles cancel based on the full 3D geometry, not simply count lone pairs.
**Concept category:** molecular polarity
**Prevalence:** 25.3% secondary school; 12.0% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017/AJEEL_51_pp_1_15_2017.md)
**Evidence:** "All molecules having lone electron pairs are polar."

## Misconception 143: Students may think intermolecular forces are not electrostatic attractions and are fundamentally different from chemical bonds.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Electrostatic nature and relationship to bonds
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces are electrostatic attractions, like other chemical bonds, though they involve different particles and are much weaker than conventional bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that attraction between particles is an electrostatic phenomenon. They should compare the interacting particles and see that intermolecular forces are attractions between molecules, not a separate non-electrostatic category.
**Concept category:** electrostatic nature of intermolecular forces
**Population:** High school chemistry students in Singapore (Grades 11 and 12); the paper is textbook analysis, not a direct student interview study.
**Source:** An analysis of two textbooks on the topic of intermolecular forces (APFSLT-5-1-3/APFSLT-5-1-3.md)
**Evidence:** "intermolecular forces were just another type of chemical bond"

## Misconception 144: Students may think intermolecular forces are not especially weak, or may not distinguish their weakness from covalent, ionic, and metallic bonds.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Strength relative to conventional bonds
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces are much weaker than conventional chemical bonds such as ionic, covalent, and metallic bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare bond types by interaction strength and particle separation, understanding that intermolecular attractions are weaker because the particles are farther apart and the electrical dipoles are weaker.
**Concept category:** electrostatic nature of intermolecular forces
**Population:** High school chemistry students in Singapore (Grades 11 and 12); inferred misconception discussed by the authors.
**Source:** An analysis of two textbooks on the topic of intermolecular forces (APFSLT-5-1-3/APFSLT-5-1-3.md)
**Evidence:** "Vander Waals' bonds are, ofcourse, much weaker than covalent and ionic bonds."

## Misconception 145: Students may think van der Waals forces do not exist between gas particles and therefore do not affect gases.

**Topic:** Gases and intermolecular forces / Real gas behavior
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces act between gas particles too and help explain why real gases deviate from ideal-gas behavior.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should consider that gas particles still attract each other, especially when they are close enough, so deviations from pV = nRT arise from intermolecular forces.
**Concept category:** gases / non-ideality
**Population:** High school chemistry students in Singapore (Grades 11 and 12); discussed as a likely student difficulty.
**Source:** An analysis of two textbooks on the topic of intermolecular forces (APFSLT-5-1-3/APFSLT-5-1-3.md)
**Evidence:** "students might think that there were no van der Waals forces between gases"

## Misconception 146: Students may think boiling breaks intermolecular forces completely so that no intermolecular forces remain in the gas state.

**Topic:** Phase changes / Boiling and intermolecular forces
**Correct concept:** Boiling overcomes enough intermolecular attraction for particles to separate into the gas phase, but it does not mean intermolecular forces cease to exist as a concept for gases.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish between overcoming attractions during a phase change and the general presence of weak attractions between particles under all conditions.
**Concept category:** phase changes
**Population:** High school chemistry students in Singapore (Grades 11 and 12); inferred from textbook language.
**Source:** An analysis of two textbooks on the topic of intermolecular forces (APFSLT-5-1-3/APFSLT-5-1-3.md)
**Evidence:** "could gtve students the itnpression that when a liquid was boiled, intermolecular forces would be broken resulting in no intermolecular forces between the particles in a gas"

## Misconception 147: Students may think molecular polarity can be identified without using the vector sum of bond dipoles and molecular geometry, or may not use net dipole moment to predict polarity.

**Topic:** Molecular polarity / Determining molecular polarity
**Correct concept:** A molecule is polar when its bond dipoles do not cancel; molecular geometry must be considered through VSEPR and the vector sum of dipole moments to determine the net dipole moment.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should first determine the molecular shape using VSEPR, then identify individual bond dipoles from electronegativity differences, and finally add them vectorially to decide whether the molecule has a net dipole moment.
**Concept category:** polarity / dipole moments
**Population:** High school chemistry students in Singapore (Grades 11 and 12); identified as a missing concept likely to contribute to misconceptions.
**Source:** An analysis of two textbooks on the topic of intermolecular forces (APFSLT-5-1-3/APFSLT-5-1-3.md)
**Evidence:** "the net dipole moment of a molecule"

## Misconception 148: Students may think bond polarity is not connected to electronegativity differences and displacement of shared electrons.

**Topic:** Molecular polarity / Electronegativity and bond polarity
**Correct concept:** Differences in electronegativity cause unequal sharing of bonding electrons, creating a polar bond with a dipole moment.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the electronegativities of the bonded atoms, infer electron density shift toward the more electronegative atom, and then conclude that the bond is polar.
**Concept category:** bond polarity / electronegativity
**Population:** High school chemistry students in Singapore (Grades 11 and 12); treated as essential but insufficiently integrated content.
**Source:** An analysis of two textbooks on the topic of intermolecular forces (APFSLT-5-1-3/APFSLT-5-1-3.md)
**Evidence:** "Differences in electronegativity between two atoms cause the displacement of the shared electrons between them."

## Misconception 149: Students may think hydrogen bonding is simply a generic strong intermolecular attraction rather than a specific interaction involving H bonded to N, O, or F and attraction to a lone pair on another N, O, or F.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Nature of hydrogen bonding
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is a specific interaction between an electron-deficient H attached to N, O, or F and a lone pair on another N, O, or F atom.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the donor atom bonded to H, the acceptor atom with a lone pair, and recognize the special requirement for N, O, or F in hydrogen bonding.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Population:** High school chemistry students in Singapore (Grades 11 and 12); the paper discusses likely misconceptions from textbook treatment.
**Source:** An analysis of two textbooks on the topic of intermolecular forces (APFSLT-5-1-3/APFSLT-5-1-3.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bonding is defined as the attraction of an electron deficient hydrogen atom"

## Misconception 150: Students may think hydrogen-bond strength follows the same order as bond polarity alone, or that water should have the strongest hydrogen bonding because it forms the highest boiling point.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Strength ranking among HF, H2O, and NH3
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen-bond strength depends on both bond polarity and lone-pair availability; in the paper’s cited comparison the strength order is HF > H2O > NH3, while water’s highest boiling point is due to more extensive hydrogen bonding, not stronger individual hydrogen bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare both donor bond polarity and acceptor lone-pair availability, then separately consider how many hydrogen bonds each molecule can form to explain boiling point trends.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding / strength trends
**Population:** High school chemistry students in Singapore (Grades 11 and 12); discussed as a possible student at a loss when comparing boiling points.
**Source:** An analysis of two textbooks on the topic of intermolecular forces (APFSLT-5-1-3/APFSLT-5-1-3.md)
**Evidence:** "The strength of the hydrogen bonding as follows: H-F>H-O>H-N"

## Misconception 151: Students may think the substance with the strongest hydrogen bonding must always have the highest boiling point.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Boiling point vs bond strength
**Correct concept:** Boiling point depends on the overall extent and pattern of intermolecular attractions, not only on the strength of an individual hydrogen bond; water’s higher boiling point is due to more extensive hydrogen bonding network formation.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare both bond strength and the number of hydrogen bonds each molecule can form per molecule, then use that to explain the macroscopic boiling point trend.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding / macroscopic properties
**Population:** High school chemistry students in Singapore (Grades 11 and 12); explicitly discussed by the authors.
**Source:** An analysis of two textbooks on the topic of intermolecular forces (APFSLT-5-1-3/APFSLT-5-1-3.md)
**Evidence:** "water had the highest boiling point of the three substances based only on the strength of hydrogen bonding"

## Misconception 152: Students may think hydrogen bonding is only intermolecular and may not recognize intramolecular hydrogen bonding when groups capable of hydrogen bonding are close together.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Intramolecular vs intermolecular hydrogen bonding
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding can be intramolecular as well as intermolecular, depending on molecular structure and proximity of donor and acceptor sites.
