**Expected reasoning:** A student should inspect the molecular structure to determine whether H-bond donors and acceptors can interact within the same molecule or between different molecules.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding / intramolecular
**Population:** High school chemistry students in Singapore (Grades 11 and 12); discussed as a topic insufficiently covered.
**Source:** An analysis of two textbooks on the topic of intermolecular forces (APFSLT-5-1-3/APFSLT-5-1-3.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bonding can be intermolecular or intramolecular in nature."

## Misconception 153: Students may think the presence of hydrogen-bonding groups always increases intermolecular hydrogen bonding and therefore does not reduce boiling or melting points.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Intramolecular hydrogen bonding effect on properties
**Correct concept:** Intramolecular hydrogen bonding can reduce the number of sites available for intermolecular hydrogen bonding, which can lower boiling point or melting point.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should determine whether hydrogen bonding is internal to one molecule or between molecules; if internal bonding ties up donor/acceptor sites, fewer intermolecular attractions are possible.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding / intramolecular
**Population:** High school chemistry students in Singapore (Grades 11 and 12); identified as an omitted idea likely to cause confusion.
**Source:** An analysis of two textbooks on the topic of intermolecular forces (APFSLT-5-1-3/APFSLT-5-1-3.md)
**Evidence:** "limit number of sites available for intermolecular hydrogen bonding"

## Misconception 154: Students may think instantaneous dipole-induced dipole interactions occur only in non-polar molecules and not in polar molecules.

**Topic:** London dispersion forces / Occurrence in polar molecules
**Correct concept:** All molecules, including polar molecules, possess instantaneous dipole-induced dipole interactions; polar molecules also have permanent dipole-permanent dipole interactions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that electron clouds fluctuate in every molecule, so dispersion forces are always present alongside any other intermolecular forces.
**Concept category:** instantaneous dipole-induced dipole interactions
**Population:** High school chemistry students in Singapore (Grades 11 and 12); highlighted as an omitted emphasis in the textbook analysis.
**Source:** An analysis of two textbooks on the topic of intermolecular forces (APFSLT-5-1-3/APFSLT-5-1-3.md)
**Evidence:** "instantaneous dipole-induced dipole interactions do exist in polar molecules"

## Misconception 155: Students may think the strength of instantaneous dipole-induced dipole interactions depends only on the number of electrons, or only on molecule size/shape, without considering surface area of contact.

**Topic:** London dispersion forces / Factors affecting strength
**Correct concept:** The strength of dispersion forces depends on both electron number/polarisability and molecular surface area or contact area.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare electron count and molecular shape/surface area to determine how easily electron clouds are distorted and how much contact can occur between molecules.
**Concept category:** instantaneous dipole-induced dipole interactions / factors
**Population:** High school chemistry students in Singapore (Grades 11 and 12); treated as a likely misconception due to insufficient discussion in the textbooks.
**Source:** An analysis of two textbooks on the topic of intermolecular forces (APFSLT-5-1-3/APFSLT-5-1-3.md)
**Evidence:** "The greater the surface area of contact between molecules"

## Misconception 156: Students may think instantaneous dipole-induced dipole interactions can never be stronger than hydrogen bonding.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces comparison / Dispersion vs hydrogen bonding strength
**Correct concept:** In some cases dispersion forces can be stronger than hydrogen bonding, depending on molecular size, surface area, and polarisability.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare all relevant intermolecular-force contributions rather than assuming one force type is always strongest in every substance.
**Concept category:** intermolecular force comparison
**Population:** High school chemistry students in Singapore (Grades 11 and 12); identified as a missing comparison in the texts.
**Source:** An analysis of two textbooks on the topic of intermolecular forces (APFSLT-5-1-3/APFSLT-5-1-3.md)
**Evidence:** "instantaneous dipole-induced dipole interactions can stronger be than hydrogen-bonding"

## Misconception 157: There are only two kinds of bonds: covalent and ionic; anything else is just a force or not a proper bond.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Bond types
**Correct concept:** Chemical bonding includes more than just covalent and ionic bonds; metallic bonding is also a real bonding type, and intermolecular forces are different from bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish among covalent, ionic, and metallic bonding, and separately identify intermolecular forces as attractions between molecules rather than as bond types within all substances.
**Concept category:** general bonding conceptions
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** There are only two kinds of bonds: covalent bonds and ionic bonds. Anything else is just a force

## Misconception 158: Electrons are ions, and bonding occurs between electrons.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Electrons and bonding
**Correct concept:** Electrons are not ions; chemical bonding involves interactions between atoms/ions, not bonds between electrons themselves.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that ions are charged atoms or groups of atoms, while electrons are subatomic particles. Bonding is explained by electrostatic attraction or electron sharing/transfer between atoms, depending on bond type.
**Concept category:** general bonding conceptions
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** Electrons are ions and bonding occurs between the

## Misconception 159: Atoms bond mainly to balance their total charge or because they are 'charged', rather than through electrostatic attraction and lower-energy configurations.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Why atoms bond
**Correct concept:** Atoms bond because electrostatic interactions and energy considerations lead to stable bonding arrangements; atoms are not bonding simply to equalize total charge.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that bonding results from attractions between oppositely charged nuclei and electrons, or ions in ionic bonding, and that bonding leads to lower-energy, more stable arrangements such as full valence shells or favorable electrostatic configurations.
**Concept category:** why atoms bond
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** atoms want to balance their total charge and thus bond with other

## Misconception 160: Atoms bond because they 'want' to get filled shells, implying a teleological goal rather than an explanation based on electronic structure and stability.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Why atoms bond
**Correct concept:** Atoms bond to achieve lower-energy, more stable electron configurations; the octet/duet pattern is an outcome of electronic structure, not a purpose.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect valence electron arrangement and electrostatic stability to bonding, explaining that atoms form bonds because the resulting configuration is energetically favorable.
**Concept category:** why atoms bond
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** to produce filled shells rather than filled shells being the

## Misconception 161: Atoms/particles 'want' to give or lose electrons because they desire to lose energy or because shells are 'full'.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Ontological beliefs
**Correct concept:** Electron transfer is explained by energetic and electrostatic considerations, not by particle desire or intention.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain ionic bonding with the tendency of systems to reach a lower-energy, more stable arrangement, not as an action motivated by atoms' intentions.
**Concept category:** ontological beliefs
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** it desire to lose energy

## Misconception 162: A covalent bond is simply the sharing of electrons, with little or no recognition of the electrostatic attraction involved.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Nature of covalent bond
**Correct concept:** A covalent bond is an attractive interaction between nuclei and shared electrons; electron sharing is part of the model, but the bond is the result of electrostatic attraction.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should describe covalent bonding as shared electron density between atoms that creates attraction between nuclei and the shared electron pair, stabilizing the molecule.
**Concept category:** general covalent bonding confusion
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** A covalent bond holds atoms together because the bond is sharing electrons.

## Misconception 163: Metals can form covalent bonds with non-metals, so metallic substances are interpreted through covalent bonding ideas.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Covalent bonding in metals
**Correct concept:** Metallic bonding is distinct from covalent bonding; metals are held together by metallic bonding, not by covalent bonds with non-metals.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify metallic bonding as a lattice of positive metal ions and delocalized electrons, separate from covalent bonding between non-metal atoms.
**Concept category:** general covalent bonding confusion
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** metals such as

## Misconception 164: All covalent bonds are equally shared; bond polarity is determined only by the number of valence electrons or is ignored.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Bond polarity
**Correct concept:** Bond polarity depends on differences in electronegativity between bonded atoms, leading to unequal sharing of electrons in polar covalent bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare electronegativities of the two atoms to determine whether electrons are shared equally or unequally, which in turn determines bond polarity.
**Concept category:** bond polarity
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** Equal sharing of the electron pair occurs in all covalent bonds.

## Misconception 165: Molecular shape is due only to repulsion between bonding electron pairs, ignoring the role of lone pairs.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Molecular shape
**Correct concept:** Molecular shape is determined by repulsions among all electron pairs around the central atom, including bonding and lone pairs.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should count both bonding and non-bonding electron pairs around the central atom and use electron-pair repulsion to predict molecular geometry.
**Concept category:** shape of molecules
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** molecules due only to the repulsion between the bonding electron pairs.

## Misconception 166: A molecule is polar simply because it contains polar bonds, or nonpolar molecules are those with atoms of similar electronegativity.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Molecular polarity
**Correct concept:** Molecular polarity depends on the vector sum of bond dipoles and molecular geometry; polar bonds do not always make a polar molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should determine bond polarity from electronegativity differences and then consider the symmetry of the molecular shape to decide whether dipoles cancel or produce a net molecular dipole.
**Concept category:** polarity of molecules
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** molecule is polar because it has polar bonds.

## Misconception 167: Nitrogen and other atoms can share electron pairs without regard to the octet rule, or students treat the octet rule as allowing incorrect bonding patterns.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Octet rule
**Correct concept:** Atoms in main-group covalent molecules generally seek stable valence shells consistent with the octet rule, with known exceptions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should count valence electrons, determine how many electrons each atom needs to complete its valence shell, and then use that to predict the number of shared electron pairs and likely bonding pattern.
**Concept category:** octet rule
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** Nitrogen atoms can share five electron pairs in bonding.

## Misconception 168: Molecular solids are interpreted as continuous covalent lattices with strong bonds, or as made of discrete molecular species in all network solids.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Lattices
**Correct concept:** Covalent network solids consist of atoms linked in extended networks; molecular solids are made of discrete molecules held together by intermolecular forces.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish between a molecular solid and a network covalent solid by identifying whether the solid consists of discrete molecules or an extended lattice of atoms.
**Concept category:** octet rule / lattices
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** molecular solids is due to strong bonds in the continuous covalent lattices.

## Misconception 169: Graphite conducts electricity because it has layers of carbon atoms that can slip over each other, linking conductivity to physical sliding rather than delocalized electrons.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Graphite conductivity
**Correct concept:** Graphite conducts electricity because it contains delocalized electrons that can move through the structure.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the presence of mobile electrons in graphite's layered structure and connect those electrons to electrical conduction.
**Concept category:** electrical conductivity of graphite
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** Graphite conducts electricity because it has layers of carbon ato

## Misconception 170: Ionic substances are made of discrete molecules or ion pairs such as Na+ and Cl− units.

**Topic:** Ionic bonding / Structure of ionic substances
**Correct concept:** Ionic substances form extended ionic lattices of oppositely charged ions, not discrete molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that ionic compounds are held together in a crystal lattice by electrostatic attractions among many ions, so formulas like NaCl represent ratios, not molecules.
**Concept category:** structure of ionic substances
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** Ionic substances are discrete

## Misconception 171: An ion forms only one ionic bond, usually to the atom/ion involved in electron transfer.

**Topic:** Ionic bonding / Number of ionic bonds in lattices
**Correct concept:** In an ionic lattice, each ion interacts electrostatically with multiple surrounding ions; ionic bonding is not a one-to-one bond.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that lattice ions are surrounded by several oppositely charged ions and that bonding in ionic solids is a network of attractions rather than discrete pairwise bonds.
**Concept category:** number of ionic bonding in lattices
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** An ionic bond only occurs between the ato

## Misconception 172: Bonds exist only for the specific ion pair that exchanged electrons with each other.

**Topic:** Ionic bonding / History conjecture
**Correct concept:** In ionic lattices, ions are attracted to multiple counter-ions; bonding is not limited to the original electron-transfer pair.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should understand that after electron transfer, ions become part of a lattice in which each ion is surrounded by many oppositely charged ions.
**Concept category:** history conjecture
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** Bonds are only for

## Misconception 173: Ionic interactions are 'just forces' and not real bonds.

**Topic:** Ionic bonding / Just forces conjecture
**Correct concept:** Ionic bonding is a real electrostatic bond arising from attraction between oppositely charged ions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain ionic bonding as mutual electrostatic attraction in a lattice, not as something weaker or qualitatively different from bonding.
**Concept category:** just forces conjecture
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** Ionic bonds are not real bonds in the sense of covalent bonds

## Misconception 174: The result of ionic bonding is neutralization or the canceling of charges leading to discrete neutral molecules.

**Topic:** Ionic bonding / Formation of ionic bonding
**Correct concept:** Ionic bonding forms electrically neutral formula units within an extended ionic lattice; ions remain charged and are held together by electrostatic attraction.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that electron transfer creates ions that attract one another in a lattice, while the compound as a whole is neutral because charges balance in the formula unit.
**Concept category:** formation of ionic bonding
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** The result of the attraction between the two oppositely charged ions

## Misconception 175: Ionic bonding is the transfer of electrons rather than the attraction of ions that results after transfer; electron transfer is the bond itself.

**Topic:** Ionic bonding / Transfer of electrons
**Correct concept:** Electron transfer produces ions, and ionic bonding is the electrostatic attraction between the resulting ions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should separate the process of electron transfer from the bonding force: transfer creates charged ions, and attraction between those ions constitutes the bond.
**Concept category:** transfer of electrons in ionic bonding
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** Ionic bonds are the transfer of electrons, rather than the attractions of the ions that result from the transfer of electrons.

## Misconception 176: A sodium atom can only form one ionic bond because it has only one valence electron to donate.

**Topic:** Ionic bonding / Valency and bond number
**Correct concept:** Ionic bonding in solids is not limited to a single one-to-one bond; an ion can attract many oppositely charged ions in the lattice.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect valence electron donation to ion formation, then understand that the resulting ion participates in multiple lattice attractions rather than a single bond.
**Concept category:** number of ionic bonding in lattices
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** A sodium atom can only form one ionic bond, because it only has one electron in its outer shell to donate

## Misconception 177: Metals are molecular in nature, and metallic bonding is not a real bond like covalent bonding.

**Topic:** Metallic bonding / Nature of metallic bonding
**Correct concept:** Metallic bonding is a real bonding type in which metal cations are held together by delocalized electrons in an extended lattice.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should describe metals as non-molecular, with positive metal ions in a lattice and delocalized electrons providing cohesion and conductivity.
**Concept category:** general metallic bonding difficulties
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** Metals are

## Misconception 178: Intermolecular forces are the forces within a molecule or within a continuous covalent solid.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Force location
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces act between molecules; intramolecular forces act within molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish whether an attraction is holding atoms together inside a molecule or attracting separate molecules to one another.
**Concept category:** general alternative conceptions on intermolecular forces
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** students often incorrectly identify intermolecular forces as the forces within a molecule or continuous covalent solid

## Misconception 179: Intermolecular forces are the same as covalent bonds.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Force equivalence
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces are weaker attractions between molecules and are different from the covalent bonds inside molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the location and strength of the interactions, recognizing that covalent bonds hold atoms together within a molecule while intermolecular forces hold molecules together.
**Concept category:** general alternative conceptions on intermolecular forces
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** equate intermolecular forces with covalent bonds

## Misconception 180: Intermolecular forces are stronger than intramolecular bonding, or ionic bonds are weaker than van der Waals forces.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Relative strength
**Correct concept:** Intramolecular covalent or ionic bonds are generally much stronger than intermolecular forces such as van der Waals forces and hydrogen bonding.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare bond energies and recognize that intermolecular forces are responsible for physical properties like boiling point, but do not usually exceed intramolecular bond strengths.
**Concept category:** general alternative conceptions on intermolecular forces
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** learners believe intermolecular bonding is stronger than intramolecular bonding

## Misconception 181: Hydrogen bonding is one of the strongest bonds and stronger than a covalent bond.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Hydrogen bonding
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is an intermolecular attraction that is much weaker than a covalent bond.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should classify hydrogen bonding as an intermolecular force and compare its strength to covalent bonding, recognizing that covalent bonds are intramolecular and stronger.
**Concept category:** general alternative conceptions on intermolecular forces
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** A hydrogen bond is stronger than a covalent bond.

## Misconception 182: Polar molecular substances such as water have no intermolecular forces.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Polar substances
**Correct concept:** Polar molecules do have intermolecular attractions; water, for example, exhibits strong intermolecular forces including hydrogen bonding.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that molecular polarity leads to intermolecular attractions and that these attractions influence state and boiling point.
**Concept category:** general alternative conceptions on intermolecular forces
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** believe it is absent in polar molecular substances such as water

## Misconception 183: Bond breaking releases energy and bond making requires energy input.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding energetics / Bond breaking and making
**Correct concept:** Breaking bonds requires energy input, and forming bonds releases energy.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use energy conservation: energy must be supplied to overcome attractions when bonds break, and energy is released when new attractive interactions form.
**Concept category:** energy in chemical bonds
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** Bond breaking is both exo- and endothermic. Energy is needed to break bonds initially, but once

## Misconception 184: Students do not connect thermodynamic quantities such as enthalpy, bond enthalpy, and entropy to bond formation and breaking.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding energetics / Enthalpy and bond formation
**Correct concept:** Bond formation and bond breaking must be understood in terms of energy changes and thermodynamics.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should relate bond formation/breaking to energy changes, using bond enthalpies and reaction enthalpy to explain whether energy is absorbed or released.
**Concept category:** energy in chemical bonds
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** students had no clear understanding of the nature of the chemical bonds and energetics involved

## Misconception 185: Molecular polarity is determined by the number of valence electrons, lone pairs, or ionic charge rather than by bond polarity and molecular geometry.

**Topic:** Electronegativity and polarity / Determinants of polarity
**Correct concept:** Molecular polarity depends on electronegativity differences and the geometry of the molecule, which together determine whether dipoles cancel.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should determine bond polarities from electronegativity, then consider molecular shape to see whether the dipoles sum to a net polarity.
**Concept category:** electronegativity
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** the number of valence electrons, the presence of lone pairs of electrons or ionic charge determine molecular polarity

## Misconception 186: Attraction between two oppositely charged species results in neutralization rather than bond formation.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Electrostatic nature of bonding
**Correct concept:** Attraction between oppositely charged species forms an ionic bond or electrostatic attraction; charges are not simply neutralized by the attraction itself.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish charge cancellation from attraction: opposite charges can attract and remain present as ions in a bonded structure.
**Concept category:** electrostatic nature of chemical bonding
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** attraction between two oppositely charged species was thought to result in neutralization rather than bond formation

## Misconception 187: Students rely on simplistic models such as the sea-of-electrons or octet rule as if they fully explain all bonding situations.

**Topic:** Mental models / Simplified bonding models
**Correct concept:** Models are limited representations; more sophisticated models are needed when simple models cannot explain phenomena.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate the explanatory power of a model and switch to a more suitable one when the simple model fails, rather than assuming one model works for all cases.
**Concept category:** students' mental models of chemical bonding
**Source:** A review of chemical bonding studies: needs, aims, methods of exploring students' conceptions, general knowledge claims and students' alternative conceptions (AREVIE~1/AREVIE~1.md)
**Evidence:** students from all three academic levels prefer simple (or realistic looking) mental models for chemical bonding

## Misconception 188: Chemical bonding in metallic substances is weak or not strong.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Bond strength
**Correct concept:** Metallic bonding is strong enough to hold metals together and account for properties such as hardness and malleability.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect observable properties of metals, such as solid form and resistance to breaking, with strong electrostatic attraction between metal ions and delocalized electrons rather than assuming weak bonding.
**Concept category:** bond strength
**Prevalence:** Prevalent across all levels; specific AC 1.
**Population:** Upper secondary, undergraduate, and postgraduate students
**Source:** Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students (Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students/Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students.md)
**Evidence:** "The alternative conception that chemical bonding in metallic, ionic and covalent substances is weak was prevalent across all levels"

## Misconception 189: Intramolecular covalent bonding is weak.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Bond strength
**Correct concept:** Covalent bonds within molecules are strong chemical bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish strong intramolecular covalent bonds from weaker intermolecular forces and use molecular structure to explain stability and bond-breaking difficulty.
**Concept category:** bond strength
**Prevalence:** Observed in some cases; AC 2.
**Population:** Upper secondary and tertiary students
**Source:** Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students (Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students/Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students.md)
**Evidence:** "Similar views were expressed about covalent bonding in some cases (AC 2)"

## Misconception 190: Ionic bonding is weak or weaker than covalent bonding.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Ionic bond strength
**Correct concept:** Ionic bonding involves strong electrostatic attraction between oppositely charged ions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain that ionic solids are held by strong electrostatic forces and that brittleness does not mean the bonds are weak; it reflects lattice structure and ion displacement.
**Concept category:** bond strength
**Prevalence:** Prevalent across all levels from secondary school to postgraduate.
**Population:** Upper secondary, undergraduate, and postgraduate students
**Source:** Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students (Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students/Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students.md)
**Evidence:** "The view that the electrostatic forces holding ionic compounds together are weak also was prevalent (AC 3, Table II)."

## Misconception 191: Continuous ionic or metallic lattices are molecular in nature or contain discrete molecules.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Molecularity of ionic and metallic lattices
**Correct concept:** Ionic and metallic solids are continuous lattices, not collections of separate molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should interpret lattice diagrams as extended repeating structures and recognize that ions or metal atoms are arranged in a network rather than in individual molecules.
**Concept category:** molecularity / lattice structure
**Prevalence:** Across all levels; AC 4.
**Population:** Upper secondary, undergraduate, and postgraduate students
**Source:** Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students (Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students/Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students.md)
**Evidence:** "some of the students across all levels possessed alternative conceptions about the nature of metallic and ionic lattices, seeming to believe that such lattices were molecular in nature or contained distinct molecular species"

## Misconception 192: Metals and ionic compounds possess intermolecular bonds such as van der Waals forces between structural units.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Intermolecular forces in ionic and metallic lattices
**Correct concept:** Metallic and ionic solids are held together by metallic bonding or ionic electrostatic attraction within the lattice, not intermolecular bonds between molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should avoid importing molecular intermolecular-force language into extended lattices and instead explain cohesion using the appropriate lattice bonding model.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces
**Prevalence:** Three students; AC 5.
**Population:** Upper secondary and tertiary students
**Source:** Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students (Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students/Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students.md)
**Evidence:** "A related alternative conception, that metals and ionic compounds possess intermolecular bonds, was evident for three students (AC 5, Table II)."

## Misconception 193: The sodium ion is larger than the chloride ion.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Ion size
**Correct concept:** Na+ is smaller than Cl− because cations lose electrons and anions gain electrons; ionic radius depends on electron count and effective nuclear attraction.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare ionic radii by considering electron loss/gain, shell occupancy, and nuclear attraction rather than assuming the cation is larger because of the parent atom or periodic trends in atomic size.
**Concept category:** ion size
**Prevalence:** Remarkably prevalent; AC 6.
**Population:** Upper secondary and tertiary students
**Source:** Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students (Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students/Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students.md)
**Evidence:** "There were two remarkably prevalent alternative conceptions related to the size of ions identified; one in which the sodium ion was viewed as being larger in size than the chloride (AC 6, Table II)"

## Misconception 194: The lithium ion is larger than the sodium ion.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Ion size
**Correct concept:** Li+ is smaller than Na+ because size increases down a group as additional electron shells are added.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare cations by atomic number and shell number, recognizing that sodium has an extra occupied shell relative to lithium, making sodium larger.
**Concept category:** ion size
**Prevalence:** Remarkably prevalent; AC 7.
**Population:** Upper secondary and tertiary students
**Source:** Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students (Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students/Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students.md)
**Evidence:** "the other that the lithium ion was larger than the sodium ion (AC 7, Table II)."

## Misconception 195: Polar covalent compounds contain fully charged species such as H+ and Cl−.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Polar covalent / ionic character
**Correct concept:** Polar covalent bonds involve partial charges, not full ionic charges, on atoms within the molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish partial charges in polar covalent bonds from actual ions formed by electron transfer, and use electronegativity differences to infer polarity.
**Concept category:** bond polarity / ionic character
**Prevalence:** Highly prevalent; AC 8.
**Population:** Upper secondary and tertiary students
**Source:** Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students (Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students/Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students.md)
**Evidence:** "polar covalent compounds contain charged species (AC 8, Table II)."

## Misconception 196: Molecular iodine contains I− ions.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Molecular charge assignment
**Correct concept:** I2 is a neutral covalent molecule with shared electrons, not an ionic compound containing iodide ions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that homonuclear diatomic molecules have no electronegativity difference and therefore share electrons equally without forming ions.
**Concept category:** bond polarity / ionic character
**Prevalence:** Observed in Mary and Rose; AC 9.
**Population:** Upper secondary and tertiary students
**Source:** Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students (Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students/Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students.md)
**Evidence:** "the same alternative conception is seen for such substances ... Mary and Rose seemed to believe that the iodine atoms in I2 carry a negative charge"

## Misconception 197: Metallic lattices contain neutral atoms rather than positive metal ions plus delocalized electrons.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Metallic bonding species
**Correct concept:** Metallic bonding consists of positive metal ions in a lattice surrounded by delocalized electrons.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the metal cations and sea of electrons in the metallic bonding model instead of treating the lattice particles as neutral atoms.
**Concept category:** metallic bonding / particle identity
**Prevalence:** Identified for some students; AC 10 and AC 11.
**Population:** Upper secondary and tertiary students
**Source:** Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students (Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students/Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students.md)
**Evidence:** "Two alternative conceptions of this nature were identified, namely, that metallic lattices contain neutral atoms and that the positive ions present are nuclei."

## Misconception 198: The positive species in metallic lattices are nuclei rather than ions.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Metallic bonding species
**Correct concept:** The positive species in metallic bonding are metal ions, not bare nuclei.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish an ion from a nucleus and explain metallic bonding as attraction between cations and delocalized electrons after valence electrons are removed.
**Concept category:** metallic bonding / particle identity
**Prevalence:** Identified for some students; AC 10 and AC 11.
**Population:** Upper secondary and tertiary students
**Source:** Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students (Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students/Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students.md)
**Evidence:** "that metallic lattices contain neutral atoms and that the positive ions present are nuclei."

## Misconception 199: Electronegativity is attraction for a single electron rather than for a shared pair of electrons.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Electronegativity
**Correct concept:** Electronegativity is the attraction of an atom in a bond for the shared pair of electrons.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that electronegativity is about unequal sharing in a bond and should not reduce it to a lone-electron attraction based on octet completion.
**Concept category:** electronegativity
**Prevalence:** Observed in Steve; AC 12.
**Population:** Upper secondary and tertiary students
**Source:** Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students (Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students/Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students.md)
**Evidence:** "Steve seemed to believe that the attraction was for a single electron rather than a greater attraction for the shared pair."

## Misconception 200: Molecular iodine is metallic in nature.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Metal vs non-metal character
**Correct concept:** I2 is a molecular covalent substance, not a metal.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use composition and bonding to identify iodine as a covalent molecular substance even if it appears shiny or metallic.
**Concept category:** metal/non-metal nature
**Prevalence:** Observed in Christine and Alan; AC 13.
**Population:** Upper secondary and tertiary students
**Source:** Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students (Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students/Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students.md)
**Evidence:** "An interesting alternative conception revealed in the study was that molecular iodine (I2 ) is metallic in nature (AC 13,Table II)."

## Misconception 201: Ionic bonding in compounds like sodium chloride is covalent.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Ionic vs covalent bonding
**Correct concept:** Sodium chloride is an ionic substance held together by electrostatic attraction between ions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish electron transfer and ion formation from electron sharing in covalent bonding, and classify NaCl as ionic rather than covalent.
**Concept category:** ionic vs covalent bonding
**Prevalence:** Two secondary school students; AC 14.
**Population:** Year 12 secondary school students
**Source:** Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students (Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students/Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students.md)
**Evidence:** "Two secondary school students, Anne and Frances, identified the bonding in ionic compounds as covalent, despite initially describing a process of electron transfer."

## Misconception 202: Bonding between carbon and iron in steel is electrostatic rather than covalent.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Bonding in alloys
**Correct concept:** Bonding in steel/alloys cannot be explained simply as electrostatic between carbon and iron; the relevant structure and bonding must be identified correctly for the alloy system.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should examine the alloy structure and avoid assuming any interaction between components is ionic/electrostatic without evidence of charge separation.
**Concept category:** alloys / bonding type
**Prevalence:** Observed in Steve; AC 17.
**Population:** Tertiary student
**Source:** Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students (Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students/Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students.md)
**Evidence:** "Steve seemed confused about the nature of bonding in the alloy steel (AC 17, Table II), viewed the bonding between the non-metal carbon and iron as electrostatic in nature rather than covalent."

## Misconception 203: Ion shape can be influenced by macroscopic pressure.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Structural effects on ions
**Correct concept:** Ion size and shape are determined by electronic structure and bonding, not by external pressure in the way suggested.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should relate ionic shape to electron configuration and crystal structure rather than to macroscopic pressure conditions.
**Concept category:** ion shape
**Prevalence:** Observed in Rose; AC 18.
**Population:** Postgraduate student
**Source:** Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students (Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students/Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students.md)
**Evidence:** "Rose, a postgraduate, seemed to believe that the shape of ions could be influenced by macroscopic factors such as pressure"

## Misconception 204: Intermolecular forces are affected by gravity.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Intermolecular forces
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces arise from electrostatic interactions, not gravity.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain intermolecular attraction using molecular polarity and dispersion-related electrostatics rather than macroscopic gravity.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces
**Prevalence:** Observed in Neil; AC 19.
**Population:** Tertiary student
**Source:** Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students (Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students/Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students.md)
**Evidence:** "Neil possessed a novel view of intermolecular forces, believing that they were affected by gravity"

## Misconception 205: Glass is a crystalline ionic substance (or silica structure with SiO4 units treated as a crystal lattice).

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Crystalline structure of glass
**Correct concept:** Glass is amorphous, not a crystalline ionic substance.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish an amorphous network solid like glass from a crystalline ionic solid by considering long-range order and composition.
**Concept category:** solid structure
**Prevalence:** Observed in Steve.
**Population:** Tertiary student
**Source:** Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students (Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students/Alternative Conceptions of Chemical Bonding Held by Upper Secondary and Tertiary Students.md)
**Evidence:** "Steve seemed to believe glass was a crystalline substance stating 'glass is basically a silica structure, that's silicon covalently bonded in a silica structure with SiO4 units'."

## Misconception 206: Students believe mass can change during a chemical reaction, or that the number of molecules must stay the same, or that atoms and moles are simply proportional in a way that ignores conservation of atoms.

**Topic:** Conservation of matter in chemical reactions / Mass, atoms, and molecules before and after reaction
**Correct concept:** In a chemical reaction, total mass is conserved in a closed system and the number of atoms of each element is conserved, although the number of molecules may change as new substances form.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify that chemical reactions rearrange atoms into new molecules without creating or destroying atoms. Therefore, the mass of the system and the number of each type of atom stay the same, while molecule count may change depending on the reaction.
**Concept category:** conservation of matter
**Prevalence:** Q1 pre-survey: 20% NPU; 30% PSM; 50% PSU. Also, 10% of all students selected the correct option but lacked a sound explanation.
**Population:** CHEM 115/115L students, Fall 2018 (N=10 pre-survey; N=8 post-survey)
**Source:** College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and the Impact on Research Instrumentation (College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and/College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and.md)
**Evidence:** "Mass can change after chemical reaction"

## Misconception 207: Students think the bubbles in boiling water are air, oxygen gas, hydrogen gas, oxygen, or heat instead of water vapor.

**Topic:** Bubbles in boiling water / Identity of gas in boiling water
**Correct concept:** The bubbles are water vapor (steam), i.e. gaseous water produced during boiling.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that heating liquid water gives water molecules enough energy to enter the gas phase; the bubbles are composed of water molecules, not air or decomposition products.
**Concept category:** phase change
**Prevalence:** The paper cites prior studies and also reports student responses on Q2; in the present study, Q2 had 50% PSU and 50% PSM in the pre-survey.
**Population:** Prior study cited in the paper: 17-year-old chemistry students; present study: CHEM 115/115L students
**Source:** College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and the Impact on Research Instrumentation (College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and/College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and.md)
**Evidence:** "25% of their sample of 17-year-old chemistry students thought that the bubbles in boiling water were made of air"

## Misconception 208: Students identify only the product formula or give an incorrect atom ratio, failing to determine how many S and O atoms are present after the reaction. Some wrote formulas such as '2SO3' without evaluating the reactant ratio, or treated the product as just a rearrangement of listed atoms without checking stoichiometric limits.

**Topic:** Chemical reaction stoichiometry and particle count / Reactants/products in the sulfur-oxygen reaction
**Correct concept:** Students should use the balanced equation 2S + 3O2 → 2SO3 to determine the product and the number of leftover atoms/molecules from the limiting reactant relationship.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should count atoms on both sides of the balanced equation, apply the 2:3 stoichiometric ratio, and determine how many SO3 units can form and what remains unreacted.
**Concept category:** stoichiometry / particle counting
**Prevalence:** Q3 pre-survey: 20% NPU; 50% PSM; 30% PSU. The most frequently selected answer was the wrong option for 50% of students.
**Population:** CHEM 115/115L students, Fall 2018
**Source:** College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and the Impact on Research Instrumentation (College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and/College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and.md)
**Evidence:** "It represents 2SO3"

## Misconception 209: Students think water 'splits up and combine,' turns into oxygen and hydrogen, or that evaporation means water disappears rather than remaining as water molecules in the gas phase.

**Topic:** Evaporation of water / Molecular-level description of phase change
**Correct concept:** Evaporation is a physical change in which water molecules remain H2O but move farther apart and disperse into the gas phase.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain that intermolecular attractions are overcome, molecules separate, and the molecules remain intact as H2O in the vapor phase.
**Concept category:** phase change / particle model
**Prevalence:** Q4 pre-survey: 40% PSM and 60% PSU; Q6 pre-survey also showed 40% PSM. Interview responses documented this misconception explicitly.
**Population:** CHEM 115/115L students, Fall 2018
**Source:** College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and the Impact on Research Instrumentation (College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and/College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and.md)
**Evidence:** "water split up and combine"

## Misconception 210: Students believe drying water no longer exists, goes into the plate, or changes into oxygen and hydrogen, rather than evaporating as water molecules into the air.

**Topic:** Conservation of matter during evaporation / What happens to water on a drying plate
**Correct concept:** When water dries, it evaporates into the air as water molecules; the substance remains matter and does not vanish or decompose.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize evaporation as a physical change where liquid water molecules escape into the gas phase, preserving matter.
**Concept category:** phase change / conservation of matter
**Prevalence:** Q6 pre-survey: 10% NPU; 40% PSM; 60% PSU. The paper also notes this as a 'lack of understanding of evaporation.'
**Population:** CHEM 115/115L students, Fall 2018
**Source:** College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and the Impact on Research Instrumentation (College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and/College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and.md)
**Evidence:** "Water does not exist anymore"

## Misconception 211: Students believe there is matter, air, water vapor, atmosphere, or even a bond between oxygen molecules in the space between gas particles, rather than empty space.

**Topic:** Particle spacing in gases / What exists between molecules in a gas
**Correct concept:** Between gas molecules there is mostly empty space; gas particles are separated and do not have matter filling all the space between them.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use the particulate model to infer that gas particles are far apart with essentially nothing between them except empty space.
**Concept category:** particle spacing / gas particles
**Prevalence:** Q7 pre-survey: 10% NPU; 20% PSM; 70% PSU. The paper states that some students thought there was something between the molecules.
**Population:** CHEM 115/115L students, Fall 2018
**Source:** College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and the Impact on Research Instrumentation (College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and/College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and.md)
**Evidence:** "There is always a matter between oxygen molecules"

## Misconception 212: Students think that decreasing pressure makes particles grow larger, that increasing pressure changes particle size, or that pressure changes the number of particles rather than their spacing and motion.

**Topic:** Gas compression and pressure / Effect of pressure on particle size and motion
**Correct concept:** Changing pressure affects the spacing and frequency of collisions among gas particles, not the size of the particles themselves.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that gas particles are essentially incompressible at this level; reduced pressure lets them spread out more, while increased pressure packs them closer together.
**Concept category:** gas behavior / pressure
**Prevalence:** Q8 pre-survey: 60% PSM and 40% PSU. The paper also notes that some students thought pressure affected molecule size.
**Population:** CHEM 115/115L students, Fall 2018
**Source:** College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and the Impact on Research Instrumentation (College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and/College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and.md)
**Evidence:** "Pressure would force the molecules to grow larger"

## Misconception 213: Students think reduced pressure means particles stop moving, do not spread out, or grow larger instead of becoming more dispersed.

**Topic:** Gas behavior under reduced pressure / Spatial distribution of gas particles
**Correct concept:** At lower pressure, gas particles occupy a larger volume and are farther apart while remaining the same particles.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should infer from gas laws and the particle model that lowering pressure increases spacing between particles, not particle size.
**Concept category:** gas behavior / pressure
**Prevalence:** Q8 pre-survey and post-survey responses included PSM; the paper discusses pressure-related misconceptions in this item.
**Population:** CHEM 115/115L students, Fall 2018
**Source:** College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and the Impact on Research Instrumentation (College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and/College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and.md)
**Evidence:** "The particles wont be moving as fast if pressure is reduced"

## Misconception 214: Students explain phase change only at the macroscopic level, for example saying 'all water is evaporated' without referring to particles, or treating evaporation as disappearance of water rather than molecular motion and separation.

**Topic:** State of matter in liquids and solids / Microscopic interpretation of evaporation and boiling
**Correct concept:** A correct explanation of phase change must describe molecular behavior: molecules remain intact and move into the gas phase with increased spacing and motion.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect the visible disappearance of liquid to particle-level motion and intermolecular forces, not to disappearance of matter.
**Concept category:** phase change / macroscopic vs microscopic reasoning
**Prevalence:** Discussed in the Q4 interview analysis as an example of a student lacking PNM-level explanation.
**Population:** CHEM 115/115L students, Fall 2018
**Source:** College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and the Impact on Research Instrumentation (College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and/College Students Conceptions of Particulate Nature of Matter and.md)
**Evidence:** "All water is evaporated"

## Misconception 215: A hydrogen bond is a covalent bond, or a bond formed by hydrogen with another atom as if it were an intramolecular covalent bond.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Definition and structure
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is an intermolecular attraction between a hydrogen atom bonded to a highly electronegative atom and a nearby electronegative atom with a lone pair; it is not itself a covalent bond.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the donor atom bonded to H, recognize the partial positive charge on H and partial negative charge/lone pair on the acceptor, and explain the attraction between molecules rather than drawing a new covalent bond.
**Concept category:** bond type / intermolecular vs covalent
**Prevalence:** 31 students in the pretest; 8 students in the posttest (wrong answers to Q1A)
**Population:** 52 grade 12 Turkish students
**Source:** Scientific Argumentation in Teaching Hydrogen Bonding (EJ1314507/EJ1314507.md)
**Evidence:** "the majority of the students who gave the wrong answer in the pretest and all the students who gave the wrong answer in the posttest depicted the hydrogen bond as a covalent bond in their drawing."

## Misconception 216: Hydrogen bonding occurs only when hydrogen is bonded to fluorine, oxygen, or nitrogen, and only these atoms can participate.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Allowed atoms in hydrogen bonding
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is not restricted to only F, O, or N; it can occur with other electronegative atoms or groups, and the key condition is a hydrogen attached to an electronegative atom interacting with a nearby acceptor.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should examine the polarity of the bond and the availability of lone pairs/partial charges, rather than memorizing only the NOF list.
**Concept category:** definition / NOF rule
**Prevalence:** 18 students in the pretests; 15 students in the posttests used surface-level symbolic representations without moving beyond this idea
**Population:** 52 grade 12 Turkish students
**Source:** Scientific Argumentation in Teaching Hydrogen Bonding (EJ1314507/EJ1314507.md)
**Evidence:** "they defined the bond over water, hydrogen fluoride, and ammonia molecules and defined the bond as weak interactions occurring intermolecule between fluoride, oxygen, and nitrogen atoms and hydrogen atom."

## Misconception 217: Students cannot identify which atom is the hydrogen-bond donor or acceptor and instead treat hydrogen bonding as merely an attraction between hydrogen and any electronegative atom.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Bond donor/acceptor identification
**Correct concept:** The hydrogen-bond donor is the X–H group where X is electronegative, and the acceptor is a nearby electronegative atom with a lone pair; the acceptor is not just any atom in the molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should determine where hydrogen is covalently bonded, then locate the nearby lone-pair-bearing electronegative atom that attracts the δ+ hydrogen.
**Concept category:** electron transfer / donor-acceptor roles
**Prevalence:** Nearly all students in the pretests and nearly half in the posttests
**Population:** 52 grade 12 Turkish students
**Source:** Scientific Argumentation in Teaching Hydrogen Bonding (EJ1314507/EJ1314507.md)
**Evidence:** "they were unable to identify the hydrogen donor or acceptor atom."

## Misconception 218: Hydrogen bonding between two water molecules is formed by a bond between the hydrogen atoms of the two water molecules.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Water interactions
**Correct concept:** In water, hydrogen bonding is the attraction between the partially positive hydrogen of one molecule and the partially negative oxygen of another molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should mark oxygen as the acceptor and hydrogen as the donor across two molecules, using partial charges and lone pairs.
**Concept category:** intermolecular attractions
**Prevalence:** 4 students in the pretests
**Population:** 52 grade 12 Turkish students
**Source:** Scientific Argumentation in Teaching Hydrogen Bonding (EJ1314507/EJ1314507.md)
**Evidence:** "four students showed this bond between the hydrogen of two water molecules in the pretests."

## Misconception 219: Hydrogen bonding in amines, ethers, aldehydes, or similar organic molecules is not recognized, or students say no hydrogen bond exists when it actually can.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Organic molecules with heteroatoms
**Correct concept:** Molecules such as amines, ethers, alcohols, carboxylic acids, and some aromatic hydroxy compounds can participate in hydrogen bonding when the structural requirements are met.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should inspect the functional group, identify electronegative atoms and lone pairs, and determine whether suitable donor and acceptor sites are present.
**Concept category:** functional groups / molecular structure
**Prevalence:** 15 students identified hydrogen bonding between amine groups; 11 correctly identified with water; common mistake noted in pretests
**Population:** 52 grade 12 Turkish students
**Source:** Scientific Argumentation in Teaching Hydrogen Bonding (EJ1314507/EJ1314507.md)
**Evidence:** "The common mistake about these molecules in the pretests was that the hydrogen bond was established between hydrogens in an amine molecule and oxygen in a water molecule."

## Misconception 220: Ether molecules cannot form hydrogen bonds with one another or with water because students focus only on the absence of an O–H bond.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Ether molecules
**Correct concept:** Although ethers cannot donate hydrogen bonds, their oxygen lone pairs allow them to accept hydrogen bonds from water; they do not hydrogen-bond to each other as donors, but they can interact with water.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish hydrogen-bond donor from acceptor and recognize the acceptor role of the ether oxygen.
**Concept category:** functional groups / lone pairs
**Prevalence:** 19 students (ether-ether); 8 students (ether-water) in pretests
**Population:** 52 grade 12 Turkish students
**Source:** Scientific Argumentation in Teaching Hydrogen Bonding (EJ1314507/EJ1314507.md)
**Evidence:** "In the pretests, 19 students stated that no hydrogen bond was established between ether molecules, and eight students asserted that no hydrogen bond was established between ether and water molecules."

## Misconception 221: Hydrogen bonding in carboxylic acids is limited to the O–H group only, and the hydrogen of the O–H group in the acid hydrogen-bonds to water oxygen as the only relevant interaction.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Ethanoic acid / carboxylic acids
**Correct concept:** Carboxylic acids can hydrogen-bond through both donor and acceptor sites; the carbonyl oxygen and hydroxyl hydrogen both matter for intermolecular interactions and interactions with water.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should examine both oxygens and the hydroxyl hydrogen, then use partial charges and lone pairs to decide donor and acceptor roles.
**Concept category:** functional groups / carboxylic acids
**Prevalence:** Described as a common error; 19 students stated an acid-water interaction involving the O–H hydrogen and water oxygen
**Population:** 52 grade 12 Turkish students
**Source:** Scientific Argumentation in Teaching Hydrogen Bonding (EJ1314507/EJ1314507.md)
**Evidence:** "The number of students who showed the hydrogen bond between ethanolic acid within only the OH group was large."

## Misconception 222: Students think a hydrogen atom attached to carbon in an organic molecule can form a hydrogen bond with oxygen in another molecule.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Hydrogen attached to carbon
**Correct concept:** A hydrogen bond generally requires hydrogen bonded to a sufficiently electronegative atom such as N, O, or F; C–H is not usually considered an effective hydrogen-bond donor in this context.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should check the electronegativity of the atom bonded to H and only treat H as hydrogen-bond donor when the X–H bond is sufficiently polar.
**Concept category:** bond polarity / donor site identification
**Prevalence:** Not quantified; reported as another misconception in Question 2
**Population:** 52 grade 12 Turkish students
**Source:** Scientific Argumentation in Teaching Hydrogen Bonding (EJ1314507/EJ1314507.md)
**Evidence:** "Another misconception some students had was the establishment of a hydrogen bond between the hydrogen (H) atom attached to carbon (C) in organic molecules and oxygen in other molecules."

## Misconception 223: Boiling point differences can be explained only by saying a substance has a stronger hydrogen bond, without connecting this to molecular structure or other factors.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and physical properties / Boiling point reasoning
**Correct concept:** Boiling point differences should be explained by comparing molecular structure, polarity, hydrogen-bonding ability, and intermolecular force strength in relation to the actual molecules involved.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify which molecules can hydrogen-bond, compare the number/strength of interactions, and connect that to energy needed for vaporization.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces / explanation quality
**Prevalence:** Shown as a pattern in Question 2 responses; many students gave only this warrant
**Population:** 52 grade 12 Turkish students
**Source:** Scientific Argumentation in Teaching Hydrogen Bonding (EJ1314507/EJ1314507.md)
**Evidence:** "The fact that students presented their warrants for their statements in a, b, c, and d as 'having stronger hydrogen bond' indicated that they understood the establishment of hydrogen bonds between molecules."

## Misconception 224: Differences in boiling point or solubility are explained by saying a substance is denser, or that density alone determines molecular attractions.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and physical properties / Density as explanation
**Correct concept:** Density is not the fundamental cause of hydrogen bonding or boiling-point differences; the relevant factors are polarity, molecular size, electronegativity, and intermolecular forces.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should relate the property to intermolecular interactions and molecular structure rather than to density alone.
**Concept category:** macroscopic property reasoning
**Prevalence:** 13 students in the pretests used density as a warrant; 4 elaborated
**Population:** 52 grade 12 Turkish students
**Source:** Scientific Argumentation in Teaching Hydrogen Bonding (EJ1314507/EJ1314507.md)
**Evidence:** "In the pretests, 13 students presented their warrants, mentioning that 'its density is greater' for a, b, and c; however, only four students detailed it..."

## Misconception 225: Students explain properties by memorized order patterns such as 'acid > alcohol > aldehyde' rather than by causal molecular reasoning.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and physical properties / Synthetic ordering instead of causal explanation
**Correct concept:** Property comparisons should be justified by underlying molecular structure and intermolecular forces, not by memorized ranking strings.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect each compound’s structure to its hydrogen-bonding ability and polarity, then infer the property trend causally.
**Concept category:** rote learning / ranking
**Prevalence:** Not quantified; reported in the pretests
**Population:** 52 grade 12 Turkish students
**Source:** Scientific Argumentation in Teaching Hydrogen Bonding (EJ1314507/EJ1314507.md)
**Evidence:** "irrelevant answers, such as efficient order, 'acid *>* alcohol > aldehyde' in d, e, and f, were encountered."

## Misconception 226: Students assume that if a compound is polar, or if it contains no hydrogen bond, then it must not interact with water or must be more volatile for that reason alone.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and physical properties / Nonpolar compounds and water solubility
**Correct concept:** Water solubility and volatility depend on the actual intermolecular interactions available, including hydrogen bonding, dipole interactions, and functional groups; polarity alone is not enough.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify possible donor/acceptor sites and compare the strength and number of interactions with water.
**Concept category:** properties / solubility and volatility
**Prevalence:** 19 students stated no hydrogen bond for ether molecules; 8 said no hydrogen bond with water
**Population:** 52 grade 12 Turkish students
**Source:** Scientific Argumentation in Teaching Hydrogen Bonding (EJ1314507/EJ1314507.md)
**Evidence:** "The majority of these students stated that it was a polar molecule and that it was more volatile than alcohol as it contained no hydrogen bond."

## Misconception 227: HF is a weak acid because of its strong hydrogen bonding or because boiling point and acidity are directly determined by hydrogen bonding strength alone.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and physical properties / Hydrogen fluoride acidity
**Correct concept:** HF is weak because its dissociation in water is incomplete; hydrogen bonding can contribute to association, but acidity depends on the extent of proton release in water.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish intermolecular hydrogen bonding from acid dissociation and use the extent of ionization in water to explain acidity.
**Concept category:** acidity / intermolecular forces
**Prevalence:** Presented as Student 10's pretest reasoning
**Population:** 52 grade 12 Turkish students
**Source:** Scientific Argumentation in Teaching Hydrogen Bonding (EJ1314507/EJ1314507.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bonds are stronger than Van der Waals bonds... This special occasion derives from the greater electronegativity compared with that of fluorine"

## Misconception 228: Students can explain London dispersion forces only by naming the attraction from nonpermanent dipoles, without explaining how those dipoles arise.

**Topic:** intermolecular forces / London dispersion forces
**Correct concept:** The origin of London dispersion forces must be explained mechanistically in terms of electron movement creating temporary, nonpermanent dipoles.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect fluctuating electron clouds to temporary charge separation, then show how these induced dipoles lead to attraction between particles.
**Concept category:** electron transfer / electron cloud mechanism
**Population:** undergraduate students
**Source:** Enhance explanations of intermolecular forces (Enhance explanations of intermolecular forces _ News _ RSC Education/Enhance explanations of intermolecular forces _ News _ RSC Education.md)
**Evidence:** they found students could often explain the cause of the attraction (nonpermanent dipoles) but rarely offered the mechanism by which these arise.

## Misconception 229: Students believe benzene contains fixed localized double bonds between particular carbon atoms rather than a delocalized bonding system.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Benzene structure and resonance
**Correct concept:** In benzene, the pi electrons are delocalized around the ring and no single Lewis structure with fixed double bonds fully represents the molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that benzene is better represented by resonance or delocalized bonding models because all C–C bonds are equivalent and the bonding cannot be captured by one localized double-bond pattern.
**Concept category:** resonance / delocalization / surface features
**Prevalence:** Secondary school learners; described as a common view, but no count given for this misconception.
**Population:** Secondary school learners
**Source:** Exploring Tertiary Students' Understanding of Covalent Bonding (Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding/Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding.md)
**Evidence:** “apparently they believed that there were double bonds in benzene.”

## Misconception 230: Students think covalent bonding means vague sharing of 'bits' of atoms or undefined material rather than sharing electrons or electron density between nuclei.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Electron sharing in covalent bonds
**Correct concept:** Covalent bonding involves sharing electron density, typically a shared pair of electrons, between atoms.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify that the bond is explained by electrons being shared between nuclei, not by atoms sharing unspecified pieces of themselves.
**Concept category:** electron sharing / bond representation
**Prevalence:** Observed in at least one secondary school learner; most learners could not clearly articulate what covalent meant.
**Population:** Secondary school learners
**Source:** Exploring Tertiary Students' Understanding of Covalent Bonding (Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding/Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding.md)
**Evidence:** “the bonding in molecular iodine as 'strong I think' ... she described the bond as consisting of the overlap of 'bits of iodines'.”

## Misconception 231: Students think the shared electrons are simply 'one from each atom' used to complete shells, without clearly distinguishing between the electron pair as the bond and shell completion as the consequence.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Octet rule and electron sharing
**Correct concept:** A covalent bond is a shared electron pair; full-shell stability is the result of sharing, not a separate material object being moved around.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain that each atom contributes electrons to a shared pair and that this shared electron density helps achieve a stable valence shell.
**Concept category:** octet rule / electron sharing
**Prevalence:** Appeared in the discussion of several learners, especially secondary school learners and undergraduates; exact count not given.
**Population:** Secondary school learners and undergraduates
**Source:** Exploring Tertiary Students' Understanding of Covalent Bonding (Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding/Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding.md)
**Evidence:** “They share two electrons because each of them needs to gain one to get a full shell.”
