## Misconception 232: Students believe the number of valence electrons and the number of covalent bonds are the same thing.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Bond count vs valence electrons
**Correct concept:** Valence electrons are available for bonding, but the number of bonds a species forms is not identical to the number of valence electrons.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish between the count of valence electrons and the bonding pattern; electrons may be paired, lone pairs, or involved in single/double bonds depending on structure.
**Concept category:** valence electrons / bonding count
**Population:** Reported in the literature review as a documented misconception among learners
**Source:** Exploring Tertiary Students' Understanding of Covalent Bonding (Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding/Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding.md)
**Evidence:** “Some learners believe that the number of valence electrons and number of covalent bonds were one and the same”

## Misconception 233: Students think non-polar molecules are formed simply between atoms of similar electronegativity, and that molecular polarity is determined by valence electron count, lone pairs, or ionic charge rather than electronegativity difference and geometry.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Polar bonds and polarity
**Correct concept:** Molecular polarity depends on bond polarity from electronegativity differences and the vector sum of bond dipoles, together with molecular geometry.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should first assess whether each bond is polar from electronegativity differences, then consider the 3D arrangement of the bonds to determine whether dipoles cancel.
**Concept category:** electronegativity / polarity
**Population:** Students reported in prior studies cited by the paper
**Source:** Exploring Tertiary Students' Understanding of Covalent Bonding (Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding/Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding.md)
**Evidence:** “the number of valence electrons, the presence of lone pairs of electrons, or ionic charge determine molecular polarity”

## Misconception 234: Students think attraction between oppositely charged species causes neutralisation rather than bond formation.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Electrostatic nature of bonding
**Correct concept:** Attraction between opposite charges can lead to bond formation; neutralisation is not the correct description of covalent bond formation.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that electrostatic attraction between nuclei and shared electrons stabilizes the bond, rather than assuming charges cancel to 'neutral'.
**Concept category:** electrostatic attraction / bond formation
**Population:** Students reported in prior studies cited by the paper
**Source:** Exploring Tertiary Students' Understanding of Covalent Bonding (Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding/Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding.md)
**Evidence:** “attraction between two oppositely charged species was thought to result in neutralisation rather than bond formation”

## Misconception 235: Students think covalent bond formation involves transfer of electrons.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Electron transfer vs sharing
**Correct concept:** Covalent bonding involves sharing electrons; electron transfer is characteristic of ionic bonding.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish covalent from ionic bonding by recognizing that covalent bonds are formed by shared electron pairs, not by complete electron transfer.
**Concept category:** sharing vs transfer
**Population:** Students reported in prior studies cited by the paper
**Source:** Exploring Tertiary Students' Understanding of Covalent Bonding (Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding/Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding.md)
**Evidence:** “believing that covalent bond formation involves the transfer of electrons”

## Misconception 236: Students use ball-and-stick models to conclude that the stick is a real individual covalent bond and that ionic substances are molecular in nature.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Covalent vs ionic substances and models
**Correct concept:** Ball-and-stick models are representations; ionic solids are not molecular substances, and sticks are not physical bond objects.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should interpret models as symbolic representations, not as literal pictures of hidden objects, and should distinguish molecular covalent substances from ionic lattices.
**Concept category:** models / surface features
**Population:** Students reported in prior studies cited by the paper
**Source:** Exploring Tertiary Students' Understanding of Covalent Bonding (Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding/Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding.md)
**Evidence:** “believing that the stick is an individual chemical covalent bond and that ionic substances are molecular in nature”

## Misconception 237: Students believe a chemical bond is a physical entity that is broken to release energy and made by requiring energy input.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Bond as physical entity and energetics
**Correct concept:** Bond breaking requires energy input and bond formation releases energy; a bond is an interaction, not a physical object.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use energy conservation to reason that making a bond lowers potential energy and releases energy, while breaking a bond raises potential energy and needs energy input.
**Concept category:** bond energetics / reification
**Population:** Students reported in prior studies cited by the paper
**Source:** Exploring Tertiary Students' Understanding of Covalent Bonding (Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding/Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding.md)
**Evidence:** “learners believed that bond breaking releases energy and bond making involves energy input.”

## Misconception 238: Students believe intermolecular bonding is stronger than intramolecular bonding.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Relative strength of intermolecular and intramolecular forces
**Correct concept:** Intramolecular covalent bonds are generally much stronger than intermolecular forces.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the energy required to break bonds within molecules versus forces between molecules and recognize that intermolecular forces are weaker.
**Concept category:** intermolecular vs intramolecular
**Population:** Students reported in prior studies cited by the paper
**Source:** Exploring Tertiary Students' Understanding of Covalent Bonding (Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding/Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding.md)
**Evidence:** “learners believe intermolecular bonding is stronger than intramolecular bonding”

## Misconception 239: Students believe intermolecular forces are absent in polar molecular substances such as water.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Presence of intermolecular forces in polar substances
**Correct concept:** Polar molecules such as water do have intermolecular forces, including hydrogen bonding and dipole-dipole interactions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that polarity creates intermolecular attractions between molecules, especially when H is bonded to highly electronegative atoms.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces / polarity
**Population:** Students reported in prior studies cited by the paper
**Source:** Exploring Tertiary Students' Understanding of Covalent Bonding (Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding/Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding.md)
**Evidence:** “believe it is absent in polar molecular substances such as water”

## Misconception 240: Students know carbon compounds are tetrahedral only as a rote label but cannot explain why; some treat tetrahedrality as just the default arrangement carbon 'always' has.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Molecular shape and tetrahedral geometry
**Correct concept:** Tetrahedral geometry arises from arrangement of electron pairs and hybridized orbitals that minimize repulsion in sp3 environments.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect molecular shape to electron-pair repulsion and orbital arrangement, not merely memorize 'tetrahedral'.
**Concept category:** molecular geometry / hybridisation
**Prevalence:** Observed in some undergraduate and graduate learners; exact count not given.
**Population:** Graduate learners
**Source:** Exploring Tertiary Students' Understanding of Covalent Bonding (Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding/Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding.md)
**Evidence:** “I know carbon is tetrahederal because carbon is always tetrahedral.”

## Misconception 241: Students think atomic orbitals are 'a myth' or no longer relevant, and use molecular orbital ideas without clearly distinguishing atomic from molecular orbitals.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Molecular orbital theory
**Correct concept:** Atomic orbitals are real conceptual entities used to build molecular orbitals; molecular orbitals extend over the whole molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should understand that molecular orbitals are formed from combinations of atomic orbitals, and that the two levels of description are distinct but related.
**Concept category:** orbital models / molecular orbital theory
**Prevalence:** Observed in one undergraduate learner's explanation.
**Population:** Undergraduate learner
**Source:** Exploring Tertiary Students' Understanding of Covalent Bonding (Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding/Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding.md)
**Evidence:** “atomic orbitals tend to be almost a myth.”

## Misconception 242: Students think depiction A or C means there is a fixed double bond between specific carbon atoms, rather than a delocalized aromatic structure.

**Topic:** Benzene / Alternative depiction and localized bonds
**Correct concept:** Benzene has delocalized pi electrons and equivalent C–C bonds; depictions with alternating single and double bonds are limited resonance representations.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should interpret resonance structures as alternative representations of a delocalized system, not as evidence for localized double bonds at fixed positions.
**Concept category:** resonance / aromaticity
**Prevalence:** Seven graduate learners disliked depictions A and C because they implied this misconception.
**Population:** Graduate learners
**Source:** Exploring Tertiary Students' Understanding of Covalent Bonding (Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding/Exploring Tertiary Students  Understanding of Covalent Bonding.md)
**Evidence:** “A and C you get the misconception that there's a double bond between two particular carbon atoms.”

## Misconception 243: Any attraction between oppositely charged species is an ionic bond, so Na+ interacting with water is treated as ionic bonding rather than ion-dipole attraction.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Ion-dipole interaction
**Correct concept:** The attraction between an ion and the partial charges on a polar molecule is an ion-dipole interaction, not an ionic bond.
**Expected reasoning:** Identify that water is a polar molecule with a partial negative charge on oxygen; the ion interacts electrostatically with that partial charge, so the correct intermolecular force is ion-dipole rather than bond formation between ions.
**Concept category:** ion-dipole interaction
**Prevalence:** Question 1: 21 (26%) had misconceptions; 11 (13%) answered correctly with correct explanation.
**Population:** 82 first-year chemistry major teacher trainees at the University of Education, Winneba
**Source:** Teacher Trainees' Alternative Conceptions about Intermolecular Forces (IJISRT21DECnew/IJISRT21DECnew.md)
**Evidence:** "any interaction between opposite charged species is ionic bond"

## Misconception 244: Na+ binds to water by forming an ionic bond, or water induces a negative charge in sodium and then bonds to it.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Ion-dipole interaction
**Correct concept:** Na+ is attracted to the partial negative oxygen end of water through ion-dipole attraction.
**Expected reasoning:** Reason from molecular polarity: the ion interacts with the negative end of a polar molecule without creating a new ionic bond or inducing charge as the explanation for the force.
**Concept category:** ion-dipole interaction
**Prevalence:** Question 1: part of the 21 (26%) with misconceptions.
**Population:** 82 first-year chemistry major teacher trainees at the University of Education, Winneba
**Source:** Teacher Trainees' Alternative Conceptions about Intermolecular Forces (IJISRT21DECnew/IJISRT21DECnew.md)
**Evidence:** "the sodium ion will form ionic bond with the negative charge in water molecules and stay together"

## Misconception 245: London dispersion forces require a charge to be present to create the temporary instantaneous dipole.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / London dispersion forces
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces arise from temporary fluctuations in electron distribution, not from pre-existing charges.
**Expected reasoning:** Recognize that electrons are constantly moving; brief uneven electron distribution creates a temporary dipole that induces attraction between molecules.
**Concept category:** London dispersion forces
**Prevalence:** Question 2: 30 (37%) had misconceptions; 20 (24%) showed correct understanding.
**Population:** 82 first-year chemistry major teacher trainees at the University of Education, Winneba
**Source:** Teacher Trainees' Alternative Conceptions about Intermolecular Forces (IJISRT21DECnew/IJISRT21DECnew.md)
**Evidence:** "a charge should be present to bring about the temporary instantaneous dipole"

## Misconception 246: London dispersion forces occur within a single molecule rather than between molecules.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / London dispersion forces
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces are intermolecular attractions between separate molecules or particles.
**Expected reasoning:** Distinguish intramolecular bonding from intermolecular attraction; LDFs act between neighboring molecules due to instantaneous/induced dipoles.
**Concept category:** London dispersion forces
**Prevalence:** Question 2: part of the 30 (37%) with misconceptions.
**Population:** 82 first-year chemistry major teacher trainees at the University of Education, Winneba
**Source:** Teacher Trainees' Alternative Conceptions about Intermolecular Forces (IJISRT21DECnew/IJISRT21DECnew.md)
**Evidence:** "the students perceived LDFs to occur within a single molecule rather than between molecules"

## Misconception 247: The major attractive force between I2 molecules is a covalent-ionic interaction.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / London dispersion forces in iodine
**Correct concept:** The major attractive force between nonpolar I2 molecules is London dispersion force.
**Expected reasoning:** Since I2 molecules are nonpolar, the intermolecular attraction must be dispersion forces caused by temporary dipoles, not covalent or ionic interactions.
**Concept category:** London dispersion forces
**Prevalence:** Question 3: 28 (33%) had misconceptions; 33 (40%) showed correct understanding.
**Population:** 82 first-year chemistry major teacher trainees at the University of Education, Winneba
**Source:** Teacher Trainees' Alternative Conceptions about Intermolecular Forces (IJISRT21DECnew/IJISRT21DECnew.md)
**Evidence:** "covalent–ionic interaction was the major attractive force that existed in different I2 molecules"

## Misconception 248: Any molecule containing O and H forms hydrogen bonding, regardless of whether H is directly bonded to O in the molecule.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Hydrogen bonding identification
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding requires hydrogen to be directly bonded to an electronegative atom such as O, N, or F, and then interact with a lone pair on another electronegative atom.
**Expected reasoning:** Check the structural arrangement: H must be covalently attached to O/N/F; simply having O and H somewhere in the formula is not sufficient.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Prevalence:** Question 4: 43 (53%) had misconceptions; 29 (35%) showed good understanding.
**Population:** 82 first-year chemistry major teacher trainees at the University of Education, Winneba
**Source:** Teacher Trainees' Alternative Conceptions about Intermolecular Forces (IJISRT21DECnew/IJISRT21DECnew.md)
**Evidence:** "hydrogen bonding in the given species occurred between hydrogen and oxygen atoms"

## Misconception 249: Hydrogen bonding only occurs between O and H, not between N-H and F-H.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Hydrogen bonding identification
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding can occur when H is directly bonded to O, N, or F.
**Expected reasoning:** Identify all three electronegative atoms that support hydrogen bonding; if hydrogen is attached to N or F, hydrogen bonding is also possible.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Prevalence:** Listed among the major findings; reported in Question 4 misconceptions (43 students, 53%).
**Population:** 82 first-year chemistry major teacher trainees at the University of Education, Winneba
**Source:** Teacher Trainees' Alternative Conceptions about Intermolecular Forces (IJISRT21DECnew/IJISRT21DECnew.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bonding only occurs between 'O' and 'H' but not between N-H and F-H."

## Misconception 250: A molecule's atomic number predicts the strength of London dispersion force and therefore its boiling point.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Hydrogen bonding and boiling point
**Correct concept:** London dispersion force generally increases with molecular size/molar mass and number of electrons, not simply atomic number.
**Expected reasoning:** Compare the molecules by electron count and polarizability; larger, more electron-rich nonpolar molecules have stronger dispersion forces and higher boiling points.
**Concept category:** London dispersion forces
**Prevalence:** Question 5: 30 (37%) demonstrated misconceptions; 28 (34%) showed correct understanding.
**Population:** 82 first-year chemistry major teacher trainees at the University of Education, Winneba
**Source:** Teacher Trainees' Alternative Conceptions about Intermolecular Forces (IJISRT21DECnew/IJISRT21DECnew.md)
**Evidence:** "Atomic number predicts the strength of London dispersion force."

## Misconception 251: Students do not distinguish among the three intermolecular force types and may treat them as interchangeable or as simple memorized labels rather than forces with different mechanisms and criteria for occurrence.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / London dispersion, dipole-dipole, and hydrogen bonding
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces, dipole-dipole interactions, and hydrogen bonding are distinct intermolecular forces with different causes and different conditions for when they occur.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the kind of particles present, determine whether bonds are polar, whether the molecule is polar, and then decide which intermolecular forces are possible based on those features before ranking their relative strengths.
**Concept category:** intermolecular force types
**Source:** Teaching Ideas and Suggestions (Intermolecular Forces - Teacher Notes/Intermolecular Forces - Teacher Notes.md)
**Evidence:** The emphasis is on three such forces - London dispersion forces, dipoledipole interactions, and hydrogen bonding.

## Misconception 252: Students may think that identifying intermolecular forces can be done without first determining whether bonds are polar and whether the molecule is polar or nonpolar.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Bond polarity and molecular polarity
**Correct concept:** To identify intermolecular forces, students must first analyze Lewis structures and electronegativity to determine bond polarity and molecular polarity.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use a Lewis structure and electronegativity differences to decide if polar bonds are present, then use the molecular geometry to decide if the molecule is polar or nonpolar, and only then infer the intermolecular forces present.
**Concept category:** polarity analysis
**Source:** Teaching Ideas and Suggestions (Intermolecular Forces - Teacher Notes/Intermolecular Forces - Teacher Notes.md)
**Evidence:** Students will have to use a Lewis structure (provided) and an electronegativity table to determine the presence/absence of polar bonds and whether the substance consists of polar or nonpolar molecules.

## Misconception 253: Students may believe the relative strength of intermolecular forces can be ranked directly from formulas alone without analyzing the type of forces present in the substances.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Ranking intermolecular force strength
**Correct concept:** Ranking intermolecular force strength requires determining which intermolecular forces are present in each substance, then comparing their relative strengths.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should infer the intermolecular forces from structure and polarity, and then compare those forces to rank the substances from weakest to strongest.
**Concept category:** force strength ranking
**Source:** Teaching Ideas and Suggestions (Intermolecular Forces - Teacher Notes/Intermolecular Forces - Teacher Notes.md)
**Evidence:** Finally, students will need to be able to rank the relative strength of the intermolecular forces for three susbtances if given their formulas.

## Misconception 254: The temperature of a material changes during phase change.

**Topic:** Phase changes / Heating curves
**Correct concept:** During a phase change, added energy goes into changing particle arrangement/intermolecular attractions rather than changing temperature.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that while a substance is melting or boiling, the temperature can remain constant because the energy input is used to overcome intermolecular attractions (increase potential energy) instead of increasing kinetic energy.
**Concept category:** phase change
**Population:** students
**Source:** Intermolecular Forces (Intermolecular-Forces-Unit-Plan/Intermolecular-Forces-Unit-Plan.md)
**Evidence:** "The temperature of a material changes during phase change."

## Misconception 255: When you get something hot enough it will always turn into a gas.

**Topic:** Phase changes / Boiling and gas formation
**Correct concept:** Whether a substance becomes a gas depends on temperature and pressure; at high enough pressure or near the critical point, a substance may not simply become a normal gas and can exist as a supercritical fluid.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that heating increases particle kinetic energy, but pressure and intermolecular attraction also matter. At certain pressure-temperature conditions, the substance may boil, reach a critical point, or behave as a supercritical fluid rather than always becoming a gas.
**Concept category:** phase change; pressure-temperature effects
**Population:** students
**Source:** Intermolecular Forces (Intermolecular-Forces-Unit-Plan/Intermolecular-Forces-Unit-Plan.md)
**Evidence:** "When you get something hot enough it will always turn into a gas."

## Misconception 256: Pressure doesn't matter for boiling temperature or state of matter.

**Topic:** Phase changes / Boiling point and pressure
**Correct concept:** Boiling point and state of matter depend on pressure as well as temperature; lowering pressure lowers boiling point.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that liquids boil when vapor pressure equals external pressure, so changing external pressure changes the temperature at which boiling occurs.
**Concept category:** pressure-temperature effects
**Population:** students
**Source:** Intermolecular Forces (Intermolecular-Forces-Unit-Plan/Intermolecular-Forces-Unit-Plan.md)
**Evidence:** "Pressure doesn't matter for boiling temperature or state of matter."

## Misconception 257: Intermolecular forces are attractions within molecules rather than between molecules.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Location of attractions
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces are attractions between separate molecules, or between distant parts of some very large molecules, not the covalent bonds holding atoms together within one molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish intramolecular bonds from intermolecular attractions and identify that melting, boiling, and sticking involve interactions between molecules, not breaking covalent bonds inside a molecule.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces
**Population:** students
**Source:** Intermolecular Forces (Intermolecular-Forces-Unit-Plan/Intermolecular-Forces-Unit-Plan.md)
**Evidence:** "Intermolecular forces are attractions between molecules not within molecules."

## Misconception 258: Hydrogen bonds are any covalent bond with hydrogen in it.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Definition of hydrogen bond
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is a specific intermolecular attraction involving hydrogen bonded to nitrogen, oxygen, or fluorine interacting with N, O, or F on another molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the atoms involved and check whether the molecule has H directly bonded to N, O, or F and whether there is a nearby N, O, or F lone-pair acceptor for the intermolecular attraction.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Population:** students
**Source:** Intermolecular Forces (Intermolecular-Forces-Unit-Plan/Intermolecular-Forces-Unit-Plan.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bonds are any covalent bond with hydrogen in it."

## Misconception 259: The bonding pair of electrons in a covalent bond is always right in the middle.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Covalent bond electron pair position
**Correct concept:** Bonding electron pairs in covalent bonds are not always centered; their position depends on the relative electronegativities of the bonded atoms and is shifted toward the more electronegative atom.
**Expected reasoning:** Compare the electronegativities of the bonded atoms. The atom with greater electronegativity attracts shared electrons more strongly, so the bonding pair is displaced toward that atom rather than sitting exactly midway.
**Concept category:** bond polarity / covalent bonding
**Prevalence:** 37% of students
**Population:** 101 tenth-grade students at a public high school in Jakarta, Indonesia
**Source:** Identifying Students' Misconceptions about Chemical Bonding Using a Four-Tier Diagnostic Test (JREP_46–62+Agung/JREP_46–62+Agung.md)
**Evidence:** "The bonding pair of electrons in a covalent bond is always right in the middle"

## Misconception 260: The size of the atom affects the position of the bonding electron pair in a covalent bond.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Covalent bond electron pair position
**Correct concept:** The position of the bonding electron pair is determined by electronegativity differences, not atomic size.
**Expected reasoning:** Identify which atom is more electronegative; shared electrons are drawn toward that atom. Atomic size is not the deciding factor for bond-pair position in the model used here.
**Concept category:** bond polarity / covalent bonding
**Prevalence:** 17% of students
**Population:** 101 tenth-grade students at a public high school in Jakarta, Indonesia
**Source:** Identifying Students' Misconceptions about Chemical Bonding Using a Four-Tier Diagnostic Test (JREP_46–62+Agung/JREP_46–62+Agung.md)
**Evidence:** "The size of the atom affects the position of the bonding electron pair in a covalent bond"

## Misconception 261: The lone pair of electrons affects the position of the bonding electron pair in a covalent bond.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding / Covalent bond electron pair position
**Correct concept:** Bond-pair position is explained by electronegativity differences between bonded atoms, not by the presence of lone pairs.
**Expected reasoning:** Determine electron density using electronegativity of the atoms involved in the bond. Lone pairs affect molecular shape, but they do not determine where a bonding pair sits within a covalent bond.
**Concept category:** bond polarity / covalent bonding
**Prevalence:** 17% of students
**Population:** 101 tenth-grade students at a public high school in Jakarta, Indonesia
**Source:** Identifying Students' Misconceptions about Chemical Bonding Using a Four-Tier Diagnostic Test (JREP_46–62+Agung/JREP_46–62+Agung.md)
**Evidence:** "The lone pair of electrons affects the position of the bonding electron pair in a covalent bond"

## Misconception 262: The shape of the molecule is only due to the repulsion of the bonding electron pair.

**Topic:** Molecular geometry / VSEPR / shape determination
**Correct concept:** Molecular geometry is determined by repulsions among all electron domains, especially bonding pairs and lone pairs, according to VSEPR theory.
**Expected reasoning:** Count all electron domains around the central atom and consider that lone pairs repel more strongly than bonding pairs. Use VSEPR to predict the 3D shape.
**Concept category:** VSEPR theory / molecular geometry
**Prevalence:** 41% of students
**Population:** 101 tenth-grade students at a public high school in Jakarta, Indonesia
**Source:** Identifying Students' Misconceptions about Chemical Bonding Using a Four-Tier Diagnostic Test (JREP_46–62+Agung/JREP_46–62+Agung.md)
**Evidence:** "The shape of the molecule is only due to the repulsion of the bonding electron pair"

## Misconception 263: Lone pairs of electrons are considered to be included in molecular geometry.

**Topic:** Molecular geometry / Molecular geometry and lone pairs
**Correct concept:** Lone pairs are part of electron geometry, but molecular geometry describes only the positions of atoms, not lone pairs.
**Expected reasoning:** First determine electron geometry from all electron domains, then derive molecular geometry by ignoring lone pairs and looking only at the arrangement of atoms.
**Concept category:** VSEPR theory / molecular geometry
**Prevalence:** 18% of students
**Population:** 101 tenth-grade students at a public high school in Jakarta, Indonesia
**Source:** Identifying Students' Misconceptions about Chemical Bonding Using a Four-Tier Diagnostic Test (JREP_46–62+Agung/JREP_46–62+Agung.md)
**Evidence:** "Lone pairs of electrons are considered to be included in molecular geometry"

## Misconception 264: Students are inverted in understanding the partial charge symbol.

**Topic:** Bond polarity / Partial charge symbols
**Correct concept:** The partial charge symbols delta-plus and delta-minus indicate the direction of electron density in a polar bond, with the more electronegative atom carrying partial negative charge.
**Expected reasoning:** Use electronegativity to decide which atom attracts electron density more strongly, then assign δ− to that atom and δ+ to the other atom.
**Concept category:** bond polarity / symbols
**Prevalence:** 33% of students
**Population:** 101 tenth-grade students at a public high school in Jakarta, Indonesia
**Source:** Identifying Students' Misconceptions about Chemical Bonding Using a Four-Tier Diagnostic Test (JREP_46–62+Agung/JREP_46–62+Agung.md)
**Evidence:** "Inverted in understanding the partial charge symbol"

## Misconception 265: Lone pairs of electrons do not affect the geometry of the molecule.

**Topic:** Molecular geometry / Effect of lone pairs on shape
**Correct concept:** Lone pairs do affect molecular geometry because they occupy electron domains and exert repulsion that changes bond angles and shape.
**Expected reasoning:** When applying VSEPR, include lone pairs as electron domains. Their stronger repulsion alters the arrangement of bonding pairs and thus the molecular shape.
**Concept category:** VSEPR theory / molecular geometry
**Prevalence:** 23% of students
**Population:** 101 tenth-grade students at a public high school in Jakarta, Indonesia
**Source:** Identifying Students' Misconceptions about Chemical Bonding Using a Four-Tier Diagnostic Test (JREP_46–62+Agung/JREP_46–62+Agung.md)
**Evidence:** "Lone pairs of electrons do not affect the geometry of the molecule"

## Misconception 266: The geometry of the molecule is only affected by the lone pair of electrons.

**Topic:** Molecular geometry / Effect of lone pairs on shape
**Correct concept:** Molecular geometry depends on the repulsions among all electron domains, not lone pairs alone.
**Expected reasoning:** Consider both bonding pairs and lone pairs together. Molecular shape emerges from the full set of electron-domain repulsions around the central atom.
**Concept category:** VSEPR theory / molecular geometry
**Prevalence:** 13% of students
**Population:** 101 tenth-grade students at a public high school in Jakarta, Indonesia
**Source:** Identifying Students' Misconceptions about Chemical Bonding Using a Four-Tier Diagnostic Test (JREP_46–62+Agung/JREP_46–62+Agung.md)
**Evidence:** "The geometry of the molecule is only affected by the lone pair of electrons"

## Misconception 267: Lone pairs of electrons cause polarity in a molecule.

**Topic:** Molecular polarity / Role of lone pairs
**Correct concept:** Molecular polarity depends on both bond polarity and the symmetry of the molecular geometry; lone pairs influence polarity indirectly by affecting shape, not by directly causing polarity themselves.
**Expected reasoning:** Determine whether the molecule has polar bonds, then assess whether the geometry is symmetrical enough for dipoles to cancel. Lone pairs matter because they can make geometry asymmetrical.
**Concept category:** molecular polarity
**Prevalence:** 18% of students
**Population:** 101 tenth-grade students at a public high school in Jakarta, Indonesia
**Source:** Identifying Students' Misconceptions about Chemical Bonding Using a Four-Tier Diagnostic Test (JREP_46–62+Agung/JREP_46–62+Agung.md)
**Evidence:** "Lone pairs of electrons cause polarity in a molecule"

## Misconception 268: Polar molecules will form when there is a large difference in electronegativity in the atoms that make up the molecule.

**Topic:** Molecular polarity / Electronegativity and polarity
**Correct concept:** A large electronegativity difference makes a bond polar, but molecular polarity also depends on molecular geometry and whether bond dipoles cancel.
**Expected reasoning:** Check bond polarity from electronegativity differences, then determine the 3D shape. If the dipoles do not cancel because of asymmetry, the molecule is polar.
**Concept category:** molecular polarity / electronegativity
**Prevalence:** 11% of students
**Population:** 101 tenth-grade students at a public high school in Jakarta, Indonesia
**Source:** Identifying Students' Misconceptions about Chemical Bonding Using a Four-Tier Diagnostic Test (JREP_46–62+Agung/JREP_46–62+Agung.md)
**Evidence:** "Polar molecules will form when there is a large difference in electronegativity in the atoms that make up the molecule"

## Misconception 269: Chemical bonds are forces between molecules.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Chemical bonds vs intermolecular forces
**Correct concept:** Chemical bonds hold atoms together within a substance, whereas intermolecular forces act between molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** Distinguish intramolecular bonding from intermolecular attractions. Determine whether the question asks about forces within a molecule/compound or between separate molecules.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces
**Prevalence:** 43% of students
**Population:** 101 tenth-grade students at a public high school in Jakarta, Indonesia
**Source:** Identifying Students' Misconceptions about Chemical Bonding Using a Four-Tier Diagnostic Test (JREP_46–62+Agung/JREP_46–62+Agung.md)
**Evidence:** "Chemical bonds are forces between molecules"

## Misconception 270: H2S is a non-polar molecule.

**Topic:** Molecular polarity / Polarity of H2S
**Correct concept:** H2S is polar because its bent shape and bond dipoles do not cancel completely.
**Expected reasoning:** Determine the H–S bond polarity from electronegativity, then evaluate the bent molecular geometry. Because the dipoles do not cancel, the molecule is polar.
**Concept category:** molecular polarity
**Prevalence:** 19% of students
**Population:** 101 tenth-grade students at a public high school in Jakarta, Indonesia
**Source:** Identifying Students' Misconceptions about Chemical Bonding Using a Four-Tier Diagnostic Test (JREP_46–62+Agung/JREP_46–62+Agung.md)
**Evidence:** "H2S is a non-polar molecule"

## Misconception 271: The electronegativity of an atom affects the geometry of the molecule.

**Topic:** Molecular geometry / Electronegativity and shape
**Correct concept:** Molecular geometry is determined by repulsions among electron domains, not by electronegativity directly.
**Expected reasoning:** Use VSEPR to count electron domains and predict shape. Electronegativity influences bond polarity, but shape comes from electron-pair repulsion.
**Concept category:** VSEPR theory / molecular geometry
**Prevalence:** 12% of students
**Population:** 101 tenth-grade students at a public high school in Jakarta, Indonesia
**Source:** Identifying Students' Misconceptions about Chemical Bonding Using a Four-Tier Diagnostic Test (JREP_46–62+Agung/JREP_46–62+Agung.md)
**Evidence:** "The electronegativity of an atom affects the geometry of the molecule"

## Misconception 272: Polar molecules are formed when the difference in electronegativity between atoms is small.

**Topic:** Molecular polarity / Electronegativity difference and polarity
**Correct concept:** A molecule with a small electronegativity difference is not necessarily polar; polarity depends on both bond polarity and molecular geometry.
**Expected reasoning:** Assess electronegativity difference to judge bond polarity, then check whether the molecular shape allows dipoles to cancel. A small difference alone does not create molecular polarity.
**Concept category:** molecular polarity / electronegativity
**Prevalence:** 19% of students
**Population:** 101 tenth-grade students at a public high school in Jakarta, Indonesia
**Source:** Identifying Students' Misconceptions about Chemical Bonding Using a Four-Tier Diagnostic Test (JREP_46–62+Agung/JREP_46–62+Agung.md)
**Evidence:** "Polar molecules are formed when the difference in electronegativity between atoms is small"

## Misconception 273: Molecules become polar when the bonds formed between atoms are polar.

**Topic:** Molecular polarity / Bond polarity vs molecular polarity
**Correct concept:** Polar bonds are necessary but not sufficient for molecular polarity; the molecular shape must also be asymmetrical so dipoles do not cancel.
**Expected reasoning:** Identify polar bonds, then examine geometry. If the bond dipoles cancel because the molecule is symmetrical, the molecule is nonpolar despite having polar bonds.
**Concept category:** molecular polarity / bond polarity
**Prevalence:** 15% of students
**Population:** 101 tenth-grade students at a public high school in Jakarta, Indonesia
**Source:** Identifying Students' Misconceptions about Chemical Bonding Using a Four-Tier Diagnostic Test (JREP_46–62+Agung/JREP_46–62+Agung.md)
**Evidence:** "Molecules become polar when the bonds formed between atoms are polar"

## Misconception 274: NaCl is a molecule.

**Topic:** Ionic bonding / Nature of NaCl
**Correct concept:** NaCl is an ionic compound and is not a molecule; it forms a crystal lattice and is represented by an empirical formula.
**Expected reasoning:** Recognize that ionic compounds do not consist of discrete molecules. Describe NaCl as a lattice of ions with an empirical formula rather than as a molecule.
**Concept category:** ionic bonding / ionic compounds
**Prevalence:** 75% of students
**Population:** 101 tenth-grade students at a public high school in Jakarta, Indonesia
**Source:** Identifying Students' Misconceptions about Chemical Bonding Using a Four-Tier Diagnostic Test (JREP_46–62+Agung/JREP_46–62+Agung.md)
**Evidence:** "NaCl is a molecule"

## Misconception 275: Metal and non-metal elements can form covalent compounds.

**Topic:** Ionic bonding / Metal + nonmetal bonding type
**Correct concept:** A metal reacting with a nonmetal typically forms an ionic compound due to electron transfer and large electronegativity difference.
**Expected reasoning:** Use the element types and electronegativity difference to infer bonding. Metal atoms tend to lose electrons and nonmetals tend to gain them, leading to ionic bonding.
**Concept category:** ionic bonding / bond type classification
**Prevalence:** 25% of students
**Population:** 101 tenth-grade students at a public high school in Jakarta, Indonesia
**Source:** Identifying Students' Misconceptions about Chemical Bonding Using a Four-Tier Diagnostic Test (JREP_46–62+Agung/JREP_46–62+Agung.md)
**Evidence:** "Metal and non-metal elements can form covalent compounds"

## Misconception 276: The octet rule is used to determine molecular geometry.

**Topic:** Octet rule / Purpose of octet rule
**Correct concept:** The octet rule is a guide for stable electron configurations; it is not a rule for determining molecular geometry.
**Expected reasoning:** Use the octet rule to check whether atoms achieve stable valence-shell electron configurations, and use VSEPR—not the octet rule—to predict molecular shape.
**Concept category:** octet rule
**Prevalence:** 20% of students
**Population:** 101 tenth-grade students at a public high school in Jakarta, Indonesia
**Source:** Identifying Students' Misconceptions about Chemical Bonding Using a Four-Tier Diagnostic Test (JREP_46–62+Agung/JREP_46–62+Agung.md)
**Evidence:** "The octet rule is used to determine molecular geometry"

## Misconception 277: VSEPR theory states that molecular geometry is caused by the repulsion of bonding atoms.

**Topic:** VSEPR theory / What VSEPR explains
**Correct concept:** VSEPR theory explains molecular geometry in terms of repulsion between electron pairs/domains, including lone pairs and bonding pairs, not repulsion between atoms.
**Expected reasoning:** Count electron domains around the central atom and apply electron-pair repulsion. Atoms themselves are not the source of the VSEPR repulsions; the electron domains are.
**Concept category:** VSEPR theory
**Prevalence:** 30% of students
**Population:** 101 tenth-grade students at a public high school in Jakarta, Indonesia
**Source:** Identifying Students' Misconceptions about Chemical Bonding Using a Four-Tier Diagnostic Test (JREP_46–62+Agung/JREP_46–62+Agung.md)
**Evidence:** "molecular geometry is caused by the repulsion of bonding atoms"

## Misconception 278: Students believe sodium chloride exists as discrete molecules rather than as an ionic lattice.

**Topic:** ionic bonding / ionic compounds as molecules
**Correct concept:** Sodium chloride is a giant ionic lattice made of alternating ions, not discrete NaCl molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** Reason from the crystal structure: electron transfer forms ions, and electrostatic attraction acts in all directions through a lattice rather than producing individual molecules.
**Concept category:** lattice structure
**Prevalence:** 80.4% of 119 Grade 10 students
**Population:** Grade 10 students in a Singapore school
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of chemical bonding (JSMESEA-24-2-40/JSMESEA-24-2-40.md)
**Evidence:** "many students believed that sodium chloride existed as molecules"

## Misconception 279: Students think one sodium ion and one chloride ion form an 'ion pair molecule'.

**Topic:** ionic bonding / ion pair molecule
**Correct concept:** Ionic compounds do not consist of ion-pair molecules; they form extended ionic lattices.
**Expected reasoning:** Recognize that ionic bonding is non-directional and each ion is attracted to many oppositely charged ions in a lattice, not to a single partner molecule.
**Concept category:** lattice structure
**Prevalence:** 46.1% of 119 Grade 10 students
**Population:** Grade 10 students in a Singapore school
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of chemical bonding (JSMESEA-24-2-40/JSMESEA-24-2-40.md)
**Evidence:** "46.1% thought that one sodium ion and one chloride ion formed an 'ion pair molecule'"

## Misconception 280: Students think that when metals and non-metals combine, they form covalent bonds instead of ionic bonds.

**Topic:** ionic bonding / metal and non-metal bonding type
**Correct concept:** Metal–non-metal combinations typically form ionic bonds via electron transfer, not covalent bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** Identify the elements involved and determine whether electrons are transferred from a metal to a non-metal to form ions held by electrostatic attraction.
**Concept category:** bond type classification
**Prevalence:** 22.5% of 119 Grade 10 students
**Population:** Grade 10 students in a Singapore school
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of chemical bonding (JSMESEA-24-2-40/JSMESEA-24-2-40.md)
**Evidence:** "22.5%) also indicated that when atoms of metals and non-metals combine, they form covalent bonds instead of ionic bonds."

## Misconception 281: Students think the number of electrons transferred in ionic bonding depends only on the number of electrons the non-metal needs to achieve an octet.

**Topic:** ionic bonding / electron transfer amount
**Correct concept:** Electron transfer in ionic bonding depends on the overall electron changes of both atoms, leading to stable ions and an extended ionic lattice; it is not determined only by the non-metal's octet need.
**Expected reasoning:** Consider both the metal's tendency to lose electrons and the non-metal's tendency to gain electrons, then determine a charge balance that yields stable ions.
**Concept category:** electron transfer
**Prevalence:** 10% of 119 Grade 10 students
**Population:** Grade 10 students in a Singapore school
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of chemical bonding (JSMESEA-24-2-40/JSMESEA-24-2-40.md)
**Evidence:** "the number of electrons transferred depends only on the number of electrons that the atoms of the non-metal need to achieve a stable octet"

## Misconception 282: Students believe an atom can form only one ionic bond because it donates only one electron, so bonding is limited to the specific atom that received that electron.

**Topic:** ionic bonding / bond count determined by donated electron
**Correct concept:** Ions in an ionic lattice are attracted to many surrounding counterions; one electron transfer does not mean one bond to one fixed partner only.
**Expected reasoning:** After electron transfer, think in terms of charge and omnidirectional electrostatic attraction in a lattice, not one-to-one molecular bonding.
**Concept category:** bond count / lattice interactions
**Prevalence:** described in interviews with Grade 12 students
**Population:** Grade 12 students
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of chemical bonding (JSMESEA-24-2-40/JSMESEA-24-2-40.md)
**Evidence:** "a sodium atom can only donate one electron, so it can form only one bond"

## Misconception 283: Students believe ions are bonded only to the specific counterion involved in the electron transfer event.

**Topic:** ionic bonding / specific donor-recipient pairing
**Correct concept:** Ionic bonding is not a one-time pairing; each ion is surrounded by and attracted to multiple oppositely charged ions in the lattice.
**Expected reasoning:** Link electron transfer to ion formation, then reason that the resulting ions interact electrostatically with all neighboring opposite charges.
**Concept category:** lattice structure
**Prevalence:** described in interviews with Grade 12 students
**Population:** Grade 12 students
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of chemical bonding (JSMESEA-24-2-40/JSMESEA-24-2-40.md)
**Evidence:** "the chloride is bonded to the specific sodium atom that donated an electron to it"

## Misconception 284: Students think an ion is bonded to only one counterion, while interactions with the other surrounding ions are merely forces, not bonds.

**Topic:** ionic bonding / bonds versus forces around ions
**Correct concept:** The ionic lattice is held together by electrostatic attraction in all directions; neighboring ion interactions are part of the lattice bonding, not merely weaker forces outside the bond.
**Expected reasoning:** Recognize that the lattice is a network of electrostatic attractions extending to all neighboring ions, not a single-bond-plus-forces model.
**Concept category:** bond versus force distinction
**Prevalence:** described in interviews with Grade 12 students
**Population:** Grade 12 students
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of chemical bonding (JSMESEA-24-2-40/JSMESEA-24-2-40.md)
**Evidence:** "a chloride ion is bonded to one sodium ion and attracted to a further five sodium ions, but just by forces and not bonds"

## Misconception 285: Students think attraction between oppositely charged ions neutralizes the charges and forms neutral molecules in the solid.

**Topic:** ionic bonding / neutral molecules from ionic attraction
**Correct concept:** Attraction between ions does not neutralize them; ions remain charged within the ionic lattice.
**Expected reasoning:** Track charge before and after bonding: electron transfer creates ions, and the lattice is stabilized by electrostatic attraction without charge cancellation.
**Concept category:** charge and lattice structure
**Prevalence:** described in interviews with 48 Grade 12 students
**Population:** 48 Grade 12 students
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of chemical bonding (JSMESEA-24-2-40/JSMESEA-24-2-40.md)
**Evidence:** "the attraction between oppositely charged ions in an ionic compound results in the neutralisation of the charges, leading to the formation of a lattice consisting of neutral molecules"

## Misconception 286: Students try to assign each electron in a covalent bond to one of the bonded atoms, as if each electron belongs to a particular atom.

**Topic:** covalent bonding / electron ownership in bonds
**Correct concept:** In a covalent bond, the bonding electrons are shared between atoms and occupy the region between nuclei.
**Expected reasoning:** Treat the pair of electrons as shared electron density between both nuclei, not as two separately owned electrons.
**Concept category:** electron sharing
**Prevalence:** described for Grade 11 and 12 students
**Population:** Grade 11 and 12 students
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of chemical bonding (JSMESEA-24-2-40/JSMESEA-24-2-40.md)
**Evidence:** "students would commonly identify and distinguish which electron in a covalent bond belonged to each of the bonded atoms"

## Misconception 287: Students think the sharing of electrons is the force that holds atoms together in a molecule.

**Topic:** covalent bonding / shared electrons as force
**Correct concept:** A covalent bond is an electrostatic attraction between the shared electrons and the nuclei of the bonded atoms; the bond is not the electrons themselves.
**Expected reasoning:** Reason from electrostatic attraction: negatively charged shared electrons are attracted to both positively charged nuclei, creating the bond.
**Concept category:** bond nature
**Prevalence:** described for Grade 11 and 12 students
**Population:** Grade 11 and 12 students
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of chemical bonding (JSMESEA-24-2-40/JSMESEA-24-2-40.md)
**Evidence:** "considered the sharing of electrons as the 'force' holding the atoms in a molecule together instead of electrostatic attraction between the shared electrons and the nuclei involved"

## Misconception 288: Students believe a covalent bond is simply a pair of shared electrons.

**Topic:** covalent bonding / pair of shared electrons equals bond
**Correct concept:** A covalent bond is the electrostatic attraction that holds the atoms together; shared electrons participate in the bond but are not themselves the bond.
**Expected reasoning:** Distinguish the physical particles (shared electrons) from the attractive interaction between nuclei and shared electrons that constitutes the bond.
**Concept category:** bond definition
**Prevalence:** described in interviews with Boo's students
**Population:** Boo's students
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of chemical bonding (JSMESEA-24-2-40/JSMESEA-24-2-40.md)
**Evidence:** "some of her students held the misconception that a covalent bond is a pair of shared electrons"

## Misconception 289: Students think ionic bonds are electrostatic in nature but covalent bonds are not.

**Topic:** covalent bonding / covalent bond not electrostatic
**Correct concept:** All chemical bonds, including covalent bonds, are electrostatic in nature.
**Expected reasoning:** Apply the same electrostatic principle to covalent bonding: shared electrons are attracted to both nuclei, so the bond is electrostatic too.
**Concept category:** bond nature
**Prevalence:** described in interviews
**Population:** Boo's interviewees
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of chemical bonding (JSMESEA-24-2-40/JSMESEA-24-2-40.md)
**Evidence:** "some of her interviewees held the misconception that an ionic bond is electrostatic in nature but not the covalent bond"

## Misconception 290: Students think covalent bonds are broken when a substance changes state.

**Topic:** covalent bonding / bond breaking during state change
**Correct concept:** For simple molecular substances, changing state usually breaks intermolecular forces between molecules, not the covalent bonds within molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** Differentiate intramolecular covalent bonds from intermolecular attractions; phase changes overcome the weaker intermolecular forces first.
**Concept category:** phase change and bonding
**Prevalence:** 21% of the students in Tan and Treagust's study
**Population:** Grade 10 students in a Singapore school
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of chemical bonding (JSMESEA-24-2-40/JSMESEA-24-2-40.md)
**Evidence:** "21% of the students believed ... that covalent bonds were broken when a substance changed state"

## Misconception 291: Students infer that covalent bonds are weaker than ionic bonds simply because covalent substances often have lower melting and boiling points.

**Topic:** covalent bonding / covalent bonds weaker than ionic bonds because of low melting/boiling points
**Correct concept:** Lower melting and boiling points of many covalent substances are due to weaker intermolecular forces, not weaker covalent bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** Compare substance structure: covalent molecular substances usually have discrete molecules held together by weak intermolecular forces, whereas ionic substances form lattices with strong electrostatic attractions.
**Concept category:** structure-property relationship
**Prevalence:** described in interviews
**Population:** Boo's interviewees
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of chemical bonding (JSMESEA-24-2-40/JSMESEA-24-2-40.md)
**Evidence:** "some of her interviewees believed that covalent bonds are weaker than ionic bonds because they had the notion that covalent substances generally have lower melting points and boiling points compared to ionic substances"

## Misconception 292: Students identify intermolecular forces as the forces within a molecule.

**Topic:** intermolecular forces / forces within molecule
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces act between molecules, while covalent bonds act within molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** Use the prefixes: 'inter' means between different molecules, and 'intra' or intramolecular means within a molecule.
**Concept category:** intermolecular vs intramolecular
**Prevalence:** described in two studies involving Grade 11 and 12 students
**Population:** Grade 11 and 12 students in Australia and Singapore
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of chemical bonding (JSMESEA-24-2-40/JSMESEA-24-2-40.md)
**Evidence:** "the students incorrectly identified intermolecular forces as the forces within a molecule"

## Misconception 293: Students think strong intermolecular forces exist in a continuous covalent network solid.

**Topic:** intermolecular forces / strong intermolecular forces in network covalent solid
**Correct concept:** A continuous covalent network solid is held together by covalent bonds throughout the network, not by intermolecular forces.
**Expected reasoning:** Recognize the structural difference between a molecular solid, where intermolecular forces dominate, and a network covalent solid, where atoms are linked by covalent bonds.
**Concept category:** structure and bonding
**Prevalence:** described in two studies involving Grade 11 and 12 students
**Population:** Grade 11 and 12 students in Australia and Singapore
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of chemical bonding (JSMESEA-24-2-40/JSMESEA-24-2-40.md)
**Evidence:** "others thought that strong intermolecular forces exist in a continuous covalent (network) solid"

## Misconception 294: Students think the number of ionic bonds formed is determined by electrovalency or valency.

**Topic:** valency / ionic bonding / ionic bond count equals electrovalency
**Correct concept:** Electrovalency refers to ionic charge, not the number of ionic bonds formed; bond number is not determined by valency.
**Expected reasoning:** Separate ionic charge from lattice bonding: an ion’s charge indicates electron loss or gain, but the ionic lattice involves multiple electrostatic attractions not fixed one-to-one bond counts.
**Concept category:** valency and bond count
**Prevalence:** presented as a misconception to avoid
**Population:** general secondary chemistry students
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of chemical bonding (JSMESEA-24-2-40/JSMESEA-24-2-40.md)
**Evidence:** "the number of ionic bonds formed is not determined by electrovalency"

## Misconception 295: Students infer from the formula NaCl that one sodium particle is bonded to one chlorine particle, analogous to HCl molecules.

**Topic:** chemical formulae and bonding / formula implies one-to-one particle pairing
**Correct concept:** NaCl is an ionic lattice formula unit, not evidence of discrete one-to-one molecules or bonded particle pairs.
**Expected reasoning:** Interpret ionic formulae as simplest ratios of ions in a lattice, unlike molecular formulae that represent actual molecules.
**Concept category:** formula interpretation
**Prevalence:** suggested as a contributing misconception
**Population:** secondary school students
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of chemical bonding (JSMESEA-24-2-40/JSMESEA-24-2-40.md)
**Evidence:** "students might have the idea that one particle of sodium is bonded to one particle of chlorine just as one atom of hydrogen is bonded to one atom of chlorine"

## Misconception 296: Students interpret the wires in a ball-and-stick model of sodium chloride as actual bonds of some sort.

**Topic:** chemical bonding models / ball-and-stick model wires as bonds
**Correct concept:** Model sticks are representational aids and do not literally correspond to individual bonds in an ionic lattice.
**Expected reasoning:** Use the model as a visualization tool while remembering that ionic bonding is a three-dimensional, omnidirectional electrostatic lattice.
**Concept category:** model interpretation
**Prevalence:** described among many Grade 12 students
**Population:** Grade 12 students
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of chemical bonding (JSMESEA-24-2-40/JSMESEA-24-2-40.md)
**Evidence:** "many interpreted the six wires attached to each ball (ion) as each representing a bond of some sort"

## Misconception 297: Students cannot determine whether a molecule is polar by analyzing the polarity of its bonds within the molecule.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Hydrogen bonding and dipole-dipole interactions
**Correct concept:** Molecular polarity is determined from the bond polarities together with the molecular geometry and the vector sum of bond dipoles.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify polar bonds, determine the 3D shape of the molecule, and then combine the bond dipoles vectorially to decide whether there is a net dipole moment.
**Concept category:** molecular polarity
**Prevalence:** many students
**Population:** Grade 11 and 12 students
**Source:** CONTENT FRAMEWORK FOR INTER- MOLECULAR FORCES (JSMESEA-26-1-61/JSMESEA-26-1-61.md)
**Evidence:** “many students could not determine the polarity of the molecules by analyzing the polarity of the bonds within the molecules”

## Misconception 298: Students hold alternative conceptions about intermolecular forces after instruction, but the provided paper excerpt does not specify the exact misconception statements.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / student conceptions
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces should be understood scientifically as attractions between particles that explain molecular properties and behavior.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should connect particle structure and interactions to observed properties using correct molecular-level reasoning.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces
**Population:** Junior college students
**Source:** Junior college students' conceptions of intermolecular forces (Junior college students' conceptions of intermolecular forces/Junior college students' conceptions of intermolecular forces.md)
**Evidence:** "Common alternative conceptions that students had pertaining to the topic of intermolecular forces were identified"

## Misconception 299: Students treat intermolecular attractions as an invisible property that is only recognized through a phenomenon, rather than as real forces between molecules that explain the phenomenon.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Nature of intermolecular attractions
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces are real attractive interactions between molecules/particles that can be used to explain observable bulk phenomena such as surface tension and solubility behavior.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect the macroscopic observation to particle-level attractions: if water can hold many paper clips above the brim, the surface is being stabilized by cohesive intermolecular attractions, especially hydrogen bonding, rather than by an unexplained "trick" of the liquid.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces
**Population:** 9th-12th grade chemistry/physical science students
**Source:** Authentic Lessons for 21st Century Learning (K20 LEARN _ The Attraction Is REAL/K20 LEARN _ The Attraction Is REAL.md)
**Evidence:** "students will explore, learn, and connect concepts of Intermolecular Forces (IMFs) to physical phenomena"

## Misconception 300: Students expect a full glass of water to hold only a small number of paper clips before spilling, underestimating water's surface tension.

**Topic:** Surface tension / Paper clip activity
**Correct concept:** Water can support many paper clips because cohesive intermolecular forces create substantial surface tension at the water's surface.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should predict that the water surface behaves like a stretched film due to cohesion; therefore, careful addition of paper clips can be supported until the surface tension is overcome.
**Concept category:** surface tension / cohesion
**Prevalence:** Most students
**Population:** students in the lesson activity (9th-12th grade chemistry/physical science students)
**Source:** Authentic Lessons for 21st Century Learning (K20 LEARN _ The Attraction Is REAL/K20 LEARN _ The Attraction Is REAL.md)
**Evidence:** "Most students will predict less than 30 paperclips, but the real answer is HUNDREDS of paperclips."

## Misconception 301: Students may think an electron literally orbits a nucleus in a simple planetary path, with a single electron moving around a single central particle like a person circling another person.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Rutherford-Bohr atom analogy
**Correct concept:** Electrons do not move in fixed planetary orbits around a nucleus; the Bohr picture is a simplification and real atoms have quantum electron distributions, with nuclei made of more than one particle and often multiple electrons.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should recognize that the atom picture used in the lesson is only an oversimplified model. They should reason that the nucleus contains protons and neutrons, and electrons occupy quantum orbitals or electron clouds rather than literal circular paths.
**Concept category:** atomic model
**Population:** 60 freshmen prospective elementary teachers in an introductory chemistry course
**Source:** TEACHING INTERMOLECULAR FORCES WITH  LOVE ANALOGY: A CASE STUDY (KHIMIYA_21_3_KARAKAS/KHIMIYA_21_3_KARAKAS.md)
**Evidence:** "the instructor projected the simple Rutherford–Bohr atom picture on to the wall as an oversimplification and explained that nucleus is made up of more than one particle, atoms have mostly more than one electron revolving around a nucleus."

## Misconception 302: Students may think London dispersion forces arise because electrons deliberately or persistently move toward one side in response to an attractive neighbor, as if the electron 'takes a look' and shifts like a person focusing attention.

**Topic:** London dispersion force / Electron movement and induced polarity
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces arise from instantaneous fluctuations in electron density that create temporary dipoles, which then induce dipoles in nearby particles and produce a brief attraction.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should reason that electron density is constantly fluctuating; a momentary uneven distribution creates a temporary dipole, which induces an opposite dipole in a neighboring atom or molecule, leading to a fleeting electrostatic attraction.
**Concept category:** electron transfer / induced dipole
**Prevalence:** 45/60 students received full grades on the final exam question about London dispersion force; 5/60 received 6/10; 4/60 received 5/10; 6/60 received 0
**Population:** 60 freshmen prospective elementary teachers in an introductory chemistry course
**Source:** TEACHING INTERMOLECULAR FORCES WITH  LOVE ANALOGY: A CASE STUDY (KHIMIYA_21_3_KARAKAS/KHIMIYA_21_3_KARAKAS.md)
**Evidence:** "The instructor further explained that the induced dipole ... causes a brief electrostatic attraction between the two atoms"

## Misconception 303: Students may think the temporary dipole forms because a molecule is near an 'attractive' or 'positive charge' in a way that electrons consciously gather on one side and retreat from the other, like reacting emotionally to another person.

**Topic:** London dispersion force / Origin of temporary charge distribution
**Correct concept:** Temporary dipoles in London dispersion forces are caused by random, instantaneous fluctuations in electron density, not by conscious attraction or intentional movement; nearby particles become polarized by this uneven distribution.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should explain that electrons are always moving, so at any instant a molecule may have more electron density on one side. This temporary asymmetry can polarize a neighboring molecule, producing a weak, short-lived attraction.
**Concept category:** surface features / induced polarization
**Population:** 60 freshmen prospective elementary teachers in an introductory chemistry course
**Source:** TEACHING INTERMOLECULAR FORCES WITH  LOVE ANALOGY: A CASE STUDY (KHIMIYA_21_3_KARAKAS/KHIMIYA_21_3_KARAKAS.md)
**Evidence:** "Electron density in a molecule may be redistributed by proximity to another multipole."

## Misconception 304: Students may think London dispersion forces are complicated but comparable to human relationship drama, rather than recognizing them as weak and transient electrostatic attractions.

**Topic:** London dispersion force / Strength and persistence of the force
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces are weak intermolecular attractions because the induced dipoles are small and the attractions are very short-lived.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should connect the temporary nature of the induced dipole to the weakness of the force: the attraction appears only briefly and disappears as electron density shifts again.
**Concept category:** force strength
**Population:** 60 freshmen prospective elementary teachers in an introductory chemistry course
**Source:** TEACHING INTERMOLECULAR FORCES WITH  LOVE ANALOGY: A CASE STUDY (KHIMIYA_21_3_KARAKAS/KHIMIYA_21_3_KARAKAS.md)
**Evidence:** "London dispersion forces are typically very weak because the attractions are so quickly broken"

## Misconception 305: Students may think a dipole-dipole interaction is caused by a 'permanent problem in the relationship' or by one molecule being personally attracted to another, rather than by unequal electron sharing from electronegativity differences.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole interactions / Cause of permanent dipoles
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole interactions occur between molecules with permanent dipoles created by differences in electronegativity that produce partial positive and partial negative ends.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should reason that a polar covalent bond forms when one atom pulls bonding electrons more strongly, creating a δ- and δ+ end; opposite partial charges on neighboring molecules then attract each other.
**Concept category:** permanent dipoles
**Population:** 60 freshmen prospective elementary teachers in an introductory chemistry course
**Source:** TEACHING INTERMOLECULAR FORCES WITH  LOVE ANALOGY: A CASE STUDY (KHIMIYA_21_3_KARAKAS/KHIMIYA_21_3_KARAKAS.md)
**Evidence:** "dipole-dipole interactions and explained that they are caused by permanent dipoles in molecules"

## Misconception 306: Students may think the more electronegative atom simply 'draws' the other partner closer like a more attractive person, instead of understanding that electronegativity differences polarize a bond by pulling shared electrons unevenly.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole interactions / Role of electronegativity
**Correct concept:** A more electronegative atom attracts the shared bonding electrons more strongly, creating partial charges across the bond and allowing electrostatic attraction between molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should identify which atom is more electronegative, infer that bonding electrons are displaced toward it, and then conclude that the molecule has a permanent dipole that can align with other dipoles.
**Concept category:** electronegativity / polarity
**Population:** 60 freshmen prospective elementary teachers in an introductory chemistry course
**Source:** TEACHING INTERMOLECULAR FORCES WITH  LOVE ANALOGY: A CASE STUDY (KHIMIYA_21_3_KARAKAS/KHIMIYA_21_3_KARAKAS.md)
**Evidence:** "the electronegative atom draws the electrons in the bond nearer to itself"

## Misconception 307: Students may think hydrogen bonding is a special 'cheating' or relationship-based attraction between people, rather than a specific strong dipole-dipole interaction involving H bonded to F, O, or N and a lone pair on a neighboring molecule.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Nature of the interaction
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is an especially strong form of dipole-dipole interaction that occurs when hydrogen is covalently bonded to F, O, or N and is attracted to a lone pair on F, O, or N in a nearby molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should check the structure: H must be attached to F, O, or N, creating a highly polar bond; then an intermolecular attraction forms between the Hδ+ and a lone pair on F/O/N of another molecule.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Population:** 60 freshmen prospective elementary teachers in an introductory chemistry course
**Source:** TEACHING INTERMOLECULAR FORCES WITH  LOVE ANALOGY: A CASE STUDY (KHIMIYA_21_3_KARAKAS/KHIMIYA_21_3_KARAKAS.md)
**Evidence:** "hydrogen bonds are form of dipole-dipole interactions"

## Misconception 308: Students may think hydrogen bonding can occur with any strongly desired partner, rather than only with highly electronegative atoms F, O, and N and hydrogen attached to them.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Atoms involved in hydrogen bonding
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding requires hydrogen bonded to fluorine, oxygen, or nitrogen, and attraction to a lone pair on F, O, or N of a neighboring molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should recognize the narrow set of atoms capable of hydrogen bonding because of their high electronegativity and small size, which create very strong bond polarity and strong intermolecular attraction.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding / specific atoms
**Population:** 60 freshmen prospective elementary teachers in an introductory chemistry course
**Source:** TEACHING INTERMOLECULAR FORCES WITH  LOVE ANALOGY: A CASE STUDY (KHIMIYA_21_3_KARAKAS/KHIMIYA_21_3_KARAKAS.md)
**Evidence:** "They occur between hydrogen ... and oxygen ... fluorine ... or nitrogen"

## Misconception 309: Students do not clearly distinguish or correctly conceptualize intermolecular forces at the submicroscopic level, leading to scientifically flawed explanations of IMF strength.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / general conceptual understanding
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces are attractive interactions between particles that can be explained using molecular structure and polarity, and they must be interpreted at the particle level rather than as macroscopic properties.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the type of intermolecular interaction present from the molecular features involved, then justify relative strength using particle-level reasoning rather than relying on everyday appearances or vague descriptions.
**Concept category:** conceptual understanding
**Prevalence:** 15.09% provided scientifically flawed justifications
**Population:** 53 first-year undergraduate chemistry majors at a teacher training university in Ghana
**Source:** Representing the Invisible: A Study of Students' Visual and Conceptual Conceptions of Intermolecular Forces (REPRES~1/REPRES~1.md)
**Evidence:** students often struggle to conceptualize and depict IMFs because of challenges with abstract thinking and visualizing submicroscopic interactions

## Misconception 310: Students produce inaccurate drawings of hydrogen bonding interactions.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / visual representation of hydrogen bonding
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding should be represented as a specific attractive interaction between a hydrogen atom bonded to N, O, or F and a lone pair on another electronegative atom in a neighboring molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should draw the molecules with correct partial charges and indicate the hydrogen bond between the appropriate atoms, showing the interaction between molecules rather than within a molecule.
**Concept category:** visual representation
**Prevalence:** 47.2% accurate drawings for hydrogen bonding; therefore 52.8% inaccurate/other responses
**Population:** 53 first-year undergraduate chemistry majors at a teacher training university in Ghana
**Source:** Representing the Invisible: A Study of Students' Visual and Conceptual Conceptions of Intermolecular Forces (REPRES~1/REPRES~1.md)
**Evidence:** fewer produced accurate drawings (47.2%, 41.5%, and 28.3% respectively)

## Misconception 311: Students produce inaccurate drawings of dipole-dipole interactions.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / visual representation of dipole-dipole interactions
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole interactions should be depicted as attractions between the partial positive end of one polar molecule and the partial negative end of another polar molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should first identify the molecular dipoles, then show the correct orientation of neighboring molecules so opposite partial charges attract.
**Concept category:** visual representation
**Prevalence:** 41.5% accurate drawings for dipole-dipole interactions; therefore 58.5% inaccurate/other responses
**Population:** 53 first-year undergraduate chemistry majors at a teacher training university in Ghana
**Source:** Representing the Invisible: A Study of Students' Visual and Conceptual Conceptions of Intermolecular Forces (REPRES~1/REPRES~1.md)
**Evidence:** fewer produced accurate drawings (47.2%, 41.5%, and 28.3% respectively)

## Misconception 312: Students produce inaccurate drawings of London dispersion forces.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / visual representation of London dispersion forces
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces should be represented as temporary induced dipole interactions arising from momentary electron density fluctuations between particles.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should show brief, induced dipoles on neighboring atoms or molecules and indicate that the attraction is temporary and present in all particles, especially more polarizable ones.
**Concept category:** visual representation
**Prevalence:** 28.3% accurate drawings for London dispersion forces; therefore 71.7% inaccurate/other responses
**Population:** 53 first-year undergraduate chemistry majors at a teacher training university in Ghana
**Source:** Representing the Invisible: A Study of Students' Visual and Conceptual Conceptions of Intermolecular Forces (REPRES~1/REPRES~1.md)
**Evidence:** fewer produced accurate drawings (47.2%, 41.5%, and 28.3% respectively)

## Misconception 313: Students think intermolecular forces occur only within a single molecule, for example only between atoms inside H2O rather than between separate water molecules.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Where intermolecular forces occur
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces act between molecules or particles, not within a single molecule's covalent bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish intramolecular bonding from intermolecular attraction: the H–O bond holds atoms together inside one water molecule, while IMFs are attractions between different H2O molecules that help explain phase behavior.
**Concept category:** location of intermolecular forces
**Population:** students (as reported from teacher interviews and cited prior studies)
**Source:** An analyses of multiple representation about intermolecular forces (Rantih_2019_J._Phys.%3A_Conf._Ser._1157_042029/Rantih_2019_J._Phys.%3A_Conf._Ser._1157_042029.md)
**Evidence:** "students' misconception about IMFs occur in molecules"

## Misconception 314: Students believe intermolecular forces are stronger than chemical bonds.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Strength relative to chemical bonds
**Correct concept:** Chemical bonds are stronger than intermolecular forces.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the energy needed to break a covalent bond with the energy needed to overcome intermolecular attractions; breaking H–O bonds requires more energy than evaporating water, showing bonds are stronger than IMFs.
**Concept category:** relative strength
**Population:** students (cited from prior studies)
**Source:** An analyses of multiple representation about intermolecular forces (Rantih_2019_J._Phys.%3A_Conf._Ser._1157_042029/Rantih_2019_J._Phys.%3A_Conf._Ser._1157_042029.md)
