**Evidence:** "IMFs are stronger than chemical bonds"

## Misconception 315: Students think intermolecular forces cause chemical reactions.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Function in chemical reactions
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces do not cause chemical reactions; reactions involve breaking and forming chemical bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that IMFs influence physical properties like boiling point or solubility, whereas chemical reactions require changes in bonding and composition.
**Concept category:** role in reactions versus physical properties
**Population:** students (cited from prior studies)
**Source:** An analyses of multiple representation about intermolecular forces (Rantih_2019_J._Phys.%3A_Conf._Ser._1157_042029/Rantih_2019_J._Phys.%3A_Conf._Ser._1157_042029.md)
**Evidence:** "IMFs is causes a chemical reaction"

## Misconception 316: Students may think ion-dipole forces are not part of intermolecular forces because IMFs are only between molecules, not ions.

**Topic:** Ion-dipole forces / Which species are involved
**Correct concept:** Ion-dipole interactions are intermolecular attractions between ions and polar molecules and can be included when discussing IMFs depending on the definition used.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that a charged ion can attract the partial charges on a polar molecule, so Na+ or Cl- interacting with H2O is an ion-dipole interaction.
**Concept category:** definition boundary of intermolecular forces
**Population:** textbook frameworks discussed for students
**Source:** An analyses of multiple representation about intermolecular forces (Rantih_2019_J._Phys.%3A_Conf._Ser._1157_042029/Rantih_2019_J._Phys.%3A_Conf._Ser._1157_042029.md)
**Evidence:** "some books assume that the concept of IMFs is a force between molecules not ions so that the concept of iondipole force, ions-induced dipole forces not include the IMFs."

## Misconception 317: Students may focus only on Na+ and omit the role of Cl- when imagining ion-dipole interactions in salt water.

**Topic:** Ion-dipole forces / Ion hydration representation
**Correct concept:** Both cations and anions interact with water molecules; cations orient water's negative end toward them, while anions orient water's positive end toward them.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should apply electrostatic attraction to both ions in NaCl: Na+ attracts the oxygen end of water and Cl- attracts the hydrogen end of water.
**Concept category:** ion-dipole orientation
**Population:** students as implied by the representation analysis
**Source:** An analyses of multiple representation about intermolecular forces (Rantih_2019_J._Phys.%3A_Conf._Ser._1157_042029/Rantih_2019_J._Phys.%3A_Conf._Ser._1157_042029.md)
**Evidence:** "the symbolic representation shown in textbooks I and II only describes the Na+ ions but not the Cl ions."

## Misconception 318: Students cannot explain paper chromatography in terms of intermolecular forces and instead give explanations that do not use intermolecular attractions between the solute, mobile phase, and stationary phase.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and chromatography / Paper chromatography
**Correct concept:** Paper chromatography should be explained by differences in intermolecular attractions between the components and the mobile and stationary phases.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should relate how strongly each substance interacts with the stationary phase versus the mobile phase; substances that are more strongly attracted to the mobile phase travel farther, while those more strongly attracted to the stationary phase move less.
**Concept category:** chromatography / intermolecular forces
**Population:** Students interviewed for the study and students taking the MACI; the MACI was administered to 407 high school students and 253 general chemistry students across the United States.
**Source:** Students' misconceptions about intermolecular forces as investigated through paper chromatography experiments and the Molecular Attractions Concept Inventory (STUDEN~3/STUDEN~3.md)
**Evidence:** “students had diWculty in explaining paper chromatography experiments by means of intermolecular forces”

## Misconception 319: Students hold misconceptions about intermolecular forces in general, as shown by multiple incorrect ideas on the Molecular Attractions Concept Inventory.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / General intermolecular attractions
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces are attractions between molecules that help explain properties such as boiling point and separation behavior.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use particle-level reasoning: identify the molecules present, determine the relevant intermolecular attractions, and connect the strength of those attractions to observable outcomes such as boiling point or chromatographic movement.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces
**Population:** 407 high school students and 253 general chemistry students across the United States.
**Source:** Students' misconceptions about intermolecular forces as investigated through paper chromatography experiments and the Molecular Attractions Concept Inventory (STUDEN~3/STUDEN~3.md)
**Evidence:** “Twenty-two misconceptions were found from the administration of MACI, consisting of nine misconceptions related to chromatography and thirteen misconceptions related to intermolecular forces.”

## Misconception 320: Students think the attractive bonds between cellulose fibers are van der Waals bonds or dipole-dipole bonds rather than hydrogen bonds.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Hydrogen bonding vs van der Waals / dispersion forces in cellulose
**Correct concept:** The fibers are held together by hydrogen bonds (with covalent/polar covalent bonds within each cellulose chain), not by van der Waals or dipole-dipole bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should identify the intermolecular attraction shown between cellulose fibers, distinguish it from the intramolecular bonds within each fiber, and recognize that hydrogen bonding is the relevant force between fibers in the teacher's representation.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces / bond classification
**Prevalence:** At least 2 students in the discussion
**Population:** Students in one observed upper-secondary chemistry class
**Source:** Qualitatively different ways of unpacking visual representations when teaching intermolecular forces in upper secondary school (Science Education - 2021 - Patron - Qualitatively different ways of unpacking visual representations when teaching/Science Education - 2021 - Patron - Qualitatively different ways of unpacking visual representations when teaching.md)
**Evidence:** Student 1: "Dipole–dipole bond…" / Student 2: "van der Waals…" / Teacher: "Hydrogen bonds, hydrogen bonds between there."

## Misconception 321: Students do not know that dipoles must be oriented with opposite partial charges facing each other for attraction; they initially orient the dipole the wrong way round.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Direction/orientation of dipole-dipole interaction
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole attraction arises when the positive end of one dipole is near the negative end of another dipole.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should use the partial charges on each dipole to infer the attractive orientation: opposite ends attract, so the plus side of one dipole should face the minus side of the other.
**Concept category:** dipole orientation / electron distribution
**Prevalence:** Many students reacted
**Population:** Students in one observed upper-secondary chemistry class
**Source:** Qualitatively different ways of unpacking visual representations when teaching intermolecular forces in upper secondary school (Science Education - 2021 - Patron - Qualitatively different ways of unpacking visual representations when teaching/Science Education - 2021 - Patron - Qualitatively different ways of unpacking visual representations when teaching.md)
**Evidence:** "many of the students reacted and pointed out that the eraser should be turned around, so that the side with the plus drawn on it was closest to the drawn dipole's minus-side."

## Misconception 322: Students may not understand what the visual elements in the dispersion-force animation represent, including that the objects are uncharged overall and only temporarily polarized.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Recognizing dispersion forces in an animation
**Correct concept:** Dispersion forces arise from temporary, momentary polarization in otherwise uncharged molecules; the animation's cloudlike objects and plus/minus signs represent this transient polarization and resulting attraction.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should connect the visual symbols in the animation to temporary electron redistribution in uncharged particles and infer that the attraction is caused by a fleeting induced dipole, not a permanent charge.
**Concept category:** dispersion forces / temporary polarity
**Population:** Students viewing the dispersion-force animation in the observed lesson
**Source:** Qualitatively different ways of unpacking visual representations when teaching intermolecular forces in upper secondary school (Science Education - 2021 - Patron - Qualitatively different ways of unpacking visual representations when teaching/Science Education - 2021 - Patron - Qualitatively different ways of unpacking visual representations when teaching.md)
**Evidence:** "the animation shows dispersion forces, that the substances are uncharged, but it is plus-minus charged during a fraction of a second"

## Misconception 323: Students may not recognize that different visual representations can depict the same phenomenon, and may fail to relate the teacher-drawn dipole-dipole diagram to the animation's representation.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Distinguishing representations of the same concept
**Correct concept:** Different representations can depict the same intermolecular phenomenon using different visual modes and conventions.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should compare the modes, identify the same underlying concept across representations, and translate the meaning from one representation to another rather than treating them as unrelated pictures.
**Concept category:** representational competence / transduction
**Population:** Students in one observed upper-secondary chemistry class
**Source:** Qualitatively different ways of unpacking visual representations when teaching intermolecular forces in upper secondary school (Science Education - 2021 - Patron - Qualitatively different ways of unpacking visual representations when teaching/Science Education - 2021 - Patron - Qualitatively different ways of unpacking visual representations when teaching.md)
**Evidence:** "The different ways in which hydrogen bonding between water molecules were illustrated in the movie and by the teacher, respectively, is shown in Figure 7."

## Misconception 324: Students believe they can make correct meaning from a chemistry visual representation without teacher unpacking or support.

**Topic:** Visual representations in intermolecular forces / Assuming representations speak for themselves
**Correct concept:** Chemical visual representations often require explicit interpretative, inferential, or explanatory unpacking to make critical aspects discernible.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should identify which features are shown, infer what is appresent or implicit, and connect the representation to the intended chemical concept with teacher guidance when needed.
**Concept category:** interpretive unpacking / representational competence
**Population:** Upper secondary school chemistry students interviewed in three schools (24 students total; 12 pairs).
**Source:** Students' Experiences of Teachers' Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the Context of Intermolecular Forces (Science Education - 2026 - Patron - Students  Experiences of Teachers  Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the/Science Education - 2026 - Patron - Students  Experiences of Teachers  Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the.md)
**Evidence:** "assuming that a representation 'speaks for itself'"

## Misconception 325: Students think a new visual representation of the same chemical idea will automatically be connected to earlier representations and thus be understandable without explicit explanation.

**Topic:** Visual representations in intermolecular forces / Interpreting unfamiliar representations
**Correct concept:** Students need explicit links between representations to recognize that different visual forms can represent the same chemical phenomenon.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should compare the new representation with previous ones, look for common critical aspects, and use teacher explanation to map correspondences across representations.
**Concept category:** transformation / representational linkage
**Population:** Interviewed upper secondary chemistry students; reported by Sam and Jonny.
**Source:** Students' Experiences of Teachers' Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the Context of Intermolecular Forces (Science Education - 2026 - Patron - Students  Experiences of Teachers  Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the/Science Education - 2026 - Patron - Students  Experiences of Teachers  Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the.md)
**Evidence:** "you don't connect them to the same things as you usually do. Then it becomes a bit strange."

## Misconception 326: Students believe they should be able to determine a molecule's structure directly from a molecular formula without unpacking.

**Topic:** Symbolic to submicroscopic reasoning / Reading molecular structure from formula
**Correct concept:** A molecular formula does not on its own show the molecule's structure; students need additional representation or explanation to infer connectivity and spatial arrangement.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should recognize that a formula gives composition, then use structural models or teacher explanation to infer how atoms are connected and arranged.
**Concept category:** symbolic representation / submicroscopic structure
**Population:** Upper secondary chemistry students interviewed; exemplified by Moa and Ella.
**Source:** Students' Experiences of Teachers' Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the Context of Intermolecular Forces (Science Education - 2026 - Patron - Students  Experiences of Teachers  Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the/Science Education - 2026 - Patron - Students  Experiences of Teachers  Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the.md)
**Evidence:** "a molecule's structure when only given its molecular formula (in which the structure is an appresent feature)"

## Misconception 327: Students cannot infer that hydrogen bonding is impossible unless the hydrogen atom is directly bonded to fluorine, and they need the teacher to tell them this explicitly.

**Topic:** Intermolecular hydrogen bonding / Identifying hydrogen-bond donors from a structural representation
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding requires a hydrogen atom bonded to a highly electronegative atom such as F, O, or N; if H is bonded to carbon instead, hydrogen bonding of that type does not occur.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should inspect the structure, identify which atom H is attached to, and apply the rule for hydrogen-bond donor requirements to decide whether hydrogen bonding is possible.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces / hydrogen bonding
**Population:** Upper secondary chemistry students in a lesson on hydrogen bonding; reported in the interview with Loke and others.
**Source:** Students' Experiences of Teachers' Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the Context of Intermolecular Forces (Science Education - 2026 - Patron - Students  Experiences of Teachers  Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the/Science Education - 2026 - Patron - Students  Experiences of Teachers  Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the.md)
**Evidence:** "you wouldn't understand anything. But when he [verbally] explained, it was very clear."

## Misconception 328: Students think they cannot explain a precipitate or other laboratory observation unless the teacher explicitly connects the experiment to particle-level causes.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Connecting macroscopic observations to submicroscopic explanations
**Correct concept:** Macroscopic observations should be explained by linking them to symbolic/submicroscopic representations of the particles and interactions involved.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should observe the experimental outcome, then use the underlying particle model to reason about why the observed macroscopic change occurs.
**Concept category:** macro–submicro coordination / explanatory unpacking
**Population:** Upper secondary chemistry students interviewed about a hydrogen-bonding lesson; reported by Ella.
**Source:** Students' Experiences of Teachers' Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the Context of Intermolecular Forces (Science Education - 2026 - Patron - Students  Experiences of Teachers  Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the/Science Education - 2026 - Patron - Students  Experiences of Teachers  Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the.md)
**Evidence:** "I do not know why a precipitate is formed."

## Misconception 329: Students may initially think the shared electrons are equally distributed between the bonded atoms in a polar covalent bond, and need the teacher to show the bond line moved toward chlorine to correct this.

**Topic:** Polar covalent bonding / Electron distribution in a polar bond
**Correct concept:** In a polar covalent bond, the shared electrons are unevenly distributed toward the more electronegative atom.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should compare electronegativities, infer unequal electron sharing, and represent the bond polarity by shifting electron density toward the atom that attracts electrons more strongly.
**Concept category:** electron distribution / polarity
**Population:** Upper secondary chemistry students interviewed during discussion of polar covalent bonding; exemplified by Emmy and Linnea.
**Source:** Students' Experiences of Teachers' Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the Context of Intermolecular Forces (Science Education - 2026 - Patron - Students  Experiences of Teachers  Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the/Science Education - 2026 - Patron - Students  Experiences of Teachers  Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the.md)
**Evidence:** "the electrons were unequally shared between the two atoms"

## Misconception 330: Students think an underspecified atomic drawing is hard to interpret because it lacks critical features such as electrons and other structural details.

**Topic:** Atomic structure representations / Need for more explicit visual detail
**Correct concept:** A representation should include the critical features needed to make the atomic structure discernible and meaningful to novices.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should look for which structural features are present, compare with a more explicit drawing, and use the added features to infer the intended atomic structure.
**Concept category:** surface features / structural representation
**Population:** Upper secondary chemistry students interviewed; reported by Anna.
**Source:** Students' Experiences of Teachers' Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the Context of Intermolecular Forces (Science Education - 2026 - Patron - Students  Experiences of Teachers  Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the/Science Education - 2026 - Patron - Students  Experiences of Teachers  Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the.md)
**Evidence:** "you can do it, like, that you draw an atom, roughly how it's structured, but then you can make a drawing next to it, to make it even clearer, with electrons and stuff"

## Misconception 331: Students believe a spoken explanation alone is enough to learn from a visual representation even when they later cannot retain or revisit the meaning.

**Topic:** Multiple representations and pedagogy / Overloading speech without persistent support
**Correct concept:** Persistent visual support in addition to speech helps students revisit meanings and stabilize understanding over time.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should use the spoken explanation to interpret the image, then rely on an additional stable representation or notes to consolidate and revisit the meaning later.
**Concept category:** verbal explanation / persistence of representation
**Population:** Upper secondary chemistry students interviewed; reported by Anna.
**Source:** Students' Experiences of Teachers' Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the Context of Intermolecular Forces (Science Education - 2026 - Patron - Students  Experiences of Teachers  Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the/Science Education - 2026 - Patron - Students  Experiences of Teachers  Ways of Unpacking Visual Representations in the.md)
**Evidence:** "when you have to look back [at the representations], then it's like, you forget a little bit."

## Misconception 332: Students think electrons are stationary.

**Topic:** Structure of matter / Atomic model
**Correct concept:** Electrons are not stationary; atomic models should represent electrons as moving in orbitals/electron clouds rather than fixed in place.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that atomic structure involves electrons in motion, and that a scientifically acceptable model must account for electron behavior as dynamic rather than fixed.
**Concept category:** atomic model
**Population:** Students interviewed in the study
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** Student believes electrons are stationary

## Misconception 333: Students adhere to a solar system/Bohr model for electron orbitals.

**Topic:** Structure of matter / Atomic model
**Correct concept:** The atom is not accurately represented by a simple planetary/solar-system model; electron behavior is better described by a quantum model/electron cloud.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish between a historical planetary model and a modern quantum model, recognizing that electrons are not on fixed circular orbits around the nucleus.
**Concept category:** atomic model
**Population:** Students interviewed in the study
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** Student adheres to a solar system model for electron orbitals.; (Bohr model)

## Misconception 334: Students believe the solar system model is wrong but replace it with an unspecified 'cloud' idea without a probabilistic understanding.

**Topic:** Structure of matter / Atomic model
**Correct concept:** The modern atomic model uses electron clouds/probability to represent electron locations and behavior.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain that electrons occupy regions of probability rather than fixed paths, and that the cloud model is grounded in electron motion and probability.
**Concept category:** atomic model
**Population:** Students interviewed in the study
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** Student understands that solar system model is wrong; uses some kind of "cloud" explanation

## Misconception 335: Students treat the atom as a simple sphere and do not know that atoms contain electrons.

**Topic:** Structure of matter / Atomic model
**Correct concept:** Atoms have internal structure, including electrons, protons, and neutrons; a scientifically correct atomic model must include this composition.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect symbolic and visual representations of atoms to their internal subatomic particles and explain what those particles do and where they are located.
**Concept category:** atomic model
**Prevalence:** 1 student case described (student 0089)
**Population:** High school chemistry student 0089
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** student 0089 was unaware that atoms contain electrons. In his model, an atom is just a sphere

## Misconception 336: Students believe atoms on the edge of metal are different from atoms in the bulk.

**Topic:** Structure of matter / Particle structure of solids
**Correct concept:** Atoms in the edge and bulk of a solid are the same type of particles; the smoothness of a cut metal edge is not because edge atoms are fundamentally different in shape.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that surface appearance is a macroscopic effect and does not require different kinds of atoms at the edge versus the interior.
**Concept category:** surface features
**Population:** Students interviewed in the study
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** Student believes that atoms on the edge of metal are different than those in the bulk.

## Misconception 337: Students think a smooth edge is smooth because particles are too small to feel.

**Topic:** Structure of matter / Particle structure of solids
**Correct concept:** The smooth edge of a solid results from the arrangement and truncation of particles at the surface, not from particles being too small to feel.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason from the particle arrangement at the cut surface and recognize that the macroscopic smoothness comes from the physical structure, not from sensory limitations alone.
**Concept category:** surface features
**Population:** Students interviewed in the study
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** Student believes that the edge is smooth because the particles are too small to feel.

## Misconception 338: Students rely only on extensive properties to identify substances.

**Topic:** Properties of matter / Substance identity and properties
**Correct concept:** Substances should be identified using intensive properties and, at a deeper level, by linking bulk properties to atomic/molecular structure.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish extensive from intensive properties and explain that intensive properties are characteristic of a substance because they do not depend on sample amount.
**Concept category:** properties of matter
**Population:** Students interviewed in the study
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** Relies only on extensive properties

## Misconception 339: Students mix extensive and intensive properties but do not understand the difference between them.

**Topic:** Properties of matter / Substance identity and properties
**Correct concept:** Extensive properties depend on amount; intensive properties do not and are more useful for identifying a substance.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should classify properties by whether they vary with amount and use that classification to decide which properties can identify a substance.
**Concept category:** properties of matter
**Population:** Students interviewed in the study
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** Extensive + intensive properties, but does not specify any meaning to the difference

## Misconception 340: Students can name intensive properties but do not understand why they are valuable for characterizing a substance.

**Topic:** Properties of matter / Substance identity and properties
**Correct concept:** Intensive properties are characteristic because they remain the same regardless of sample size and thus help identify substances.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain that intensive properties are diagnostic of substance identity precisely because they do not change with amount.
**Concept category:** properties of matter
**Population:** Students interviewed in the study
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** Extensive + intensive properties; understands the value of intensive properties

## Misconception 341: Students do not separate bulk properties from atomic/molecular properties.

**Topic:** Properties of matter / Substance identity and properties
**Correct concept:** Bulk observable properties should be distinguished from atomic/molecular-level properties, and both levels can be used to explain substance identity and behavior.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect macroscopic properties to microscopic structure and explain which properties are observed directly and which emerge from particle-level structure.
**Concept category:** properties of matter
**Population:** Students interviewed in the study
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** Separates the bulk properties (intensive + extensive) from the atomic/molecular properties

## Misconception 342: Students do not recognize that intensive properties likely change with changes at the atomic/molecular level.

**Topic:** Properties of matter / Substance identity and properties
**Correct concept:** Changes at the atomic or molecular level can alter intensive properties because those properties arise from microscopic structure and interactions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that if composition or structure changes, the characteristic properties of a substance can change as well, even if sample size does not.
**Concept category:** properties of matter
**Population:** Students interviewed in the study
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** Intensive properties likely change at the

## Misconception 343: Students do not know what forces keep atoms together in a solid.

**Topic:** Forces and interactions / Interparticle forces
**Correct concept:** Particles in solids are held together by interparticle/electrical forces that act at the atomic and molecular scale.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect solid structure to attractive forces between particles and explain that these forces maintain the solid arrangement.
**Concept category:** interparticle forces
**Population:** Students interviewed in the study
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** students cannot explain the forces that keep the atoms together in a solid (inter-particle forces).

## Misconception 344: Students do not spontaneously think that the arrangement of atoms gives a substance its properties.

**Topic:** Forces and interactions / Substance properties from arrangement
**Correct concept:** The arrangement of atoms/molecules is a key determinant of a substance's properties and identity.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that different arrangements of the same particles can produce different properties and even different substances.
**Concept category:** structure-property relationships
**Population:** Students interviewed in the study
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** Nor do they volunteer that the arrangement of atoms gives a substance its properties

## Misconception 345: Students often believe bonding can only be intramolecular.

**Topic:** Forces and interactions / Intermolecular forces
**Correct concept:** Electrical interactions also occur between molecules and particles; bonding/attraction is not limited to intramolecular bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize both intramolecular bonding and intermolecular attractions, and use the appropriate type of force to explain a phenomenon.
**Concept category:** bonding vs forces
**Population:** Students in the literature cited by the paper; used to interpret the interview findings
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** students often believe that bonding can only be intramolecular

## Misconception 346: Students rely only on the octet model to explain inter-atomic interactions.

**Topic:** Forces and interactions / Intermolecular forces
**Correct concept:** Interatomic and intermolecular interactions are governed by electrical forces and cannot be reduced only to octet-rule reasoning.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain bonding and interactions using electron distribution, electronegativity, and electrical forces rather than only octet completion.
**Concept category:** bonding vs forces
**Population:** Students in the literature cited by the paper; used to interpret the interview findings
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** Often, they rely only the octet model to explain inter-atomic interactions

## Misconception 347: Students define bonding in terms of atoms 'needing' or 'wanting' to give away electrons.

**Topic:** Forces and interactions / Chemical bonding language
**Correct concept:** Bonding should be explained in terms of electron interactions and electrical forces, not anthropomorphic 'need' or 'want' language.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use particle-level and electrostatic reasoning to explain why atoms form bonds rather than attributing intent to atoms.
**Concept category:** bonding concepts
**Population:** Students in the literature cited by the paper; used to interpret the interview findings
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** students learn to apply the octet model and begin to define bonding in terms of atoms *needing* or *wanting to give away* electrons

## Misconception 348: Students think grinding granulated sugar into powdered sugar changes the substance at the molecular level.

**Topic:** Forces and interactions / Substance identity and physical vs chemical change
**Correct concept:** Grinding sugar is a physical change; the substance remains sugar even though particle size and surface behavior change.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish changes in size or form from changes in composition and reason that no new substance is formed by grinding.
**Concept category:** physical vs chemical change
**Prevalence:** 4 of 6 high school chemistry students in the described subgroup
**Population:** Six high school students who had completed a year of chemistry
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** four of the six believed that grinding the granulated sugar to make powdered sugar resulted in a change on the molecular level.

## Misconception 349: Students think powdered and granulated sugar are different substances, or they are not the same substance.

**Topic:** Forces and interactions / Substance identity and physical vs chemical change
**Correct concept:** Powdered and granulated sugar are the same substance, differing mainly in particle size and resulting behavior, not composition.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use composition and structure to decide substance identity, recognizing that different forms of the same sugar can behave differently without becoming different substances.
**Concept category:** substance identity
**Prevalence:** 6 of 6 high school chemistry students in the described subgroup
**Population:** Six high school students who had completed a year of chemistry
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** These six students in this study believed that granulated and powdered sugar are the same substance.

## Misconception 350: Some students think powdered and granulated sugar are composed of different molecules.

**Topic:** Forces and interactions / Substance identity and physical vs chemical change
**Correct concept:** Different particle-size forms of sugar are built from the same molecules; particle size change does not change molecular identity.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that if a change is physical, the molecules remain the same even though their arrangement or size distribution changes.
**Concept category:** substance identity
**Prevalence:** some of the 6 students in the subgroup
**Population:** Six high school students who had completed a year of chemistry
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** Some even went as far to say that the two sugars were composed of the same molecules.

## Misconception 351: Students think powdered sugar contains more particles because it is powder rather than crystal.

**Topic:** Forces and interactions / Intermolecular structure of sugar
**Correct concept:** Powdered and granulated sugar samples contain the same substance; differences in sticking or packing are due to particle size, surface area, and intermolecular/electrostatic effects, not more particles of a different kind.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason from structure and surface interactions rather than assuming one sample has more particles or a different molecular makeup.
**Concept category:** particle model
**Prevalence:** quoted from one student example; four of six showed the broader misconception about molecular-level change
**Population:** High school chemistry student (quoted example)
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** I'd think the powdered, I think there'd be a lot more particles in the powder than there would be in the crystal of the sugar.

## Misconception 352: Students think the make-up is different on a molecular level and that there is less space between the particles in powdered sugar.

**Topic:** Forces and interactions / Intermolecular structure of sugar
**Correct concept:** Differences in texture or flow do not mean the molecular makeup is different; sugar remains the same substance, and observed behavior can be explained by particle size and surface interactions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should separate macroscopic behavior from molecular identity and explain behavior using arrangement, size, and forces rather than a different molecular make-up.
**Concept category:** particle model
**Prevalence:** quoted from one student example
**Population:** High school physics student with one year of high school chemistry
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** I like guess the, uh, make up is kinda' different, like on a molecular level…there's not as much space between them.

## Misconception 353: Students think the intermolecular structure of crystallized sugar is stronger than powdered sugar's structure.

**Topic:** Forces and interactions / Intermolecular structure of sugar
**Correct concept:** Strength of attraction should be explained in terms of particle interactions and structure, not by assuming the powdered form has a fundamentally weaker or different intermolecular structure.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect the different behaviors of powdered and granulated sugar to surface area and intermolecular interactions, not infer a different substance structure.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces
**Prevalence:** quoted from one student example
**Population:** High school chemistry student
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** …the intermolecular structure isn't as strong …as crystallized sugar.

## Misconception 354: Students do not realize that atoms contain electrons.

**Topic:** Forces and interactions / Atomic composition and symbols
**Correct concept:** Atoms are composed of subatomic particles, including electrons, protons, and neutrons.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should interpret atomic diagrams and descriptions as having internal substructure, including electrons outside the nucleus.
**Concept category:** atomic structure
**Prevalence:** 1 student case described (student 0089)
**Population:** High performing high school chemistry student
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** student 0089 was unaware that atoms contain electrons.

## Misconception 355: Students think atoms are just spheres and cannot explain their composition.

**Topic:** Forces and interactions / Atomic composition and symbols
**Correct concept:** Atoms are not merely solid spheres; they have internal composition and structure.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should describe the nucleus and electrons rather than treating the atom as an undifferentiated ball.
**Concept category:** atomic model
**Prevalence:** 1 student case described (student 0089)
**Population:** High performing high school chemistry student
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** In his model, an atom is just a sphere

## Misconception 356: Students think the color of atomic spheres is important.

**Topic:** Forces and interactions / Atomic composition and symbols
**Correct concept:** The color in a textbook model is generally representational and not a property of actual atoms.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish features of a model from real properties of atoms and recognize which aspects are symbolic conventions.
**Concept category:** model interpretation
**Prevalence:** 1 student case described (student 0089)
**Population:** High performing high school chemistry student
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** He believed that the color of the spheres was important.

## Misconception 357: Students think symbols like Na and Cl mean 'elements' only, while atoms are a totally separate entity.

**Topic:** Forces and interactions / Atomic composition and symbols
**Correct concept:** Chemical symbols represent elements whose atoms participate in bonding; atoms are the entities that make up elements and compounds.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect element symbols to atoms of those elements and use that link to explain how substances are formed.
**Concept category:** chemical symbols and elements
**Prevalence:** 1 student case described (student 0089)
**Population:** High performing high school chemistry student
**Source:** Using Learning Progressions to Inform Curriculum, Instruction and Assessment Design (Shawn_etal_NARST_07/Shawn_etal_NARST_07.md)
**Evidence:** In his model, elements make up molecules and atoms are a totally separate entity.

## Misconception 358: Students think interactions between oppositely charged species are ionic bonds rather than ion-dipole interactions.

**Topic:** intermolecular forces / ion-dipole interaction
**Correct concept:** Ion-dipole interactions are intermolecular attractions between an ion and the partial charges of a polar molecule; ionic bonds are intramolecular electrostatic attractions in an ionic lattice between full charges.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify whether the species involved are a charged ion and a polar molecule. If so, the attraction is an intermolecular ion-dipole force, not an ionic bond, because no new ionic lattice is formed and the attraction is between unlike charges across separate particles.
**Concept category:** ion-dipole interaction
**Prevalence:** 26% of the teacher trainees had alternative conceptions on Ion–dipole interaction
**Population:** 82 firstyear chemistry major teacher trainees at the University of Education, Winneba
**Source:** Teacher trainees' alternative conceptions about intermolecular forces (Teacher trainees' alternative conceptions about intermolecular forces _ University of Education, Winneba/Teacher trainees' alternative conceptions about intermolecular forces _ University of Education, Winneba.md)
**Evidence:** "interactions between oppositely charged species give ionic bonds"

## Misconception 359: Students think London dispersion force occurs within a single molecule rather than between molecules.

**Topic:** intermolecular forces / London dispersion forces
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces are intermolecular forces arising from temporary induced dipoles between atoms or molecules, not forces within one molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should first decide whether the interaction is inside a molecule or between separate molecules. Dispersion forces act between neighboring particles due to momentary electron fluctuations, so they cannot be located within a single molecule as a bond type.
**Concept category:** London dispersion forces
**Prevalence:** 37% of the teacher trainees had alternative conceptions on London dispersion forces
**Population:** 82 firstyear chemistry major teacher trainees at the University of Education, Winneba
**Source:** Teacher trainees' alternative conceptions about intermolecular forces (Teacher trainees' alternative conceptions about intermolecular forces _ University of Education, Winneba/Teacher trainees' alternative conceptions about intermolecular forces _ University of Education, Winneba.md)
**Evidence:** "London dispersion force occurs within a single molecule rather than between molecules"

## Misconception 360: Students think the major intermolecular force between I2 molecules is a covalent-ionic interaction.

**Topic:** intermolecular forces / diiodine intermolecular forces
**Correct concept:** Between I2 molecules, the relevant intermolecular attraction is London dispersion force; covalent or ionic bonding does not occur between separate neutral I2 molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that I2 is a nonpolar diatomic molecule. Nonpolar molecules interact primarily through dispersion forces, so the force between distinct I2 molecules cannot be covalent or ionic.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces between nonpolar molecules
**Population:** 82 firstyear chemistry major teacher trainees at the University of Education, Winneba
**Source:** Teacher trainees' alternative conceptions about intermolecular forces (Teacher trainees' alternative conceptions about intermolecular forces _ University of Education, Winneba/Teacher trainees' alternative conceptions about intermolecular forces _ University of Education, Winneba.md)
**Evidence:** "Covalent –ionic interaction is the major intermolecular force that exists in different I2 molecules"

## Misconception 361: Students think any molecule containing O and H must form hydrogen bonding regardless of where those atoms appear in the structure.

**Topic:** intermolecular forces / hydrogen bonding in organic structures
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding requires a hydrogen atom covalently bonded to N, O, or F and an available lone pair on N, O, or F in another molecule or part of the same molecule, with molecular structure and proximity determining whether the interaction is possible.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should inspect the bonding context, not just the presence of O and H symbols. They must check whether H is directly attached to O, N, or F and whether a suitable acceptor atom is available for intermolecular donation/acceptance.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Prevalence:** more than 50% of the teacher trainees demonstrated alternative conceptions on hydrogen bonding
**Population:** 82 firstyear chemistry major teacher trainees at the University of Education, Winneba
**Source:** Teacher trainees' alternative conceptions about intermolecular forces (Teacher trainees' alternative conceptions about intermolecular forces _ University of Education, Winneba/Teacher trainees' alternative conceptions about intermolecular forces _ University of Education, Winneba.md)
**Evidence:** "the perception that any molecule which contains 'O' and 'H' forms hydrogen bonding irrespective of their position in the molecule"

## Misconception 362: Students think hydrogen bonding only exists for O-H groups and not for N-H or F-H groups.

**Topic:** intermolecular forces / hydrogen bonding donors
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding can occur when hydrogen is bonded to N, O, or F, so N-H and F-H can also participate in hydrogen bonding.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should apply the general rule that H-bond donors are X-H where X is N, O, or F. They should not restrict hydrogen bonding to only hydroxyl groups.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Prevalence:** more than 50% of the teacher trainees demonstrated alternative conceptions on hydrogen bonding
**Population:** 82 firstyear chemistry major teacher trainees at the University of Education, Winneba
**Source:** Teacher trainees' alternative conceptions about intermolecular forces (Teacher trainees' alternative conceptions about intermolecular forces _ University of Education, Winneba/Teacher trainees' alternative conceptions about intermolecular forces _ University of Education, Winneba.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bonding only exists between 'O' and 'H' but not between N-H and F-H"

## Misconception 363: Students think all molecules containing hydrogen exhibit hydrogen bonding, even when H is not directly bonded to F, O, or N.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and hydrogen bonding / Hydrogen bonding requires only hydrogen in a molecule
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding occurs only when hydrogen is directly bonded to a highly electronegative atom such as F, O, or N, creating a sufficiently polarized H bond.
**Expected reasoning:** To decide whether a substance can hydrogen-bond, first check whether H is covalently bonded to F, O, or N. If not, the molecule may have dipole-dipole or dispersion forces, but not strong hydrogen bonding of the usual kind.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Population:** Commenters / students in the article discussion
**Source:** The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points (The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points/The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bonding occurs in molecules containing the highly electronegative elements F, O, or N directly bound to hydrogen."

## Misconception 364: Students believe all molecules with hydrogen have hydrogen bonding, but that it is just very weak in many cases.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and hydrogen bonding / All H-containing molecules have hydrogen bonding
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is not a general property of any H-containing molecule; it depends on the specific bonding environment of H, especially H attached to F, O, or N.
**Expected reasoning:** Examine whether hydrogen is bonded to F, O, or N. If hydrogen is bonded to carbon or another less electronegative atom, the molecule does not have meaningful hydrogen bonding; other intermolecular forces dominate.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Population:** Commenter / student statement in discussion
**Source:** The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points (The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points/The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points.md)
**Evidence:** "All molecules with hydrogen have 'hydrogen bonding', but it is to such a very weak degree that it doesn't really matter."

## Misconception 365: Students think boiling point is determined mainly by the number of hydrogen bonds a molecule can form rather than by the overall balance of intermolecular forces, molecular size, and structure.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and boiling point / Counting hydrogen bonds determines boiling point
**Correct concept:** Boiling point depends on the total set of intermolecular forces, not just a simple count of hydrogen bonds; hydrogen bonding is one contributor among others such as dispersion forces and molecular weight/surface area.
**Expected reasoning:** Compare all relevant intermolecular forces: hydrogen-bonding capability, polarity, dispersion forces, and molecular size/surface area. Then predict boiling point from the combined strength of these attractions, not just the number of H-bond donors/acceptors.
**Concept category:** boiling point trends
**Population:** Commenter / student response in discussion
**Source:** The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points (The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points/The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points.md)
**Evidence:** "Count the number of hydrogen bonds. More hydrogen bonds means stronger IM force"

## Misconception 366: Students think a larger molar mass automatically means stronger intermolecular forces and therefore a higher boiling point.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and boiling point / Molar mass alone explains boiling point
**Correct concept:** While boiling point often increases with molecular weight because dispersion forces increase, molecular structure and the type of intermolecular forces can outweigh mass alone; functional groups and bonding type matter.
**Expected reasoning:** Use molar mass as one clue, but also examine polarity, hydrogen bonding, ionic character, and surface area/branching. A lighter molecule with stronger specific intermolecular forces can boil higher than a heavier one.
**Concept category:** boiling point trends
**Population:** Commenter / student response in discussion
**Source:** The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points (The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points/The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points.md)
**Evidence:** "The larger the molar mass (in some cases), the stronger the IMFs."

## Misconception 367: Students attribute boiling-point differences only to molar mass and ignore functional group identity and intermolecular-force type.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and structure / Molecular weight is the sole reason for boiling-point differences
**Correct concept:** Boiling point differences reflect the strength and nature of intermolecular forces; a molecule with a specific functional group that enables hydrogen bonding or ionic interactions can have a higher boiling point than a heavier molecule lacking those interactions.
**Expected reasoning:** Identify what intermolecular forces each molecule can have. Then compare those forces before relying on mass. For example, an alcohol can boil higher than a smaller ammonium-containing or otherwise differently interacting compound because of the relevant forces present.
**Concept category:** boiling point trends
**Population:** Commenter / student response in discussion
**Source:** The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points (The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points/The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points.md)
**Evidence:** "Yes, Me OH has a higher mass total than ammonium, but the fact that you are dealing with an alcohol versus an ion affects bp."

## Misconception 368: Students treat ionic bonds as completely non-covalent and think there is no shared-electron character in ionic compounds.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and ionic character / Ionic compounds are fully ionic with no covalent character
**Correct concept:** Real ionic bonds are highly ionic but often retain some covalent character; bonding exists on a continuum rather than as a strict either/or category.
**Expected reasoning:** Recognize that bond polarity is continuous. When electronegativity differences are very large, the bond is mostly ionic, but not necessarily 100% ionic; classify it based on degree, not an absolute divide.
**Concept category:** ionic bonding / bond polarity
**Population:** Commenter / student response in discussion
**Source:** The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points (The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points/The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points.md)
**Evidence:** "nearly every bond that we refer to as 'ionic' actually has a little bit of covalent (i.e., shared electron) character."

## Misconception 369: Students think a bond is either polar or nonpolar based on a fixed electronegativity cutoff, rather than understanding polarity as a continuum and relative measure.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and bond polarity / Any electronegativity difference means a bond is not polar only above a threshold
**Correct concept:** Bond polarity is relative to the electronegativity difference between the two bonded atoms; any nonzero difference produces some polarity, though conventions may be used to label especially large differences as ionic.
**Expected reasoning:** Compare electronegativities of the two atoms and reason about the size of the difference. Small differences give weak polarity, larger differences give stronger polarity, and very large differences approach ionic character.
**Concept category:** bond polarity / electronegativity
**Population:** Expert commenter clarifying a common student idea
**Source:** The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points (The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points/The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points.md)
**Evidence:** "polarity is always relative to the electronegativity difference between TWO atoms"

## Misconception 370: Students believe HCl can only exhibit dipole-dipole interactions and does not have any hydrogen bonding.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and hydrogen bonding / HCl has no hydrogen bonding at all
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is strongest for H bonded to F, O, or N, but less typical cases like HCl may still show some very weak hydrogen-bond-like interaction; however, this is much weaker than in HF, alcohols, or amines.
**Expected reasoning:** Check the electronegativity of the atom bonded to hydrogen. If it is not F/O/N, hydrogen bonding is much weaker or not conventionally counted. Therefore classify HCl primarily by dipole-dipole rather than strong hydrogen bonding.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Population:** Commenter / student question and reply in discussion
**Source:** The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points (The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points/The Four Intermolecular Forces and How They Affect Boiling Points.md)
**Evidence:** "HCl has *some* hydrogen bonding, it's just not particularly strong."

## Misconception 371: Students do not correctly understand what intermolecular forces are and may confuse them with other kinds of forces or phenomena.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Basic definition
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces are attractive forces between molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify that the term refers to forces acting between separate molecules, not within a molecule, and use that definition to classify examples and explain properties such as boiling point.
**Concept category:** definition of intermolecular forces
**Population:** 88 university chemistry students
**Source:** Validity and Reliability of the Two-tier Diagnostic Test to Identify Students' Alternative Conceptions of Intermolecular Forces (Validity_and_Reliability_of_the_Two-tier_Diagnosti/Validity_and_Reliability_of_the_Two-tier_Diagnosti.md)
**Evidence:** “Definition of IMFs”

## Misconception 372: Students do not correctly relate the strength of intermolecular forces to boiling point.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Boiling point
**Correct concept:** Stronger intermolecular forces generally lead to higher boiling points because more energy is needed to separate molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the strength of intermolecular attractions in substances and infer that substances with stronger attractions require more heat to boil.
**Concept category:** properties affected by IMFs
**Population:** 88 university chemistry students
**Source:** Validity and Reliability of the Two-tier Diagnostic Test to Identify Students' Alternative Conceptions of Intermolecular Forces (Validity_and_Reliability_of_the_Two-tier_Diagnosti/Validity_and_Reliability_of_the_Two-tier_Diagnosti.md)
**Evidence:** “The influence of the strength of Intermolecular forces to boiling point”

## Misconception 373: Students do not correctly identify or define London forces (dispersion forces).

**Topic:** London forces / Recognition and definition
**Correct concept:** London forces are a type of intermolecular attraction arising from temporary induced dipoles and are also called dispersion forces.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize London forces as a weak intermolecular force present among all molecules and nonpolar substances, then connect the name to the dispersion-force concept.
**Concept category:** surface features / force identification
**Population:** 88 university chemistry students
**Source:** Validity and Reliability of the Two-tier Diagnostic Test to Identify Students' Alternative Conceptions of Intermolecular Forces (Validity_and_Reliability_of_the_Two-tier_Diagnosti/Validity_and_Reliability_of_the_Two-tier_Diagnosti.md)
**Evidence:** “Example dan definition of London force (Dispersion force).”

## Misconception 374: Students do not correctly understand what determines the strength of London forces.

**Topic:** London forces / Strength of London forces
**Correct concept:** The strength of London forces depends on factors such as electron cloud polarizability and molecular size/surface area.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that larger, more polarizable molecules generally have stronger London forces because temporary dipoles are easier to induce.
**Concept category:** London forces
**Population:** 88 university chemistry students
**Source:** Validity and Reliability of the Two-tier Diagnostic Test to Identify Students' Alternative Conceptions of Intermolecular Forces (Validity_and_Reliability_of_the_Two-tier_Diagnosti/Validity_and_Reliability_of_the_Two-tier_Diagnosti.md)
**Evidence:** “The strength of London Force”

## Misconception 375: Students incorrectly compare London forces and dipole-dipole forces, often failing to distinguish which is stronger under given conditions.

**Topic:** London forces vs dipole-dipole forces / Comparison of force strengths
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole forces generally involve attractions between permanent dipoles, while London forces arise from temporary induced dipoles; their relative strengths must be compared based on the substances involved.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify whether molecules are polar or nonpolar, determine the type of intermolecular force present, and then compare the strengths using the correct molecular basis.
**Concept category:** comparison of intermolecular forces
**Population:** 88 university chemistry students
**Source:** Validity and Reliability of the Two-tier Diagnostic Test to Identify Students' Alternative Conceptions of Intermolecular Forces (Validity_and_Reliability_of_the_Two-tier_Diagnosti/Validity_and_Reliability_of_the_Two-tier_Diagnosti.md)
**Evidence:** “Comparison of London force and dipole-dipole force”

## Misconception 376: Students do not correctly understand what dipole-dipole forces are or cannot identify examples of dipole-dipole interactions.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole forces / Definition and example
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole forces are intermolecular attractions between polar molecules with permanent dipoles.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should first determine whether a molecule is polar, then infer that polar molecules attract each other through dipole-dipole interactions.
**Concept category:** dipole-dipole interactions
**Population:** 88 university chemistry students
**Source:** Validity and Reliability of the Two-tier Diagnostic Test to Identify Students' Alternative Conceptions of Intermolecular Forces (Validity_and_Reliability_of_the_Two-tier_Diagnosti/Validity_and_Reliability_of_the_Two-tier_Diagnosti.md)
**Evidence:** “Definition and example of dipole-dipole molecules”

## Misconception 377: Students do not correctly define hydrogen bonding.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Definition
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is a special strong intermolecular attraction involving hydrogen bonded to highly electronegative atoms such as N, O, or F and a lone pair on another electronegative atom.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should check the bonding environment of hydrogen and the presence of lone pairs on nearby electronegative atoms before identifying hydrogen bonding.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Population:** 88 university chemistry students
**Source:** Validity and Reliability of the Two-tier Diagnostic Test to Identify Students' Alternative Conceptions of Intermolecular Forces (Validity_and_Reliability_of_the_Two-tier_Diagnosti/Validity_and_Reliability_of_the_Two-tier_Diagnosti.md)
**Evidence:** “Definition of Hydrogen bond”

## Misconception 378: Students do not correctly understand the strength of hydrogen bonding.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Strength of hydrogen bond
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is stronger than ordinary dipole-dipole interactions and London forces, though weaker than covalent bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the interacting species and recognize that hydrogen bonding is an especially strong intermolecular force because of the high electronegativity contrast and charge concentration.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Population:** 88 university chemistry students
**Source:** Validity and Reliability of the Two-tier Diagnostic Test to Identify Students' Alternative Conceptions of Intermolecular Forces (Validity_and_Reliability_of_the_Two-tier_Diagnosti/Validity_and_Reliability_of_the_Two-tier_Diagnosti.md)
**Evidence:** “The strength of the Hydrogen bond”

## Misconception 379: Students do not correctly understand how hydrogen bonds form.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Formation process
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonds form when a hydrogen atom covalently bonded to N, O, or F is attracted to a lone pair on another electronegative atom in a nearby molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify both the donor molecule with H attached to N/O/F and the acceptor atom with lone pairs, then explain the intermolecular attraction between them.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding formation
**Population:** 88 university chemistry students
**Source:** Validity and Reliability of the Two-tier Diagnostic Test to Identify Students' Alternative Conceptions of Intermolecular Forces (Validity_and_Reliability_of_the_Two-tier_Diagnosti/Validity_and_Reliability_of_the_Two-tier_Diagnosti.md)
**Evidence:** “The process of the forming of Hydrogen bond”

## Misconception 380: Students think the yellowish-green color of chlorine gas is a property of chlorine atoms or chlorine molecules rather than a macroscopic property of the substance.

**Topic:** chemical bonding and substance properties / color of chlorine gas
**Correct concept:** The color is a physical property of chlorine gas as a substance, not of individual atoms or molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish the macroscopic substance from its microscopic particles and explain color as a property emerging from the substance under the stated conditions, not from isolated atoms or molecules.
**Concept category:** macroscopic properties attributed to particles
**Prevalence:** 8.5% secondary school; 15.2% university for chlorine molecules; 8.5% secondary school for chlorine atoms
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "the yellowish green colour of chlorine is due to chlorine atoms"

## Misconception 381: Students think the water molecule itself has a boiling point of 100 °C.

**Topic:** boiling points / water boiling point
**Correct concept:** Boiling point is a property of the substance water, not of a single molecule or of ice.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify boiling point as a bulk property that applies to the liquid substance under specified pressure conditions, not to an isolated molecule.
**Concept category:** macroscopic properties attributed to particles
**Prevalence:** 10.0% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "The water molecule has a boiling point of 100 °C at 1-atm pressure."

## Misconception 382: Students believe branched organic compounds have stronger van der Waals forces and therefore higher boiling points.

**Topic:** boiling points / organic compounds and van der Waals forces
**Correct concept:** Branching generally reduces surface contact and weakens intermolecular van der Waals interactions, lowering boiling point relative to less branched isomers.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare molecular shape and surface area, recognizing that a straight-chain molecule can have stronger intermolecular attractions than a branched isomer of the same molar mass.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces / boiling points
**Prevalence:** 34.0% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "Van der Waals forces are more intense in branching organic compounds."

## Misconception 383: Students think van der Waals forces become more intense simply because molar mass increases.

**Topic:** boiling points / organic compounds and molar mass
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular force strength depends on molecular size/shape and polarizability, not molar mass alone.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should not use molar mass as the only criterion; they should consider molecular structure and the resulting intermolecular contact surface and attractions.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces / boiling points
**Prevalence:** 8.0% secondary school; 23.0% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "Van der Waals forces are more intense as molar mass increases."

## Misconception 384: Students think sodium iodide dissolves in nonpolar solvents, or that it is a molecule that dissolves there because sodium transfers an electron to iodine to form NaI molecules.

**Topic:** solubility / ionic compounds in solvents
**Correct concept:** Sodium iodide is an ionic compound and dissolves in polar solvents like water because ion-dipole interactions can stabilize the ions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify NaI as an ionic lattice, then reason that polar solvents can solvate Na+ and I- ions through dipole interactions, while nonpolar solvents cannot.
**Concept category:** ionic compounds / solubility
**Prevalence:** 10.0% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "It dissolves in nonpolar solvents, since sodium transfers its valence electron to iodine to form a molecule of NaI."

## Misconception 385: Students think solid, molten, or dissolved NaCl conducts because it has free electrons, like a metal.

**Topic:** electrical conductivity / ionic compounds
**Correct concept:** NaCl conducts when molten or in solution because it contains mobile ions, not free electrons.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish ionic conduction from metallic conduction and explain that ions become mobile when the lattice is disrupted by melting or dissolving.
**Concept category:** conductivity / ionic bonding
**Prevalence:** 22.0% secondary school; 13.0% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "NaCl solid, melted or in solution, has free electrons that conduct electricity."

## Misconception 386: Students think NaCl is a molecule that dissociates into ions which conduct current, instead of recognizing an ionic compound/lattice.

**Topic:** electrical conductivity / ionic compounds
**Correct concept:** NaCl is an ionic compound; when molten or in solution, mobile ions carry current, but the substance is not molecular.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should classify NaCl as an ionic substance and explain conductivity by ion mobility, not by a molecular dissociation model.
**Concept category:** ionic compounds / molecular-vs-ionic classification
**Prevalence:** 14.0% secondary school; 11.0% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "NaCl molecule, melted or in solution, dissociates into a sodium ion and a chloride ion which are the conductors of the electric current."

## Misconception 387: Students think graphite conducts because its layers can slide over each other.

**Topic:** electrical conductivity / graphite
**Correct concept:** Graphite conducts because it has delocalized electrons that are mobile within the layers.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect conductivity to delocalized electrons in graphite's structure, not to mechanical sliding of layers.
**Concept category:** conductivity / covalent structures
**Prevalence:** 22.0% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "Graphite conducts electricity because the layers of hexagonal carbon rings can slide over each other."

## Misconception 388: Students think graphite conducts because its layers are held together by van der Waals forces.

**Topic:** electrical conductivity / graphite
**Correct concept:** Graphite conducts because of delocalized electrons, not because of van der Waals forces between layers.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should separate interlayer attractions from the actual charge carriers and identify electrons as the cause of conductivity.
**Concept category:** conductivity / covalent structures
**Prevalence:** 20.0% secondary school; 12.0% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "Graphite conducts electricity because the layers of hexagonal carbon rings are bonded by Van der Waals forces."

## Misconception 389: Students think diamond conducts electricity because it is a three-dimensional covalent network with free electrons.

**Topic:** electrical conductivity / diamond
**Correct concept:** Diamond does not conduct electricity because all electrons are localized in covalent bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that a covalent network can still be an insulator if it has no mobile charge carriers.
**Concept category:** conductivity / covalent structures
**Prevalence:** 13.0% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "Diamond conducts electricity because is a three-dimensional network with covalent bonds between carbon atoms. The electrons are free and can conduct electrical current."

## Misconception 390: Students think NaCl is a molecular substance.

**Topic:** ionic bonding and molecular structure / classification of sodium chloride
**Correct concept:** NaCl is an ionic compound made of an ionic lattice of Na+ and Cl- ions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish an ionic lattice from a molecular substance and avoid describing ionic compounds as composed of discrete molecules.
**Concept category:** molecular-vs-ionic classification
**Prevalence:** 16.0% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
