**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "NaCl is a molecular substance."

## Misconception 391: Students think hydrogen bonds form in any molecule containing hydrogen plus N, O, or F, even if H is not directly bonded to those atoms.

**Topic:** hydrogen bonding / condition for hydrogen-bond formation
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding requires hydrogen directly bonded to N, O, or F and interaction with a lone pair on a neighboring electronegative atom.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should check the bonding environment of hydrogen, not merely the presence of H and electronegative atoms somewhere in the molecule.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Prevalence:** 19.0% secondary school; 16.0% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "the condition to form H-bonds is a molecule having H atoms plus atoms of N, or O or F."

## Misconception 392: Students think hydrogen-bond strength is greater in nitrogen-containing compounds because nitrogen is more electronegative than oxygen.

**Topic:** hydrogen bonding strength / ethanol vs ethylamine
**Correct concept:** Oxygen is more electronegative than nitrogen, so ethanol forms stronger hydrogen bonds than ethylamine in this comparison.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare electronegativities correctly and connect stronger hydrogen bonding to a more electronegative donor/acceptor environment.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding / electronegativity
**Prevalence:** 15.2% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "N atom is more electronegative than O in the ethylamine"

## Misconception 393: Students think hydrogen bonds are stronger in molecules with more hydrogen atoms.

**Topic:** hydrogen bonding strength / molecule size
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen-bond strength depends on electronegativity and molecular environment, not on how many hydrogen atoms are present.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should not use hydrogen count as a proxy for hydrogen-bond strength; they should evaluate the bonding pattern and electronegativity difference.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding / structure-property reasoning
**Prevalence:** 12.7% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "attributing the H-bond strength to the quantity of H atoms in the molecule"

## Misconception 394: Students think iodine is molecular and that covalent bonds between iodine atoms are broken at the boiling point.

**Topic:** intermolecular vs intramolecular forces / boiling point of iodine
**Correct concept:** At the boiling point of a molecular substance, intermolecular forces are overcome; covalent intramolecular bonds are not broken.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish intermolecular attractions from the covalent bonds within the molecule and know that boiling does not break intramolecular bonds in molecular substances.
**Concept category:** intra- vs intermolecular forces
**Prevalence:** 34.2% secondary school; 19.2% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "iodine (molecular substance) breaks the covalent bonds between atoms of iodine"

## Misconception 395: Students think HF is covalent simply because it is made of two non-metals that share electrons.

**Topic:** substance classification / HF bonding type
**Correct concept:** HF is a molecular substance with covalent H-F bonds and intermolecular forces including van der Waals forces and hydrogen bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should classify the substance by considering both intramolecular bonds and intermolecular forces, not just the presence of shared electrons.
**Concept category:** substance classification / bonding
**Prevalence:** 27.8% secondary school; 8.0% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "HF is a covalent substance since consists of two non-metallic elements bonded by sharing electrons."

## Misconception 396: Students think HF is ionic because the electronegativity difference between H and F allows an ionic bond.

**Topic:** substance classification / HF ionic misconception
**Correct concept:** HF is not ionic; it is a polar covalent molecular substance with hydrogen bonding.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use bonding type criteria correctly and recognize that a large electronegativity difference does not automatically mean an ionic substance.
**Concept category:** substance classification / bonding
**Prevalence:** 15.2% secondary school; 9.0% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "HF is an ionic substance since the difference in electronegativity of H and F atoms allows establishing and ionic bond between them."

## Misconception 397: Students think SiO2 is molecular because it consists of two non-metallic elements linked by covalent bonds.

**Topic:** substance classification / SiO2 as molecular substance
**Correct concept:** SiO2 is a covalent network solid, not a molecular substance.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish a covalent network from discrete molecules and recognize that covalent bonding does not automatically imply a molecular substance.
**Concept category:** molecular-vs-covalent-network classification
**Prevalence:** 29.0% secondary school; 17.2% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "SiO2 is a molecular substance since it consists of two non-metallic elements linked by covalent bonds."

## Misconception 398: Students identify the arrangement of electron pairs around the central atom with the geometry of the molecule.

**Topic:** molecular geometry / electron pairs vs molecular shape
**Correct concept:** Molecular geometry is the arrangement of atomic nuclei/atoms around the central atom, while electron-pair arrangement is the electron-domain geometry used to predict it.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should first determine electron-pair arrangement with VSEPR, then distinguish that from the actual molecular shape traced by atoms.
**Concept category:** molecular geometry / VSEPR
**Prevalence:** 19.0% secondary school; 83.8% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "attribute the shape of the molecule to the electron pairs, both lone and bonded."

## Misconception 399: Students think bond polarity determines the shape of a molecule.

**Topic:** molecular geometry / bond polarity determines shape
**Correct concept:** Molecular shape is determined by the arrangement of atoms/electron domains around the central atom, not by bond polarity.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should separate geometry from polarity and use electron-domain repulsion and atomic arrangement to infer shape.
**Concept category:** molecular geometry / polarity
**Prevalence:** 15.2% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "Bond polarity determines the shape of a molecule."

## Misconception 400: Students think tetrahedral geometry is sufficient to make bond dipoles cancel regardless of the atoms bonded to the central atom.

**Topic:** molecular polarity / tetrahedral geometry and polarity cancellation
**Correct concept:** Polarity depends on both molecular geometry and the nature/polarities of the bonds; tetrahedral geometry alone is not sufficient to guarantee a nonpolar molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate both symmetry and the identity/electronegativity of substituent atoms before concluding that dipoles cancel.
**Concept category:** molecular polarity / symmetry
**Prevalence:** 21.5% secondary school; 15.2% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "Assume that tetrahedral geometry is a necessary and sufficient condition to cancel the bond dipole moments regardless of the atoms bonded to the central atom."

## Misconception 401: Students identify the Lewis structure or planar symbolic drawing with the actual shape of the molecule and therefore make wrong geometry/polarity predictions.

**Topic:** molecular geometry and polarity / Lewis structure vs molecular shape
**Correct concept:** Lewis structures are symbolic representations; the actual shape is the three-dimensional arrangement of atoms and electron pairs around the central atom.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use the Lewis structure to count electron domains, then infer 3D geometry and polarity rather than equating the drawing itself with molecular shape.
**Concept category:** molecular geometry / symbolic representation
**Prevalence:** 15.0% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "Identify the Lewis structure with the shape of the molecule"

## Misconception 402: Students think H2S is linear because the two lone pairs are balanced and the dipole moment is zero.

**Topic:** molecular polarity / H2S geometry
**Correct concept:** H2S has a bent geometry and a nonzero dipole moment.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use VSEPR to predict a bent shape for H2S and then assess dipole vectors to see that they do not cancel.
**Concept category:** molecular polarity / geometry
**Prevalence:** 10.0% secondary school
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "H2S molecule is linear because the two lone electron pairs are balanced and the resultant dipole moment is zero."

## Misconception 403: Students think any molecule with lone electron pairs must be polar.

**Topic:** molecular polarity / lone pairs imply polarity
**Correct concept:** Molecular polarity depends on the vector sum of bond dipoles and molecular geometry; lone pairs alone do not guarantee polarity.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate symmetry and dipole cancellation, not simply count lone pairs.
**Concept category:** molecular polarity
**Prevalence:** 25.3% secondary school; 12.0% university
**Population:** 79 high school students; 99 first-year university students
**Source:** Student's Misconceptions on Chemical Bonding: A Comparative Study between High School and First Year University Students (admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative/admin,+1_Studentâ€™s+Misconceptions+on+Chemical+Bonding+a+Comparative.md)
**Evidence:** "All molecules having lone electron pairs are polar."

## Misconception 404: Students group molecules only by arbitrary boiling-point cutoffs and ignore structural characteristics.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Initial grouping by boiling point
**Correct concept:** Molecules should be grouped by structural features that reflect intermolecular forces, not by arbitrary boiling-point ranges alone.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare molecular structures, identify shared functional groups or patterns, and then use boiling point as evidence to refine the structural categories.
**Concept category:** structure-boiling point relationship
**Population:** high school students in tenth grade chemistry classes
**Source:** An Inquiry Experience with High School Students To Develop an Understanding of Intermolecular Forces by Relating Boiling Point Trends and Molecular Structure (an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating/an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating.md)
**Evidence:** "Students often first group solely by arbitrary boiling point cut-offs and ignore structural characteristics."

## Misconception 405: Students believe that molecules with more carbons have higher boiling points in all cases, without considering exceptions such as molecules with strong hydrogen bonding.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Size within a homologous series
**Correct concept:** Within a comparable structural family, larger molecules often have higher boiling points because of stronger dispersion forces, but functional groups can override size trends.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should first compare similar molecules, then account for functional groups and intermolecular forces rather than relying only on carbon count.
**Concept category:** size/electron count
**Population:** high school students
**Source:** An Inquiry Experience with High School Students To Develop an Understanding of Intermolecular Forces by Relating Boiling Point Trends and Molecular Structure (an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating/an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating.md)
**Evidence:** "Molecules with more carbons have higher boiling points (within a group)."

## Misconception 406: Students think molecules with more hydrogens have higher boiling points.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Mass/electron count confusion
**Correct concept:** Boiling point is not determined by the number of hydrogens; molecules with the same number of hydrogens can have very different boiling points because of differences in structure and intermolecular forces.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should look beyond hydrogen count and compare molecular structure, polarity, and the presence of hydrogen bonding or other intermolecular forces.
**Concept category:** size/electron count
**Population:** high school students
**Source:** An Inquiry Experience with High School Students To Develop an Understanding of Intermolecular Forces by Relating Boiling Point Trends and Molecular Structure (an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating/an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating.md)
**Evidence:** "Molecules with more hydrogens have higher boiling points"

## Misconception 407: Students believe that simply having oxygen in a molecule makes its boiling point higher than molecules without oxygen.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Element-specific boiling point reasoning
**Correct concept:** Boiling point depends on the type of intermolecular forces present and molecular size; oxygen is not automatically the determining factor.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare molecules with similar size and identify whether the oxygen contributes to dipole-dipole forces or hydrogen bonding, rather than assuming oxygen alone raises boiling point.
**Concept category:** functional group/element presence
**Population:** high school students
**Source:** An Inquiry Experience with High School Students To Develop an Understanding of Intermolecular Forces by Relating Boiling Point Trends and Molecular Structure (an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating/an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating.md)
**Evidence:** "Molecules with oxygen have higher boiling points"

## Misconception 408: Students believe sulfur-containing molecules always have higher boiling points than oxygen-containing molecules.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Element-specific boiling point reasoning
**Correct concept:** Boiling point must be explained by the overall balance of intermolecular forces and molecular size; sulfur is not inherently 'stronger' than oxygen in all cases.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare sulfur- and oxygen-containing molecules of similar size and consider polarity and dispersion forces, rather than ranking the elements themselves.
**Concept category:** functional group/element presence
**Population:** high school students
**Source:** An Inquiry Experience with High School Students To Develop an Understanding of Intermolecular Forces by Relating Boiling Point Trends and Molecular Structure (an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating/an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating.md)
**Evidence:** "Molecules with sulfur have boiling points higher than those with oxygen."

## Misconception 409: Students think molecules with an oxygen on the end necessarily have higher boiling points than molecules with oxygen in the middle, without distinguishing hydroxyl groups from other oxygen-containing groups.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / End-vs-middle atom placement
**Correct concept:** The key issue is the type of intermolecular force and whether the molecule can hydrogen bond; oxygen position alone is not sufficient.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify whether the molecule has an -OH group capable of hydrogen bonding, then compare similar-sized molecules to determine why boiling points differ.
**Concept category:** functional group/position
**Population:** high school students
**Source:** An Inquiry Experience with High School Students To Develop an Understanding of Intermolecular Forces by Relating Boiling Point Trends and Molecular Structure (an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating/an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating.md)
**Evidence:** "Molecules with an oxygen on the end have boiling points higher than those with the oxygen in the middle (not addressing specialty of hydroxyl group)."

## Misconception 410: Students conclude that oxygen is necessarily special and the main reason for higher boiling points.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Specialness of oxygen in the chain
**Correct concept:** Different atoms and functional groups matter because of the intermolecular forces they allow; oxygen is important in some contexts, but hydrogen bonding and molecular shape must be considered.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should test whether an observed trend holds across comparison molecules, then refine the rule to the specific intermolecular force involved instead of assuming oxygen is universally special.
**Concept category:** functional group/element presence
**Population:** high school students
**Source:** An Inquiry Experience with High School Students To Develop an Understanding of Intermolecular Forces by Relating Boiling Point Trends and Molecular Structure (an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating/an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating.md)
**Evidence:** "Some students start sorting by whether or not the molecule contains an oxygen atom"

## Misconception 411: Students place all outlier molecules into one group simply because they do not fit the others, rather than explaining each using intermolecular forces.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Outlier handling
**Correct concept:** Each molecule should be assigned based on the specific intermolecular force or structural feature that governs its boiling point.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare each outlier against reference molecules and determine whether it fits through dispersion, dipole-dipole, or hydrogen bonding patterns.
**Concept category:** categorization/reasoning
**Population:** weaker groups of high school students
**Source:** An Inquiry Experience with High School Students To Develop an Understanding of Intermolecular Forces by Relating Boiling Point Trends and Molecular Structure (an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating/an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating.md)
**Evidence:** "Some weaker groups of students will end up placing all of the outliers in one group and stating their reasoning as, 'they did not fit with the other ones'."

## Misconception 412: Students think molecules themselves break apart into atoms when a substance boils.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Phase changes
**Correct concept:** Boiling involves overcoming intermolecular forces between molecules; covalent bonds within molecules remain intact.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish between intermolecular attractions between molecules and intramolecular covalent bonds within molecules, and recognize that boiling separates molecules rather than decomposing them.
**Concept category:** phase change/bond breaking
**Population:** high school students
**Source:** An Inquiry Experience with High School Students To Develop an Understanding of Intermolecular Forces by Relating Boiling Point Trends and Molecular Structure (an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating/an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating.md)
**Evidence:** "avoid the misconception of molecules breaking apart into constituent atoms upon boiling"

## Misconception 413: Students fail to recognize that an O-H bond is the key feature that enables hydrogen bonding, not simply the presence of oxygen alone.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Hydrogen bonding recognition
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding occurs when hydrogen is bonded to O, N, or F and interacts with lone pairs on neighboring molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should inspect the Lewis/structural formula for an O-H, N-H, or F-H bond and then infer the possibility of hydrogen bonding between molecules.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Population:** high school students
**Source:** An Inquiry Experience with High School Students To Develop an Understanding of Intermolecular Forces by Relating Boiling Point Trends and Molecular Structure (an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating/an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating.md)
**Evidence:** "The molecules of this group each had an O that was attached to an H."

## Misconception 414: Students think molecules containing sulfur should be assigned to a stronger intermolecular-force category than molecules containing oxygen simply because sulfur is present.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Relative strength of IMFs by element type
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular-force type must be determined by molecular structure and polarity; the presence of sulfur does not automatically mean stronger forces than oxygen-containing analogues.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare sulfur and oxygen analogues of similar size and shape, then reason from dispersion and dipole interactions rather than elemental identity alone.
**Concept category:** functional group/element presence
**Population:** high school students
**Source:** An Inquiry Experience with High School Students To Develop an Understanding of Intermolecular Forces by Relating Boiling Point Trends and Molecular Structure (an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating/an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating.md)
**Evidence:** "To avoid having students come to the conclusion that oxygen is necessarily special, diethylsulfide is included"

## Misconception 415: Students do not distinguish the hydroxyl group as special and instead treat any oxygen-containing group as equivalent for boiling-point purposes.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Hydroxyl group specificity
**Correct concept:** Hydroxyl groups enable hydrogen bonding and typically lead to higher boiling points than similar molecules lacking O-H groups.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the presence of an -OH group and use hydrogen bonding to explain why alcohols and polyols boil higher than comparable ethers or thioethers.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Population:** high school students
**Source:** An Inquiry Experience with High School Students To Develop an Understanding of Intermolecular Forces by Relating Boiling Point Trends and Molecular Structure (an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating/an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating.md)
**Evidence:** "It is critical to help the students identify when oxygen is important as opposed to any element other than carbon so that molecules which can form dipole—dipole forces can be distinguished from those with hydrogen bonding capabilities."

## Misconception 416: Students infer that having oxygen at the end of a molecule is enough to explain the highest boiling points, without recognizing that butanol and butanthiol differ because of hydrogen bonding.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Overgeneralizing oxygen position
**Correct concept:** The higher boiling point of alcohols like butanol is due to hydrogen bonding from the hydroxyl group, not oxygen placement alone.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare butanol with butanthiol and related molecules, identify the O-H group in alcohols, and connect it to hydrogen bonding strength.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Population:** high school students
**Source:** An Inquiry Experience with High School Students To Develop an Understanding of Intermolecular Forces by Relating Boiling Point Trends and Molecular Structure (an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating/an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating.md)
**Evidence:** "why butanol has a boiling point higher than that of butanethiol"

## Misconception 417: Students fail to account for the fact that London dispersion forces can dominate boiling point for sufficiently large molecules.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Size dependence of London dispersion forces
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces increase with electron count and molecular size, and can outweigh weaker intermolecular forces in large molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the number of electrons and size of the electron cloud when judging boiling points, especially for large nonpolar molecules.
**Concept category:** London dispersion forces
**Population:** high school students
**Source:** An Inquiry Experience with High School Students To Develop an Understanding of Intermolecular Forces by Relating Boiling Point Trends and Molecular Structure (an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating/an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating.md)
**Evidence:** "the strength of LDFs dominates."

## Misconception 418: Students think boiling requires breaking molecular bonds rather than intermolecular attractions.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Boiling and bond breaking
**Correct concept:** Boiling is a phase change caused by overcoming intermolecular attractions; molecular covalent bonds are not broken.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should describe liquid particles separating as IMFs are overcome while the molecules themselves remain chemically intact.
**Concept category:** phase change/bond breaking
**Population:** high school students
**Source:** An Inquiry Experience with High School Students To Develop an Understanding of Intermolecular Forces by Relating Boiling Point Trends and Molecular Structure (an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating/an-inquiry-experience-with-high-school-students-to-develop-an-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-by-relating.md)
**Evidence:** "phase changes involve breaking IMFs, not molecular bonds"

## Misconception 419: Students think boiling involves breaking covalent bonds within molecules, so more stable or less branched molecules have the lowest boiling point because they 'need more energy to break the bonds'.

**Topic:** boiling points / organic compounds and branching
**Correct concept:** Boiling is a physical change governed by intermolecular forces; lower boiling point corresponds to weaker intermolecular attractions, not covalent bond breaking or molecular stability.
**Expected reasoning:** Compare the strength of intermolecular forces among isomers. Branched molecules usually have smaller surface area and weaker van der Waals forces, so they boil at lower temperatures without covalent bonds being broken.
**Concept category:** phase change / covalent vs intermolecular bonds
**Prevalence:** 26 of 87 students who chose n-butane (30%) used instability or bond-breaking reasoning; 10 of 55 students (18%) who gave the correct answer also used bond-breaking reasoning.
**Population:** Upper secondary school students in grades 11 to 13 (n=205 for Item 1).
**Source:** Students' understanding of boiling points and intermolecular forces (b920829c/b920829c.md)
**Evidence:** "Many students incorrectly assumed that boiling involved breaking bonds."

## Misconception 420: Students believe branched molecules have higher boiling points than straight-chain molecules because branching makes molecules more stable or stronger.

**Topic:** boiling points / organic compounds and branching
**Correct concept:** Among isomers, branching usually lowers boiling point because it reduces surface area and weakens van der Waals forces.
**Expected reasoning:** Identify that a branched isomer has a smaller surface area than a straight-chain isomer, leading to weaker van der Waals attractions and therefore a lower boiling point.
**Concept category:** surface area / van der Waals forces
**Prevalence:** Observed in responses to Item 1; no exact percentage given for this specific misconception beyond the item-level distributions.
**Population:** Upper secondary school students in grades 11 to 13 (Item 1, n=205).
**Source:** Students' understanding of boiling points and intermolecular forces (b920829c/b920829c.md)
**Evidence:** "This student was about to arrive at the correct answer but was wrong in assuming that compounds composed of branched molecules have higher boiling points than those containing straight-chain molecules."

## Misconception 421: Students think branching prevents molecules from coming close together because of steric hindrance, so van der Waals forces cannot become fully effective and boiling point is lower.

**Topic:** boiling points / organic compounds and branching
**Correct concept:** Molecular shape affects surface contact and intermolecular attraction; branched molecules have reduced surface area, giving weaker van der Waals forces and lower boiling points.
**Expected reasoning:** Use molecular shape to compare surface area and thus the strength of van der Waals forces between molecules.
**Concept category:** steric hindrance / surface area
**Prevalence:** 6 students (18%) of the 33 who chose dimethylpropane (3).
**Population:** Upper secondary school students in grades 11 to 13 answering Item 1.
**Source:** Students' understanding of boiling points and intermolecular forces (b920829c/b920829c.md)
**Evidence:** "branched molecules cannot come as close together as straight-chain molecules because of steric hindrance. Therefore, the van der Waals forces cannot become fully effective."

## Misconception 422: Students believe hydrogen bonding can only occur if a molecule contains oxygen and hydrogen, or that oxygen is required for hydrogen bonding.

**Topic:** hydrogen bonding / oxygen as prerequisite
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding occurs when H is directly bonded to N, O, or F and interacts with a lone pair on N, O, or F in another molecule; oxygen is not required in every hydrogen-bonding molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** Check whether the molecule has H bonded to N/O/F and whether a neighboring molecule has a lone pair on N/O/F to accept the hydrogen bond.
**Concept category:** hydrogen-bond donor/acceptor requirements
**Prevalence:** Around 45% of the students who selected the dimethyl ether distractor reasoned this way.
**Population:** Upper secondary school students answering hydrogen-bond items (items 2-7).
**Source:** Students' understanding of boiling points and intermolecular forces (b920829c/b920829c.md)
**Evidence:** "A molecule need only contain oxygen and hydrogen in order for hydrogen bonding to occur."

## Misconception 423: Students conclude dimethyl ether can form hydrogen bonds because it contains oxygen, often by wrongly generalizing from water.

**Topic:** hydrogen bonding / oxygen as prerequisite
**Correct concept:** Dimethyl ether cannot hydrogen-bond to itself because it has oxygen but no H attached to O, N, or F; hydrogen bonding requires a hydrogen-bond donor and acceptor.
**Expected reasoning:** Recognize that an ether oxygen can accept hydrogen bonds, but the molecule lacks an O-H, N-H, or F-H bond, so identical dimethyl ether molecules do not hydrogen-bond with each other.
**Concept category:** oxygen-only rule / analogy to water
**Prevalence:** Around 45% of the students who selected the dimethyl ether distractor reasoned this way.
**Population:** Students responding to items 2, 3, 4, and 5.
**Source:** Students' understanding of boiling points and intermolecular forces (b920829c/b920829c.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bridges between molecules can only be formed via oxygen and hydrogen atoms."

## Misconception 424: Students think any dipolar molecules can form hydrogen bonds if the positive end of one molecule is near the negative end of another.

**Topic:** hydrogen bonding / dipole attraction as hydrogen bonding
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is a specific intermolecular interaction requiring H bonded to N, O, or F; general dipole-dipole attraction is not the same as hydrogen bonding.
**Expected reasoning:** First determine whether the molecule has an H attached to N/O/F. Then decide whether hydrogen bonding is possible; mere molecular polarity is insufficient.
**Concept category:** dipole attraction / molecular polarity
**Prevalence:** Used by some students in items 2, 3, 4, and 5; exact percentage not given.
**Population:** Upper secondary school students answering hydrogen-bond items.
**Source:** Students' understanding of boiling points and intermolecular forces (b920829c/b920829c.md)
**Evidence:** "A hydrogen bond is formed if dipolar molecules line up so that the positive end of one molecule is close to the negative end of another"

## Misconception 425: Students believe methyl fluoride can form hydrogen bonds because the molecule is polar and the C-F bond is highly electronegative.

**Topic:** hydrogen bonding / polarity of molecules with fluorine
**Correct concept:** Methyl fluoride is polar but cannot hydrogen-bond to itself because its hydrogen atoms are bonded to carbon, not to F, O, or N.
**Expected reasoning:** Identify that hydrogen bonding requires a donor hydrogen attached to N/O/F; a polar C-F bond alone does not create hydrogen bonding between methyl fluoride molecules.
**Concept category:** polarity / hydrogen-bond donor requirement
**Prevalence:** The methyl fluoride option was described as very attractive; exact percentage not given.
**Population:** Students answering items 2, 3, 4, and 5.
**Source:** Students' understanding of boiling points and intermolecular forces (b920829c/b920829c.md)
**Evidence:** "The difference in electronegativity of C-H is much smaller than the difference of F-H. Consequently in (2) and (3) hydrogen bonds do not exist."

## Misconception 426: Students infer hydrogen bonding from a molecule's tetrahedral structure and high bond electronegativity differences, treating polarity and shape as sufficient conditions.

**Topic:** hydrogen bonding / polarity plus shape
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding depends on a hydrogen directly bonded to N, O, or F and a lone pair on N/O/F in another molecule; molecular shape or general polarity alone is insufficient.
**Expected reasoning:** Use electronegativity and bonding context to check whether H is attached to N/O/F, not just whether the molecule is polar or tetrahedral.
**Concept category:** molecular polarity / shape
**Prevalence:** Appeared among students choosing the NH3/CH3F distractor in item 3; exact percentage not given.
**Population:** Upper secondary school students answering item 3.
**Source:** Students' understanding of boiling points and intermolecular forces (b920829c/b920829c.md)
**Evidence:** "NH3 and CH3F have tetrahedral structure. The differences in the electronegativities are high. They (the molecules), therefore, have polar structures. Polar molecules form hydrogen bridge bonds."

## Misconception 427: Students think hydrogen bonding occurs in compounds such as acetaldehyde because oxygen is present, or in acetic acid only because it has an OH group, without checking the full donor-acceptor requirement.

**Topic:** hydrogen bonding / wrong compound pairings
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding in a substance requires specific donor and acceptor sites; for example, methylamine and acetic acid can hydrogen-bond, whereas acetaldehyde cannot hydrogen-bond to itself as a donor.
**Expected reasoning:** Inspect each formula for H attached to N/O/F and for lone pairs on N/O/F. Distinguish donor sites from mere oxygen atoms in carbonyls or ethers.
**Concept category:** oxygen/hydrogen presence rule / OH-group heuristic
**Prevalence:** 31% of the students who selected the acetaldehyde/acetic acid distractor reasoned this way; 32% of the students selecting acetic acid as the only compound mentioned the OH group criterion.
**Population:** Upper secondary school students answering item 6.
**Source:** Students' understanding of boiling points and intermolecular forces (b920829c/b920829c.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bridges can only be formed between the oxygen of one molecule and the hydrogen of the other....."

## Misconception 428: Students think the lowest-boiling compound is the one that is most branched or that branching makes molecules more stable and therefore more able to withstand higher temperatures, so less energy is needed to break the bonds during boiling.

**Topic:** Boiling points / Relative boiling points of branched vs straight-chain alkanes
**Correct concept:** Boiling point depends on intermolecular attractions, not breaking covalent bonds; for isomeric alkanes, increased branching lowers surface area and weakens van der Waals forces, giving a lower boiling point.
**Expected reasoning:** Compare molecular shape and surface area: a branched isomer has smaller contact surface than a straight-chain isomer, so its van der Waals forces are weaker and it boils at a lower temperature.
**Concept category:** surface area / branching
**Prevalence:** 26 of 87 students (30%) who chose n-butane as the lowest-boiling compound
**Population:** Upper secondary school students in grades 11 to 13 (n=205 for item 1)
**Source:** Students' understanding of boiling points and intermolecular forces (b920829c-3/b920829c-3.md)
**Evidence:** "Mass and degree of branching ....determine the boiling point ....Molecule (1) is not branched."

## Misconception 429: Students think boiling involves breaking covalent bonds within molecules, or that molecules split apart during boiling.

**Topic:** Boiling points / Boiling as bond breaking
**Correct concept:** Boiling is a physical change in which intermolecular forces are overcome; covalent bonds within molecules remain intact.
**Expected reasoning:** Recognize that energy supplied during boiling separates molecules from each other by overcoming intermolecular attractions, not by breaking the bonds inside the molecules.
**Concept category:** bond breaking vs intermolecular forces
**Prevalence:** Observed in multiple responses; 18% of students who gave the correct answer to item 1 reasoned this way, and 26 of 87 students choosing n-butane used this idea
**Population:** Upper secondary school students in grades 11 to 13
**Source:** Students' understanding of boiling points and intermolecular forces (b920829c-3/b920829c-3.md)
**Evidence:** "boiling involved breaking bonds within the molecules"

## Misconception 430: Students think boiling is equivalent to splitting the compound into ions or smaller fragments, such as releasing H atoms as H+ during boiling.

**Topic:** Boiling points / Boiling as bond breaking / splitting
**Correct concept:** Boiling does not ionize molecules or split them into ions; it is a phase change governed by intermolecular attractions.
**Expected reasoning:** A correct explanation should stay at the level of molecules leaving the liquid phase because intermolecular forces are overcome; no covalent bond cleavage or ion formation occurs.
**Concept category:** bond breaking vs intermolecular forces
**Prevalence:** Appeared among students who gave the correct answer to item 1
**Population:** Students answering item 1
**Source:** Students' understanding of boiling points and intermolecular forces (b920829c-3/b920829c-3.md)
**Evidence:** "because the compound is least stable and gives off the H-atom as an  H^+  ion relatively quickly. For boiling = splitting."

## Misconception 431: Students think branched molecules cannot come close enough together because of steric hindrance, so van der Waals forces cannot become fully effective.

**Topic:** Boiling points / Branched molecules and intermolecular attractions
**Correct concept:** Branching lowers boiling point mainly because it reduces molecular surface area and thus weakens van der Waals attractions, not because molecules are too bulky to approach at all.
**Expected reasoning:** Assess how branching changes surface area and dispersion interactions; a more compact branched molecule has less intermolecular contact and weaker attractions.
**Concept category:** surface features / steric hindrance
**Prevalence:** 6 of 33 students (18%) who chose dimethylpropane as the lowest-boiling compound
**Population:** Upper secondary school students answering item 1
**Source:** Students' understanding of boiling points and intermolecular forces (b920829c-3/b920829c-3.md)
**Evidence:** "branched molecules cannot come as close together as straight-chain molecules because of steric hindrance. Therefore, the van der Waals forces cannot become fully effective."

## Misconception 432: Students think hydrogen bonds can only occur if a molecule contains oxygen and hydrogen, so dimethyl ether can hydrogen bond because it contains oxygen, while molecules without oxygen cannot.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Oxygen-only rule for hydrogen bonding
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding requires a hydrogen atom covalently bonded to N, O, or F and an acceptor lone pair on N, O, or F in another molecule; oxygen alone is not sufficient.
**Expected reasoning:** Check whether the molecule has an H directly bonded to N, O, or F and whether another molecule has a lone pair on N, O, or F to accept the bond.
**Concept category:** hydrogen-bond donor/acceptor criteria
**Prevalence:** About 45% of the students who selected the dimethyl ether distractor reasoned this way
**Population:** Students answering items 2-5 on hydrogen bonding
**Source:** Students' understanding of boiling points and intermolecular forces (b920829c-3/b920829c-3.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bridges between molecules can only be formed via oxygen and hydrogen atoms."

## Misconception 433: Students think any dipolar molecules can form hydrogen bonds if their positive and negative ends align, so methyl fluoride with ammonia or hydrogen fluoride is treated as hydrogen-bonding.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Hydrogen bonding based on dipole attraction only
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is more specific than general dipole-dipole attraction; it requires H bonded to N, O, or F interacting with a lone pair on N, O, or F.
**Expected reasoning:** Determine whether the molecules satisfy the structural hydrogen-bond requirement, rather than relying only on overall polarity or shape.
**Concept category:** dipole attraction vs hydrogen bonding
**Prevalence:** Described as an attractive distractor in items 2, 3, 4, and 5
**Population:** Students answering the hydrogen-bond prediction items
**Source:** Students' understanding of boiling points and intermolecular forces (b920829c-3/b920829c-3.md)
**Evidence:** "Polar molecules form hydrogen bridge bonds."

## Misconception 434: Students think methyl fluoride can hydrogen bond because it is a polar, tetrahedral molecule with large electronegativity differences.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Treating methyl fluoride as hydrogen-bonding because it is polar and tetrahedral
**Correct concept:** Methyl fluoride cannot act as a hydrogen-bond donor because its hydrogens are bonded to carbon, not directly to fluorine, oxygen, or nitrogen.
**Expected reasoning:** Recognize that C-H bonds do not qualify as hydrogen-bond donors even if the molecule is polar; the hydrogen must be attached to N, O, or F.
**Concept category:** polarity / molecular shape
**Prevalence:** Observed among students selecting the ammonia/methyl fluoride distractor in item 3
**Population:** Students answering item 3
**Source:** Students' understanding of boiling points and intermolecular forces (b920829c-3/b920829c-3.md)
**Evidence:** "NH_3  and  CH_3F  have tetrahedral structure. The differences in the electronegativities are high. They (the molecules), therefore, have polar structures. Polar molecules form hydrogen bridge bonds."

## Misconception 435: Students think dimethyl ether forms hydrogen bonds simply because it contains oxygen, often by analogy with water.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Oxygen as the sole requirement for hydrogen bonding in dimethyl ether
**Correct concept:** Dimethyl ether has an oxygen atom but no O-H bond, so it cannot donate hydrogen bonds between its own molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** Look for an H directly bonded to O, N, or F; dimethyl ether lacks such a donor hydrogen even though its oxygen has lone pairs.
**Concept category:** oxygen presence / water analogy
**Prevalence:** Around 45% of the students who selected the dimethyl ether distractor reasoned this way
**Population:** Students answering items 2-5
**Source:** Students' understanding of boiling points and intermolecular forces (b920829c-3/b920829c-3.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bridges between molecules can only be formed via oxygen and hydrogen atoms. (Because hydrogen fluoride- and methyl fluoride-molecules do not contain oxygen, hydrogen bridges cannot be formed.)"

## Misconception 436: Students think hydrogen bonding occurs between the oxygen of one molecule and the hydrogen of the other molecule, without specifying that the hydrogen must be attached to an electronegative atom in the donor molecule.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Hydrogen bonding requires only a hydrogen attached to oxygen of one molecule and hydrogen of another
**Correct concept:** A hydrogen bond involves a hydrogen atom covalently bonded to N, O, or F interacting with a lone pair on N, O, or F of another molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** Identify both donor and acceptor roles correctly: the donor H must already be bonded to an electronegative atom, and the acceptor must have a lone pair.
**Concept category:** hydrogen-bond donor/acceptor criteria
**Prevalence:** 31% of the students who selected the acetaldehyde/acetic acid distractor in item 6
**Population:** Students answering item 6
**Source:** Students' understanding of boiling points and intermolecular forces (b920829c-3/b920829c-3.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bridges can only be formed between the oxygen of one molecule and the hydrogen of the other....."

## Misconception 437: Students think acetic acid is the only compound that can form hydrogen bonds because it has an OH group, and they treat the OH group itself as the criterion.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Acetic acid is the only hydrogen-bonding compound because it has OH groups
**Correct concept:** Acetic acid can form hydrogen bonds, but methylamine can also form hydrogen bonds because N-H bonds and nitrogen lone pairs satisfy the hydrogen-bond condition.
**Expected reasoning:** Check all candidate molecules for H attached to N, O, or F and the presence of lone pairs on N, O, or F; do not rely only on an OH group.
**Concept category:** functional group cue / OH-group heuristic
**Prevalence:** 32% of the students who selected the acetic acid distractor in item 6
**Population:** Students answering item 6
**Source:** Students' understanding of boiling points and intermolecular forces (b920829c-3/b920829c-3.md)
**Evidence:** "The OH group of one molecule and the OH group of another molecule form hydrogen bridges."

## Misconception 438: Students think intermolecular forces are forces that occur between atoms to form molecules or compounds, i.e. they confuse intermolecular forces with intramolecular bonding.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces are attractions between separate molecules, not forces that create molecules or compounds from atoms.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish the scale of interaction: bonds within a molecule hold atoms together, while intermolecular forces act between molecules and explain properties such as boiling point and phase changes.
**Concept category:** definition
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 1; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "Intermolecular forces are forces that occur between atoms to form molecules or compound"

## Misconception 439: Students believe that a higher boiling point means more heat is needed to break the liquid's bonds, and that higher boiling point directly means stronger intermolecular forces.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Strength and boiling point
**Correct concept:** Boiling point is related to the strength of intermolecular attractions between molecules; stronger intermolecular forces generally give higher boiling points because more energy is needed to separate molecules, not to break covalent bonds within molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that boiling separates molecules from each other in the liquid phase, so the energy change reflects overcoming intermolecular attractions rather than breaking intramolecular bonds.
**Concept category:** strength of IMFs and boiling point
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 2; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "It takes a high boiling point to break the bonds in a liquid phase."

## Misconception 440: Students think that a greater boiling point means the intermolecular forces between molecules are stronger and therefore harder to break.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Strength and boiling point
**Correct concept:** A higher boiling point indicates stronger intermolecular attractions, but students should connect this to the need for more energy to separate molecules; it does not mean covalent bonds in molecules are being broken.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect macroscopic boiling behavior to microscopic molecular attractions and distinguish separating molecules from breaking bonds within molecules.
**Concept category:** strength of IMFs and boiling point
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 3; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "The greater the boiling point, the more difficult it is for the forces between the molecules of the compound to be broken so that the forces between the molecules are stronger"

## Misconception 441: Students think London forces are interactions between nonpolar molecules and permanent dipoles.

**Topic:** London forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces arise from temporary, instantaneous dipoles induced in electron clouds; they are present in all atoms and molecules, especially important in nonpolar substances.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that dispersion forces do not require a permanent dipole; they originate from momentary electron fluctuations that induce attraction between neighboring particles.
**Concept category:** London force definition
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 4; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "London forces are forces that occur between nonpolar molecules and dipole permanent."

## Misconception 442: Students think London forces occur in ethanol specifically between the ethyl group and the OH group.

**Topic:** London forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces can occur between all molecules and between regions of electron density; ethanol also exhibits dipole-dipole interactions and hydrogen bonding due to its polar O-H group.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should separate the different intermolecular forces present in ethanol and not assign dispersion only to named functional groups in a molecule.
**Concept category:** London force definition
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 5; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "London forces occur in ethanol i.e. between ethyl and OH"

## Misconception 443: Students think London forces in CH4 are caused by the tetrahedral shape of the molecule.

**Topic:** London forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces in CH4 are due to temporary induced dipoles; tetrahedral shape makes CH4 nonpolar overall, but shape itself is not the cause of dispersion forces.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should understand that molecular shape affects overall polarity, while dispersion forces come from instantaneous electron fluctuations present in all molecules.
**Concept category:** London force definition
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 6; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "London forces occur in CH4 due to the tetrahedral shape of the CH4 molecule"

## Misconception 444: Students think London force is simply a nonpolar force.

**Topic:** London forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces are not defined as a 'nonpolar force'; they are attractive forces arising from temporary dipoles and can occur in all species, though they dominate in nonpolar substances.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain the source of the force, not label it only by the polarity class of the molecule.
**Concept category:** London force definition
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 7; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "London force is a nonpolar force"

## Misconception 445: Students think London forces occur between positive and negative ions on a molecule.

**Topic:** London forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces are attractions between instantaneous and induced dipoles, not ion-ion attractions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the interacting particles correctly: dispersion involves electron cloud fluctuations, not ionic charges.
**Concept category:** London force definition
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 8; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "London force occurs between positive and negative ions on a molecule"

## Misconception 446: Students think London force is simply a force of attraction that occurs between atoms.

**Topic:** London forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces are intermolecular attractions arising from induced dipoles; saying 'between atoms' is too vague and can incorrectly imply ordinary atomic bonding.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should specify that these are transient dipole-based attractions between neighboring atoms or molecules, not generic attraction or covalent bonding.
**Concept category:** London force definition
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 9; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "The London force is a force of attraction that occurs between atoms"

## Misconception 447: Students think nonpolar molecules can undergo permanent dipoles.

**Topic:** London forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** Nonpolar molecules do not have permanent dipoles; they can only have temporary, induced dipoles that give rise to London dispersion forces.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that permanent dipoles require an uneven, persistent charge distribution, which nonpolar molecules lack.
**Concept category:** London force definition
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 10; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "Nonpolar molecules can undergo permanent dipoles"

## Misconception 448: Students think the bonds in CH4 and O2 are weak bonds in the context of London forces.

**Topic:** London forces / Strength and boiling point
**Correct concept:** CH4 and O2 are molecular substances held together within molecules by covalent bonds; London forces are the weak intermolecular attractions between molecules, not the covalent bonds inside them.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish intramolecular covalent bonding from intermolecular attraction and recognize that London forces explain physical properties of the molecular substance.
**Concept category:** London force strength
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 11; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "The bond in the compounds CH4 and O2 is a weak bond"

## Misconception 449: Students think a low boiling point is due to London forces being very weak and unrelated to the number of electrons.

**Topic:** London forces / Strength and boiling point
**Correct concept:** London dispersion strength generally increases with the number of electrons and polarizability; larger electron clouds usually lead to stronger dispersion and higher boiling points.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should relate dispersion strength to electron count and polarizability, then connect stronger dispersion to greater energy needed to separate molecules and thus higher boiling point.
**Concept category:** London force strength
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 12; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "Students think that the low boiling point is due to the London force which is indeed very weak and is not related to the number of electrons"

## Misconception 450: Students think any polar molecule such as HCl is automatically described only as having 'polar intermolecular force' rather than as having specific dipole-dipole interactions.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole forces are specific attractions between the positive end of one polar molecule and the negative end of another polar molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the partial charges on polar molecules and explain the attraction between opposite ends of neighboring molecules.
**Concept category:** dipole-dipole definition
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 13; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "HCl molecules are polar molecules, so the interaction between HCl molecules is a polar intermolecular force"

## Misconception 451: Students think the attractive force in HCl is simply because H is positive and Cl is negative as whole atoms.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole attraction is due to partial charges within polar molecules, not full ionic charges on atoms.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use the idea of partial positive and partial negative ends arising from electronegativity differences and molecular polarity.
**Concept category:** dipole-dipole definition
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 14; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "H atoms as positive atoms and Cl as negative atoms produce attractive forces"

## Misconception 452: Students think polar molecules have a positive dipole and a negative dipole in the polar region as if dipoles were objects or regions rather than a charge separation vector.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** A dipole is a separation of partial positive and partial negative charge within a molecule; dipole-dipole attraction is the interaction between those molecular dipoles.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should describe the polarity of the molecule and the alignment of partial charges, rather than treating the dipole as a separate substance or region.
**Concept category:** dipole-dipole definition
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 15; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "This polar molecule has a positive dipole and negative dipole in the polar region"

## Misconception 453: Students think dipole-dipole attraction is just positive dipoles binding to negative dipoles without explaining the molecular polarity and partial charge distribution.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole forces / Definition
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole forces are electrostatic attractions between the partially positive end of one polar molecule and the partially negative end of another polar molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify polar molecules, partial charges, and how orientation of molecules leads to attraction.
**Concept category:** dipole-dipole definition
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 16; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "Polar molecules have permanent dipoles so that positive dipoles will bind to negative dipoles."

## Misconception 454: Students think hydrogen bonds are formed because electrons are shared in a covalent bond.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Definition
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is an intermolecular attraction involving H bonded to N, O, or F interacting with a lone pair on another electronegative atom; it is not formed by shared electron use in a covalent bond.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish covalent electron sharing within a molecule from hydrogen bonding between molecules based on H attached to N/O/F and attraction to a lone pair.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding definition
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 17; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bonds are formed due to shared electron usage"

## Misconception 455: Students think hydrogen bonds occur between N and H atoms specifically.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Definition
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding occurs when hydrogen covalently bonded to N, O, or F interacts with a lone pair on another N, O, or F atom in a neighboring molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the donor atom bonded to hydrogen and the acceptor lone pair on a nearby electronegative atom, not just any N-H pair.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding definition
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 18; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bonds occur between N and H atoms"

## Misconception 456: Students think hydrogen bonding uses PEB for bonding.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Definition
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is explained by electrostatic attraction involving a hydrogen donor and lone-pair acceptor; it is not a use of 'PEB' as a bonding mechanism.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should apply the accepted model of hydrogen bonding: H attached to N/O/F and attraction to a lone pair on a neighboring molecule.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding definition
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 19; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bonds uses PEB fot bonding"

## Misconception 457: Students think hydrogen bonds occur between atoms within a molecule rather than between molecules.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Definition
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is an intermolecular force; it occurs between molecules or molecular regions, not as an intramolecular atom-to-atom bond within one molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish internal covalent bonds from external intermolecular attractions and place hydrogen bonding between molecules.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding definition
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 20; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "students define that hydrogen bonds occur between atoms in a molecule, not intermolecular"

## Misconception 458: Students think differences in electronegativity of covalent compounds affect boiling point in the same way as hydrogen-bond strength.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Strength
**Correct concept:** Boiling point depends on the overall intermolecular forces present; hydrogen bonding strength depends on the specific H-bonding interactions involving H bonded to N, O, or F and the acceptor lone pair.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect electronegativity differences to polarity and then determine whether hydrogen bonding is possible, rather than directly equating covalent bond electronegativity difference with boiling point.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding strength
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 21; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "Students assume that the difference in electronegativity of covalent compounds affects the boiling point."

## Misconception 459: Students think hydrogen bond strength is directly determined by the electronegativity difference between H and N/O/F.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Strength
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bond strength depends on factors such as the polarity of the H-X bond, the identity and proximity of the acceptor atom, and the environment; it is not simply a direct function of electronegativity difference alone.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason from molecular structure and intermolecular geometry, not from a single electronegativity-difference rule.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding strength
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 22; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "The strength of the hydrogen bond is affected by the difference in electronegativity between H and the elements N, O, and F."

## Misconception 460: Students think hydrogen bond strength is affected by PEB.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Strength
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bond strength is determined by the nature of the donor and acceptor atoms and the intermolecular electrostatic interaction, not by an undefined factor labeled PEB.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use the accepted structural criteria for hydrogen bonding and evaluate donor-acceptor interactions.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding strength
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 23; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bond strength is affected by PEB"

## Misconception 461: Students think hydrogen bond strength is related to the acidity of the substance.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Strength
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bond strength is not the same as acid strength; it depends on the intermolecular attraction between a hydrogen donor and acceptor, not on how acidic the substance is.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should separate acid-base behavior from intermolecular hydrogen bonding and identify the molecular features that allow hydrogen bonding.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding strength
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 24; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "The strength of hydrogen bonds is related to the acidity of the substance"

## Misconception 462: Students think hydrogen bonding occurs whenever H is attached to one of F, O, or N atoms, without requiring interaction with a neighboring acceptor.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Formation
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding requires a donor H bonded to F, O, or N and an acceptor lone pair on a neighboring electronegative atom; mere presence of H-F, H-O, or H-N is not sufficient.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should check both donor and acceptor requirements and recognize that hydrogen bonding is an intermolecular interaction between molecules.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bond formation
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 25; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "Students assume that hydrogen bonding occurs at the H atom with one of the F, O, and, N atoms."

## Misconception 463: Students think ether molecules form hydrogen bonds between O and H atoms within the ether molecule.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Formation
**Correct concept:** Ethers do not hydrogen bond with themselves in the same way alcohols do because they lack an O-H bond; hydrogen bonding requires a hydrogen donor and an acceptor on another molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should inspect functional groups and determine whether a molecule can donate hydrogen bonds; ethers can accept but not donate hydrogen bonds.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bond formation
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 26; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bonding occurs between O and H atoms in ether molecules"

## Misconception 464: Students think the O-H bond in an alcohol molecule is a hydrogen bond.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Formation
**Correct concept:** The O-H bond inside an alcohol is a covalent bond; hydrogen bonding is the intermolecular attraction between the alcohol molecule and another molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish intramolecular covalent bonds from intermolecular hydrogen bonds and identify the latter between molecules.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bond formation
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 27; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "The bond between the elements H and O in the alcohol molecule is a hydrogen bond"

## Misconception 465: Students think hydrogen bonding is present in the OH group of any polar molecule.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Formation
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding requires a hydrogen atom directly bonded to N, O, or F and an acceptor lone pair; not every OH-containing or polar molecule necessarily exhibits the same hydrogen-bonding behavior.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate the donor-acceptor pattern and not infer hydrogen bonding from polarity alone.
