**Concept category:** hydrogen bond formation
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 28; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bonding is present in the OH group of polar molecules"

## Misconception 466: Students think hydrogen bonding occurs in methane between carbon and hydrogen atoms.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Formation
**Correct concept:** Methane does not exhibit hydrogen bonding because C-H bonds do not create the highly polarized donor required for hydrogen bonding, and methane lacks the required electronegative atoms and lone-pair acceptors.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should determine whether the molecule contains H bonded to N/O/F and whether another molecule provides an acceptor lone pair; methane does not meet these criteria.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bond formation
**Prevalence:** Documented as misconception 29; no item-level percentage reported.
**Population:** 88 chemistry education students
**Source:** Misconceptions of Intermolecular Forces in General Chemistry Courses (b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c/b9f90a74bc2a9ed24641948001542f83316c.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bonding occurs in methane (C and H atoms)"

## Misconception 467: Students believe that both bond formation and bond breaking require energy, rather than recognizing that bond formation releases energy and bond breaking requires energy input.

**Topic:** Bond energetics / bond formation and bond breaking
**Correct concept:** Forming a chemical bond releases energy, while breaking a chemical bond requires energy input.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish the two processes: when atoms come together to form a bond, the system moves to a lower potential energy state and energy is released; when a bond is broken, energy must be supplied to overcome the attraction between atoms.
**Concept category:** bond energetics
**Prevalence:** Only about 20% chose both correct statements (B + C); 63% of the CH14 cohort chose combinations with D
**Population:** Swedish upper secondary students, Swedish first-term university chemistry students (CH13/CH14), and University of Cape Town first-term chemistry students (UCT)
**Source:** Teaching of chemical bonding: a study of Swedish and South African students' conceptions of bonding (c6rp00106h/c6rp00106h.md)
**Evidence:** "many students created a mental model where both bond formation and bond breaking require energy"

## Misconception 468: Students blend individual bond formation with exothermic/endothermic reaction concepts and may think that endothermic reaction diagrams mean bond formation requires energy.

**Topic:** Bond energetics / bond formation vs exothermic/endothermic reactions
**Correct concept:** Individual bond formation/breaking should be separated from overall reaction enthalpy; bond formation releases energy even though some reactions may be endothermic overall.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should not infer bond energetics from the overall heat flow of a reaction. They should reason about bond formation as a local process at the level of individual bonds and interpret energy diagrams correctly in that context.
**Concept category:** bond energetics
**Prevalence:** 63% of the CH14 cohort chose combinations with D
**Population:** CH14 Swedish first-year university chemistry students after studying bonding
**Source:** Teaching of chemical bonding: a study of Swedish and South African students' conceptions of bonding (c6rp00106h/c6rp00106h.md)
**Evidence:** "many students choose the alternative D which is interpreted as an indication of a blending of the concepts of individual bond forming and the concept of reactions being exo- or endothermic"

## Misconception 469: Students think that macroscopic properties such as hardness and conductivity determine the type of bond in a substance.

**Topic:** Chemical bonding and properties / properties determine bond type
**Correct concept:** Bond type determines many macroscopic properties of a substance, not the other way around.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason from microscopic structure to macroscopic properties: the bonding arrangement leads to characteristic properties such as conductivity and hardness.
**Concept category:** properties and bonding
**Population:** Secondary and tertiary chemistry students discussed in the literature review and survey design
**Source:** Teaching of chemical bonding: a study of Swedish and South African students' conceptions of bonding (c6rp00106h/c6rp00106h.md)
**Evidence:** "students are inclined to believe that the inherent (macroscopic) properties of the substance determine the bond type and not vice versa"

## Misconception 470: Students select a two-dimensional or non-tetrahedral representation as the closest shape of methane, indicating difficulty relating molecules to three-dimensional structures.

**Topic:** Molecular geometry / representation of methane shape
**Correct concept:** Methane has a three-dimensional tetrahedral shape.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use VSEPR-style reasoning or space-filling/3D models to recognize that four bonding pairs around carbon arrange tetrahedrally in 3D space.
**Concept category:** molecular geometry
**Prevalence:** Almost none chose the ceramic tile; 47% of South African students chose the tetrahedron
**Population:** Swedish upper secondary students, Swedish first-term university chemistry students, and University of Cape Town first-term chemistry students
**Source:** Teaching of chemical bonding: a study of Swedish and South African students' conceptions of bonding (c6rp00106h/c6rp00106h.md)
**Evidence:** "Students choosing the wheel spanner and the ceramic tile may have problems relating atoms and molecules to threedimensional structures."

## Misconception 471: Students think methane atoms are held together by intermolecular forces rather than covalent bonds.

**Topic:** Methane bonding / intermolecular vs covalent bonding in methane
**Correct concept:** The carbon and hydrogen atoms in methane are held together by covalent bonds within the molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify electron sharing between carbon and hydrogen atoms as the intramolecular covalent bonding mechanism, not intermolecular attraction.
**Concept category:** covalent bonding
**Population:** Students responding to Q4 about bonding in methane
**Source:** Teaching of chemical bonding: a study of Swedish and South African students' conceptions of bonding (c6rp00106h/c6rp00106h.md)
**Evidence:** "Indication of the use of concepts and with evidence of that the view of the interaction is intermolecular."

## Misconception 472: Students explain covalent bonding in methane as ionic attraction due to partial charges, rather than electron sharing.

**Topic:** Methane bonding / ionic/charge-attraction model for covalent bonds
**Correct concept:** Covalent bonding in methane is explained by shared electrons and orbital reasoning, not by ionic attraction between oppositely charged atoms.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that carbon and hydrogen share electrons to achieve stable valence configurations; the bond is not formed because like or unlike charges attract as if it were ionic.
**Concept category:** covalent bonding
**Population:** Students responding to Q4 about bonding in methane
**Source:** Teaching of chemical bonding: a study of Swedish and South African students' conceptions of bonding (c6rp00106h/c6rp00106h.md)
**Evidence:** "Indication of the use of concepts with evidence of the view of the interaction is of an ionic nature and/ or due to charge attraction."

## Misconception 473: Students think potassium chloride exists as molecules rather than as an ionic lattice.

**Topic:** Ionic bonding and lattices / molecular view of potassium chloride
**Correct concept:** Potassium chloride is an ionic solid consisting of a lattice of potassium ions and chloride ions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that ionic compounds form extended lattices, not discrete molecules, and should distinguish formula units from molecules.
**Concept category:** ionic bonding
**Prevalence:** 55-60% of students from all educational levels or curricula
**Population:** Swedish upper secondary students, Swedish first-term university chemistry students (CH13/CH14), and University of Cape Town first-term chemistry students (UCT)
**Source:** Teaching of chemical bonding: a study of Swedish and South African students' conceptions of bonding (c6rp00106h/c6rp00106h.md)
**Evidence:** "Potassium chloride exists in the form of molecules!"

## Misconception 474: Students believe that after electron transfer, the potassium ion and chloride ion form a molecule together.

**Topic:** Ionic bonding and lattices / ion-pair molecule model of KCl
**Correct concept:** After ion formation, KCl is held together in a non-directional ionic lattice; it does not become a molecule of ion pairs.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that electron transfer creates ions, but the resulting attraction organizes them into a repeating lattice rather than discrete molecular units.
**Concept category:** ionic bonding
**Population:** Students responding to Q6 on potassium chloride
**Source:** Teaching of chemical bonding: a study of Swedish and South African students' conceptions of bonding (c6rp00106h/c6rp00106h.md)
**Evidence:** "After donating its valence electron to the chlorine atom, the potassium ion forms a molecule with the chloride ion!"

## Misconception 475: Students think potassium chloride is a lattice of covalently bonded potassium and chlorine atoms.

**Topic:** Ionic bonding and lattices / covalent-bonded salt lattice
**Correct concept:** Potassium chloride is an ionic lattice of potassium ions and chloride ions, not a covalent network of atoms.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use the ionic model: electron transfer forms ions, and electrostatic attraction between oppositely charged ions holds the lattice together.
**Concept category:** ionic bonding
**Population:** Students responding to Q6 on potassium chloride
**Source:** Teaching of chemical bonding: a study of Swedish and South African students' conceptions of bonding (c6rp00106h/c6rp00106h.md)
**Evidence:** "Potassium chloride exists as a lattice of covalently bonded potassium and chlorine atoms!"

## Misconception 476: Students see ionic bonding as a bond only between two ions in a pair and fail to understand the extended, non-directional lattice.

**Topic:** Ionic bonding and lattices / bonding only between an ion pair rather than a lattice
**Correct concept:** Ionic bonding is a non-directional electrostatic attraction operating throughout the crystal lattice.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason from repeating ion arrangement and charge attraction across the whole solid, not from a single bond between one cation and one anion.
**Concept category:** ionic bonding
**Population:** Students discussing KCl lattice structure and ionic bonding
**Source:** Teaching of chemical bonding: a study of Swedish and South African students' conceptions of bonding (c6rp00106h/c6rp00106h.md)
**Evidence:** "they fail to see ionic bonding as being non-directional; instead it is strictly seen as a bond between the two ions."

## Misconception 477: Students treat the formula unit of an ionic compound as if it were a molecule.

**Topic:** Ionic bonding and formulas / formula unit mistaken for molecule
**Correct concept:** A formula unit is a representation of the simplest ion ratio in an ionic solid; it is not a discrete molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish molecular compounds from ionic solids and interpret formulas like NaCl or KCl as repeating units in a lattice.
**Concept category:** ionic bonding
**Population:** CH14 interview students and students discussing ionic compounds
**Source:** Teaching of chemical bonding: a study of Swedish and South African students' conceptions of bonding (c6rp00106h/c6rp00106h.md)
**Evidence:** "we didn't differentiate between molecules and ionic compounds"

## Misconception 478: Intermolecular forces, including London dispersion forces, occur within atoms or molecules rather than between separate species.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Where LDFs occur
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces are interactions between separate atoms or molecules, not within a single atom or molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish intramolecular structure from intermolecular interaction and recognize that the attractive force acts across the space between two separate helium atoms.
**Concept category:** surface features / location of interaction
**Population:** General chemistry students discussed in prior work cited by the paper; also reflected in the paper's framing of student misconceptions
**Source:** Characterizing Students' Mechanistic Reasoning about London Dispersion Forces (characterizing-students-mechanistic-reasoning-about-london-dispersion-forces/characterizing-students-mechanistic-reasoning-about-london-dispersion-forces.md)
**Evidence:** students ... indicated that IMFs (hydrogen bonding, dipole-dipole, or London dispersion forces [LDFs]) are interactions within (rather than between) atoms.

## Misconception 479: London dispersion forces are only present between molecules, not between atoms such as helium atoms.

**Topic:** London dispersion forces / Atoms vs molecules
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces can occur between neutral atoms as well as molecules; helium atoms can interact via LDFs.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that any neutral species with electron clouds can experience temporary charge fluctuations that lead to attraction, including atoms like helium.
**Concept category:** surface features / scope of interaction
**Population:** Devon, an interview participant
**Source:** Characterizing Students' Mechanistic Reasoning about London Dispersion Forces (characterizing-students-mechanistic-reasoning-about-london-dispersion-forces/characterizing-students-mechanistic-reasoning-about-london-dispersion-forces.md)
**Evidence:** he believed that intermolecular forces take place between molecules only (and not atoms).

## Misconception 480: Helium atoms will overcome repulsion and form an He2 molecule/covalent bond when they interact.

**Topic:** London dispersion forces / Covalent bonding expectation
**Correct concept:** Helium atoms do not form a covalent bond or stable He2 molecule in the context discussed; the key interaction is a weak intermolecular London dispersion attraction.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that helium atoms remain separate neutral atoms and that any attraction is due to temporary induced dipoles, not electron sharing or molecule formation.
**Concept category:** bonding misconception / molecule formation
**Population:** Devon, an interview participant
**Source:** Characterizing Students' Mechanistic Reasoning about London Dispersion Forces (characterizing-students-mechanistic-reasoning-about-london-dispersion-forces/characterizing-students-mechanistic-reasoning-about-london-dispersion-forces.md)
**Evidence:** then once they get over those repulsive forces, then they're gonna form a molecule of helium.

## Misconception 481: Atoms are attracted because of generic stability, the octet rule, or because atoms 'want' to bond, rather than because of electrostatic interactions from electron-cloud fluctuations.

**Topic:** London dispersion forces / Driving cause
**Correct concept:** The attraction arises from electrostatic interactions caused by momentary electron density fluctuations that create an instantaneous dipole and an induced dipole.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain that random electron motion can create an uneven charge distribution, which induces a dipole in a neighboring atom and leads to attraction.
**Concept category:** non-electrostatic cause
**Prevalence:** Observed in the interview study; E1 was the least sophisticated category and more common in the online assessment
**Population:** Undergraduate general chemistry students; one interview participant and more students in the online assessment
**Source:** Characterizing Students' Mechanistic Reasoning about London Dispersion Forces (characterizing-students-mechanistic-reasoning-about-london-dispersion-forces/characterizing-students-mechanistic-reasoning-about-london-dispersion-forces.md)
**Evidence:** These included the notions of "stability" or the octet rule as driving atoms to share electrons.

## Misconception 482: Atoms attract because they have an overall positive and negative charge, or because protons and electrons simply attract without a dipole/charge separation concept.

**Topic:** London dispersion forces / Meaning of charge in atoms
**Correct concept:** Neutral helium atoms attract through temporary separation of charge within the atom, producing instantaneous and induced dipoles rather than overall atomic charge.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect the arrangement of electrons and protons to a temporary dipole, not just say that positive and negative charges attract in a generic way.
**Concept category:** electrostatic cause without dipole
**Prevalence:** This was the E2 type; 43% of the 250 online responses were E2 explanations
**Population:** 250 undergraduate general chemistry online responses
**Source:** Characterizing Students' Mechanistic Reasoning about London Dispersion Forces (characterizing-students-mechanistic-reasoning-about-london-dispersion-forces/characterizing-students-mechanistic-reasoning-about-london-dispersion-forces.md)
**Evidence:** I believe that helium atoms move towards each other because of the positive and negative charges within the atom. Since the atoms both have opposite charges, they attract to one another

## Misconception 483: A student can explain the interaction by saying electrons fluctuate or dipoles are temporary, without being able to explain how electron-density fluctuation produces the dipole.

**Topic:** London dispersion forces / Mechanism of dipole formation
**Correct concept:** Students should explain that electrons are in constant motion; random fluctuations can create an instantaneous uneven electron distribution, which makes one side of an atom partially negative and the other partially positive.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should trace the causal sequence from electron motion to uneven charge distribution to dipole formation and then to attraction.
**Concept category:** electrostatic cause / incomplete mechanism
**Prevalence:** Three interview participants were E3; two additional participants used terms like temporary dipole/fluctuation superficially
**Population:** Interview participants in Study 1
**Source:** Characterizing Students' Mechanistic Reasoning about London Dispersion Forces (characterizing-students-mechanistic-reasoning-about-london-dispersion-forces/characterizing-students-mechanistic-reasoning-about-london-dispersion-forces.md)
**Evidence:** She mentioned that electrons "fluctuate", but seemed unsure of what this term meant.

## Misconception 484: Both interacting atoms must simultaneously experience temporary dipoles for attraction to occur.

**Topic:** London dispersion forces / Mechanism of interaction timing
**Correct concept:** A temporary dipole in one atom can induce a dipole in a neighboring atom; simultaneous temporary dipoles are not required.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that one atom's momentary dipole polarizes the neighbor, creating an induced dipole and thus attraction.
**Concept category:** mechanistic timing / induced dipole
**Prevalence:** This idea was expressed by 2 of the 12 interview participants
**Population:** 12 semi-structured interview participants
**Source:** Characterizing Students' Mechanistic Reasoning about London Dispersion Forces (characterizing-students-mechanistic-reasoning-about-london-dispersion-forces/characterizing-students-mechanistic-reasoning-about-london-dispersion-forces.md)
**Evidence:** She did not seem to recognize that a more likely scenario is that a temporary dipole in one atom would induce a dipole in a second.

## Misconception 485: Atoms interact because their electron cloud is 'floppy' or overlaps, without a coherent explanation of dipole formation from electron density fluctuation.

**Topic:** London dispersion forces / Charge separation mechanism
**Correct concept:** The interaction comes from an uneven electron distribution creating a temporary dipole, which then induces a dipole in the neighboring atom.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the electrostatic origin of the force and explain how shifting electron density creates partial charges across the atom.
**Concept category:** electrostatic cause / flawed mechanism
**Population:** Macayla, an interview participant
**Source:** Characterizing Students' Mechanistic Reasoning about London Dispersion Forces (characterizing-students-mechanistic-reasoning-about-london-dispersion-forces/characterizing-students-mechanistic-reasoning-about-london-dispersion-forces.md)
**Evidence:** Umm, their electron cloud is...floppy. So then it moves one way for an instant.

## Misconception 486: The paper identifies a misconception of the interaction as simply 'holding on' or electrons linking atoms without recognizing the need for a dipole; however the student did correctly reason that closer distance strengthens attraction.

**Topic:** London dispersion forces / Distance dependence of attraction
**Correct concept:** Electrostatic attraction depends on distance, but the key mechanism is the presence of a dipole and induced dipole, not just generic holding together or linking of electrons.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect distance-dependent electrostatic force to a specific charge separation in the atoms.
**Concept category:** electrostatic cause / incomplete mechanism
**Population:** Ryan, an interview participant
**Source:** Characterizing Students' Mechanistic Reasoning about London Dispersion Forces (characterizing-students-mechanistic-reasoning-about-london-dispersion-forces/characterizing-students-mechanistic-reasoning-about-london-dispersion-forces.md)
**Evidence:** Um, it was kind of just a representation of a, some sort of...like a, not dipole—dipole. It's a London dispersion bond.

## Misconception 487: Strength of electrical interaction is not understood as inversely related to distance between interacting particles.

**Topic:** Electrostatic interactions / Distance dependence
**Correct concept:** Electrical attraction becomes weaker as distance increases and stronger as particles get closer together.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that the nearer electron and nucleus experience a stronger attraction than more distant ones.
**Concept category:** electrostatic reasoning
**Prevalence:** Identified across levels; students with all levels used this idea
**Population:** Students across explanation levels in both studies
**Source:** Characterizing Students' Mechanistic Reasoning about London Dispersion Forces (characterizing-students-mechanistic-reasoning-about-london-dispersion-forces/characterizing-students-mechanistic-reasoning-about-london-dispersion-forces.md)
**Evidence:** he correctly identified that the strength of an electrical interaction would be inversely related to the distance between particles

## Misconception 488: Students use visual estimation from an image to judge bond length, rather than inferring bond length from bond order and atomic identity.

**Topic:** Bond length / Bond order and bond length
**Correct concept:** For SO2, both S–O bonds are the same length because they are equivalent bonds with the same bond order.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify that each bond is between the same pair of atoms (S and O) and that each S–O bond has bond order 2; equivalent bonds with the same bond order should have the same length.
**Concept category:** visual estimation
**Prevalence:** Visual estimation was coded in 5.8% of bond-length responses (63/1080).
**Population:** 1086 second-semester general chemistry students; bond-length open-ended responses analyzed N=1080
**Source:** The impact of representations of chemical bonding on students' predictions of chemical properties (d1rp00070e/d1rp00070e.md)
**Evidence:** "students may attempt a visual estimation of the length and predict that the bonds are of different lengths based on what their visual estimation of length from the image provided"

## Misconception 489: Students treat the space-filling model's apparent atom sizes as a basis for deciding that the S–O bonds are unequal or for misjudging bond length.

**Topic:** Bond length / Bond order and bond length
**Correct concept:** Bond length should be inferred from chemical structure, especially bond order and whether the bonds are equivalent, not from superficial visual size cues in the model.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that the model is a representation and that bond length depends on the underlying bonding pattern; because both S–O bonds are equivalent, their lengths should be the same.
**Concept category:** surface features
**Prevalence:** Size of atom was coded in 6.7% of bond-length responses (72/1080); it was the notable deleterious feature for correctness.
**Population:** 1086 second-semester general chemistry students; space-filling group especially cued to size of atom
**Source:** The impact of representations of chemical bonding on students' predictions of chemical properties (d1rp00070e/d1rp00070e.md)
**Evidence:** "students might make an inaccurate visual estimate of the representation given or may be cued to features that are unrelated to the chemical property"

## Misconception 490: Students rely on resonance or Lewis structure conventions they infer from a chemical formula, even though the formula itself does not show bonding details.

**Topic:** Bond length / Chemical formula and bond length
**Correct concept:** The chemical formula alone does not show bond order or resonance; to infer equal bond lengths, students need to use actual structural information and recognize the equivalent S–O bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should not infer bond length directly from formula symbols; instead they should use a structure that shows connectivity and bond order, or reason from known chemistry that the two S–O bonds are equivalent.
**Concept category:** chemical conventions
**Prevalence:** Resonance was coded in 4.1% of bond-length responses (44/1080).
**Population:** 1086 second-semester general chemistry students
**Source:** The impact of representations of chemical bonding on students' predictions of chemical properties (d1rp00070e/d1rp00070e.md)
**Evidence:** "students with chemical formula were more often cued to Lewis dot structures and resonance"

## Misconception 491: Students think a linear shape means SO2 is nonpolar.

**Topic:** Polarity / Molecular shape and polarity
**Correct concept:** SO2 is bent, so its bond dipoles do not cancel and the molecule is polar.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should determine the molecular geometry from the structure, recognize SO2 is bent, then infer that the polar S–O bonds produce a net dipole that does not cancel.
**Concept category:** shape/molecular geometry
**Prevalence:** Linear shape was coded in 1.5% of polarity responses (16/1081).
**Population:** 1086 second-semester general chemistry students; polarity open-ended responses analyzed N=1081
**Source:** The impact of representations of chemical bonding on students' predictions of chemical properties (d1rp00070e/d1rp00070e.md)
**Evidence:** "No students citing linear shape made a correct polarity prediction"

## Misconception 492: Students assume a Lewis dot structure explicitly shows molecular shape, and may therefore misread the geometry as linear.

**Topic:** Polarity / Molecular shape and polarity
**Correct concept:** Lewis dot structures do not explicitly determine the molecular shape; the bent geometry of SO2 must be inferred using electron domains/VSEPR.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should count electron groups around sulfur, apply VSEPR, and conclude the molecule is bent rather than reading shape directly off the Lewis structure.
**Concept category:** Lewis structure interpretation
**Prevalence:** 3.4% of students receiving Lewis dot structure described a linear shape, and all of those students were incorrect on polarity.
**Population:** Students receiving Lewis dot structure in the polarity task
**Source:** The impact of representations of chemical bonding on students' predictions of chemical properties (d1rp00070e/d1rp00070e.md)
**Evidence:** "a small proportion of students attribute molecular shape as an explicit property of Lewis dot structures"

## Misconception 493: Students believe the bond dipoles in SO2 cancel out, making the molecule nonpolar.

**Topic:** Polarity / Dipole cancellation
**Correct concept:** In bent SO2, the bond dipoles do not cancel, so the molecule has a net dipole moment and is polar.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the S–O bonds as polar, then use the bent shape to conclude that the dipole vectors add rather than cancel.
**Concept category:** dipole moment
**Prevalence:** Net dipole moment zero was coded in 1.8% of polarity responses (19/1081).
**Population:** 1086 second-semester general chemistry students
**Source:** The impact of representations of chemical bonding on students' predictions of chemical properties (d1rp00070e/d1rp00070e.md)
**Evidence:** "the dipole forces cancel each other out making the structure of the molecule non-polar."

## Misconception 494: Students think the S–O bonds are nonpolar because electrons are shared equally or because the molecule has equal sharing of bonds.

**Topic:** Polarity / Bond polarity
**Correct concept:** S and O have different electronegativities, so each S–O bond is polar and contributes to the molecule's overall polarity.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare electronegativities of sulfur and oxygen, conclude the bond is polar, then combine that with bent geometry to infer the molecule is polar.
**Concept category:** bond polarity
**Prevalence:** Nature of nonpolar bonds was coded in 1.1% of polarity responses (12/1081).
**Population:** 1086 second-semester general chemistry students
**Source:** The impact of representations of chemical bonding on students' predictions of chemical properties (d1rp00070e/d1rp00070e.md)
**Evidence:** "This molecule is a nonpolar, because there is equal sharing of bonds in the electrons."

## Misconception 495: Students think the bond dipoles or charges simply cancel because of symmetry-like reasoning without applying the actual bent geometry of SO2.

**Topic:** Polarity / Interpreting dipole moment language
**Correct concept:** The bent molecular shape prevents cancellation of the bond dipoles, so the molecule remains polar.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason from actual geometry and vector addition of bond dipoles, not from a vague idea that opposite charges always cancel.
**Concept category:** dipole moment
**Prevalence:** Net dipole moment nonzero was coded in 4.7% of polarity responses (51/1081).
**Population:** 1086 second-semester general chemistry students
**Source:** The impact of representations of chemical bonding on students' predictions of chemical properties (d1rp00070e/d1rp00070e.md)
**Evidence:** "the bond dipoles of the two s=o bonds do not cancel"

## Misconception 496: Students identify the strongest intermolecular force as occurring within H2O, confusing an intermolecular attraction with an intramolecular bond.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Location of intermolecular force
**Correct concept:** The strongest intermolecular force between SO2 and H2O occurs between H of water and O of SO2, not within one molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish intermolecular attraction between two molecules from intramolecular bonding within a molecule, then locate the attraction between the partially positive H in water and partially negative O in SO2.
**Concept category:** intermolecular vs intramolecular
**Prevalence:** The most common distractor for location was between O in H2O and H in H2O; exact percentage not given in text.
**Population:** Students answering the intermolecular-force location prompt
**Source:** The impact of representations of chemical bonding on students' predictions of chemical properties (d1rp00070e/d1rp00070e.md)
**Evidence:** "students mistake an intermolecular force for an intramolecular bond"

## Misconception 497: Students select dipole-dipole interaction instead of hydrogen bonding as the strongest force between SO2 and H2O.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Type of intermolecular force
**Correct concept:** The strongest intermolecular force between SO2 and H2O is hydrogen bonding, because water hydrogen interacts with oxygen in SO2.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify that water has H bonded to O and that SO2 has a suitable electronegative O site, then apply the definition of hydrogen bonding rather than choosing the weaker generic dipole-dipole interaction.
**Concept category:** intermolecular force type
**Prevalence:** Among incorrect responses, 41.4% of chemical-formula students selected dipole-dipole interaction.
**Population:** Students receiving chemical formula in the intermolecular-force type prompt
**Source:** The impact of representations of chemical bonding on students' predictions of chemical properties (d1rp00070e/d1rp00070e.md)
**Evidence:** "students receiving chemical formula were more likely to select dipole-dipole interaction"

## Misconception 498: Students think hydrogen bonding requires H bonded to O in the wrong molecule or otherwise misassign hydrogen bonding as an intramolecular feature.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Hydrogen bonding location
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding between SO2 and H2O occurs between the H of water and the O of sulfur dioxide.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should apply the hydrogen-bonding definition and identify the donor/acceptor sites across the two molecules, not within one molecule.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Prevalence:** Hydrogen bonding – intramolecular was coded in 16.7% of intermolecular-force responses (179/1070).
**Population:** 1086 second-semester general chemistry students; intermolecular-force open-ended responses analyzed N=1070
**Source:** The impact of representations of chemical bonding on students' predictions of chemical properties (d1rp00070e/d1rp00070e.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bond is between hydrogen and oxygen"

## Misconception 499: Students state that hydrogen bonding is present without specifying the correct molecular location or applying it to the pair of molecules.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Hydrogen bonding definition
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding must be identified as an intermolecular interaction between specific atoms on two different molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should name hydrogen bonding and explicitly connect it to H in water and O in SO2 to justify the strongest interaction.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Prevalence:** Hydrogen bonding – ambiguous was coded in 16.6% of intermolecular-force responses (178/1070).
**Population:** 1086 second-semester general chemistry students
**Source:** The impact of representations of chemical bonding on students' predictions of chemical properties (d1rp00070e/d1rp00070e.md)
**Evidence:** "Student mentions hydrogen bonding without specifying location"

## Misconception 500: Students treat hydrogen bonds as covalent bonds between water molecules, including hydrogen-to-hydrogen or oxygen-to-oxygen bonds between neighboring molecules.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding in water / Intermolecular vs intramolecular interactions
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonds are intermolecular electrostatic attractions, not covalent bonds, and in water they form between a hydrogen atom on one molecule and a lone pair on the oxygen atom of another molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that covalent bonds hold atoms together within a water molecule, while hydrogen bonds are weaker attractions between separate molecules caused by polarity; therefore, the interaction is drawn between H of one molecule and O lone pairs of another, not as a shared covalent bond between molecules.
**Concept category:** intermolecular vs intramolecular
**Prevalence:** 10/60 diagrams
**Population:** 60 first- and second-year undergraduate chemistry students
**Source:** Analysis of students' diagrams of water molecules in snowflakes to reveal their conceptual understanding of hydrogen bonds (d2rp00175f/d2rp00175f.md)
**Evidence:** "intermolecular interactions are represented as covalent bonds between molecules rather than as electrostatic interactions; hydrogen bonds are formed between hydrogen and hydrogen atoms, or oxygen and oxygen atoms."

## Misconception 501: Students think a hydrogen atom in water can form more than one covalent bond, or that oxygen can make double bonds to hydrogen to complete an octet like carbon dioxide.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding in water / Structure of water and bonding capacity
**Correct concept:** In water, each hydrogen forms only one covalent bond, and the water molecule has a bent structure with two O-H covalent bonds and two lone pairs on oxygen.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should apply valence and bonding rules: hydrogen can only have one bond, oxygen typically forms two covalent bonds in water, and lone pairs on oxygen influence molecular shape but do not create extra covalent bonds to hydrogen.
**Concept category:** bonding capacity/structure of water
**Prevalence:** part of 3/10 category A diagrams
**Population:** 60 first- and second-year undergraduate chemistry students
**Source:** Analysis of students' diagrams of water molecules in snowflakes to reveal their conceptual understanding of hydrogen bonds (d2rp00175f/d2rp00175f.md)
**Evidence:** "the oxygen atom formed double bonds with hydrogen atoms to complete an octet"

## Misconception 502: Students are unsure how lone pairs participate and may think lone pairs on oxygen collectively form one negatively charged region that attracts neighboring molecules without a clear directional lone-pair-to-hydrogen interaction.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding in water / Role of lone pairs
**Correct concept:** Each lone pair on oxygen can serve as a hydrogen-bond acceptor site, and hydrogen bonds are directional interactions between a specific hydrogen atom and a specific lone pair on a neighboring molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify individual lone pairs as distinct electron-rich sites on oxygen and match them with hydrogen atoms on adjacent molecules, showing a directional interaction rather than a diffuse attraction from an undifferentiated negative region.
**Concept category:** lone pairs/electron density
**Prevalence:** 20/60 diagrams
**Population:** 60 first- and second-year undergraduate chemistry students
**Source:** Analysis of students' diagrams of water molecules in snowflakes to reveal their conceptual understanding of hydrogen bonds (d2rp00175f/d2rp00175f.md)
**Evidence:** "the role of lone pairs of electrons in forming a hydrogen bond is unclear. The lone pairs form an electron dense region around the oxygen atom to form one or numerous hydrogen bonds"

## Misconception 503: Students think the two lone pairs on oxygen collectively form a single partially negative region that attracts multiple neighbors without directionality.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding in water / Role of lone pairs
**Correct concept:** The lone pairs are separate electron domains on oxygen and hydrogen bonding involves specific donor-acceptor directions between particular sites on adjacent molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should represent each lone pair as a distinct accepting site and show how each site participates in a limited number of directional hydrogen bonds according to molecular geometry.
**Concept category:** lone pairs/electron density
**Prevalence:** about half of category B diagrams; 8/20 category B diagrams
**Population:** 60 first- and second-year undergraduate chemistry students
**Source:** Analysis of students' diagrams of water molecules in snowflakes to reveal their conceptual understanding of hydrogen bonds (d2rp00175f/d2rp00175f.md)
**Evidence:** "the two lone pairs of electrons collectively formed a single partially negative ( δ - ) region"

## Misconception 504: Students think a hydrogen atom in water can attract more than one neighboring oxygen, allowing one hydrogen to make multiple hydrogen bonds.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding in water / Hydrogen-bond donor capacity
**Correct concept:** Each hydrogen atom can donate only one hydrogen bond because it has only one covalently bonded hydrogen available as the donor site.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that a single hydrogen participates in one covalent O-H bond and can serve as one hydrogen-bond donor to one acceptor at a time, constrained by water's geometry.
**Concept category:** hydrogen-bond donor capacity
**Prevalence:** reported in category B; specific count not given
**Population:** 60 first- and second-year undergraduate chemistry students
**Source:** Analysis of students' diagrams of water molecules in snowflakes to reveal their conceptual understanding of hydrogen bonds (d2rp00175f/d2rp00175f.md)
**Evidence:** "each hydrogen atom of a central molecule appeared to have enough positive charge to attract more than one oxygen atom from neighbouring water molecules"

## Misconception 505: Students think each individual nonbonding electron on oxygen can independently form a hydrogen bond, as if each electron is a separate bonding site.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding in water / Hydrogen bond acceptor capacity
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding involves the lone pair as the acceptor site on oxygen; it is not each electron individually that forms a bond.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason in terms of lone pairs/electron domains and molecular geometry, not individual electrons; the oxygen atom offers acceptor lone-pair sites for hydrogen bonding.
**Concept category:** lone pairs/electron density
**Prevalence:** 8/20 category B diagrams
**Population:** 60 first- and second-year undergraduate chemistry students
**Source:** Analysis of students' diagrams of water molecules in snowflakes to reveal their conceptual understanding of hydrogen bonds (d2rp00175f/d2rp00175f.md)
**Evidence:** "each of the four nonbonding electrons on the oxygen atom in a water molecule can individually form a hydrogen bond with a hydrogen atom in a neighbouring water molecule"

## Misconception 506: Students think hydrogen bonds are nondirectional attractions between broad positive and negative regions and can be represented in a flat 2D plane.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding in water / Directionality and spatial arrangement
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonds are directional intermolecular interactions between specific hydrogen atoms and lone pairs on neighboring molecules, and real water networks in snowflakes are three-dimensional.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should place molecules according to water's tetrahedral electron-domain geometry and show specific H-to-lone-pair interactions in 3D rather than a flat, undirected arrangement.
**Concept category:** spatial reasoning/directionality
**Prevalence:** 25/60 diagrams for 2D space; 5/60 for 3D space
**Population:** 60 first- and second-year undergraduate chemistry students
**Source:** Analysis of students' diagrams of water molecules in snowflakes to reveal their conceptual understanding of hydrogen bonds (d2rp00175f/d2rp00175f.md)
**Evidence:** "hydrogen bonds were not covalent in nature – they are electrostatic interactions between molecules but the role of lone pairs of electrons in forming a hydrogen bond is unclear"

## Misconception 507: Students think water molecules in snowflakes interact only in a flat plane rather than in three dimensions.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding in water / Hydrogen-bond geometry in snowflakes
**Correct concept:** In snowflakes, water molecules form a 3D tetrahedral hydrogen-bond network with four hydrogen bonds per water molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should infer from water's molecular geometry that hydrogen bonding extends in three dimensions, with two donor and two acceptor sites arranged tetrahedrally.
**Concept category:** spatial reasoning
**Prevalence:** 25/60 diagrams
**Population:** 60 first- and second-year undergraduate chemistry students
**Source:** Analysis of students' diagrams of water molecules in snowflakes to reveal their conceptual understanding of hydrogen bonds (d2rp00175f/d2rp00175f.md)
**Evidence:** "the interactions among water molecules were represented in 2D space"

## Misconception 508: Students think a water molecule can form only two hydrogen bonds because only the lone pairs on oxygen participate, or otherwise fail to recognize the four-bond maximum in snowflakes.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding in water / Hydrogen-bond network size
**Correct concept:** Each water molecule can form up to four hydrogen bonds: two as donor through its hydrogens and two as acceptor through its lone pairs.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should count both donor and acceptor sites on water and use the tetrahedral arrangement to explain why the maximum number is four.
**Concept category:** hydrogen-bond network size
**Prevalence:** present in some category C diagrams; specific count not given
**Population:** 60 first- and second-year undergraduate chemistry students
**Source:** Analysis of students' diagrams of water molecules in snowflakes to reveal their conceptual understanding of hydrogen bonds (d2rp00175f/d2rp00175f.md)
**Evidence:** "one water molecule in a snowflake could form a maximum of two hydrogen bonds with neighbouring molecules in 2D"

## Misconception 509: Students think a water molecule can form more than four hydrogen bonds because molecules can approach too closely in a 2D representation.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding in water / Hydrogen-bond network size
**Correct concept:** A water molecule has only four hydrogen-bonding sites in total, so it cannot exceed four hydrogen bonds in the snowflake network.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use the two hydrogens and two lone pairs as the limit on hydrogen-bond formation and recognize that geometry prevents more than four total hydrogen bonds per molecule.
**Concept category:** hydrogen-bond network size
**Prevalence:** in one category C example (Albert); not quantified for all students
**Population:** 60 first- and second-year undergraduate chemistry students
**Source:** Analysis of students' diagrams of water molecules in snowflakes to reveal their conceptual understanding of hydrogen bonds (d2rp00175f/d2rp00175f.md)
**Evidence:** "water molecules could approach each other so closely that a hydrogen atom in one water molecule could form more than one hydrogen bond and a water molecule could form more than four hydrogen bonds with neighbouring molecules"

## Misconception 510: Students think two oxygen atoms from different molecules can share a common hydrogen atom as part of hydrogen bonding.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding in water / Shared-hydrogen misconception
**Correct concept:** A hydrogen bond is not a shared covalent hydrogen between two oxygens; it is an attraction between a hydrogen bonded to one oxygen and a lone pair on another oxygen.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish covalent O-H bonds within a molecule from hydrogen-bond attractions between molecules and avoid depicting a hydrogen atom as being jointly shared by two oxygens.
**Concept category:** intermolecular vs intramolecular
**Prevalence:** listed among alternative conceptions in the conclusion; specific count not given
**Population:** 60 first- and second-year undergraduate chemistry students
**Source:** Analysis of students' diagrams of water molecules in snowflakes to reveal their conceptual understanding of hydrogen bonds (d2rp00175f/d2rp00175f.md)
**Evidence:** "two oxygen atoms of different molecules sharing a common hydrogen atom between them"

## Misconception 511: Students think dipole-dipole interactions occur within a single molecule rather than between two molecules.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Location of dipole-dipole interactions
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole interactions are intermolecular forces that occur between separate molecules, not within one molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the partial positive region of one polar molecule and the partial negative region of another polar molecule, then connect the interacting atoms across the two molecules.
**Concept category:** intermolecular force location
**Prevalence:** a large portion of the students (no exact count given)
**Population:** students in a study of intermolecular forces cited in the paper
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole-dipole interactions (d4rp00025k/d4rp00025k.md)
**Evidence:** “a large portion of the students believed that intermolecular forces were within a single molecule rather than between”

## Misconception 512: Students treat lone pairs as bonding sites that should be shared or filled by forming a new bond between molecules.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Lone pairs and covalent bonding
**Correct concept:** Lone pairs are nonbonding electron pairs; they do not become shared covalent bonds in a dipole-dipole interaction task.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use lone pairs to infer molecular polarity or geometry when appropriate, but should still locate dipole-dipole attraction between partial charges on separate molecules, not create a new covalent bond.
**Concept category:** intermolecular force location / bonding availability
**Population:** participants in this study
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole-dipole interactions (d4rp00025k/d4rp00025k.md)
**Evidence:** “participants carried a conception that these interactions could be thought of in a similar fashion to covalent bonding.”

## Misconception 513: Students think lone pairs are ‘available’ or ‘free to bond’ with other atoms, so they identify the lone pair itself as the interaction site.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Lone pairs
**Correct concept:** Lone pairs are electron pairs on an atom; they can influence polarity and geometry, but the intermolecular attraction is between molecular dipoles, not a lone pair bonding with another atom.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that lone pairs contribute to electron density and molecular shape, then use the resulting dipole orientation to determine where the attraction between molecules occurs.
**Concept category:** lone pairs / bonding availability
**Prevalence:** 5 students in Version 1 (Sawyer, Sasha, Sadie, Sabrina, Sebastian)
**Population:** Version 1 students working with the Lewis dot representation
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole-dipole interactions (d4rp00025k/d4rp00025k.md)
**Evidence:** “the lone pairs represent sites that are free to bond with other atoms”

## Misconception 514: Students think a molecule has an ‘open spot’ or incomplete octet that must be filled, and use that as the dipole-dipole interaction site.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Open site / octet reasoning
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole interactions are not determined by filling an octet; they depend on the alignment of partial charges in polar molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate electronegativity and molecular geometry to locate partial positive and negative ends, rather than searching for empty bonding sites.
**Concept category:** octet / open-site misconception
**Prevalence:** observed in Version 1 and discussed as recurring across participants; exact count not given
**Population:** students in Version 1 and in the discussion of the study overall
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole-dipole interactions (d4rp00025k/d4rp00025k.md)
**Evidence:** “students considered the space around each atom as the location where dipole-dipole interactions could occur”

## Misconception 515: Students assume molecular geometry is explicit in Lewis dot structures and can be read directly without considering lone pairs.

**Topic:** Molecular geometry / Lewis dot structures
**Correct concept:** Lewis dot structures do not explicitly show 3D geometry; geometry must be inferred from electron domains and lone pairs.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should count bonding pairs and lone pairs around the central atom to infer shape, then use that shape to determine polarity and the dipole-dipole interaction site.
**Concept category:** geometry from representation
**Prevalence:** 3 students in Version 2 (Regan, Rachel, Rebecca)
**Population:** Version 2 students working with the Lewis dot representation
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole-dipole interactions (d4rp00025k/d4rp00025k.md)
**Evidence:** “three students carried the assumption that molecular geometry was explicit in Lewis dot structures”

## Misconception 516: Students think selenium dioxide is linear and nonpolar when lone pairs are ignored.

**Topic:** Molecular geometry and polarity / SeO2 Lewis dot task
**Correct concept:** SeO2 has lone pairs on selenium that create a bent molecular geometry, making the molecule polar.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should include selenium’s lone pairs in the electron-domain arrangement, infer a bent shape, and then recognize that the dipoles do not cancel.
**Concept category:** geometry / polarity
**Prevalence:** 3 students in Version 2 initially (Regan, Rachel, Rebecca)
**Population:** Version 2 students in the Lewis dot structure task for selenium dioxide
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole-dipole interactions (d4rp00025k/d4rp00025k.md)
**Evidence:** “designated the molecule as linear and non-polar, without taking into account the influence of lone pairs”

## Misconception 517: Students think bigger atoms produce stronger dipole-dipole interactions, or that size itself determines the interaction site.

**Topic:** Atom size and intermolecular forces / Ball-and-stick and space-filling models
**Correct concept:** Atom size in these models is not the basis for dipole-dipole attraction; the interaction depends on molecular polarity and partial charges.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use electronegativity and molecular geometry to determine the dipole direction, and treat size as a representational feature not directly tied to interaction strength.
**Concept category:** surface features / atom size
**Prevalence:** 3 students in Version 1 (Sandeep, Sebastian, Spencer) used size in the ball-and-stick task; Sandeep remained consistent with size in space-filling
**Population:** Version 1 students working with ball-and-stick and space-filling representations
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole-dipole interactions (d4rp00025k/d4rp00025k.md)
**Evidence:** “The bigger the two atoms that are bonding are, the weaker the forces are.”

## Misconception 518: Students think the number of bonds indicates the strength of dipole-dipole interactions or that more bonds mean a stronger intermolecular force.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Bond order and strength
**Correct concept:** Bond order describes intramolecular bonding; dipole-dipole strength is determined by molecular polarity and the arrangement of partial charges between molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should separate intramolecular bond order from intermolecular force strength, then compare electronegativity differences and geometry to locate the dipole-dipole interaction.
**Concept category:** bond order / force strength
**Prevalence:** observed in Version 2 ball-and-stick task; exact count not given
**Population:** Version 2 students in the ball-and-stick representation
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole-dipole interactions (d4rp00025k/d4rp00025k.md)
**Evidence:** “the number of bonds signifies the strength of dipole-dipole interactions in the molecule”

## Misconception 519: Students assign ionic or valence charges to atoms and use opposite charges as the basis for dipole-dipole attraction.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Valence charge / ionic interpretation
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole interactions arise from partial charges in polar covalent molecules, not from assigning full ionic charges to atoms.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should infer partial positive and partial negative regions from electronegativity and molecular geometry, rather than treating atoms as ions with fixed charges.
**Concept category:** valence charge / ionic reasoning
**Prevalence:** 4 students in Version 2 (Ria, Rachel, Rebecca, Richard)
**Population:** Version 2 students working with the space-filling representation
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole-dipole interactions (d4rp00025k/d4rp00025k.md)
**Evidence:** “students began assigning charges to each of the atoms in the molecules”

## Misconception 520: Students think dipole-dipole interaction could cause atoms to rearrange into new products, as if the molecules were undergoing a reaction.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Bond formation / double-displacement reasoning
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole interaction is an intermolecular attraction in the present molecules; it does not imply atom rearrangement or product formation.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should locate the most positive and negative ends of the two molecules and draw an attraction between them without changing the molecules’ connectivity.
**Concept category:** reaction vs interaction
**Population:** Richard in Version 2
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole-dipole interactions (d4rp00025k/d4rp00025k.md)
**Evidence:** “I guess you could rearrange the atoms so that it would make each-- HCl would be a product.”

## Misconception 521: Students think a central atom cannot participate in dipole-dipole interactions because it is central or less accessible.

**Topic:** Molecular polarity / Central atom reasoning
**Correct concept:** The central atom can still contribute to molecular polarity and can be part of the dipole orientation; being central does not prevent intermolecular attraction.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate the full molecular dipole from electronegativity and geometry, including the central atom’s role in bond polarity.
**Concept category:** central atom / accessibility
**Prevalence:** observed in Version 2 chemical formula task; exact count not given
**Population:** Rachel and also Ranger/Richard with central carbon in Version 2
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole-dipole interactions (d4rp00025k/d4rp00025k.md)
**Evidence:** “chlorine is the central… and I don't really know how it's going to connect to the hydrogen”

## Misconception 522: Students think any bent geometry automatically means a molecule has dipole moments or that geometry alone determines polarity.

**Topic:** Molecular geometry and polarity / Bent geometry
**Correct concept:** Bent geometry can contribute to polarity, but polarity must be evaluated by considering bond polarity and whether dipoles cancel.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify molecular shape from lone pairs and then determine whether the vector sum of bond dipoles is nonzero.
**Concept category:** geometry / polarity
**Prevalence:** 1 student explicitly: Roger in Version 2
**Population:** Version 2 ball-and-stick task
**Source:** How ordering concrete and abstract representations in intermolecular force chemistry tasks influences students' thought processes on the location of dipole-dipole interactions (d4rp00025k/d4rp00025k.md)
**Evidence:** “molecules with bent geometry always have dipole moments”

## Misconception 523: Atoms form bonds because they "want" an octet.

**Topic:** bonding and atomic stability / reason bonds form
**Correct concept:** Atoms form bonds because the bonded system is more stable than the unbonded system, i.e. bonding lowers the system's energy.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that bonding occurs when atom-atom interactions decrease potential energy and produce a more stable arrangement; the octet rule is a guideline for drawing Lewis structures, not the causal explanation for bond formation.
**Concept category:** bond formation / octet rule
**Population:** students
**Source:** Development and Assessment of a Molecular Structure and Properties Learning Progression (development-and-assessment-of-a-molecular-structure-and-properties-learning-progression/development-and-assessment-of-a-molecular-structure-and-properties-learning-progression.md)
**Evidence:** One common misconception that students hold is that atoms form bonds because they "want" an octet, rather than because bonded atoms are more stable than unbonded ones.

## Misconception 524: Lewis structures are the same as the actual three-dimensional molecular structure, rather than simplified representations of it.

**Topic:** Lewis structures / purpose of Lewis structures
**Correct concept:** Lewis structures are two-dimensional representations ("cartoons") that encode information about underlying three-dimensional molecular structure.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should understand that a Lewis structure is a symbolic, simplified model; they should translate between 2D drawings and 3D geometry rather than treating the drawing as the molecule itself.
**Concept category:** representations / Lewis structures
**Population:** students
**Source:** Development and Assessment of a Molecular Structure and Properties Learning Progression (development-and-assessment-of-a-molecular-structure-and-properties-learning-progression/development-and-assessment-of-a-molecular-structure-and-properties-learning-progression.md)
**Evidence:** we stress the fact that Lewis structures are merely a convenient way to represent something more complex.

## Misconception 525: Students can read only explicit structural information from Lewis structures and do not recognize that they also encode information needed to infer physical and chemical properties.

**Topic:** structure-property relationships / predicting properties from Lewis structures
**Correct concept:** Lewis structures can be used, together with molecular shape, electronegativity, and intermolecular forces, to infer properties such as boiling point, melting point, acidity/basicity, and reactivity.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the structural features in the Lewis structure, infer 3D shape and bond polarity, determine intermolecular forces, and then use those forces to predict macroscopic properties.
**Concept category:** implicit information / property prediction
**Prevalence:** End of Fall: physical properties 13% control vs 38% treatment; reactivity 13% control vs 40% treatment; acidity/basicity 14% control vs 19% treatment. End of Spring: physical properties 27% control vs 54% treatment; reactivity 37% control vs 63% treatment; acidity/basicity 38% control vs 70% treatment.
**Population:** general chemistry students in control and CLUE treatment cohorts
**Source:** Development and Assessment of a Molecular Structure and Properties Learning Progression (development-and-assessment-of-a-molecular-structure-and-properties-learning-progression/development-and-assessment-of-a-molecular-structure-and-properties-learning-progression.md)
**Evidence:** the information that can be determined from a Lewis structure

## Misconception 526: Students treat boiling point and melting point as isolated facts to rank, but do not generalize to the broader category of physical properties.

**Topic:** structure-property relationships / general physical properties from structure
**Correct concept:** From molecular structure, students should infer broader physical properties, not only specific ranking tasks such as boiling point or melting point.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should move from structure to intermolecular forces and then to a wider class of physical properties, recognizing that boiling/melting point are examples rather than the whole category.
**Concept category:** surface features vs generalization
**Prevalence:** End of Fall: relative boiling points 20% control vs 56% treatment; relative melting points 18% control vs 50% treatment; physical properties 13% control vs 38% treatment. End of Spring: relative boiling points 46% control vs 54% treatment; relative melting points 38% control vs 48% treatment; physical properties 27% control vs 54% treatment.
**Population:** general chemistry students in control and CLUE treatment cohorts
**Source:** Development and Assessment of a Molecular Structure and Properties Learning Progression (development-and-assessment-of-a-molecular-structure-and-properties-learning-progression/development-and-assessment-of-a-molecular-structure-and-properties-learning-progression.md)
**Evidence:** some students from the control group chose melting and boiling points, but not physical properties.

## Misconception 527: Students do not recognize that a molecular structure can indicate acidic or basic behavior.

**Topic:** structure-property relationships / acidity/basicity from structure
**Correct concept:** Functional groups and local structure can signal whether a substance may have acidic and/or basic properties.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should inspect the structure for proton-donating or proton-accepting sites and reason from those functional groups to acidity/basicity.
**Concept category:** implicit information / acid-base properties
**Prevalence:** End of Fall: 14% control vs 19% treatment. End of Spring: 38% control vs 70% treatment; alanine prediction: 51% CLUE vs 23% control recognized acidic/basic properties.
**Population:** general chemistry students in control and CLUE treatment cohorts
**Source:** Development and Assessment of a Molecular Structure and Properties Learning Progression (development-and-assessment-of-a-molecular-structure-and-properties-learning-progression/development-and-assessment-of-a-molecular-structure-and-properties-learning-progression.md)
**Evidence:** CLUE students appear to have a better understanding of the uses of and information that is encoded into a Lewis structure.

## Misconception 528: Students treat gaseous HCl as an ionic compound made of H+ and Cl− ions, or think it breaks apart into ions like NaCl when dissolved in water.

**Topic:** Dissolution of ionic and molecular compounds / Hydrogen chloride dissolution
**Correct concept:** HCl(g) is a molecular compound; it first dissolves as HCl(aq), and only then reacts with water to form hydronium and chloride ions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish a molecular gas from an ionic solid: HCl has covalent bonding in the gas phase, so dissolution should be represented as HCl(g) -> HCl(aq), followed by the acid reaction with water if describing the aqueous acid equilibrium.
**Concept category:** molecular vs ionic compounds
**Prevalence:** Teachers: 54% used HCl(g) -> H+(aq) + Cl−(aq); 46% used HCl(g) + H2O(l) -> H3O+(aq) + Cl−(aq). Students: 2/13 wrote HCl dissociating into ions initially; 3/13 drew ions forming.
**Population:** High school chemistry teachers (N=13) and first-semester general chemistry students (N=13)
**Source:** Disentangling the Dissolution of Ionic and Molecular Compounds: Alternative Conceptions and Teaching Considerations in Chemistry (disentangling-the-dissolution-of-ionic-and-molecular-compounds-alternative-conceptions-and-teaching-considerations-in/disentangling-the-dissolution-of-ionic-and-molecular-compounds-alternative-conceptions-and-teaching-considerations-in.md)
**Evidence:** “the most common representations displayed HCl gas as an ionic compound that breaks up into H+ and Cl- ions”

## Misconception 529: Students think the dissolution of HCl is simply HCl -> H+ + Cl−, with no separate solvation step, as if HCl were an ionic solute.

**Topic:** Dissolution of ionic and molecular compounds / Hydrogen chloride dissolution
**Correct concept:** The correct process is first solvation/dissolution of the molecular gas into aqueous HCl, then acid ionization in water; the molecular compound itself is not initially ionic.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that a molecular solute can dissolve without immediately producing ions; the equation should show HCl(g) -> HCl(aq) when the focus is dissolution, and only then show reaction with water if focusing on acid behavior.
**Concept category:** symbolic representations
**Prevalence:** 13/13 teachers discussed this issue; among students, 6/13 continued to describe or depict HCl as ionic despite instruction.
**Population:** High school chemistry teachers and first-semester general chemistry students
**Source:** Disentangling the Dissolution of Ionic and Molecular Compounds: Alternative Conceptions and Teaching Considerations in Chemistry (disentangling-the-dissolution-of-ionic-and-molecular-compounds-alternative-conceptions-and-teaching-considerations-in/disentangling-the-dissolution-of-ionic-and-molecular-compounds-alternative-conceptions-and-teaching-considerations-in.md)
**Evidence:** “the more pressing issue was the ‘simplification’ of the dissolution, resulting in H+ and Cl- ions, effectively portraying HCl as an ionic compound.”

## Misconception 530: Students conflate dissolution and acid reaction, writing HCl(g) + H2O(l) -> H3O+(aq) + Cl−(aq) as if the dissolution and ionization happen as a single step.

**Topic:** Dissolution of ionic and molecular compounds / Hydrogen chloride dissolution
**Correct concept:** Dissolution of HCl gas and its acid reaction with water are distinct processes; the molecular gas first becomes HCl(aq), then can react with water to form ions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should separate the physical process of dissolving from the chemical reaction in water, and should not collapse both into one equation unless explicitly modeling the acid-base equilibrium in solution.
**Concept category:** dissolution vs reaction
**Prevalence:** 5/13 teachers and 3/13 students initially used a direct reaction with water for HCl(g)
**Population:** High school chemistry teachers and first-semester general chemistry students
**Source:** Disentangling the Dissolution of Ionic and Molecular Compounds: Alternative Conceptions and Teaching Considerations in Chemistry (disentangling-the-dissolution-of-ionic-and-molecular-compounds-alternative-conceptions-and-teaching-considerations-in/disentangling-the-dissolution-of-ionic-and-molecular-compounds-alternative-conceptions-and-teaching-considerations-in.md)
**Evidence:** “reacting gaseous HCl as an acid without representing the solvation of HCl in addition to the acid-base reaction that occurs in solution”

## Misconception 531: Students believe pure HCl(g) is already composed of H+ and F−/Cl− ions, or otherwise has ionic composition before dissolving.

**Topic:** Dissolution of ionic and molecular compounds / Hydrogen chloride dissolution
**Correct concept:** Pure gaseous HCl is a molecular substance with covalent bonding, not an ionic assembly of ions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use the state and bonding information: a gas-phase molecular compound consists of molecules held together by covalent bonds, not pre-existing ions.
**Concept category:** molecular vs ionic compounds
**Prevalence:** HF question on summative assessment: 22% selected ‘is made up of H+ and F− ions’; 15% selected both that and ‘is held together by a sigma bond’; total >33% endorsed ionic composition.
**Population:** First-semester general chemistry students (N=686)
**Source:** Disentangling the Dissolution of Ionic and Molecular Compounds: Alternative Conceptions and Teaching Considerations in Chemistry (disentangling-the-dissolution-of-ionic-and-molecular-compounds-alternative-conceptions-and-teaching-considerations-in/disentangling-the-dissolution-of-ionic-and-molecular-compounds-alternative-conceptions-and-teaching-considerations-in.md)
**Evidence:** “more than a third of students answered that pure gaseous HF is in some way composed of H+ and F- ions despite being explicitly told in the prompt that HF is a molecular compound.”

## Misconception 532: Students think NaCl remains intact in water and should be written as NaCl(aq).

**Topic:** Dissolution of ionic and molecular compounds / Sodium chloride dissolution
**Correct concept:** NaCl is an ionic compound that dissociates into hydrated Na+(aq) and Cl−(aq) ions in water.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that ionic lattices separate into ions upon dissolution, and the aqueous notation should represent separated hydrated ions rather than intact NaCl molecules.
**Concept category:** ionic dissociation
**Prevalence:** 4 students represented NaCl remaining intact in solution; 30% of students relied on NaCl(aq).
**Population:** First-semester general chemistry students
**Source:** Disentangling the Dissolution of Ionic and Molecular Compounds: Alternative Conceptions and Teaching Considerations in Chemistry (disentangling-the-dissolution-of-ionic-and-molecular-compounds-alternative-conceptions-and-teaching-considerations-in/disentangling-the-dissolution-of-ionic-and-molecular-compounds-alternative-conceptions-and-teaching-considerations-in.md)
**Evidence:** “students relied on inaccurate alternative conceptions of ionic compounds remaining intact upon dissolving—i.e., NaCl(aq).”

## Misconception 533: Students interpret HCl as a strong acid that simply ‘splits’ or ‘breaks apart’ into H+ and Cl− because the bond breaks when it dissolves.

**Topic:** Dissolution of ionic and molecular compounds / Hydrogen chloride dissolution
**Correct concept:** Acid behavior in solution must be distinguished from the molecular nature of HCl(g); dissolution and ion formation are not the same as an ionic compound simply breaking apart.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should first identify that HCl is molecular, then distinguish dissolution into water from acid ionization; strong acid behavior does not mean the pure gas is ionic.
**Concept category:** acidic molecular compounds
**Prevalence:** Quoted student response; 6/13 students overall still described/drew HCl as ionic after instruction.
**Population:** First-semester general chemistry student
**Source:** Disentangling the Dissolution of Ionic and Molecular Compounds: Alternative Conceptions and Teaching Considerations in Chemistry (disentangling-the-dissolution-of-ionic-and-molecular-compounds-alternative-conceptions-and-teaching-considerations-in/disentangling-the-dissolution-of-ionic-and-molecular-compounds-alternative-conceptions-and-teaching-considerations-in.md)
**Evidence:** “Yes, it's a strong acid, so it will split, the bond will break, and it will go into H plus ions, and Cl minus.”

## Misconception 534: Students think that once HCl is described as molecular, nothing further happens in solution; they stop at HCl(aq) and deny subsequent acidic reaction with water.

**Topic:** Dissolution of ionic and molecular compounds / Hydrogen chloride dissolution
**Correct concept:** HCl(aq) can further react with water to produce H3O+(aq) and Cl−(aq); being molecular does not mean it remains unchanged in aqueous solution.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that molecular identity and acidic behavior are compatible: after dissolution, an acid can ionize in water, so the process is not complete at HCl(aq).
**Concept category:** acidic molecular compounds
**Prevalence:** Several students who wrote HCl(g) -> HCl(aq) paused; 5 students (N, U, V, X, Z) believed the process was complete, and 1 student (X) said nothing else would occur.
**Population:** First-semester general chemistry students
**Source:** Disentangling the Dissolution of Ionic and Molecular Compounds: Alternative Conceptions and Teaching Considerations in Chemistry (disentangling-the-dissolution-of-ionic-and-molecular-compounds-alternative-conceptions-and-teaching-considerations-in/disentangling-the-dissolution-of-ionic-and-molecular-compounds-alternative-conceptions-and-teaching-considerations-in.md)
**Evidence:** “they completely disregarded its acidic nature once dissolved”

## Misconception 535: Students classify pure HCl(g) as having ion-ion interactions, implying it is made of ions.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces in molecular hydrogen chloride / Pure HCl(g)
**Correct concept:** Pure gaseous HCl has London dispersion forces and dipole-dipole interactions; it is molecular, not ionic.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the compound type from bonding and state of matter, then assign intermolecular forces appropriate to polar molecules rather than ion-ion attractions.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces
**Prevalence:** 27% on second-semester general chemistry assessment (N=675)
**Population:** Second-semester general chemistry students
**Source:** Disentangling the Dissolution of Ionic and Molecular Compounds: Alternative Conceptions and Teaching Considerations in Chemistry (disentangling-the-dissolution-of-ionic-and-molecular-compounds-alternative-conceptions-and-teaching-considerations-in/disentangling-the-dissolution-of-ionic-and-molecular-compounds-alternative-conceptions-and-teaching-considerations-in.md)
**Evidence:** “the most common mistake was selecting ‘ion-ion’ interactions in their responses, which accounted for 27% of students describing HCl(g) as an ionic compound composed of ions.”

## Misconception 536: Covalent bonding is formed between a metal and a nonmetal atom.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Type of atoms forming covalent bonds
**Correct concept:** Covalent bonding is typically formed between nonmetal atoms that share electrons.
