**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the element types and compare their tendency to share rather than transfer electrons; two nonmetals form covalent bonds by sharing valence electrons, whereas metal–nonmetal pairs usually form ionic bonds by electron transfer.
**Concept category:** type of atoms / ionic-vs-covalent confusion
**Prevalence:** Held by multiple students; Appendix-2 lists S4 and S5, and the discussion says a "great deal of students" had this misconception.
**Population:** 11th grade students (n=58); also 10 interviewed students
**Source:** Secondary School Students' Misconceptions of Covalent Bonding (enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031/enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031.md)
**Evidence:** "covalent bonding is formed between a metal and a nonmetal atom"

## Misconception 537: Students think covalent bonding is formed only when atoms 'want' or 'need' to gain electrons to achieve noble-gas configuration, as if atoms have human-like intentions.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Type of atoms forming covalent bonds
**Correct concept:** Atoms do not 'want' anything; bonding depends on electronic structure, electronegativity, and energetic stability, not intentional desire.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain bonding in terms of valence electrons, electronegativity, and attraction between nuclei and shared electron pairs rather than anthropomorphic 'wants' or 'needs'.
**Concept category:** animistic reasoning / electron affinity
**Prevalence:** Observed in interview responses (e.g., S1); Appendix-2 notes this idea for S1 and S9.
**Population:** Interviewed 11th grade students
**Source:** Secondary School Students' Misconceptions of Covalent Bonding (enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031/enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031.md)
**Evidence:** "They form covalent bonding, because they want to gain electrons to resemble stable noble gases configuration."

## Misconception 538: Hydrogen is treated as a metal atom, so students classify H-containing covalent compounds as if they involved metal–nonmetal bonding.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Type of atoms forming covalent bonds
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen is not a metal in this context; whether a bond is covalent depends on the bonding partners and electron sharing/transfer, not on misclassifying hydrogen as a metal.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize hydrogen’s role in bonding and use periodic-table category plus electron behavior correctly; hydrogen in HCl, NH3, and H2O participates in covalent bonding with nonmetals by sharing electrons.
**Concept category:** classification of hydrogen / ionic-vs-covalent confusion
**Prevalence:** Reported for H2O in Appendix-1; also linked to HCl and NH3 misconceptions in Appendix-2.
**Population:** 11th grade students (n=58)
**Source:** Secondary School Students' Misconceptions of Covalent Bonding (enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031/enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031.md)
**Evidence:** "Because hydrogen is a metal atom and oxygen is a nonmetal atom, they bond to each other by the electron transfer"

## Misconception 539: Covalent bonds are formed by electron transfer rather than electron sharing.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Formation of covalent bonds
**Correct concept:** Covalent bonds are formed when atoms share one or more pairs of electrons; electron transfer is characteristic of ionic bonding.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should describe covalent bond formation as mutual sharing of valence electrons and identify electron transfer as producing ions and ionic bonds, not covalent bonds.
**Concept category:** bond formation / electron transfer vs sharing
**Prevalence:** Documented across several items and interviews; Appendix-1 and discussion indicate this was common.
**Population:** 11th grade students (n=58); also interviewed students
**Source:** Secondary School Students' Misconceptions of Covalent Bonding (enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031/enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031.md)
**Evidence:** "covalent bonding is formed by the electron transfer within hydrogen chloride and ammonia"

## Misconception 540: Water contains ionic bonding because hydrogen is a metal and oxygen is a nonmetal, so electrons are transferred between them.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Formation of covalent bonds in water
**Correct concept:** Within H2O, the O–H bonds are covalent; oxygen and hydrogen share electrons, with oxygen attracting the bonding electrons more strongly.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify H2O as a covalent molecule and reason from electron sharing and electronegativity, not from metal/nonmetal transfer logic.
**Concept category:** ionic-vs-covalent confusion / water bonding
**Prevalence:** Shown in item 3 and Appendix-1 (7% for this specific version; also 9% for ionic bonding in water).
**Population:** 11th grade students (n=58)
**Source:** Secondary School Students' Misconceptions of Covalent Bonding (enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031/enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031.md)
**Evidence:** "Ionic bonding is formed between oxygen and hydrogen atom within a water molecule, because hydrogen is a metal and oxygen is a nonmetal atom. They bond with each other by the electron transfer."

## Misconception 541: Hydrogen bonding is the bond within a water molecule, instead of covalent bonding.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Formation of covalent bonds in water
**Correct concept:** The bonds within a water molecule are covalent O–H bonds; hydrogen bonding is an intermolecular attraction between molecules, not the intramolecular bond in H2O.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish intramolecular covalent bonds from intermolecular hydrogen bonds and apply the term hydrogen bonding only to attractions between molecules.
**Concept category:** chemical bonds vs intermolecular forces
**Prevalence:** Item 3 and Appendix-1 report this misconception (5%); Appendix-2 lists it for interview student S9.
**Population:** 11th grade students (n=58)
**Source:** Secondary School Students' Misconceptions of Covalent Bonding (enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031/enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031.md)
**Evidence:** "Hydrogen bonding is formed between oxygen and hydrogen atoms in a water molecule."

## Misconception 542: Polar and nonpolar covalent bonds are reversed: identical atoms are said to form polar covalent bonds and different atoms nonpolar covalent bonds.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Type of covalent bonding
**Correct concept:** Identical atoms generally form nonpolar covalent bonds, while different atoms with different electronegativities generally form polar covalent bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use electronegativity difference to determine bond polarity and keep the definitions of polar and nonpolar bonds consistent with electron distribution.
**Concept category:** polar vs nonpolar covalent bonding
**Prevalence:** Appeared in item 2 and interview 3; Appendix-1 lists several variants, e.g. 12% polar in H2 and 14% nonpolar in H2S.
**Population:** 11th grade students (n=58); also interviewed students
**Source:** Secondary School Students' Misconceptions of Covalent Bonding (enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031/enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031.md)
**Evidence:** "Nonpolar covalent bonding is formed between different nonmetal atoms, while polar covalent bonding is formed between identical nonmetal atoms."

## Misconception 543: Bond polarity is the same as molecule polarity; students use 'polar/nonpolar molecule' instead of 'polar/nonpolar bond'.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Type of covalent bonding
**Correct concept:** Bond polarity refers to unequal or equal sharing of bonding electrons in a bond; molecular polarity depends on both bond polarity and molecular shape.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish the polarity of a bond from the overall polarity of a molecule and reason from electronegativity and geometry separately.
**Concept category:** bond polarity vs molecular polarity
**Prevalence:** Documented in the discussion and Appendix-1 note (5).
**Population:** 11th grade students (n=58)
**Source:** Secondary School Students' Misconceptions of Covalent Bonding (enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031/enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031.md)
**Evidence:** "students confused 'polar and nonpolar molecule' with 'polar and nonpolar covalent bonding'"

## Misconception 544: Bonding electrons are equally distant from both atoms in all covalent molecules, regardless of electronegativity differences.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Electron position in covalent bonds
**Correct concept:** In polar covalent bonds, bonding electrons are drawn closer to the more electronegative atom; only in nonpolar bonds are electrons shared equally.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the electronegativities of the bonded atoms and place the electron pair closer to the atom that attracts electrons more strongly.
**Concept category:** electron distribution / electronegativity
**Prevalence:** Appendix-1 reports 7%.
**Population:** 11th grade students (n=58)
**Source:** Secondary School Students' Misconceptions of Covalent Bonding (enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031/enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031.md)
**Evidence:** "Bonding electrons place equidistantly to the bonded atoms in all covalent molecules"

## Misconception 545: There is no distinction among covalent bonds; all covalent bonds are the same and are based on electron transfer.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Type of covalent bonding and bond formation
**Correct concept:** Covalent bonds can differ, for example as polar and nonpolar covalent bonds, and they are formed by electron sharing, not transfer.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize bond types based on electronegativity differences and electron sharing patterns, not collapse all covalent bonds into one category or into ionic-like transfer.
**Concept category:** absence of bond-type distinction / overgeneralization
**Prevalence:** Observed in interview question 3 for students S4 and S5.
**Population:** Interviewed 11th grade students
**Source:** Secondary School Students' Misconceptions of Covalent Bonding (enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031/enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031.md)
**Evidence:** "No, all covalent bonds are the same. Covalent bonding is formed between atoms by sharing of single electrons."

## Misconception 546: Van der Waals forces, dipole-dipole forces, and hydrogen bonding are types of covalent bonding.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Types of covalent bonding
**Correct concept:** Van der Waals forces, dipole-dipole interactions, and hydrogen bonding are intermolecular forces, not types of covalent bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should separate intramolecular covalent bonding from intermolecular forces and classify each interaction according to whether it holds atoms within a molecule or molecules together.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces vs covalent bond types
**Prevalence:** Observed in interview student S9.
**Population:** Interviewed 11th grade students
**Source:** Secondary School Students' Misconceptions of Covalent Bonding (enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031/enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031.md)
**Evidence:** "Van der Waals, dipole-dipole and hydrogen bonding are the types of covalent bonding"

## Misconception 547: Graphite conducts electricity because it contains free carbon atoms that are not bonded to anything and can move through the structure.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Graphite electrical conductivity
**Correct concept:** Graphite conducts electricity because it has delocalized electrons that are free to move through its layers; carbon atoms are still covalently bonded in a network.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain conductivity in terms of mobile delocalized electrons in graphite’s structure, not free carbon atoms.
**Concept category:** giant covalent structures / conductivity
**Prevalence:** Appendix-1 reports 26%.
**Population:** 11th grade students (n=58)
**Source:** Secondary School Students' Misconceptions of Covalent Bonding (enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031/enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031.md)
**Evidence:** "there is a free carbon atom which was not bonded to any carbon atom. These free carbon atoms move through the structure of graphite. Graphite conducts electricity due to these free carbon atoms."

## Misconception 548: Graphite conducts electricity because its carbon layers can slide over each other.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Graphite electrical conductivity
**Correct concept:** Electrical conductivity in graphite is due to delocalized electrons, not the sliding of layers.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the role of delocalized pi electrons and distinguish mechanical layering from electrical conduction.
**Concept category:** giant covalent structures / conductivity
**Prevalence:** Appendix-1 reports 17%.
**Population:** 11th grade students (n=58)
**Source:** Secondary School Students' Misconceptions of Covalent Bonding (enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031/enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031.md)
**Evidence:** "graphite has the layers of carbon atoms which slide over each other, it conducts electricity due to these layers which could be able to move"

## Misconception 549: Diamond is viewed as a 'unitary' structure without layers, contrasted with graphite layers, rather than as a giant covalent network.

**Topic:** Covalent bonding / Graphite and diamond structure
**Correct concept:** Diamond and graphite are both giant covalent structures, but diamond is a 3D tetrahedral network and graphite is layered with delocalized electrons between layers.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should describe each allotrope with the correct extended covalent structure and connect structure to properties like conductivity.
**Concept category:** giant covalent structures / structure-property relations
**Prevalence:** Linked to item 4 responses; no exact percentage provided for this wording.
**Population:** 11th grade students (n=58)
**Source:** Secondary School Students' Misconceptions of Covalent Bonding (enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031/enes,+v7i2haziran2010_p005-031.md)
**Evidence:** "Diamond has a unitary structure. It has no layers."

## Misconception 550: Students think that when water is heated, the molecules simply move faster without necessarily connecting that speed change to particle-level motion in the boiling process, or they describe motion only in macroscopic terms.

**Topic:** Particulate nature of matter / Motion of particles during heating
**Correct concept:** Heating increases the kinetic energy of water molecules, so they move faster at the molecular level.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect added heat to increased molecular kinetic energy and infer that water molecules move faster as temperature rises and during boiling.
**Concept category:** motion
**Prevalence:** Mentioned as a common pattern; no exact percentage given.
**Population:** 35 high school students in six Scientific Visualization classes
**Source:** Using Student-Generated Animations about Water Boiling to Impact Student Understanding of the Particulate Nature of Matter (etd/etd.md)
**Evidence:** "most students understood that as the temperature rises, the particles/molecules move faster"

## Misconception 551: Students think the bubbles are not steam, or that bubble composition is something like air, oxygen/hydrogen, or unspecified gas rather than water vapor.

**Topic:** Particulate nature of matter / Composition of bubbles in boiling water
**Correct concept:** The bubbles in boiling water are steam (water vapor).
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use the idea that boiling converts liquid water to gaseous water and infer that the bubbles are water vapor/steam, not air or split elements.
**Concept category:** phase change
**Prevalence:** The paper notes that there was no change on this item across groups, but gives no exact misconception count.
**Population:** Students in the 35-student analysis sample; also the full pre/posttest population of 94 originally participated.
**Source:** Using Student-Generated Animations about Water Boiling to Impact Student Understanding of the Particulate Nature of Matter (etd/etd.md)
**Evidence:** "What are the bubbles made of?"

## Misconception 552: Students think water can keep getting hotter while it is boiling, or that temperature continues to rise during boiling.

**Topic:** Particulate nature of matter / Boiling point and temperature change
**Correct concept:** At the boiling point, added energy goes into the phase change and the temperature remains constant until boiling is complete.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that once water reaches its boiling point, additional energy is used to overcome intermolecular attractions rather than increase temperature, so temperature plateaus.
**Concept category:** phase change
**Prevalence:** Observed in student interview responses; no percentage given.
**Population:** Student T3-2 and others in the interview sample
**Source:** Using Student-Generated Animations about Water Boiling to Impact Student Understanding of the Particulate Nature of Matter (etd/etd.md)
**Evidence:** "you cannot get hotter than boiling water. Like I thought that there was boiling and then you could still get higher."

## Misconception 553: Students define boiling point only as a specific temperature value or as the moment water is very hot, without relating it to vapor pressure and atmospheric pressure.

**Topic:** Particulate nature of matter / Boiling point definition
**Correct concept:** Boiling point is the temperature at which a liquid’s vapor pressure equals atmospheric pressure.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect boiling to pressure balance, recognizing that boiling occurs when vapor pressure matches external pressure and that the numerical temperature depends on pressure.
**Concept category:** phase change
**Prevalence:** Appears in the rubric as a low-level response category; no exact count reported.
**Population:** Students responding to WISE question 6
**Source:** Using Student-Generated Animations about Water Boiling to Impact Student Understanding of the Particulate Nature of Matter (etd/etd.md)
**Evidence:** "When water reaches 212°F (100°C) –or just before it does (The temperature only)"

## Misconception 554: Students think boiling bubbles rise simply because 'heat rises' or because they contain gas, rather than because of pressure differences between the vapor in the bubble and surrounding water.

**Topic:** Particulate nature of matter / Intermolecular pressure in bubbles
**Correct concept:** Vapor bubbles rise because their internal vapor pressure is lower than the surrounding water pressure; density differences also matter.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason from pressure and density: vapor bubbles form, and if internal pressure is less than surrounding pressure the bubbles rise to the surface.
**Concept category:** pressure
**Prevalence:** Reported as a student misconception in Table 4.10; no percentage given.
**Population:** Student T3-7
**Source:** Using Student-Generated Animations about Water Boiling to Impact Student Understanding of the Particulate Nature of Matter (etd/etd.md)
**Evidence:** "I didn't know that the, um, the bubbles didn't have like as much pressure as the outside area did."

## Misconception 555: Students do not distinguish particle spacing across phases, treating proximity as similar in solids, liquids, and gases, or failing to represent gas particles as far apart and liquid particles as close together.

**Topic:** Particulate nature of matter / Particle spacing/proximity in phases
**Correct concept:** Particles are closer together in liquids than in gases; gas particles have negligible interactions and are far apart.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use a particulate model in which particle arrangement changes with phase: tightly packed in condensed phases and much farther apart in gases.
**Concept category:** particle arrangement
**Prevalence:** Discussed qualitatively; rubric data show Group 1 average PROXIMITY 0.67, Group 2 0.78, Group 3 1.42, but no direct misconception percentage given.
**Population:** 35 students across Groups 1-3
**Source:** Using Student-Generated Animations about Water Boiling to Impact Student Understanding of the Particulate Nature of Matter (etd/etd.md)
**Evidence:** "This may represent an incomplete knowledge of the actual positioning and configuration of molecules in each phase"

## Misconception 556: Students rely on macroscopic images and do not include molecules/particles in their representations, or treat the animation as only a macroscopic boiling scene.

**Topic:** Particulate nature of matter / Representation of molecules in animations
**Correct concept:** A scientifically accurate animation should include a particulate-level representation of water molecules and their motion during boiling, alongside macroscopic features.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect the text to both macro and micro levels, adding molecular representations to show how boiling occurs at the particulate level.
**Concept category:** surface features vs. particle-level representation
**Prevalence:** 22.9% included molecules in their animations.
**Population:** 8 of 35 analyzed students
**Source:** Using Student-Generated Animations about Water Boiling to Impact Student Understanding of the Particulate Nature of Matter (etd/etd.md)
**Evidence:** "only 22.9% of students including molecules in their animations"

## Misconception 557: Students think particles in one phase move together or all at the same speed, instead of showing random motion and different speeds across phases.

**Topic:** Particulate nature of matter / Movement of particles in a liquid vs. gas
**Correct concept:** Particles move randomly, and motion varies with phase; in general gas particles move faster than liquid particles.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that thermal energy increases random molecular motion and that gaseous particles move faster and more randomly than particles in the liquid state.
**Concept category:** motion
**Prevalence:** Appears in the project rubric as a low-level motion category; no direct count reported.
**Population:** Student animations from the 35-student sample
**Source:** Using Student-Generated Animations about Water Boiling to Impact Student Understanding of the Particulate Nature of Matter (etd/etd.md)
**Evidence:** "Shows particles in motion but they are all moving together (coordinated movement)"

## Misconception 558: Students believe water molecules move at the same speed in the solid, liquid, and gaseous phases.

**Topic:** Particulate nature of matter / Constant particle speed across phases
**Correct concept:** Molecular speed differs by phase; molecules move fastest in the gaseous phase.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare kinetic energy across phases and infer that heating increases molecular speed and that gas particles move fastest.
**Concept category:** motion
**Prevalence:** This was a specific incorrect option on the pre/posttest; no percentage reported.
**Population:** Pre/posttest respondents
**Source:** Using Student-Generated Animations about Water Boiling to Impact Student Understanding of the Particulate Nature of Matter (etd/etd.md)
**Evidence:** "Water molecules move at the same speed in the solid, liquid, and gaseous phases."

## Misconception 559: Students think matter is continuous or that there is matter between atoms/molecules in a substance.

**Topic:** Particulate nature of matter / Space between particles
**Correct concept:** There is empty space between atoms and molecules, especially in liquids and gases.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that substances are particulate and that the spaces between particles are not filled with matter.
**Concept category:** particle nature
**Prevalence:** Presented as a documented misconception from prior research cited in the paper; not tied to a new count in this study.
**Population:** Prior literature cited in the paper
**Source:** Using Student-Generated Animations about Water Boiling to Impact Student Understanding of the Particulate Nature of Matter (etd/etd.md)
**Evidence:** "matter is continuous and there is no vacuum or space between them"

## Misconception 560: Students think atoms are large enough to be seen under a microscope, or they confuse atom size with macroscopic objects.

**Topic:** Particulate nature of matter / Atomic/molecular size and scale
**Correct concept:** Atoms and molecules are too small to be seen directly with classroom microscopes.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize the nanoscopic scale of atoms and infer that they require indirect models and instruments rather than direct viewing.
**Concept category:** size and scale
**Prevalence:** Listed as a common misconception in cited literature.
**Population:** Grade-12 students in Griffiths & Preston, as cited by the paper
**Source:** Using Student-Generated Animations about Water Boiling to Impact Student Understanding of the Particulate Nature of Matter (etd/etd.md)
**Evidence:** "atoms are large enough to be seen under a microscope"

## Misconception 561: Students think molecules in a solid do not move.

**Topic:** Particulate nature of matter / Molecule and atom movement in solids
**Correct concept:** Molecules in solids do move; they vibrate in place.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should understand that solid particles have kinetic energy even though the solid maintains shape, so the particles vibrate rather than remain motionless.
**Concept category:** motion
**Prevalence:** This item appears on the pre/posttest as a misconception probe; no percentage reported.
**Population:** Pre/posttest respondents
**Source:** Using Student-Generated Animations about Water Boiling to Impact Student Understanding of the Particulate Nature of Matter (etd/etd.md)
**Evidence:** "Molecules in a rock are not moving"

## Misconception 562: Students think molecules and atoms are all the same size.

**Topic:** Particulate nature of matter / Particle size equality
**Correct concept:** Atoms and molecules differ in size; molecules are composed of atoms and can vary in size.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason from molecular structure that different substances have different particle sizes and that molecules are built from atoms.
**Concept category:** size and scale
**Prevalence:** Listed as an assessment item targeting a known misconception.
**Population:** Pre/posttest respondents
**Source:** Using Student-Generated Animations about Water Boiling to Impact Student Understanding of the Particulate Nature of Matter (etd/etd.md)
**Evidence:** "Molecules and atoms are all the same size"

## Misconception 563: Students think molecules in liquids are always further apart than in solids, with no distinction based on phase-specific packing.

**Topic:** Particulate nature of matter / Liquid molecular spacing
**Correct concept:** Molecules in liquids are close together but can move past one another; gases are much farther apart.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare phase structures and recognize that liquids remain relatively dense while allowing flow, unlike gases.
**Concept category:** particle arrangement
**Prevalence:** Included in the ParNoMA instrument; no study-specific percentage provided.
**Population:** Pre/posttest respondents
**Source:** Using Student-Generated Animations about Water Boiling to Impact Student Understanding of the Particulate Nature of Matter (etd/etd.md)
**Evidence:** "Molecules in liquids are always further apart than in solids"

## Misconception 564: Students think only visible things are made of molecules.

**Topic:** Particulate nature of matter / Matter made of visible objects only
**Correct concept:** All matter, visible or not, is made of molecules/atoms.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should infer that invisibility to the eye does not mean absence of molecules, because molecules make up all matter.
**Concept category:** particle nature
**Prevalence:** Included in the ParNoMA instrument; no study-specific percentage provided.
**Population:** Pre/posttest respondents
**Source:** Using Student-Generated Animations about Water Boiling to Impact Student Understanding of the Particulate Nature of Matter (etd/etd.md)
**Evidence:** "Only things you can see are made of molecules"

## Misconception 565: Students think something other than empty space exists between molecules in a gas, such as air or atmosphere.

**Topic:** Particulate nature of matter / Composition of matter in a gas
**Correct concept:** There is empty space between molecules in a gas.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that gas particles are widely separated and that the space between them is not filled with matter.
**Concept category:** particle arrangement
**Prevalence:** Included in the pre/posttest; no percentages reported.
**Population:** Pre/posttest respondents
**Source:** Using Student-Generated Animations about Water Boiling to Impact Student Understanding of the Particulate Nature of Matter (etd/etd.md)
**Evidence:** "In a pure sample of oxygen gas, what exists between the oxygen molecules?"

## Misconception 566: Students think heat causes molecules to expand or makes molecules bigger.

**Topic:** Particulate nature of matter / Heating and expansion of molecules
**Correct concept:** Heat increases particle motion and, in phase changes, can separate particles; it does not make molecules themselves expand.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should separate molecular size from thermal motion and understand that heating changes motion and arrangement rather than molecule size.
**Concept category:** thermal effects
**Prevalence:** Included as a wrong option on the pre/posttest.
**Population:** Pre/posttest respondents
**Source:** Using Student-Generated Animations about Water Boiling to Impact Student Understanding of the Particulate Nature of Matter (etd/etd.md)
**Evidence:** "Heat causes molecules to expand."

## Misconception 567: Students believe chemical bonds and intermolecular forces are the same force.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Chemical bonds vs intermolecular forces
**Correct concept:** Chemical bonds are intramolecular forces that hold atoms together within a substance, whereas intermolecular forces are attractions between particles of different molecules or ions and are distinct interactions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish where each force acts: first identify bonding within a molecule or ionic lattice, then identify weaker attractions between separate molecules or particles. Because these act at different structural levels and have different roles, they cannot be treated as synonymous.
**Concept category:** chemical bonds vs intermolecular forces
**Population:** high school students; 1st year class (22 students) and two 3rd year classes (47 students)
**Source:** Intermolecular forces: a methodological proposal of teaching based on experimentation (intermolecular-forces-a-methodological-proposal-of-teaching-20doyybg/intermolecular-forces-a-methodological-proposal-of-teaching-20doyybg.md)
**Evidence:** they presented some conceptual errors about intermolecular forces, leaving to understand that for them the chemical bonds and the intermolecular forces were the same force.

## Misconception 568: Students believe intermolecular forces act only in homogeneous mixtures and do not exist in heterogeneous mixtures.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and mixtures / Homogeneous vs heterogeneous mixtures
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces exist between particles in all पदार्थ/mixtures where particles are present; whether a mixture is homogeneous or heterogeneous depends on miscibility and interactions among components, not on the existence of intermolecular forces itself.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that all substances have intermolecular interactions, but the strength and type of interactions can lead to complete mixing or phase separation. Heterogeneous mixtures still involve intermolecular forces between the species present, even if they are not sufficient to produce one uniform phase.
**Concept category:** mixtures / intermolecular interactions
**Population:** students in the experiment classes
**Source:** Intermolecular forces: a methodological proposal of teaching based on experimentation (intermolecular-forces-a-methodological-proposal-of-teaching-20doyybg/intermolecular-forces-a-methodological-proposal-of-teaching-20doyybg.md)
**Evidence:** the students demonstrated to believe that intermolecular forces act only in homogeneous mixtures, thus not existing intermolecular forces in heterogeneous mixtures.

## Misconception 569: Students think only homogeneous mixtures have interactions between components, and heterogeneous mixtures do not.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and mixtures / Homogeneous vs heterogeneous mixtures
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular interactions occur between components in both homogeneous and heterogeneous mixtures; the difference is how completely the components mix or separate into phases.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify that particles of different substances can interact regardless of whether the final mixture looks uniform. The visible phase behavior does not mean interactions are absent; rather, the balance of attractions, polarity, and other properties determines the mixture type.
**Concept category:** mixtures / intermolecular interactions
**Population:** students discussed in the paper, as cited from classroom observations and literature context
**Source:** Intermolecular forces: a methodological proposal of teaching based on experimentation (intermolecular-forces-a-methodological-proposal-of-teaching-20doyybg/intermolecular-forces-a-methodological-proposal-of-teaching-20doyybg.md)
**Evidence:** only has interactions between compounds that form a homogeneous mixture, and in the heterogeneous mixture, there are no interactions between the components that compose it.

## Misconception 570: Some students attributed the density difference as responsible for whether mixtures are homogeneous or heterogeneous.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and mixtures / Cause of miscibility and homogeneity
**Correct concept:** Miscibility and mixture homogeneity are explained by intermolecular interactions, especially polarity and the relative strength of attractions between components, not simply by density differences.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the polarity and intermolecular attractions of the substances. If attractions between unlike particles are favorable, the substances mix to form a homogeneous mixture; if not, phase separation occurs. Density alone cannot explain why one pair mixes while another with different densities may still be homogeneous.
**Concept category:** mixtures / density vs polarity
**Population:** students in the observed classes
**Source:** Intermolecular forces: a methodological proposal of teaching based on experimentation (intermolecular-forces-a-methodological-proposal-of-teaching-20doyybg/intermolecular-forces-a-methodological-proposal-of-teaching-20doyybg.md)
**Evidence:** Some of them attributed the density difference as responsible for the heterogeneity of the mixtures. But when confronted with other mixtures with liquids of different densities that resulted in a homogeneous mixture, the students could not argue about the possible causes.

## Misconception 571: Most students thought surface tension is the factor responsible for the dye floating in milk.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and physical properties / Surface tension and dye behavior
**Correct concept:** The behavior of the dye in milk is explained by intermolecular interactions and the effect of detergent on the milk's fat and surface properties; surface tension alone is not the complete explanation for the observed phenomenon.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect the dye movement to how detergent disrupts interactions in milk, especially between fat and water, changing the local balance of forces and allowing the dye to spread. The explanation should involve particle interactions rather than naming surface tension as the sole cause.
**Concept category:** surface tension / intermolecular interactions
**Prevalence:** most of the students
**Population:** students in the Psychedelic Milk activity
**Source:** Intermolecular forces: a methodological proposal of teaching based on experimentation (intermolecular-forces-a-methodological-proposal-of-teaching-20doyybg/intermolecular-forces-a-methodological-proposal-of-teaching-20doyybg.md)
**Evidence:** On the surface tension, most of the students pointed out to be the same, the one responsible for the floating of the dye in the milk.

## Misconception 572: Students think molecular structure and macroscopic properties are not explicitly connected.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Structure-property relationships
**Correct concept:** Molecular structure is directly connected to observable properties such as boiling point and melting point through intermolecular forces.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should use the molecular structure to infer the dominant intermolecular forces and then connect those forces to the observed property trend (e.g., stronger intermolecular forces generally correspond to higher boiling or melting points).
**Concept category:** structure-property relationships
**Source:** Intermolecular Forces and the Languages of Chemistry (intermolecular-forces-and-the-languages-of-chemistry/intermolecular-forces-and-the-languages-of-chemistry.md)
**Evidence:** "for many students structure and properties were not explicitly connected"

## Misconception 573: Students fail to represent an intermolecular force as an interaction between molecules.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Representation of intermolecular forces
**Correct concept:** An intermolecular force is an attraction between separate molecules, not a property of one molecule alone.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should identify two or more molecules and reason about the attractions between them, rather than treating the force as something within a single molecule.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces
**Prevalence:** only 10–30% of students represented each IMF as an interaction between molecules
**Source:** Intermolecular Forces and the Languages of Chemistry (intermolecular-forces-and-the-languages-of-chemistry/intermolecular-forces-and-the-languages-of-chemistry.md)
**Evidence:** "only 10−30% of students represented each IMF as an interaction between molecules"

## Misconception 574: Students think boiling involves breaking covalent bonds.

**Topic:** Boiling point and intermolecular forces / Phase change
**Correct concept:** Boiling is a phase change that overcomes intermolecular forces, not covalent bonds within molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should recognize that molecules remain intact during boiling; energy is used to separate molecules from each other, not to break the bonds within them.
**Concept category:** phase change
**Source:** Intermolecular Forces and the Languages of Chemistry (intermolecular-forces-and-the-languages-of-chemistry/intermolecular-forces-and-the-languages-of-chemistry.md)
**Evidence:** "boiling involves breaking covalent bonds"

## Misconception 575: Students think any molecule containing oxygen and hydrogen can form hydrogen bonds.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Functional group recognition
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding requires a hydrogen bonded to a highly electronegative atom and an appropriate acceptor; not every O/H-containing molecule qualifies in the same way.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should examine the bonding arrangement and molecular structure, not just the presence of O and H atoms, to determine whether hydrogen bonding is possible.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Source:** Intermolecular Forces and the Languages of Chemistry (intermolecular-forces-and-the-languages-of-chemistry/intermolecular-forces-and-the-languages-of-chemistry.md)
**Evidence:** "any molecule with O and H (even CH3OCH3 and CH3COH) can form hydrogen bonds"

## Misconception 576: Students think hydrogenation must decrease molecular weight to explain the large drop in melting point.

**Topic:** Melting point / Effect of hydrogenation
**Correct concept:** Hydrogenation changes molecular shape and saturation, which alters packing and intermolecular forces; it does not need to decrease molecular weight to lower melting point.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should compare the structures before and after hydrogenation, note changes in unsaturation and packing, and then relate those structural changes to melting point rather than assuming mass decrease.
**Concept category:** shape and packing
**Prevalence:** a few students
**Population:** students in the 2023–2024 general chemistry course (n = 63 for language identification; n = 70 for prediction assessments)
**Source:** Intermolecular Forces and the Languages of Chemistry (intermolecular-forces-and-the-languages-of-chemistry/intermolecular-forces-and-the-languages-of-chemistry.md)
**Evidence:** "because of the large decrease in melting point, hydrogenation must somehow decrease the molecular weight rather than change the molecular shape"

## Misconception 577: Students think a double bond directly makes intermolecular forces stronger and therefore requires a higher melting point.

**Topic:** Melting point / Double bond effect
**Correct concept:** Double bonds, especially cis double bonds, change molecular shape and packing, often lowering melting point by reducing intermolecular attractions between molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should reason that molecular geometry affects how well molecules pack together; poorer packing weakens intermolecular interactions and lowers melting point.
**Concept category:** shape and packing
**Prevalence:** a number of students
**Population:** students in a follow-up 3 weeks after the activity
**Source:** Intermolecular Forces and the Languages of Chemistry (intermolecular-forces-and-the-languages-of-chemistry/intermolecular-forces-and-the-languages-of-chemistry.md)
**Evidence:** "making the forces stronger which requires a higher melting point"

## Misconception 578: Students think a C=C bond has higher energy than a C–C bond, so more heat is needed to break it during melting.

**Topic:** Melting point / Bond energy vs phase change
**Correct concept:** Melting does not involve breaking covalent C=C or C–C bonds; the change in melting point is due to intermolecular forces and molecular packing, not bond strength within the molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should distinguish covalent bond energies from intermolecular interactions and use structural packing arguments to explain melting point differences.
**Concept category:** bond energy
**Prevalence:** a number of students
**Population:** students in a follow-up 3 weeks after the activity
**Source:** Intermolecular Forces and the Languages of Chemistry (intermolecular-forces-and-the-languages-of-chemistry/intermolecular-forces-and-the-languages-of-chemistry.md)
**Evidence:** "a C=C bond has higher energy than a C–C bond, thus requiring more energy (heat) to break down and melt"

## Misconception 579: Students think intermolecular bonding is as strong as intramolecular bonding.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Relative strength of intermolecular vs intramolecular forces
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces are much weaker than intramolecular bonds such as covalent or ionic bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish between forces holding molecules together and bonds holding atoms together within a molecule. Boiling overcomes intermolecular attractions without breaking covalent bonds, so far less energy is needed for vaporization than for bond breaking.
**Concept category:** strength comparison
**Prevalence:** 26.3% of responses
**Population:** control group students on the post-test (N=38)
**Source:** Problem-Based Learning in 9th Grade Chemistry Class: 'Intermolecular Forces' (s11165-007-9050-0/s11165-007-9050-0.md)
**Evidence:** "The alternate conception that intermolecular bonding is as strong as intramolecular bonding was quite prevalent"

## Misconception 580: Students think there are hydrogen bonds between all molecules that contain hydrogen.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Presence of hydrogen bonds in molecules containing hydrogen
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding only occurs when hydrogen is directly bonded to a highly electronegative atom such as N, O, or F and interacts with a lone pair on another electronegative atom.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should check the bonding environment of hydrogen, not merely the presence of hydrogen. Hydrogen must be covalently attached to N, O, or F and the acceptor atom must have lone pairs; otherwise hydrogen bonding does not occur.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding criteria
**Prevalence:** 34.2% of responses
**Population:** control group students on the post-test (N=38)
**Source:** Problem-Based Learning in 9th Grade Chemistry Class: 'Intermolecular Forces' (s11165-007-9050-0/s11165-007-9050-0.md)
**Evidence:** "There are hydrogen bonds between all molecules which contain hydrogen."

## Misconception 581: Students think nonpolar molecules can become polar and therefore all molecules have dipole characteristics / dipole-dipole forces.

**Topic:** Molecular polarity and intermolecular forces / All molecules have dipole character
**Correct concept:** Only polar molecules have permanent dipoles and dipole-dipole forces; nonpolar molecules do not become polar just because they are liquid or because London dispersion forces are present.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should determine polarity from molecular structure and electronegativity differences, then decide whether permanent dipole-dipole interactions are possible. Nonpolar molecules can still have London dispersion forces, but they do not have permanent dipoles.
**Concept category:** polarity / dipole-dipole forces
**Prevalence:** 21.1% of responses
**Population:** control group students on the post-test (N=38)
**Source:** Problem-Based Learning in 9th Grade Chemistry Class: 'Intermolecular Forces' (s11165-007-9050-0/s11165-007-9050-0.md)
**Evidence:** "nonpolar molecules can become polar, therefore all existing molecules have dipole characteristics"

## Misconception 582: Intermolecular forces are forces within a molecule rather than attractions between molecules.

**Topic:** intermolecular forces / basic definition
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces are attractive forces between separate molecules or particles, not bonds within a molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish intramolecular bonding from intermolecular attraction: covalent bonds hold atoms together inside a molecule, whereas intermolecular forces act between molecules and influence bulk properties like solubility.
**Concept category:** intermolecular forces
**Population:** general chemistry students
**Source:** Student Discovery of the Relationship between Molecular Structure, Solubility, and Intermolecular Forces (student-discovery-of-the-relationship-between-molecular-structure-solubility-and-intermolecular-forces/student-discovery-of-the-relationship-between-molecular-structure-solubility-and-intermolecular-forces.md)
**Evidence:** students maintain their initial misconceptions, such as that intermolecular forces are attractive forces within a molecule.

## Misconception 583: Any molecule containing hydrogen atoms can hydrogen-bond.

**Topic:** hydrogen bonding / overgeneralization from hydrogen presence
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding requires hydrogen covalently bonded to a highly electronegative atom such as N, O, or F, plus a suitable lone-pair acceptor.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should check the bonding environment of hydrogen, not just its presence. If hydrogen is bonded to carbon only, the molecule does not serve as a hydrogen-bond donor in the usual chemistry sense.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Population:** students taking the prelaboratory assessment
**Source:** Student Discovery of the Relationship between Molecular Structure, Solubility, and Intermolecular Forces (student-discovery-of-the-relationship-between-molecular-structure-solubility-and-intermolecular-forces/student-discovery-of-the-relationship-between-molecular-structure-solubility-and-intermolecular-forces.md)
**Evidence:** intuitive reasoning could cause students to incorrectly guess that all molecules containing hydrogen atoms are capable of hydrogen-bonding.

## Misconception 584: Students think intermolecular forces are located within a single molecule rather than between separate molecules.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / General location of IMFs
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces act between molecules, not inside one molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify two or more separate molecules and place the interaction in the space between them, because IMFs arise from attractions between molecules.
**Concept category:** within vs between
**Prevalence:** Cohort 1 drawings: hydrogen bonding 72% (N=68) within; dipole-dipole 61% (N=57) within; LDFs 55% (N=52) within. Cohort 2 drawings: hydrogen bonding 56% (N=90) within; dipole-dipole 58% (N=93) within; LDFs 56% (N=89) within.
**Population:** General chemistry college students; Cohort 1 N=94, Cohort 2 N=160
**Source:** Student Understanding of Intermolecular Forces: A Multimodal Study (student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-a-multimodal-study/student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-a-multimodal-study.md)
**Evidence:** "a majority of students draw representations showing that IMFs are located within a single molecule rather than between separate molecules"

## Misconception 585: Students think hydrogen bonding is the covalent O-H bond within ethanol, or otherwise a bond inside one molecule.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Hydrogen bond location and identity
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is an intermolecular attraction between a hydrogen attached to O, N, or F in one molecule and a lone pair on O, N, or F in another molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should locate hydrogen bonding between two molecules and recognize that the covalent O-H bond is not the hydrogen bond; the hydrogen donor must be bonded to O, N, or F and attract a heteroatom on a different molecule.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Prevalence:** 72% (N=68) within; only 15% (N=14) correctly indicated hydrogen bonding occurs between separate molecules
**Population:** Cohort 1 general chemistry students, N=94
**Source:** Student Understanding of Intermolecular Forces: A Multimodal Study (student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-a-multimodal-study/student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-a-multimodal-study.md)
**Evidence:** "72% (N = 68) clearly represented hydrogen bonding as an O−H bond within a single molecule of ethanol."

## Misconception 586: Students think a hydrogen-bonding representation can show only one molecule or no molecule at all.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Number of molecules in representation
**Correct concept:** To represent hydrogen bonding, students should draw at least two molecules and show the interaction between them.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should include multiple molecules because hydrogen bonding is an intermolecular interaction; a single isolated molecule cannot show the between-molecule attraction.
**Concept category:** representation of molecules
**Prevalence:** 54% (N=51) drew only one molecule or none
**Population:** Cohort 1 general chemistry students, N=94
**Source:** Student Understanding of Intermolecular Forces: A Multimodal Study (student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-a-multimodal-study/student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-a-multimodal-study.md)
**Evidence:** "54% (N = 51) of students drew only one molecule or none at all (even though they were explicitly asked to draw three molecules)."

## Misconception 587: Students think dipole-dipole interactions occur within a single molecule, for example as bonds or interactions among parts of the same ethanol molecule.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole interactions / Location of interaction
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole interactions are attractions between the partial charges of different polar molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify two polar molecules and place the interaction between them, because dipole-dipole is an intermolecular force arising from charge separation across separate molecules.
**Concept category:** dipole-dipole
**Prevalence:** Cohort 1: 61% (N=57) within; Cohort 2: 58% (N=93) within
**Population:** General chemistry college students; Cohort 1 N=94, Cohort 2 N=160
**Source:** Student Understanding of Intermolecular Forces: A Multimodal Study (student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-a-multimodal-study/student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-a-multimodal-study.md)
**Evidence:** "the majority of students (61%, N = 57) in Cohort 1 drew dipole−dipole interactions as occurring within the ethanol molecule"

## Misconception 588: Students describe dipole-dipole as a bond rather than an intermolecular interaction.

**Topic:** Dipole-dipole interactions / Bond vs interaction language
**Correct concept:** Dipole-dipole is an intermolecular attraction, not a covalent bond.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish between a covalent bond that holds atoms together within a molecule and an intermolecular attraction that acts between molecules; the term bond should not be used for dipole-dipole.
**Concept category:** bond vs interaction
**Prevalence:** Documented in student written responses; no overall percentage given in the paper
**Population:** Cohort 1 general chemistry students
**Source:** Student Understanding of Intermolecular Forces: A Multimodal Study (student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-a-multimodal-study/student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-a-multimodal-study.md)
**Evidence:** "Dipole−dipole is 'a pretty weak bond between polar molecules.'"

## Misconception 589: Students think London dispersion forces occur within a single molecule, or as bonds within the molecule.

**Topic:** London dispersion forces / Location of interaction
**Correct concept:** London dispersion forces are intermolecular attractions between molecules caused by temporary induced dipoles.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify that dispersion forces arise from interactions between separate particles/molecules, not from bonds inside one molecule.
**Concept category:** London dispersion forces
**Prevalence:** Cohort 1: 55% (N=52) within; Cohort 2: 56% (N=89) within
**Population:** General chemistry college students; Cohort 1 N=94, Cohort 2 N=160
**Source:** Student Understanding of Intermolecular Forces: A Multimodal Study (student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-a-multimodal-study/student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-a-multimodal-study.md)
**Evidence:** "55% (N = 52) for LDFs"

## Misconception 590: Students think London dispersion forces are simply a property that all substances have, rather than an interaction between particles.

**Topic:** London dispersion forces / Universality / always present idea
**Correct concept:** All molecules can experience London dispersion forces, but LDFs are still intermolecular forces arising from temporary dipoles between molecules or atoms.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should say that dispersion forces exist because of temporary electron fluctuations that create attractions between particles; universality does not mean they are an intrinsic property located within one molecule.
**Concept category:** London dispersion forces
**Prevalence:** 26% (N=24) explicitly said LDFs are present for all molecules or always present
**Population:** Cohort 1 general chemistry students, N=94
**Source:** Student Understanding of Intermolecular Forces: A Multimodal Study (student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-a-multimodal-study/student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-a-multimodal-study.md)
**Evidence:** "26% (N=24) of students who explicitly stated in their written responses that LDFs are present for all molecules or always present."

## Misconception 591: Students use atom and molecule interchangeably when describing IMFs, such as referring to an atom when they mean a molecule or vice versa.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces terminology / Atom vs molecule confusion
**Correct concept:** Atoms and molecules are different entities; IMFs usually describe interactions between molecules (or particles), not confusion between the two terms.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should name the correct level of organization: an atom is a single atom, while a molecule is a bonded collection of atoms; the interaction should be described using the appropriate entity.
**Concept category:** terminology
**Prevalence:** Documented qualitatively in student responses; no overall percentage given
**Population:** Cohort 1 general chemistry students
**Source:** Student Understanding of Intermolecular Forces: A Multimodal Study (student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-a-multimodal-study/student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-a-multimodal-study.md)
**Evidence:** "students often used atom to mean molecule (or vice versa)"

## Misconception 592: Students use bond and interaction interchangeably, treating IMFs as bonds in the same sense as covalent bonds.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces terminology / Bond vs interaction confusion
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces are attractions or interactions between molecules; covalent bonds are intramolecular bonds within a molecule, except that hydrogen bond is a special term for an IMF.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish intramolecular covalent bonding from intermolecular attraction and reserve bond language for covalent bonds or clearly identified hydrogen bonds when appropriate.
**Concept category:** terminology
**Prevalence:** Documented qualitatively in student responses; no overall percentage given
**Population:** Cohort 1 general chemistry students
**Source:** Student Understanding of Intermolecular Forces: A Multimodal Study (student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-a-multimodal-study/student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces-a-multimodal-study.md)
**Evidence:** "some students used the terms bond and interaction interchangeably."

## Misconception 593: Students think intermolecular forces are bonds within molecules rather than attractions between molecules.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Definition and scope
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces are attractions between molecules, not within molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish intramolecular bonding from intermolecular attraction by asking whether the interaction is holding atoms together inside one molecule or attracting separate molecules to each other.
**Concept category:** intermolecular vs intramolecular
**Source:** Tactile Models for the Visualization, Conceptualization, and Review of Intermolecular Forces in the College Chemistry Classroom (tactile-models-for-the-visualization-conceptualization-and-review-of-intermolecular-forces-in-the-college-chemistry-2/tactile-models-for-the-visualization-conceptualization-and-review-of-intermolecular-forces-in-the-college-chemistry-2.md)
**Evidence:** "They are forces, often referred to as 'bonds', between (not within) molecules."

## Misconception 594: Students treat intermolecular forces as comparable in strength to, or not clearly weaker than, ionic and covalent bonds.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Relative strength vs covalent/ionic bonds
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces are generally much weaker than intramolecular ionic and covalent bonds.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the type of interaction and recognize that IMFs are secondary attractions between molecules, so they are weaker than chemical bonds holding atoms together within a molecule.
**Concept category:** intermolecular vs intramolecular
**Source:** Tactile Models for the Visualization, Conceptualization, and Review of Intermolecular Forces in the College Chemistry Classroom (tactile-models-for-the-visualization-conceptualization-and-review-of-intermolecular-forces-in-the-college-chemistry-2/tactile-models-for-the-visualization-conceptualization-and-review-of-intermolecular-forces-in-the-college-chemistry-2.md)
**Evidence:** "these forces are ... almost always weaker than intramolecular bonds such as ionic and covalent"

## Misconception 595: Students believe hydrogen bonding can only occur between hydrogen and nitrogen, oxygen, fluorine, or carbon.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Hydrogen bonding participants
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding involves hydrogen bonded to N, O, or F interacting with a lone pair on N, O, or F; carbon is not a hydrogen-bonding partner in the usual definition.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify that H-bond donors are H attached to N/O/F and acceptors are lone pairs on N/O/F, then verify whether both parts of the interaction are present.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Population:** Student preactivity response
**Source:** Tactile Models for the Visualization, Conceptualization, and Review of Intermolecular Forces in the College Chemistry Classroom (tactile-models-for-the-visualization-conceptualization-and-review-of-intermolecular-forces-in-the-college-chemistry-2/tactile-models-for-the-visualization-conceptualization-and-review-of-intermolecular-forces-in-the-college-chemistry-2.md)
**Evidence:** "A hydrogen bond is a rather strong bond and - can only occur between hydrogen and nitrogen, oxygen, fluorine, or carbon."

## Misconception 596: Students think a hydrogen bond is simply a weak bond between hydrogen atoms.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Hydrogen bonding strength and identity
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding is an intermolecular attraction involving hydrogen attached to an electronegative atom and a lone pair on another electronegative atom; it is not a bond between two hydrogen atoms.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should check the donor-acceptor requirement for hydrogen bonding and recognize that hydrogen atoms do not hydrogen-bond to each other directly.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Population:** Student preactivity response
**Source:** Tactile Models for the Visualization, Conceptualization, and Review of Intermolecular Forces in the College Chemistry Classroom (tactile-models-for-the-visualization-conceptualization-and-review-of-intermolecular-forces-in-the-college-chemistry-2/tactile-models-for-the-visualization-conceptualization-and-review-of-intermolecular-forces-in-the-college-chemistry-2.md)
**Evidence:** "Weak bond between hydrogen atoms."

## Misconception 597: Students can memorize textbook definitions of IMFs without understanding how molecules interact in three dimensions.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / General conceptualization
**Correct concept:** Students should connect verbal definitions of IMFs to the 3D arrangement and interaction of molecules at the microscopic level.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use molecular geometry and intermolecular contact points to explain attraction rather than only reciting a definition.
**Concept category:** representational understanding
**Population:** College chemistry students discussed in cited literature
**Source:** Tactile Models for the Visualization, Conceptualization, and Review of Intermolecular Forces in the College Chemistry Classroom (tactile-models-for-the-visualization-conceptualization-and-review-of-intermolecular-forces-in-the-college-chemistry-2/tactile-models-for-the-visualization-conceptualization-and-review-of-intermolecular-forces-in-the-college-chemistry-2.md)
**Evidence:** "students often can provide textbook level definitions but leave general chemistry and enter into organic chemistry without a thorough conceptual understanding of IMFs"

## Misconception 598: Students may be able to give correct definitions from drawings but still lack a 2D-to-3D mental model of intermolecular interactions.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Lewis/drawing vs 3D understanding
**Correct concept:** Students should translate among symbolic, 2D, and 3D representations and use 3D structure to reason about intermolecular interactions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should interpret a structural drawing in terms of 3D molecular shape, electron distribution, and possible intermolecular contacts before deciding on IMF type or strength.
**Concept category:** representational competence
**Population:** Students in CLUE-related assessments cited by the paper
**Source:** Tactile Models for the Visualization, Conceptualization, and Review of Intermolecular Forces in the College Chemistry Classroom (tactile-models-for-the-visualization-conceptualization-and-review-of-intermolecular-forces-in-the-college-chemistry-2/tactile-models-for-the-visualization-conceptualization-and-review-of-intermolecular-forces-in-the-college-chemistry-2.md)
**Evidence:** "These drawings reveal students' ability to provide definitions, but this does not necessarily translate to a 2D or 3D understanding."

## Misconception 599: Students who memorize the term hydrogen bonding may have an incomplete or incorrect understanding of what it is.

**Topic:** Hydrogen bonding / Incomplete or incorrect understanding from memorization
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding should be understood as a specific intermolecular attraction with defined structural and electronic requirements.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should move beyond memorized wording and determine whether the molecular structures satisfy the requirements for hydrogen bonding.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Population:** Organic chemistry students cited in prior work
**Source:** Tactile Models for the Visualization, Conceptualization, and Review of Intermolecular Forces in the College Chemistry Classroom (tactile-models-for-the-visualization-conceptualization-and-review-of-intermolecular-forces-in-the-college-chemistry-2/tactile-models-for-the-visualization-conceptualization-and-review-of-intermolecular-forces-in-the-college-chemistry-2.md)
**Evidence:** "when students solely memorize the terms, they have an incomplete or incorrect understanding of hydrogen bonding"

## Misconception 600: Students fail to connect macroscopic properties such as boiling point to the molecular-level arrangement and interactions causing them.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Physical properties and molecular level causes
**Correct concept:** Boiling point and similar properties should be explained by the strength and nature of intermolecular interactions among molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should relate a higher or lower boiling point to the energy required to overcome intermolecular attractions and to molecular structure and polarity.
**Concept category:** property-to-particle reasoning
**Population:** College chemistry students discussed in cited literature
**Source:** Tactile Models for the Visualization, Conceptualization, and Review of Intermolecular Forces in the College Chemistry Classroom (tactile-models-for-the-visualization-conceptualization-and-review-of-intermolecular-forces-in-the-college-chemistry-2/tactile-models-for-the-visualization-conceptualization-and-review-of-intermolecular-forces-in-the-college-chemistry-2.md)
**Evidence:** "students often fail to connect these concepts to what is occurring on the molecular level in terms of the consequential three-dimensional arrangements of the interacting molecules"

## Misconception 601: Students misconceive the factors that determine the strength of intermolecular forces.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Factors affecting IMF strength
**Correct concept:** IMF strength depends on molecular features such as polarity, shape, accessibility of lone pairs, and the type of intermolecular interaction present.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should examine molecular structure, polarity, and geometry to decide which intermolecular attractions are possible and how strong they are likely to be.
**Concept category:** IMF strength factors
**Population:** College chemistry students discussed in cited literature
**Source:** Tactile Models for the Visualization, Conceptualization, and Review of Intermolecular Forces in the College Chemistry Classroom (tactile-models-for-the-visualization-conceptualization-and-review-of-intermolecular-forces-in-the-college-chemistry-2/tactile-models-for-the-visualization-conceptualization-and-review-of-intermolecular-forces-in-the-college-chemistry-2.md)
**Evidence:** "Students may also misconceive the factors that affect the strength of IMFs"

## Misconception 602: Students believe dispersion (London) forces are negligible compared with hydrogen bonding and dipole-dipole forces.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Dispersion forces
**Correct concept:** Dispersion forces are significant intermolecular forces and often increase with the number of electrons in a molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should recognize that electron-rich, more polarizable molecules experience stronger dispersion forces, which can strongly affect physical properties such as boiling point.
**Concept category:** dispersion forces
**Population:** Introductory chemistry students (implied by the paper's discussion of first-year instruction)
**Source:** Teaching Intermolecular Forces to First-Year Undergraduate Students (teaching-intermolecular-forces-to-first-year-undergraduate-students/teaching-intermolecular-forces-to-first-year-undergraduate-students.md)
**Evidence:** "the widespread misconception that dispersion (London) forces are of negligible strength when compared with hydrogen-bonding and dipole—dipole (Keesom) forces."

## Misconception 603: Students think that intermolecular forces have a simple, universal, one-to-one correlation with boiling points across all compounds.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and physical properties / Boiling point correlations
**Correct concept:** Boiling point is often correlated with intermolecular-force strength, but there are exceptions and anomalies, so boiling point is only a reasonably good measure rather than an exact universal proxy.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should compare structures, intermolecular interactions, and additional factors such as molecular polarity and surface charge effects before inferring IMF strength from boiling point alone.
**Concept category:** physical property correlation
**Population:** First-year undergraduate students
**Source:** Teaching Intermolecular Forces to First-Year Undergraduate Students (teaching-intermolecular-forces-to-first-year-undergraduate-students/teaching-intermolecular-forces-to-first-year-undergraduate-students.md)
**Evidence:** "it may be assumed that all of these physical properties necessarily have a similar, simple correspondence to IMFs as that which is generally shown by boiling points."

## Misconception 604: Students believe melting point should show the same neat trend with intermolecular forces as boiling point does.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and physical properties / Melting points
**Correct concept:** Melting point is influenced by both enthalpy and entropy factors, so it may not correlate simply or consistently with intermolecular-force strength.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should reason that melting depends on $Delta_{fus}H$ and $Delta_{fus}S$ and that symmetry, crystal packing, and disorder can change melting points independently of IMF strength.
**Concept category:** melting point misconceptions
**Population:** First-year undergraduate students
**Source:** Teaching Intermolecular Forces to First-Year Undergraduate Students (teaching-intermolecular-forces-to-first-year-undergraduate-students/teaching-intermolecular-forces-to-first-year-undergraduate-students.md)
**Evidence:** "the correlation of IMFs with many physical properties is not necessarily as straightforward as is generally the case with boiling points and vapor pressures."

## Misconception 605: Students assume viscosity should track intermolecular-force strength in the same straightforward way as boiling point.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and physical properties / Viscosity
**Correct concept:** Viscosity depends on both intermolecular forces and molecular shape/entanglement, so structural effects can dominate and invert the expected trend.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should consider how molecular shape affects surface contact and entanglement when comparing viscosities, not just the number or type of intermolecular forces present.
**Concept category:** viscosity misconceptions
**Population:** First-year undergraduate students
**Source:** Teaching Intermolecular Forces to First-Year Undergraduate Students (teaching-intermolecular-forces-to-first-year-undergraduate-students/teaching-intermolecular-forces-to-first-year-undergraduate-students.md)
**Evidence:** "their viscosities show a strong and unexpected trend in the opposite direction."

## Misconception 606: Students assume surface tension should always increase in the same direction as intermolecular-force strength across a homologous series.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces and physical properties / Surface tension
**Correct concept:** Surface tension can increase or decrease depending on whether enthalpy or entropy effects dominate; it is not guaranteed to follow a simple IMF trend.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should evaluate the balance of enthalpic and entropic contributions to surface tension rather than expecting a universal monotonic relationship with IMF strength.
**Concept category:** surface tension misconceptions
**Population:** First-year undergraduate students
**Source:** Teaching Intermolecular Forces to First-Year Undergraduate Students (teaching-intermolecular-forces-to-first-year-undergraduate-students/teaching-intermolecular-forces-to-first-year-undergraduate-students.md)
**Evidence:** "the surface tension, in defiance of expectations, shows exactly the opposite trend."

## Misconception 607: Students believe intermolecular forces are the sole influence governing physical properties such as melting point, viscosity, or surface tension.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Only IMF influence
**Correct concept:** Physical properties are affected by intermolecular forces along with other factors such as molecular symmetry, shape, entropy, crystal structure, and bonding directionality.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should identify all relevant factors for the property in question and not attribute changes to IMFs alone.
**Concept category:** overgeneralization of IMFs
**Population:** Students in introductory chemistry contexts
**Source:** Teaching Intermolecular Forces to First-Year Undergraduate Students (teaching-intermolecular-forces-to-first-year-undergraduate-students/teaching-intermolecular-forces-to-first-year-undergraduate-students.md)
**Evidence:** "may mistakenly lead students to believe that IMFs are the sole influence that needs to be considered."

## Misconception 608: Surface tension is a force, or force per unit area, rather than energy required to increase unit surface area.

**Topic:** Surface tension / Definition and unit
**Correct concept:** Surface tension is work/energy required to increase the unit surface area (J/m^2).
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect the macroscopic phenomenon to the definition of surface tension as energy needed to create more surface; the unit should be interpreted as energy per area, not force.
**Concept category:** definition/unit
**Prevalence:** 12/16 students gave incorrect unit responses; only 1/16 gave the correct answer.
**Population:** 16 undergraduates majoring in chemistry education in Korea
**Source:** Undergraduate Students' Understanding of Surface Tension Considering Molecular Area (undergraduate-students-understanding-of-surface-tension-considering-molecular-area/undergraduate-students-understanding-of-surface-tension-considering-molecular-area.md)
**Evidence:** Most responses were "force (N)" or "force per unit area  (N/m^2)".

## Misconception 609: Surface tension can be predicted only from intermolecular interaction indicators such as boiling point, hydrogen bonding, or molecular weight; molecular area is not considered as a factor.

**Topic:** Surface tension / Factors affecting surface tension
**Correct concept:** Surface tension depends on both intermolecular interaction and molecular area (or number of molecules per unit area).
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason from the definition of surface tension as work per unit area and recognize that both the energy contribution from intermolecular interaction and the area occupied by molecules affect the value; therefore, liquids with similar or stronger interactions can still have lower surface tension if molecular area differs.
**Concept category:** intermolecular interaction vs molecular area
**Prevalence:** 16/16 students used only intermolecular interaction in the preconception test for question 2.
**Population:** 16 undergraduates majoring in chemistry education in Korea
**Source:** Undergraduate Students' Understanding of Surface Tension Considering Molecular Area (undergraduate-students-understanding-of-surface-tension-considering-molecular-area/undergraduate-students-understanding-of-surface-tension-considering-molecular-area.md)
**Evidence:** Students attempted to predict the degree of surface tension only by intermolecular interaction. No one described it in terms of molecular area or unit area.

## Misconception 610: Surface tension acts inward on the liquid surface.

**Topic:** Surface tension / Direction of surface tension
**Correct concept:** Surface tension acts parallel to the surface.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish between fewer interactions at the surface and the actual direction of the tension force/work; the surface molecules are in force equilibrium, and the surface tension direction is parallel to the interface.
**Concept category:** force direction
**Prevalence:** 12/16 students answered inward; 3/16 outward; 1/16 inward and outward; 0/16 correct.
**Population:** 16 undergraduates majoring in chemistry education in Korea
**Source:** Undergraduate Students' Understanding of Surface Tension Considering Molecular Area (undergraduate-students-understanding-of-surface-tension-considering-molecular-area/undergraduate-students-understanding-of-surface-tension-considering-molecular-area.md)
**Evidence:** Most students expressed that an inward direction.

## Misconception 611: If surface tension data do not match the predicted order based on intermolecular forces, the data are likely invalid due to experimental error.

**Topic:** Surface tension anomalies / Explaining the water/ethylene glycol/diethylene glycol/ethanol counterexample
**Correct concept:** The measured anomaly should be accepted as valid and explained by a scientifically grounded model that includes both intermolecular interaction and molecular area.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate whether the measurements are trustworthy, then revise the explanation if the data are valid rather than dismissing them; the anomaly should trigger reconsideration of the model, not rejection of the results.
