**Concept category:** response to anomalous data / rejection
**Prevalence:** 1/16 students
**Population:** 16 undergraduates majoring in chemistry education in Korea
**Source:** Undergraduate Students' Understanding of Surface Tension Considering Molecular Area (undergraduate-students-understanding-of-surface-tension-considering-molecular-area/undergraduate-students-understanding-of-surface-tension-considering-molecular-area.md)
**Evidence:** I think intermolecular forces, that is, the number of hydrogen bonding and the boiling point have a great influence. In the experiment, between diethylene glycol and ethylene glycol, the surface tension of the latter was larger, which was contrary to my expectation. But the difference could be regarded as an experimental error, so my thought has not changed.

## Misconception 612: Anomalous surface tension measurements can be accepted as valid, but no explanation is needed or possible.

**Topic:** Surface tension anomalies / Inability to explain anomalous data
**Correct concept:** Valid anomalous data should be explained using a coherent scientific account, ideally integrating intermolecular interaction with molecular area.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use the anomaly to test and refine their conception, producing an explanation that links the observed values to molecular structure and unit area rather than stopping at description.
**Concept category:** response to anomalous data / abeyance
**Prevalence:** 2/16 students
**Population:** 16 undergraduates majoring in chemistry education in Korea
**Source:** Undergraduate Students' Understanding of Surface Tension Considering Molecular Area (undergraduate-students-understanding-of-surface-tension-considering-molecular-area/undergraduate-students-understanding-of-surface-tension-considering-molecular-area.md)
**Evidence:** They believed anomalous data were valid, but there were not any attempts to explain the anomaly.

## Misconception 613: Surface tension is influenced more by hydrogen bonding than by dispersion force, without a basis in the definition of surface tension.

**Topic:** Surface tension anomalies / Modified factor explanation
**Correct concept:** Surface tension should be explained from the work/energy per unit area, considering both intermolecular interaction and molecular area, not by an unsupported ranking of interaction types alone.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect the strength/type of intermolecular interactions to surface tension through the surface-area definition and explain why those interactions matter in relation to molecular area.
**Concept category:** response to anomalous data / peripheral theory change
**Prevalence:** 2/16 students
**Population:** 16 undergraduates majoring in chemistry education in Korea
**Source:** Undergraduate Students' Understanding of Surface Tension Considering Molecular Area (undergraduate-students-understanding-of-surface-tension-considering-molecular-area/undergraduate-students-understanding-of-surface-tension-considering-molecular-area.md)
**Evidence:** "Surface tension is influenced greater by hydrogen bonding than by dispersion force."

## Misconception 614: Surface tension is determined by intermolecular interaction plus intermolecular accessibility, where molecular spacing/accessibility is treated as the causal factor instead of molecular area.

**Topic:** Surface tension anomalies / Modified factor explanation
**Correct concept:** Surface tension is explained by intermolecular interaction together with molecular area (number of molecules per unit area), not by a vaguely defined accessibility factor.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should relate any structural factor to the surface-area definition and show how molecular area changes the number of molecules per unit area and thus the energy required to create surface.
**Concept category:** response to anomalous data / peripheral theory change
**Prevalence:** 1/16 students
**Population:** 16 undergraduates majoring in chemistry education in Korea
**Source:** Undergraduate Students' Understanding of Surface Tension Considering Molecular Area (undergraduate-students-understanding-of-surface-tension-considering-molecular-area/undergraduate-students-understanding-of-surface-tension-considering-molecular-area.md)
**Evidence:** Student S12 thought that the difference in molecular area could affect intermolecular accessibility (the degree to which molecules approach each other), resulting in changes in intermolecular interaction and hence in surface tension.

## Misconception 615: Surface tension depends on molecular weight because heavier molecules cannot be pulled apart well during measurement, so gravity affects the measured surface tension directly.

**Topic:** Surface tension anomalies / Gravity explanation
**Correct concept:** Surface tension is a surface property arising from intermolecular interactions and molecular area; it is not directly explained by molecular weight or gravity in the way described.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should avoid attributing surface tension to gravitational weight of molecules and instead reason about surface energy, intermolecular interactions, and the surface-area term.
**Concept category:** response to anomalous data / peripheral theory change
**Prevalence:** 1/16 students
**Population:** 16 undergraduates majoring in chemistry education in Korea
**Source:** Undergraduate Students' Understanding of Surface Tension Considering Molecular Area (undergraduate-students-understanding-of-surface-tension-considering-molecular-area/undergraduate-students-understanding-of-surface-tension-considering-molecular-area.md)
**Evidence:** "The reason why the surface tension of diethylene glycol is lower than that of ethylene glycol is that diethylene glycol is much heavier. Surface tension is a force that pulls molecules from each other, and if molecules are too heavy, it will be difficult to pull them from each other."

## Misconception 616: Students believe a molecule with polar bonds must be polar, even if the molecular geometry is symmetrical.

**Topic:** molecular polarity / symmetry and bond polarity
**Correct concept:** Molecular polarity depends on the vector sum of bond dipoles; symmetrical molecules can be nonpolar when bond dipoles cancel.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should determine whether the molecule has polar bonds, then consider the 3D shape and whether the bond dipoles cancel. If the geometry is symmetrical, the molecule can have zero net dipole even with polar bonds.
**Concept category:** molecular polarity / symmetry
**Prevalence:** Control group scored 0.9/2 vs experimental group 1.6/2 on classifying symmetric molecules with polar bonds as nonpolar.
**Population:** Traditional students majoring in natural sciences in two sections of second-semester introductory chemistry (N=36 control, N=36 experimental).
**Source:** Using Computer-Based Visualization Strategies to Improve Students' Understanding of Molecular Polarity and Miscibility (using-computer-based-visualization-strategies-to-improve-students-understanding-of-molecular-polarity-and-miscibility/using-computer-based-visualization-strategies-to-improve-students-understanding-of-molecular-polarity-and-miscibility.md)
**Evidence:** "symmetrical molecules with polar bonds are non-polar because they have no positive or negative end."

## Misconception 617: Students explain that oil and water do not mix only by stating that one is polar and the other is nonpolar, treating 'like dissolves like' as a memorized rule rather than an explanation.

**Topic:** miscibility / oil and water
**Correct concept:** Oil and water are immiscible because water molecules are more strongly attracted to each other than to nonpolar oil molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare intermolecular attractions: water-water attractions are stronger than water-oil attractions, so mixing is unfavorable and the liquids separate.
**Concept category:** miscibility / like dissolves like
**Prevalence:** More than 80% of the control group; just over 30% of the experimental group gave the rule without explanation.
**Population:** Introductory chemistry students in the control and experimental groups.
**Source:** Using Computer-Based Visualization Strategies to Improve Students' Understanding of Molecular Polarity and Miscibility (using-computer-based-visualization-strategies-to-improve-students-understanding-of-molecular-polarity-and-miscibility/using-computer-based-visualization-strategies-to-improve-students-understanding-of-molecular-polarity-and-miscibility.md)
**Evidence:** "More than 80% of the students in the control group responded with the idea that water is polar and oil is nonpolar or that 'like dissolves like' a mere statement of the rule with no real explanation of why it is valid."

## Misconception 618: Students believe there are no attractions between oil and water molecules.

**Topic:** miscibility / oil and water attractions
**Correct concept:** There are attractions between all molecules; oil and water simply have much weaker cross-attractions than water-water attractions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize intermolecular attractions are universal but differ in strength. Water molecules preferentially attract each other more strongly than they attract nonpolar oil molecules.
**Concept category:** intermolecular attractions / miscibility
**Prevalence:** 18% in each group.
**Population:** Students in both control and experimental groups.
**Source:** Using Computer-Based Visualization Strategies to Improve Students' Understanding of Molecular Polarity and Miscibility (using-computer-based-visualization-strategies-to-improve-students-understanding-of-molecular-polarity-and-miscibility/using-computer-based-visualization-strategies-to-improve-students-understanding-of-molecular-polarity-and-miscibility.md)
**Evidence:** "similar numbers of students in each group suggested that there are no attractions between oil and water molecules (18%)"

## Misconception 619: Students believe oil and water molecules repel each other.

**Topic:** miscibility / oil and water attractions
**Correct concept:** Oil and water do not repel each other; they are immiscible because water-water attractions are stronger than water-oil attractions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish repulsion from weak attraction. Separation occurs because the system lowers energy by keeping stronger like-like interactions rather than because unlike molecules repel.
**Concept category:** intermolecular attractions / miscibility
**Prevalence:** 7% in each group.
**Population:** Students in both control and experimental groups.
**Source:** Using Computer-Based Visualization Strategies to Improve Students' Understanding of Molecular Polarity and Miscibility (using-computer-based-visualization-strategies-to-improve-students-understanding-of-molecular-polarity-and-miscibility/using-computer-based-visualization-strategies-to-improve-students-understanding-of-molecular-polarity-and-miscibility.md)
**Evidence:** "similar numbers of students in each group suggested that there are no attractions between oil and water molecules (18%) or that oil and water molecules repel each other (7%)."

## Misconception 620: Students think hydrogen molecules attract water oxygen atoms, or more generally that all H and O atoms attract each other regardless of charge.

**Topic:** intermolecular attractions / water and hydrogen gas
**Correct concept:** Water molecules attract each other through the H of one water to the O of another water, but hydrogen gas does not provide opposite charges for strong H-O attraction to water.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify partial charges first: attraction occurs between opposite charges. Water has polar O-H bonds and partial charges; H2 is nonpolar and should not be shown attracting water's O atoms in this context.
**Concept category:** partial charges / intermolecular forces
**Population:** Students responding to the conceptual question on intermolecular attractions in immiscible mixtures.
**Source:** Using Computer-Based Visualization Strategies to Improve Students' Understanding of Molecular Polarity and Miscibility (using-computer-based-visualization-strategies-to-improve-students-understanding-of-molecular-polarity-and-miscibility/using-computer-based-visualization-strategies-to-improve-students-understanding-of-molecular-polarity-and-miscibility.md)
**Evidence:** "This question was designed to determine whether students believed that all H and O atoms are attracted to each other or if they recognized that H and O atoms are attracted to each other only when they have opposite charges."

## Misconception 621: Students omit the attraction between sodium ions and chloride ions when describing an aqueous sodium chloride system, focusing only on ion-water attractions.

**Topic:** ionic solutions / water with sodium chloride
**Correct concept:** A complete description includes water-water attractions, Na+ to water oxygen attractions, Cl- to water hydrogen attractions, and Na+ to Cl- attractions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should account for all relevant intermolecular/ion-ion attractions in the system, not only solvation interactions. Both ion-water and ion-ion attractions matter in the aqueous mixture.
**Concept category:** ionic attraction / solvation
**Prevalence:** A majority of the control group; experimental students also tended to ignore only the sodium ion-chloride ion attractions.
**Population:** Students in the control group and experimental group answering the aqueous sodium chloride attraction question.
**Source:** Using Computer-Based Visualization Strategies to Improve Students' Understanding of Molecular Polarity and Miscibility (using-computer-based-visualization-strategies-to-improve-students-understanding-of-molecular-polarity-and-miscibility/using-computer-based-visualization-strategies-to-improve-students-understanding-of-molecular-polarity-and-miscibility.md)
**Evidence:** "A majority of the responses from students in the control group recognized attractions between the water molecules and the ions in sodium chloride but ignored the attractions of the water molecules to each other and of the sodium ions to the chloride ions"

## Misconception 622: Students give incomplete explanations of how soap removes grease, omitting the dual hydrophobic/hydrophilic nature of soap and its role as a molecular bridge.

**Topic:** soap and surfactants / grease removal mechanism
**Correct concept:** Soap molecules have both hydrophobic and hydrophilic ends; the hydrophobic end interacts with grease, the hydrophilic end interacts with water, and soap helps emulsify grease so it can be washed away.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain that soap is amphiphilic, orient itself with the hydrophobic tail in grease and hydrophilic head in water, and thereby form micelles/emulsions that allow grease to disperse in water.
**Concept category:** surfactants / amphiphilic molecules
**Prevalence:** Students who viewed the elpot maps gave more complete responses; 61% experimental vs 39% control provided completely correct responses.
**Population:** Students in the control and experimental groups answering the soap/grease question.
**Source:** Using Computer-Based Visualization Strategies to Improve Students' Understanding of Molecular Polarity and Miscibility (using-computer-based-visualization-strategies-to-improve-students-understanding-of-molecular-polarity-and-miscibility/using-computer-based-visualization-strategies-to-improve-students-understanding-of-molecular-polarity-and-miscibility.md)
**Evidence:** "Both instructors agreed that a correct response should include four concepts: (i) soap is a special molecule in that it has a hydrophobic end and a hydrophilic end; (ii) soap is attracted to water by its hydrophilic end; (iii) soap is attracted to the grease by its hydrophobic end; and (iv) soap acts as a 'molecular bridge'"

## Misconception 623: Students think hydrogen bonds are fixed bonds rather than dynamic intermolecular interactions.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Static versus dynamic hydrogen bonding
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonds are temporary intermolecular interactions that continually form and break as molecules move.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that intermolecular attractions depend on molecular positions and thermal motion, so hydrogen bonding is not permanent; as molecules move, bonds can form and dissociate repeatedly.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Population:** students in the chemistry courses described in the article
**Source:** Using Molecular Dynamics Simulation To Reinforce Student Understanding of Intermolecular Forces (using-molecular-dynamics-simulation-to-reinforce-student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces/using-molecular-dynamics-simulation-to-reinforce-student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces.md)
**Evidence:** students have a tendency to think of hydrogen bonds as fixed bonds rather than dynamic intermolecular forces.

## Misconception 624: Students may think molecular dynamics simulations can model the dissociation of strong acids in water by breaking and forming covalent bonds.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Acid dissociation and molecular dynamics limitations
**Correct concept:** These simulations do not change covalent bonds; they are suitable for intermolecular interactions, not chemical dissociation reactions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish intermolecular forces from intramolecular covalent bonding and reason that a model based on fixed covalent structures cannot represent proton transfer or bond breaking/forming in acid dissociation.
**Concept category:** limitations of model
**Population:** students using molecular dynamics simulations
**Source:** Using Molecular Dynamics Simulation To Reinforce Student Understanding of Intermolecular Forces (using-molecular-dynamics-simulation-to-reinforce-student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces/using-molecular-dynamics-simulation-to-reinforce-student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces.md)
**Evidence:** trying to simulate the dissociation of a strong acid in water will fail, because in that case, intramolecular bonds are being broken and formed.

## Misconception 625: Students may expect molecular dynamics simulations to give exact quantitative values, such as the exact boiling point of a substance.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Exact quantitative prediction from simulation
**Correct concept:** MDS is better for qualitative or relative predictions, such as comparing boiling points, not exact agreement with experiment.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use the simulation to compare relative intermolecular strength and relative boiling behavior, but not infer an exact boiling point from the temperature at which aggregation ceases.
**Concept category:** limitations of model
**Population:** students using molecular dynamics simulations
**Source:** Using Molecular Dynamics Simulation To Reinforce Student Understanding of Intermolecular Forces (using-molecular-dynamics-simulation-to-reinforce-student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces/using-molecular-dynamics-simulation-to-reinforce-student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces.md)
**Evidence:** trying to determine the exact boiling point of a substance by finding the temperature at which the molecules no longer associate with each other would be unwise.

## Misconception 626: Students may think the empty space in a low-density simulation cell is physically unrealistic and therefore invalidates the simulation.

**Topic:** Intermolecular forces / Periodic boundary conditions and empty space
**Correct concept:** In these simulations, empty space can be treated as a nonpolar environment, and low density can be used to improve efficiency without changing the final outcome.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should understand that the model is designed to represent intermolecular interactions efficiently; the low-density setup is an intentional simplification, not a claim that real condensed phases contain that much empty space.
**Concept category:** simulation setup
**Population:** students constructing simulation cells
**Source:** Using Molecular Dynamics Simulation To Reinforce Student Understanding of Intermolecular Forces (using-molecular-dynamics-simulation-to-reinforce-student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces/using-molecular-dynamics-simulation-to-reinforce-student-understanding-of-intermolecular-forces.md)
**Evidence:** While the use of low densities may seem unrealistic, they need not be cause for concern. Empty space in a cell should be viewed as a nonpolar environment.

## Misconception 627: Students think a state change breaks the covalent bonds within a molecule, so the molecule changes into different substances.

**Topic:** Intermolecular vs intramolecular forces / State change and bond breaking
**Correct concept:** During a physical change of state, intermolecular forces are overcome but intramolecular covalent bonds remain intact.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish between forces between molecules and bonds within molecules, and reason that melting/boiling changes molecular arrangement and spacing, not molecular composition.
**Concept category:** intermolecular vs intramolecular forces
**Prevalence:** Question 2: 12.70% alternative conception; Question 4: 11.11% alternative conception
**Population:** 64 Grade 10 Thai students
**Source:** Using Open-Ended Questions to Diagnose Students' Understanding of Inter- and Intramolecular Forces (using-open-ended-questions-to-diagnose-students-4n1is4ta1v/using-open-ended-questions-to-diagnose-students-4n1is4ta1v.md)
**Evidence:** "when water changes in state, its chemical bond in the molecule does not be destroyed"

## Misconception 628: Students believe water changes its composition when ice melts, so liquid water is not the same substance as ice.

**Topic:** Water state change / Composition after melting
**Correct concept:** Ice, liquid water, and water vapor are the same substance; the molecular composition of water remains H2O across state changes.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that state changes alter physical arrangement and intermolecular attractions, while the molecule H2O itself stays the same.
**Concept category:** composition during phase change
**Prevalence:** Question 2: 12.70% alternative conception
**Population:** 64 Grade 10 Thai students
**Source:** Using Open-Ended Questions to Diagnose Students' Understanding of Inter- and Intramolecular Forces (using-open-ended-questions-to-diagnose-students-4n1is4ta1v/using-open-ended-questions-to-diagnose-students-4n1is4ta1v.md)
**Evidence:** "water will change to hydrogen gas"

## Misconception 629: Students think that when water becomes gas, the oxygen and hydrogen atoms separate and become free atoms or separate gases.

**Topic:** Water state change / Molecular breakup in gas state
**Correct concept:** When water evaporates or boils, water molecules separate from each other, but each molecule remains H2O; atoms do not split apart.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that vaporization changes the spacing and motion of molecules, not the atoms within each molecule.
**Concept category:** molecular structure during phase change
**Prevalence:** Question 3: 46.03% alternative conceptions
**Population:** 64 Grade 10 Thai students
**Source:** Using Open-Ended Questions to Diagnose Students' Understanding of Inter- and Intramolecular Forces (using-open-ended-questions-to-diagnose-students-4n1is4ta1v/using-open-ended-questions-to-diagnose-students-4n1is4ta1v.md)
**Evidence:** "oxygen atom and hydrogen atom will separate when it reaches the gas state"

## Misconception 630: Students believe that phase change produces hydrogen gas and/or oxygen gas from water.

**Topic:** Water state change / Formation of hydrogen and oxygen gases
**Correct concept:** Phase change does not decompose water; it remains H2O unless a chemical reaction occurs.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that boiling or melting is a physical change, so only the arrangement/state changes, not the chemical identity of the substance.
**Concept category:** composition during phase change
**Prevalence:** Described qualitatively in the discussion; included within Question 3 alternative conceptions (46.03%)
**Population:** 64 Grade 10 Thai students
**Source:** Using Open-Ended Questions to Diagnose Students' Understanding of Inter- and Intramolecular Forces (using-open-ended-questions-to-diagnose-students-4n1is4ta1v/using-open-ended-questions-to-diagnose-students-4n1is4ta1v.md)
**Evidence:** "water will change to hydrogen and oxygen gas when the state changed"

## Misconception 631: Students think intramolecular covalent bonds are the forces that break during a change of state.

**Topic:** Intermolecular vs intramolecular forces / Force responsible for phase change
**Correct concept:** Intermolecular forces are the attractions relevant to changes of state; intramolecular covalent bonds are not broken in ordinary phase changes.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify that the change of state depends on forces between molecules, so the correct force name is intermolecular force.
**Concept category:** force type
**Prevalence:** Question 4: 11.11% alternative conception
**Population:** 64 Grade 10 Thai students
**Source:** Using Open-Ended Questions to Diagnose Students' Understanding of Inter- and Intramolecular Forces (using-open-ended-questions-to-diagnose-students-4n1is4ta1v/using-open-ended-questions-to-diagnose-students-4n1is4ta1v.md)
**Evidence:** "the bond between N and H atom is destroyed"

## Misconception 632: Students believe ammonia is an ionic substance rather than a covalent molecular substance.

**Topic:** Substance classification and bonding / Ammonia considered ionic
**Correct concept:** Ammonia (NH3) is a covalent molecular compound; its phase behavior is governed by intermolecular forces such as hydrogen bonding.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify NH3 as molecular/covalent, then reason that phase change involves overcoming intermolecular attractions, not ionic bonding.
**Concept category:** substance classification
**Prevalence:** Question 4: included in the 11.11% alternative conception responses
**Population:** 64 Grade 10 Thai students
**Source:** Using Open-Ended Questions to Diagnose Students' Understanding of Inter- and Intramolecular Forces (using-open-ended-questions-to-diagnose-students-4n1is4ta1v/using-open-ended-questions-to-diagnose-students-4n1is4ta1v.md)
**Evidence:** "it was an ionic substance"

## Misconception 633: Students classify a covalent bond as an intermolecular force.

**Topic:** Intermolecular force types / Covalent bond treated as intermolecular force
**Correct concept:** Covalent bonds are intramolecular bonds within a molecule; intermolecular forces are separate attractions between molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish bonds within NH3 from attractions between NH3 molecules and select an intermolecular force such as hydrogen bonding.
**Concept category:** intermolecular vs intramolecular forces
**Prevalence:** Question 5: 7.94% alternative conception
**Population:** 64 Grade 10 Thai students
**Source:** Using Open-Ended Questions to Diagnose Students' Understanding of Inter- and Intramolecular Forces (using-open-ended-questions-to-diagnose-students-4n1is4ta1v/using-open-ended-questions-to-diagnose-students-4n1is4ta1v.md)
**Evidence:** "Covalence bond as an intermolecular force"

## Misconception 634: Students think the boiling point difference between ammonia and methane is explained by covalent bonding or by the number of hydrogen atoms, rather than by intermolecular forces.

**Topic:** Intermolecular force types / Wrong force named for boiling point difference
**Correct concept:** Ammonia has hydrogen bonding, while methane has only London dispersion forces; these intermolecular forces explain the boiling point difference.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the intermolecular forces present in each substance and link stronger intermolecular attraction to a higher boiling point.
**Concept category:** boiling point and intermolecular forces
**Prevalence:** Question 6: 23.81% alternative conception
**Population:** 64 Grade 10 Thai students
**Source:** Using Open-Ended Questions to Diagnose Students' Understanding of Inter- and Intramolecular Forces (using-open-ended-questions-to-diagnose-students-4n1is4ta1v/using-open-ended-questions-to-diagnose-students-4n1is4ta1v.md)
**Evidence:** "the difference of boiling point depending on the number of hydrogen atom in a molecule"

## Misconception 635: Students believe that any substance containing hydrogen must have hydrogen bonding, including methane.

**Topic:** Intermolecular force types / Hydrogen presence implies hydrogen bonding
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding requires hydrogen bonded to N, O, or F; mere presence of hydrogen is not sufficient.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should check which atom hydrogen is bonded to and whether the molecule has an appropriate electronegative partner before naming hydrogen bonding.
**Concept category:** hydrogen bonding
**Prevalence:** Question 6: included in the 23.81% alternative conception responses
**Population:** 64 Grade 10 Thai students
**Source:** Using Open-Ended Questions to Diagnose Students' Understanding of Inter- and Intramolecular Forces (using-open-ended-questions-to-diagnose-students-4n1is4ta1v/using-open-ended-questions-to-diagnose-students-4n1is4ta1v.md)
**Evidence:** "methane and ammonia have hydrogen bond because both of them have hydrogen in their molecules"

## Misconception 636: Students believe substances with covalent bonds always have the highest boiling points compared with other substances.

**Topic:** Boiling point and bonding / Covalent substances have highest boiling points
**Correct concept:** Boiling point depends on the strength of intermolecular forces and particle interactions, not simply on whether a substance is covalent.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the actual intermolecular forces present and recognize that covalent bonding within molecules does not determine ordinary boiling point by itself.
**Concept category:** boiling point and bonding
**Prevalence:** Question 6: included in the 23.81% alternative conception responses
**Population:** 64 Grade 10 Thai students
**Source:** Using Open-Ended Questions to Diagnose Students' Understanding of Inter- and Intramolecular Forces (using-open-ended-questions-to-diagnose-students-4n1is4ta1v/using-open-ended-questions-to-diagnose-students-4n1is4ta1v.md)
**Evidence:** "substance with covalent will have the highest boiling point"
